**Why correct:** PCI DSS sets security standards for organizations that handle branded credit cards.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: HIPAA covers health data.
- D: GINA covers genetic information.
- A: FISMA covers US federal agencies.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Important Laws and Regulations*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0092 — Legal and Regulatory / Cross-national compliance  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company is contracting with vendors located in three different countries. From a compliance standpoint, the security professional should:

- **A.** Consider the laws and regulations of each jurisdiction that applies to the engagement
- **B.** Assume all countries share identical regulations
- **C.** Defer entirely to whichever vendor has the lowest price
- **D.** Apply only the laws of the company's home country and ignore the rest

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** When contracting across countries, laws vary by jurisdiction, so each applicable legal framework must be understood and addressed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Home-country law alone may not satisfy foreign jurisdictions' requirements.
- B: Laws differ substantially between countries.
- C: Price has no bearing on legal compliance obligations.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Determine Compliance Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0093 — Legal and Regulatory / US financial regulations  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO regulations are most associated with the financial sector? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (GLBA)
- **B.** Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA)
- **C.** Genetic Information Nondiscrimination Act (GINA)
- **D.** Sarbanes-Oxley Act (SOX)

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** GLBA governs financial-data privacy and SOX governs corporate financial disclosures—both financial-sector focused.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: HIPAA is healthcare-focused.
- C: GINA concerns genetic-information discrimination.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Important Laws and Regulations*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0094 — Intellectual Property / Trade secret  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A company protects its proprietary manufacturing process by keeping it secret and giving it competitive advantage, without registering it publicly. This protection is a:

- **A.** Trademark
- **B.** Patent
- **C.** Copyright
- **D.** Trade secret

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A trade secret protects secret methods or processes (recipes, designs, algorithms) that give a competitive advantage, relying on secrecy rather than public registration.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A patent requires public disclosure in exchange for time-limited exclusivity.
- A: A trademark protects brand identifiers like names and logos.
- C: A copyright protects original creative/literary works.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Licensing and Property Rights*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0095 — Intellectual Property / Patent vs trademark vs copyright  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which IP protection covers brand identifiers such as a unique product name, logo, or slogan?

- **A.** Patent
- **B.** Trademark
- **C.** Trade secret
- **D.** Copyright

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A trademark protects brand identifiers like names, logos, and slogans.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A patent protects inventions/designs.
- D: A copyright protects creative/literary works.
- C: A trade secret protects confidential competitive processes.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Licensing and Property Rights*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0096 — Intellectual Property / Copyright  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Using someone else's copyrighted material generally requires:

- **A.** Nothing, since copyright never restricts reuse
- **B.** A patent filing
- **C.** A trademark registration
- **D.** Permission or attribution as required by the license

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Use of copyrighted material requires permission or attribution per the applicable terms.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Copyright does restrict reuse without permission.
- B: Patents protect inventions, not reuse of creative works.
- C: Trademarks protect brand identifiers, unrelated to copyrighted-work reuse.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Licensing and Property Rights*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0097 — Intellectual Property / EULA  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An End User License Agreement (EULA) is best described as:

- **A.** An internal company data classification label
- **B.** A government statute regulating software exports
- **C.** A binding agreement between the user and the license issuer governing product use
- **D.** A free public-domain dedication of a work

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A EULA is a binding agreement between the user and the license issuer defining how the product may be used.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Export regulation is governed by EXIM controls, not a EULA.
- D: Public-domain dedication is different from a EULA's binding terms.
- A: Classification labels are internal asset-security artifacts.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Licenses*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0098 — Intellectual Property / Perpetual license  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A license that grants lifetime authorization to use a product is a:

- **A.** Subscription license
- **B.** Trade secret
- **C.** Perpetual license
- **D.** Creative Commons license

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A perpetual license grants lifetime authorization to use the product.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A subscription license is time-limited and renews.
- D: Creative Commons is a free-use license for copyrighted materials.
- B: A trade secret is an IP protection, not a license type.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Licenses*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0099 — Intellectual Property / Creative Commons  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Creative Commons (CC) licenses are primarily used to:

- **A.** Grant free use of copyrighted materials under defined conditions
- **B.** Control the export of encryption technology
- **C.** Register a corporate trademark
- **D.** Patent a novel invention

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Creative Commons provides free-use licensing for copyrighted materials under specified conditions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Patents protect inventions, not free reuse of works.
- C: Trademark registration protects brand identifiers.
- B: Encryption export is an EXIM matter.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Licenses*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0100 — Intellectual Property / Key IP protections  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized categories of intellectual property protection? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Baseline
- **B.** Copyright
- **C.** Tactical plan
- **D.** Patent

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** Patents and copyrights are key intellectual property protections.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A baseline is a minimum set of security controls, not an IP protection.
- C: A tactical plan is a mid-term security plan, not an IP protection.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Licensing and Property Rights*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0101 — Intellectual Property / Corporate espionage  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A competitor secretly obtains a company's confidential product formulas by bribing an insider. This activity is BEST characterized as:

- **A.** A protected exercise of fair use
- **B.** Legitimate competitive intelligence gathering
- **C.** A standard licensing arrangement
- **D.** Corporate espionage, which may constitute criminal activity beyond ethical competitive research

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Secretly acquiring protected information without the owner's permission is corporate espionage, going beyond ethical research and potentially criminal.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Legitimate competitive intelligence uses public/ethical sources, not bribery and theft.
- C: No license grants access; this is unauthorized acquisition.
- A: Fair use concerns limited copyrighted-work use, not theft of trade secrets.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Corporate Espionage*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0102 — Intellectual Property / WIPO  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The World Intellectual Property Organization (WIPO) primarily:

- **A.** Provides technical assistance for international intellectual property protection
- **B.** Sets EU data-subject rights
- **C.** Enforces US federal financial-disclosure rules
- **D.** Issues payment-card security standards

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** WIPO provides technical assistance for international IP protection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Financial-disclosure rules are SOX (US).
- D: Card standards are PCI DSS.
- B: EU data-subject rights come from GDPR.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > International Protection of Intellectual Property*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0103 — Export and Import Controls / EXIM purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Export and Import Controls (EXIM) primarily aim to:

- **A.** Set minimum password complexity requirements
- **B.** Prevent unauthorized access to trade secrets, controlled technologies, and sensitive data across borders
- **C.** Define data-subject rights for EU residents
- **D.** Standardize corporate financial reporting

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** EXIM controls regulate cross-border transfer of technology, goods, and services to prevent unauthorized access to controlled technologies and sensitive data.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Financial reporting is SOX, not EXIM.
- C: EU data-subject rights are GDPR.
- A: Password rules are technical/operational controls, not EXIM.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Export and Import Controls*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0104 — Export and Import Controls / Wassenaar Arrangement  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The Wassenaar Arrangement is most relevant to which security-related export concern?

- **A.** Accuracy of public companies' financial statements
- **B.** Protection of US health information
- **C.** Privacy rights of EU data subjects
- **D.** Controls on the export of items such as encryption technology across borders

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The Wassenaar Arrangement governs export controls, including categories covering encryption technology (e.g., Category 5).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: EU privacy rights come from GDPR.
- A: Financial-statement accuracy is SOX.
- B: Health-information protection is HIPAA.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Export and Import Controls*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0105 — Export and Import Controls / Dual-use goods  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Mechanical components that could be used in both civilian products and military systems are an example of:

- **A.** A perpetual license
- **B.** Data localization
- **C.** A privacy impact assessment
- **D.** Dual-use goods

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Dual-use goods have both civilian and military applications and are therefore subject to special export controls.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Data localization concerns keeping data within a country's borders.
- A: A perpetual license is a software-use term.
- C: A PIA assesses privacy controls.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Export and Import Controls*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0106 — Export and Import Controls / Data localization  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A country requires that its citizens' personal data be stored on servers physically located within its borders. This requirement is known as:

- **A.** Data portability
- **B.** Data classification
- **C.** Transborder data flow
- **D.** Data localization

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Data localization mandates that certain data be stored (and sometimes processed) within a specific country's borders.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Transborder data flow refers to data crossing borders, the opposite emphasis.
- A: Data portability is a GDPR right to reuse data across services.
- B: Data classification is labeling data by sensitivity.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Export and Import Controls*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0107 — Export and Import Controls / Encryption export  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A US company plans to ship its strong-encryption software to customers in several countries. To avoid violating export controls, it should FIRST:

- **A.** Verify compliance with applicable export-control categories (e.g., Wassenaar Category 5) before shipping
- **B.** Remove all encryption to avoid any controls, regardless of customer needs
- **C.** Ship immediately and address any legal issues only if challenged
- **D.** Assume software is exempt because it is intangible

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Encryption software exported without proper compliance can violate export-control categories (e.g., Wassenaar Category 5 Part 2), so compliance must be verified first.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Shipping first and reacting later risks legal violations and penalties.
- B: Stripping needed functionality is not a compliance strategy and harms customers.
- D: Software, including intangible delivery, is subject to export controls.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Export and Import Controls*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0108 — Cybercrime and Threat Actors / Active vs passive attacks  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An attacker passively eavesdrops on network traffic to learn information without altering any system resources. This is a:

- **A.** Passive attack
- **B.** Zero-day attack
- **C.** Destruction crime
- **D.** Active attack

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A passive attack attempts to learn or use information from the system without affecting its resources.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: An active attack alters system resources or affects operation.
- B: A zero-day exploits a previously unknown vulnerability, not defined by passivity.
- C: A destruction crime deliberately destroys data or systems.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Cyber Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0109 — Cybercrime and Threat Actors / Zero-day  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A zero-day attack occurs when attackers exploit:

- **A.** A misconfigured but documented setting
- **B.** A vulnerability that was patched years ago
- **C.** A user who shares their password willingly
- **D.** A previously unknown vulnerability that developers have not yet patched

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A zero-day exploits a previously unknown vulnerability that developers have not had time to address and patch.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: An old patched vulnerability is not a zero-day.
- A: A documented misconfiguration is a known issue, not a zero-day.
- C: That describes social engineering, not a zero-day.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Cyber Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0110 — Cybercrime and Threat Actors / Insider threat  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A disgruntled systems administrator with legitimate network access copies confidential files to sell to a competitor. This threat actor is BEST classified as:

- **A.** Hacktivist
- **B.** Script kiddie
- **C.** State actor
- **D.** Insider threat

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An insider threat is an employee who has access to corporate networks and might exploit that access to steal information.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A script kiddie uses prebuilt tools and lacks sophisticated access/expertise; the key here is trusted insider access.
- C: A state actor is government-sponsored; nothing indicates that.
- A: A hacktivist is motivated by a cause; here the motive is personal/financial misuse of insider access.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Threat Actors*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0111 — Cybercrime and Threat Actors / Script kiddie  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

An individual who uses existing scripts or tools to attack systems but lacks sophisticated expertise is a:

- **A.** State actor
- **B.** Script kiddie
- **C.** Insider threat
- **D.** Data steward

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A script kiddie uses existing scripts or code to hack, lacking sophisticated expertise.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A state actor is a sophisticated government-sponsored attacker.
- C: An insider threat misuses legitimate access.
- D: A data steward is an internal data-expertise role, not a threat actor.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Threat Actors*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0112 — Cybercrime and Threat Actors / Threat actor types  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are types of threat actors described in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** State actors
- **B.** Insider threats
- **C.** Tactical plans
- **D.** Baselines

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** State actors (government-sponsored) and insider threats (trusted employees who misuse access) are both threat-actor types.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A baseline is a minimum set of controls, not a threat actor.
- C: A tactical plan is a planning horizon, not a threat actor.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Threat Actors*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0113 — Cybercrime and Threat Actors / State actor  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A highly resourced, government-sponsored group conducts a long-term espionage campaign against a defense contractor. This threat actor is BEST described as a:

- **A.** Script kiddie
- **B.** State actor
- **C.** Disgruntled customer
- **D.** Casual hacker

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** State actors are government-sponsored groups engaging in espionage and other cyber operations, typically well-resourced and persistent.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Script kiddies lack the sophistication and resources described.
- D: A casual hacker would not mount a sustained, well-funded espionage campaign.
- C: A disgruntled customer does not match a government-sponsored espionage profile.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Threat Actors*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0114 — Investigations / Investigation types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An internal investigation conducted to determine whether an employee violated company policy, used to support an HR decision, is which type of investigation?

- **A.** Administrative
- **B.** Civil
- **C.** Regulatory
- **D.** Criminal

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Administrative investigations address internal policy violations and support internal/organizational decisions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Criminal investigations involve potential violations of criminal law and law enforcement.
- B: Civil investigations involve disputes between parties (e.g., contracts, damages).
- C: Regulatory investigations involve compliance with a regulator's rules.

*Source: Domain 1 > Incident Management > Investigations (administrative, criminal, civil, regulatory)*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0115 — Investigations / Standards of proof  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A case that could send a defendant to prison generally requires a higher standard of proof than a lawsuit seeking monetary damages. This contrast is BETWEEN which two investigation/case types?

- **A.** Criminal (beyond a reasonable doubt) vs civil (preponderance of the evidence)
- **B.** Civil vs Creative Commons
- **C.** Regulatory vs perpetual
- **D.** Administrative vs awareness

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Criminal cases require the highest standard (beyond a reasonable doubt), while civil cases use the lower preponderance-of-the-evidence standard.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: "Awareness" is a training concept, not a case type.
- C: "Perpetual" is a license type, not a case standard.
- B: Creative Commons is a license, not an investigation type.

*Source: Domain 1 > Incident Management > Investigations (administrative, criminal, civil, regulatory)*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0116 — Investigations / Regulatory investigation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An inquiry initiated by a government regulator to determine whether an organization complied with a specific industry rule is a(n):

- **A.** Administrative investigation
- **B.** Regulatory investigation
- **C.** Criminal investigation
- **D.** Civil investigation

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A regulatory investigation examines compliance with a regulator's rules or standards for an industry.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Administrative investigations are internal policy matters.
- C: Criminal investigations pursue violations of criminal law.
- D: Civil investigations concern disputes between private parties.

*Source: Domain 1 > Incident Management > Investigations (administrative, criminal, civil, regulatory)*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0117 — Security Documentation / Policy vs standard vs procedure  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which security document provides detailed, step-by-step instructions on how to implement a standard?

- **A.** Procedure
- **B.** Guideline
- **C.** Baseline
- **D.** Policy

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A procedure provides detailed, step-by-step instructions on how to implement standards.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A policy is a high-level statement of management intent and goals.
- C: A baseline defines the minimum security controls required to meet a standard.
- B: A guideline offers recommendations/best practices when standards are unclear or non-specific.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Security Compliance Artifacts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0118 — Security Documentation / Baseline  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The minimum set of security controls required to meet a standard, adjustable only through formal change management, is a:

- **A.** Procedure
- **B.** Policy
- **C.** Guideline
- **D.** Baseline

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A baseline is the minimum security controls required to meet standards, changeable through formal change management.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A guideline is a non-mandatory recommendation.
- A: A procedure is step-by-step implementation detail.
- B: A policy is high-level management direction.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Security Compliance Artifacts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0119 — Security Documentation / Guideline  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which document type is a recommendation or best practice used when standards or procedures are unclear or non-specific, and is generally NOT mandatory?

- **A.** Standard
- **B.** Guideline
- **C.** Baseline
- **D.** Procedure

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A guideline provides recommendations/best practices when standards or procedures are unclear or non-specific, and is advisory rather than mandatory.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A standard is a mandatory, specific requirement.
- C: A baseline is a mandatory minimum control set.
- D: A procedure is mandatory step-by-step instruction.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Security Compliance Artifacts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0120 — Security Documentation / Standard  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A document that sets specific, mandatory requirements aligning with organizational policy (for example, requiring AES-256 for data at rest) is a:

- **A.** Guideline
- **B.** Policy
- **C.** Standard
- **D.** Baseline

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A standard sets specific mandatory requirements that align with and detail how to comply with policy.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A guideline is advisory, not mandatory.
- B: A policy is higher-level intent, not the specific requirement.
- D: A baseline is the minimum control set; the specific mandatory requirement is the standard.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Security Compliance Artifacts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0121 — Security Documentation / Policy types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A policy that defines the organization's overall security goals and responsibilities at the highest level is a(n):

- **A.** Acceptable use procedure
- **B.** Issue-specific policy
- **C.** System-specific policy
- **D.** Organizational (program) policy

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An organizational (program) policy defines organization-wide security goals and responsibilities.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: An issue-specific policy addresses a particular topic (e.g., email use).
- C: A system-specific policy applies to a particular system.
- A: A procedure is implementation detail, not a policy tier.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Types of Policies*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0122 — Security Documentation / Document hierarchy  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO statements about security documentation are correct? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Standards are mandatory and detail specific requirements that support policy
- **B.** Baselines are optional suggestions that require no change control
- **C.** Guidelines are advisory recommendations used when standards are unclear
- **D.** Policies are step-by-step technical instructions for administrators

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** Standards are mandatory and specific; guidelines are advisory best practices—both stated correctly.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Policies are high-level intent; procedures (not policies) are step-by-step instructions.
- B: Baselines are mandatory minimums adjusted only through formal change management, not optional.

*Source: Domain 1 > Security Governance and Compliance > Security Compliance Artifacts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0123 — Risk Management Concepts / Risk formula  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

According to the guide, risk arises from the combination of:

- **A.** Policies and procedures
- **B.** Awareness and training
- **C.** Threats and vulnerabilities
- **D.** Patents and trademarks

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Threats and vulnerabilities together create risk; risk management identifies, assesses, and mitigates that risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Policies and procedures are governance documents, not the components of risk.
- B: Awareness and training are controls that reduce risk, not its definition.
- D: Patents and trademarks are IP protections, unrelated to the risk formula.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Management Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0124 — Risk Management Concepts / Risk response - transfer  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A company purchases cyber-insurance so that a third party bears much of the financial loss from a potential breach. Which risk response is this?

- **A.** Risk avoidance
- **B.** Risk acceptance
- **C.** Risk assignment (transfer)
- **D.** Risk deterrence

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Transferring the responsibility/accountability of a risk to another party (e.g., via insurance) is risk assignment (transfer).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Avoidance means not undertaking the risky activity at all.
- B: Acceptance means acknowledging and bearing the residual risk.
- D: Deterrence implements controls to discourage the threat, not shift the loss.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Response and Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0125 — Risk Management Concepts / Risk response - avoidance  _(medium, scenario_based)_

After assessing the risk, a company decides NOT to launch a new product line that would expose it to unacceptable regulatory risk. Which risk response is this?

- **A.** Risk acceptance
- **B.** Risk assignment
- **C.** Risk mitigation
- **D.** Risk avoidance

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Choosing not to engage in an activity that introduces higher risk is risk avoidance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Acceptance would mean proceeding and bearing the residual risk.
- C: Mitigation would mean proceeding but reducing the risk with controls.
- B: Assignment would mean transferring the risk to another party.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Response and Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0126 — Risk Management Concepts / Residual risk & acceptance  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

After applying controls, management formally acknowledges and decides to live with the remaining risk. The remaining risk and the decision are, respectively:

- **A.** Inherent risk; risk avoidance
- **B.** Residual risk; risk acceptance
- **C.** Threat; risk deterrence
- **D.** Total risk; risk assignment

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The risk left after responses are applied is residual risk; choosing to live with it is risk acceptance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Inherent risk is the risk before controls; avoidance is declining the activity.
- D: Total risk is before controls; assignment transfers risk to another party.
- C: A threat is a potential cause of harm; deterrence discourages it.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Response and Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0127 — Risk Management Concepts / Risk mitigation  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Implementing controls to reduce a risk to an acceptable level is known as:

- **A.** Risk transfer
- **B.** Risk acceptance
- **C.** Risk avoidance
- **D.** Risk mitigation

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Risk mitigation reduces risk to an acceptable level through various controls.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Acceptance bears the risk without reducing it.
- C: Avoidance declines the risky activity entirely.
- A: Transfer shifts the risk to another party.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Response and Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0128 — Risk Management Concepts / Risk response strategies  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are recognized risk response strategies in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Risk amplification
- **B.** Risk capitalization
- **C.** Risk deterrence
- **D.** Risk acceptance

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** Risk deterrence (deterrent controls to reduce likelihood/impact) and risk acceptance (bearing residual risk) are recognized responses.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: "Risk amplification" is not a recognized response—organizations seek to reduce, not amplify, risk.
- B: "Risk capitalization" is not a defined risk response strategy.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Response and Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0129 — Controls and Countermeasures / Control categories  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A security awareness policy that employees must follow is which CATEGORY of control?

- **A.** Physical
- **B.** Technical (logical)
- **C.** Recovery
- **D.** Administrative

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Administrative controls are procedures and processes (soft/process controls), such as policies and training.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Technical/logical controls protect assets through hardware/software mechanisms.
- A: Physical controls protect tangible assets and facilities.
- C: "Recovery" is a control TYPE (function), not one of the three categories.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Controls and Countermeasures*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0130 — Controls and Countermeasures / Control types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A locked door with a "Authorized Personnel Only" sign that discourages intruders before any attempt is primarily which TYPE of control?

- **A.** Compensating
- **B.** Corrective
- **C.** Recovery
- **D.** Deterrent

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Deterrent controls discourage unauthorized actions before they occur; signage that dissuades intrusion is deterrent.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Corrective controls modify the environment to fix issues after an event.
- C: Recovery controls restore operations after an event.
- A: Compensating controls substitute for a primary control that is infeasible.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Types of Controls*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0131 — Controls and Countermeasures / Control types  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization cannot apply the required encryption on a legacy system, so it isolates that system on a restricted network segment to provide equivalent protection. The network isolation is BEST classified as which type of control?

- **A.** Detective
- **B.** Directive
- **C.** Deterrent
- **D.** Compensating

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A compensating control provides an alternative that supports or substitutes for a primary control that cannot be implemented.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Detective controls discover events after they occur; isolation is preventive/compensating here.
- C: Deterrent controls discourage actions psychologically; isolation actively restricts access.
- B: Directive controls direct behavior via policy; this is a technical substitute control.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Types of Controls*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0132 — Controls and Countermeasures / Defense in depth  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Combining administrative, technical, and physical controls in multiple layers so that no single failure exposes the asset is the strategy known as:

- **A.** Defense in depth
- **B.** Risk acceptance
- **C.** Data localization
- **D.** Single sign-on

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Defense in depth layers multiple, varied controls so the failure of one does not compromise the whole.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Single sign-on is an authentication convenience, not a layered-defense strategy.
- B: Risk acceptance is a risk response, not a control architecture.
- C: Data localization concerns where data is stored geographically.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Controls and Countermeasures*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0133 — Controls and Countermeasures / Cost-benefit principle  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A proposed safeguard costs $50,000 per year to protect an asset whose total value is $20,000. According to the cost-benefit principle, the organization should:

- **A.** Reconsider, because a control should not cost more than the value of the asset it protects
- **B.** Implement it immediately, since more security is always better
- **C.** Transfer the asset's ownership to avoid the decision
- **D.** Implement it because all assets must have maximum protection

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The cost-benefit principle states a control's cost should not exceed the asset value or the benefit gained; spending $50k to protect a $20k asset is not justified.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: "More is always better" ignores cost-benefit and wastes resources.
- D: Maximum protection regardless of value contradicts the cost-benefit principle.
- C: Transferring ownership does not address the disproportionate safeguard cost.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Controls and Countermeasures*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0134 — Controls and Countermeasures / Control categories  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are categories (not types) of security controls? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Technical (logical) controls
- **B.** Physical controls
- **C.** Corrective controls
- **D.** Detective controls

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** Technical/logical and physical are control CATEGORIES (alongside administrative). Detective and corrective are control TYPES/functions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Detective is a control type/function, not a category.
- C: Corrective is a control type/function, not a category.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Controls and Countermeasures*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0135 — Continuous Monitoring / NIST SP 800-137  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which framework is identified as the primary CISSP-exam reference for Information Security Continuous Monitoring (ISCM)?

- **A.** NIST SP 800-30
- **B.** ISO 28000
- **C.** PCI DSS
- **D.** NIST SP 800-137

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-137 (ISCM) is highlighted as the primary continuous-monitoring reference for the exam.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: ISO 28000 is a supply-chain security management standard.
- C: PCI DSS is a payment-card security standard.
- A: NIST SP 800-30 covers risk assessment, not continuous monitoring specifically.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Continuous Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0136 — Continuous Monitoring / Scope  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

How does continuous monitoring (continuous improvement) differ from simple system monitoring?

- **A.** It evaluates the effectiveness of all types of security controls, not just log/system status review
- **B.** It replaces the need for any security controls
- **C.** It only runs once during system authorization
- **D.** It is limited strictly to reviewing firewall logs

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Continuous monitoring focuses on evaluating security control implementations and effectiveness across all control types—broader than log-file or system-status review.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: It is not restricted to firewall logs or log review.
- C: It is ongoing, not a one-time authorization activity.
- B: It assesses controls; it does not replace them.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Continuous Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0137 — Risk Frameworks / NIST RMF steps  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Place the following NIST Risk Management Framework (RMF) steps in their correct order, beginning after the Prepare step. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Implement
- **B.** Monitor
- **C.** Assess
- **D.** Categorize
- **E.** Select
- **F.** Authorize

**Correct answer:** D,E,A,C,F,B

**Why correct:** Following Prepare, the RMF proceeds: Categorize the system, Select controls, Implement them, Assess effectiveness, Authorize operation based on residual risk, then Monitor continuously.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Frameworks*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0138 — Risk Frameworks / NIST CSF core functions  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which set correctly lists the five core functions of the NIST Cybersecurity Framework (CSF)?

- **A.** Avoid, Transfer, Mitigate, Accept, Deter
- **B.** Plan, Do, Check, Act, Review
- **C.** Prepare, Categorize, Select, Implement, Authorize
- **D.** Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, Recover

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The NIST CSF core functions are Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, and Recover.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Plan-Do-Check-Act is the Deming cycle, not the CSF core.
- C: Those are RMF steps, not CSF functions.
- A: Those are risk-response strategies, not CSF functions.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > NIST Cybersecurity Framework*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0139 — Risk Frameworks / COSO vs ISO 31000  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which framework is an enterprise risk management framework used primarily for corporate governance and compliance with Sarbanes-Oxley?

- **A.** NIST SP 800-137
- **B.** SABSA
- **C.** COSO
- **D.** ISO 31000

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** COSO is an enterprise risk management framework used primarily for corporate governance and SOX compliance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: ISO 31000 is a general, industry-agnostic risk management framework.
- A: NIST SP 800-137 covers continuous monitoring.
- B: SABSA is a business-driven security architecture framework.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Risk Frameworks*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0140 — Risk Frameworks / SABSA  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

SABSA is best described as a framework for:

- **A.** Protecting payment card data in retail environments
- **B.** Governing the export of dual-use technologies
- **C.** Defining EU data-subject privacy rights
- **D.** Developing business-driven, risk-focused security architectures aligned to business objectives

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SABSA provides a structured approach to business-driven, risk-focused security architecture that traces decisions back to business requirements.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Payment-card protection is PCI DSS.
- B: Dual-use export is governed by EXIM/Wassenaar.
- C: EU privacy rights come from GDPR.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Understanding SABSA*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0141 — Risk Frameworks / ISO/IEC 27001  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

ISO/IEC 27001 is primarily a standard for:

- **A.** Securing the physical supply chain of goods
- **B.** Rating the severity of software vulnerabilities
- **C.** Establishing and managing an information security management system (ISMS) to manage information security risk
- **D.** Controlling cross-border encryption exports

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** ISO/IEC 27001 defines requirements for an ISMS to manage information security risks and ensure compliance, grounded in the CIA triad.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Supply-chain security management is ISO 28000.
- B: Vulnerability severity rating is associated with CVSS, not ISO 27001.
- D: Encryption export is an EXIM/Wassenaar matter.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > ISO/IEC 27001*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0142 — Quantitative Risk / SLE calculation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An asset is valued at $200,000. A particular fire scenario is expected to destroy 25% of the asset. What is the Single Loss Expectancy (SLE)?

- **A.** $800,000
- **B.** $50,000
- **C.** $25,000
- **D.** $200,000

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** SLE = Asset Value × Exposure Factor = $200,000 × 0.25 = $50,000.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: $25,000 would result from a 12.5% EF, not 25%.
- D: $200,000 is the full asset value (100% EF), not 25%.
- A: $800,000 incorrectly multiplies rather than applies the 25% exposure factor.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0143 — Quantitative Risk / ALE calculation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A single loss event costs $50,000 (SLE) and is expected to occur twice per year (ARO = 2). What is the Annualized Loss Expectancy (ALE)?

- **A.** $25,000
- **B.** $100,000
- **C.** $50,000
- **D.** $2,000

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** ALE = SLE × ARO = $50,000 × 2 = $100,000.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: $50,000 is the SLE for a single occurrence, not annualized.
- A: $25,000 would halve rather than double the SLE.
- D: $2,000 confuses the ARO value with a dollar amount.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0144 — Quantitative Risk / EF definition  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The Exposure Factor (EF) represents:

- **A.** The total monetary cost of acquiring the asset
- **B.** The annual cost of a safeguard
- **C.** The number of times per year a risk occurs
- **D.** The percentage of an asset's value likely to be lost if a risk is realized

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The EF is the percentage of asset value that could be lost when a risk is realized.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: That describes the Annualized Rate of Occurrence (ARO).
- A: That describes the Asset Value (AV).
- B: That describes the annual cost of a safeguard.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0145 — Quantitative Risk / ARO definition  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The Annualized Rate of Occurrence (ARO) is:

- **A.** The estimated frequency at which a specific risk is expected to occur within a year
- **B.** The expected loss from a single event
- **C.** The percentage of asset value lost per event
- **D.** The total value of the asset

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** ARO is the estimated number of times a specific risk is expected to occur within a year.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: That is the Exposure Factor (EF).
- D: That is the Asset Value (AV).
- B: That is the Single Loss Expectancy (SLE).

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0146 — Quantitative Risk / Calculation sequence  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A server worth $40,000 faces a threat with a 50% exposure factor that is expected to occur 3 times per year. What is the ALE?

- **A.** $20,000
- **B.** $120,000
- **C.** $6,000
- **D.** $60,000

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SLE = $40,000 × 0.50 = $20,000; ALE = SLE × ARO = $20,000 × 3 = $60,000.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: $20,000 is the SLE, not the annualized figure.
- B: $120,000 applies the full asset value (100% EF) instead of 50%.
- C: $6,000 misplaces a decimal in the calculation.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0147 — Quantitative Risk / Quantitative vs qualitative  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which statement BEST distinguishes quantitative from qualitative risk assessment?

- **A.** They produce identical outputs and are interchangeable
- **B.** Quantitative is always faster and requires no data
- **C.** Quantitative uses numerical/monetary values; qualitative uses subjective rankings such as high/medium/low
- **D.** Quantitative uses color codes; qualitative uses precise dollar figures

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Quantitative assessment uses numerical/monetary values (AV, SLE, ALE), while qualitative uses subjective judgment and categories like high/low/moderate.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Reversed: color codes/categories are qualitative; dollar figures are quantitative.
- B: Quantitative analysis requires substantial data and is not effort-free.
- A: They differ in method and output and are not interchangeable.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0148 — Quantitative Risk / Calculation order  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO equations are correct as defined in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** SLE = Asset Value × Exposure Factor
- **B.** ALE = SLE × Annualized Rate of Occurrence
- **C.** SLE = ALE × Annualized Rate of Occurrence
- **D.** ALE = Asset Value × Exposure Factor

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** SLE = AV × EF and ALE = SLE × ARO are the correct definitions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: AV × EF yields SLE, not ALE.
- C: This inverts the relationship; ALE is derived from SLE, not vice versa.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0149 — Qualitative Risk / Delphi technique  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Delphi technique supports qualitative risk assessment by:

- **A.** Gathering anonymous expert consensus to reduce bias and undue influence
- **B.** Automatically scanning systems for vulnerabilities
- **C.** Encrypting data at rest
- **D.** Calculating exact monetary loss figures

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The Delphi technique gathers consensus among experts through anonymous feedback, helping make unbiased decisions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Exact monetary figures are the realm of quantitative assessment.
- B: Vulnerability scanning is a technical assessment activity, not Delphi.
- C: Encryption is a control, not a consensus technique.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Conducting Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0150 — Risk Assessment Process / Risk tolerance/appetite  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The level of risk an organization is willing to accept is referred to as its:

- **A.** Risk tolerance (risk appetite)
- **B.** Annualized loss expectancy
- **C.** Exposure factor
- **D.** Residual control

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Risk tolerance/risk appetite is the level of risk an organization is willing to accept.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Exposure factor is the percentage of asset value lost in an event.
- D: "Residual control" is not a defined term; residual risk is what remains after controls.
- B: ALE is a calculated annual loss figure, not a willingness threshold.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Understanding Risk Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0151 — Risk Assessment Process / NIST SP 800-30  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Under NIST SP 800-30, after conducting the assessment a team must communicate findings to senior management. The guidance emphasizes that this reporting should be:

- **A.** Limited to a single numerical score with no context
- **B.** Withheld from management to avoid alarm
- **C.** Expressed in understandable business language to aid management decision-making
- **D.** Written only in deep technical jargon for engineers

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-30 reporting communicates findings to senior management in understandable business language to aid decision-making.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Technical jargon impairs management decision-making.
- B: Withholding findings defeats the purpose of the assessment.
- A: A bare score without context does not support informed decisions.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > NIST Special Publication 800-30 Risk Assessment Steps*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0152 — Threat Modeling / STRIDE  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In the STRIDE threat-modeling methodology, the "R" represents which threat category?

- **A.** Redundancy
- **B.** Recovery
- **C.** Repudiation
- **D.** Reconnaissance

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** STRIDE stands for Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information disclosure, Denial of service, and Elevation of privilege; "R" is Repudiation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Reconnaissance is an attack phase, not a STRIDE element.
- B: Recovery is a control function, not a STRIDE element.
- A: Redundancy is an availability technique, not a STRIDE element.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Threat Modeling Methodologies*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0153 — Threat Modeling / Methodology selection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which of the following is a recognized threat-modeling methodology referenced in the guide?

- **A.** PIPEDA
- **B.** PERT
- **C.** PCI DSS
- **D.** PASTA

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** PASTA (Process for Attack Simulation and Threat Analysis) is a recognized threat-modeling methodology, alongside STRIDE and DREAD.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: PIPEDA is a Canadian privacy law.
- C: PCI DSS is a payment-card security standard.
- B: PERT is a project-scheduling technique, not threat modeling.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Threat Modeling Methodologies*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0154 — Threat Modeling / Attack surface  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In threat modeling, the sum of all points where an attacker could attempt to enter or extract data from a system is the:

- **A.** Exposure factor
- **B.** Attack surface
- **C.** Security baseline
- **D.** Residual risk

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The attack surface is the total set of points (interfaces, inputs) through which an attacker could interact with or compromise a system.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Exposure factor is a percentage of loss in quantitative risk.
- D: Residual risk is the risk remaining after controls.
- C: A baseline is a minimum control set.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Threat Modeling Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0155 — Threat Modeling / Proactive vs reactive  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A development team performs threat modeling during the DESIGN phase of a new application, before any code is written, to anticipate attacks. This approach is BEST described as:

- **A.** Penetration testing
- **B.** Incident response
- **C.** Proactive threat modeling
- **D.** Reactive threat modeling

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Anticipating threats early (e.g., at design, before exploitation) is a proactive approach to threat modeling.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A reactive approach responds after threats or incidents have manifested.
- A: Penetration testing actively tests a running system, not design-phase modeling.
- B: Incident response handles events after they occur.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Assessment > Threat Modeling Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0156 — Supply Chain Risk Management / SCRM definition  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Supply Chain Risk Management (SCRM) primarily addresses risks arising from:

- **A.** An organization's dependence on external vendors and suppliers for goods and services
- **B.** Employees' personal travel itineraries
- **C.** The classification level of internal documents
- **D.** The accuracy of corporate financial statements

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** SCRM addresses security risks in the supply chain due to dependencies on external sources for operational components, goods, and services.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Travel safety is a personnel-security topic, not SCRM.
- D: Financial-statement accuracy is a SOX/governance concern.
- C: Document classification is an asset-security activity.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Supply Chain Risk Management*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0157 — Supply Chain Risk Management / Supply chain risk types  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized categories of supply-chain risk described in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Awareness risks such as fewer posters in the lobby
- **B.** Software risks such as vulnerabilities or malicious code
- **C.** Risk appetite risks such as choosing the wrong color code
- **D.** Hardware risks such as counterfeit components

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** The guide lists hardware risks (counterfeits, embedded spyware), software risks (vulnerabilities, malcode, licensing), and service risks as supply-chain risk categories.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: "Risk appetite color codes" relate to qualitative assessment, not a supply-chain risk category.
- A: Poster counts relate to awareness delivery, not supply-chain risk.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Supply Chain Risk Management*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0158 — Supply Chain Risk Management / SLAs and assessments  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization wants to hold a critical cloud supplier contractually accountable for specific security requirements and define how compliance will be verified. The MOST appropriate mechanism is to:

- **A.** Assume the vendor's marketing claims are sufficient assurance
- **B.** Rely on a verbal understanding with the vendor's sales representative
- **C.** Avoid documenting requirements to remain flexible
- **D.** Establish a Service Level Agreement (SLA) that defines the security requirements and verification

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SLAs with suppliers define specific security requirements and acquisition policies, and support assessment of compliance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Verbal understandings are unenforceable and unverifiable.
- A: Marketing claims are not contractual assurance.
- C: Failing to document requirements undermines accountability and verification.

*Source: Domain 1 > Risk Management > Supply Chain Risk Management*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0159 — Business Continuity / BC requirements  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Objective 1.7 focuses on identifying, analyzing, assessing, prioritizing, and implementing requirements for:

- **A.** Marketing campaigns
- **B.** Cryptographic key generation
- **C.** Business Continuity (BC)
- **D.** Software licensing

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Objective 1.7 concerns identifying, analyzing, assessing, prioritizing, and implementing Business Continuity (BC) requirements.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Software licensing is part of legal/IP topics.
- B: Key generation is a cryptography topic.
- A: Marketing campaigns are unrelated to continuity planning.

*Source: Domain 1 > Business Continuity and Disaster Recovery > Business Continuity (BC) requirements*

---

### CISSP-D01-Q0160 — Business Continuity / Prioritization via impact  _(hard, scenario_based)_

When implementing Business Continuity requirements, an organization must decide which processes to restore first after a disruption. This prioritization should be driven primarily by:

- **A.** The alphabetical order of system names
- **B.** Which department complained most loudly
- **C.** The relative criticality and impact of each business process on the organization
- **D.** The personal preference of the newest employee

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** BC requirements are prioritized by analyzing and assessing the criticality and impact of business processes, so the most critical are restored first.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Loudness of complaints is not an objective impact-based criterion.
- A: Alphabetical ordering ignores business criticality.
- D: Personal preference is not a valid prioritization basis.

*Source: Domain 1 > Business Continuity and Disaster Recovery > Business Continuity (BC) requirements*

---


## Domain 2: Asset Security

### CISSP-D02-Q0001 — Asset Classification / Purpose of classification  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

What is the PRIMARY purpose of classifying information and assets?

- **A.** To make all data publicly accessible by default
- **B.** To increase the total number of assets an organization owns
- **C.** To determine how data should be handled and secured based on its criticality, sensitivity, and value
- **D.** To eliminate the need for any security controls

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Classification identifies and ranks information/assets by criticality, sensitivity, and value so the right handling and protection are applied.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Classification organizes and protects existing assets; it does not increase asset count.
- D: Classification drives appropriate controls; it does not remove the need for them.
- A: Classification restricts sensitive data; it does not make everything public.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classifying Data and Assets*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0002 — Asset Classification / Classification criteria  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

When assigning a classification level, an organization should evaluate data primarily on the basis of its:

- **A.** File size and storage format
- **B.** Number of employees who created it
- **C.** Criticality, sensitivity, and value to the organization
- **D.** Age of the storage hardware

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Data is classified based on its criticality, sensitivity, and value to the organization, which determine the required protection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: File size/format does not determine sensitivity or required protection.
- D: Hardware age is an asset-lifecycle concern, not a classification criterion.
- B: The number of creators is irrelevant to data sensitivity.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classifying Data and Assets*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0003 — Asset Classification / Government classification levels  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the government classification scheme described in the guide, which level applies to information whose disclosure would cause EXTREMELY high impact and the most significant damage?

- **A.** Secret
- **B.** Unclassified
- **C.** Top Secret
- **D.** Confidential

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Top Secret denotes extremely high impact on disclosure with significant damage potential—the highest level.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Secret causes serious (high) damage, but less than Top Secret.
- D: Confidential causes moderate/noticeable damage.
- B: Unclassified has low or no impact.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classification Levels*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0004 — Asset Classification / Government classification levels  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which government classification level corresponds to information that would cause MODERATE impact (noticeable damage) if disclosed?

- **A.** Top Secret
- **B.** Secret
- **C.** Confidential
- **D.** Unclassified

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Confidential corresponds to moderate impact—noticeable damage if disclosed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Top Secret is extremely high impact.
- B: Secret is high impact with serious damage.
- D: Unclassified is low or no impact.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classification Levels*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0005 — Asset Classification / Classification levels mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each government classification level (1-4) to the impact of its disclosure (A-D).

1. Top Secret
2. Secret
3. Confidential
4. Unclassified

- **A.** Extremely high impact; significant damage potential
- **B.** Low or no impact; minimal value information
- **C.** Moderate impact; noticeable damage
- **D.** High impact; serious damage if disclosed

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-D,3-C,4-B

**Why correct:** Top Secret → extremely high impact (B); Secret → high/serious damage (C); Confidential → moderate/noticeable (D); Unclassified → low/no impact (A).

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classification Levels*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0006 — Asset Classification / Commercial vs government labels  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A private retail company wants a classification scheme that fits its business rather than military labels. Which set of levels is MOST appropriate for a commercial organization?

- **A.** No classification, since commercial data is never sensitive
- **B.** Top Secret, Secret, Confidential (military-only labels)
- **C.** Public, Internal, Sensitive (commercial tiers aligned to business needs)
- **D.** Tier 0, Tier 1, Tier 2 hardware tiers used for data sensitivity

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Commercial organizations typically use business-aligned levels such as public, internal, and sensitive; classification should align with business needs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Top Secret/Secret/Confidential are government/military labels, not the natural commercial fit.
- D: Tier 0/1/2 are asset (system) tiers, not data sensitivity labels.
- A: Commercial data (e.g., PII, proprietary data) is frequently sensitive and must be classified.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classifying Data and Assets*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0007 — Asset Classification / Asset classification tiers  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Using the asset classification tiers in the guide, critical servers, databases, and network devices belong to which tier?

- **A.** Tier 2 (Non-Essential)
- **B.** Tier 1 (Important)
- **C.** Tier 3 (Disposable)
- **D.** Tier 0 (Essential)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Tier 0 (Essential) covers critical servers, databases, and network devices.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Tier 1 (Important) covers development environments and backup systems.
- A: Tier 2 (Non-Essential) covers workstations, mobile devices, and printers.
- C: "Tier 3 (Disposable)" is not a tier defined in the guide.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Asset Classification Tiers*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0008 — Asset Classification / Asset classification tiers  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Workstations, mobile devices, and printers are categorized in which asset tier?

- **A.** Tier 1 (Important)
- **B.** Tier 0 (Critical-Plus)
- **C.** Tier 0 (Essential)
- **D.** Tier 2 (Non-Essential)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Tier 2 (Non-Essential) includes workstations, mobile devices, and printers.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Tier 0 is essential infrastructure such as critical servers and databases.
- A: Tier 1 is important systems like development environments and backups.
- B: "Tier 0 (Critical-Plus)" is not a defined tier.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Asset Classification Tiers*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0009 — Asset Classification / Asset classification tiers  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Development environments and backup systems are classified under which asset tier?

- **A.** Tier 0 (Essential)
- **B.** Unclassified
- **C.** Tier 2 (Non-Essential)
- **D.** Tier 1 (Important)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Tier 1 (Important) includes development environments and backup systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Tier 0 is essential infrastructure (critical servers, databases, network devices).
- C: Tier 2 is non-essential (workstations, mobile devices, printers).
- B: Unclassified is a data classification level, not an asset tier.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Asset Classification Tiers*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0010 — Sensitive Data Types / PII  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Data that can uniquely identify an individual and is subject to strict regulatory standards is known as:

- **A.** Personally Identifiable Information (PII)
- **B.** Protected Health Information (PHI)
- **C.** Public data
- **D.** Proprietary data

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** PII is data that can uniquely identify an individual and is subject to strict regulatory standards.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: PHI is specifically health-related information protected under laws like HIPAA.
- D: Proprietary data gives a business competitive advantage.
- C: Public data has minimal sensitivity.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Types of Sensitive Data*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0011 — Sensitive Data Types / PHI  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Health-related information that requires protection under laws such as HIPAA is categorized as:

- **A.** Protected Health Information (PHI)
- **B.** Tier 0 asset
- **C.** Personally Identifiable Information (PII)
- **D.** Proprietary data

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** PHI is health-related information requiring protection under laws like HIPAA.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: PII identifies individuals generally but is not specifically health data.
- D: Proprietary data provides competitive advantage, unrelated to health.
- B: Tier 0 is an asset tier, not a data type.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Types of Sensitive Data*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0012 — Sensitive Data Types / Proprietary data  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Information that gives a business a competitive advantage and therefore requires high-level security is BEST described as:

- **A.** Unclassified data
- **B.** Personally Identifiable Information (PII)
- **C.** Proprietary data
- **D.** Public data

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Proprietary data gives a business competitive advantage and requires high-level security.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Public data carries minimal sensitivity.
- A: Unclassified data has low or no impact.
- B: PII identifies individuals; it is not defined by competitive advantage.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Types of Sensitive Data*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0013 — Sensitive Data Types / Identifying sensitive data  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are categories of sensitive data that warrant heightened protection? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Press releases already distributed to the media
- **B.** Personally Identifiable Information (PII)
- **C.** Protected Health Information (PHI)
- **D.** Public marketing brochures posted on the company website

**Correct answer:** B, C

**Why correct:** PII and PHI are sensitive data types subject to regulatory protection; public materials are not sensitive.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Public marketing brochures are intentionally public and not sensitive.
- A: Distributed press releases are public information, not sensitive data.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Types of Sensitive Data*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0014 — Data Classification / NIST SP 800-60  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which NIST Special Publication is referenced as essential for understanding data types and classification standards?

- **A.** NIST SP 800-60
- **B.** NIST SP 800-37
- **C.** NIST SP 1800-5
- **D.** NIST SP 800-88

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-60 provides guidance on mapping information and information system types to security categories (data types and classification).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: NIST SP 800-88 covers media sanitization.
- B: NIST SP 800-37 is the Risk Management Framework.
- C: NIST SP 1800-5 relates to IT asset management.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Important Reference*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0015 — Data Classification / Sensitivity assessment  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A sensitivity assessment evaluates a data set's need for protection based primarily on its:

- **A.** Number of backup copies that exist
- **B.** The vendor that supplied the storage device
- **C.** Color and font formatting
- **D.** Confidentiality, integrity, and availability requirements

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Sensitivity assessment evaluates the data's need for protection based on its confidentiality, integrity, and availability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Formatting does not determine protection requirements.
- A: Backup count is a resilience detail, not a sensitivity measure.
- B: Storage vendor is unrelated to data sensitivity.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Classification*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0016 — Data Classification / Data inventory  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Why is maintaining a comprehensive inventory of all data and assets important to classification?

- **A.** It automatically encrypts all data
- **B.** It guarantees data can never be breached
- **C.** It helps assess each item's criticality and impact on business operations
- **D.** It removes the need to classify data at all

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A comprehensive inventory helps assess the criticality and business impact of data and assets, which underpins classification and protection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: An inventory supports protection but does not guarantee breach immunity.
- D: Inventory enables classification; it does not replace it.
- A: Inventory is a listing activity; it does not perform encryption.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Classification*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0017 — Data Classification / Classification scope  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

When identifying data types for classification, an organization should account for data in:

- **A.** Neither format, because classification applies only to hardware
- **B.** Digital formats only, since paper is never sensitive
- **C.** Both physical and digital formats
- **D.** Physical formats only, since digital data is automatically secure

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Data classification must consider both physical and digital formats of information.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Paper records can be highly sensitive and must be classified.
- D: Digital data is not automatically secure and must be classified.
- A: Classification applies to data/information, including but not limited to hardware.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Classification*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0018 — Asset Classification / Benefits of classification  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are stated benefits of properly classifying assets? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Improved security awareness
- **B.** Guaranteed elimination of all cyber risk
- **C.** Permanent exemption from all audits
- **D.** Compliance assurance

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Benefits of proper classification include improved security awareness, compliance assurance, and enhanced planning.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Classification reduces and manages risk but cannot eliminate it entirely.
- C: Classification supports compliance; it does not exempt an organization from audits.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Asset Classification Tiers*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0019 — Asset Classification / Flow of data  _(hard, scenario_based)_

During asset classification, a team maps exactly how sensitive data moves into, within, and out of the organization. The PRIMARY security value of tracking this data flow is to:

- **A.** Increase the volume of data the organization stores
- **B.** Replace the need to classify the data itself
- **C.** Guarantee the data never needs encryption
- **D.** Ensure appropriate security measures are applied wherever the data travels, including in transit

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Tracking the flow of data ensures appropriate controls are applied as data moves within and outside the organization, protecting it in all states including transit.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Mapping flow does not increase stored volume.
- B: Flow analysis complements—not replaces—classification.
- C: Data in transit often specifically requires encryption.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Asset Classification*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0020 — Data Classification / Governance and compliance  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Policies governing data creation, collection, management, and disposal primarily help ensure:

- **A.** Data quality, authenticity, and reliability, and regulatory compliance
- **B.** That all data is deleted immediately upon creation
- **C.** That data is never classified
- **D.** That security controls are unnecessary

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Governance, regulation, and compliance policies over the data lifecycle help ensure data quality, authenticity, and reliability while meeting regulations.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Such governance supports classification rather than preventing it.
- B: Immediate deletion would destroy needed data and ignore retention requirements.
- D: Governance reinforces—not removes—the need for controls.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Classification*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0021 — Asset Classification / High watermark  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A single system stores both public data and highly sensitive PII together (co-mingled). According to the high-watermark principle, the system should be protected at the level of:

- **A.** The average sensitivity of all data on the system
- **B.** The most sensitive data it contains (the PII)
- **C.** Whatever level is cheapest to implement
- **D.** The least sensitive data it contains (the public data)

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The high-watermark principle applies the highest level of protection required by the most sensitive data type present on a system.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Protecting to the lowest level would leave the sensitive PII exposed.
- A: Averaging sensitivity under-protects the most sensitive data.
- C: Cost cannot override the protection needs of the most sensitive data.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Baseline and Tailoring*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0022 — Sensitive Data Types / Regulated data  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which of the following is an example of "regulated information" that must be handled according to legal requirements?

- **A.** A blank spreadsheet template
- **B.** A public blog post
- **C.** Cardholder data subject to PCI DSS
- **D.** The company lunch menu

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Regulated information includes data governed by laws/standards such as cardholder data under PCI DSS, which mandates specific handling.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A blank template contains no sensitive or regulated content.
- D: A lunch menu is non-sensitive public information.
- B: A public blog post is intentionally public, not regulated sensitive data.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Types of Sensitive Data*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0023 — Asset Classification / Aligning classification to business  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Aligning a classification scheme with business needs primarily helps to:

- **A.** Avoid documenting any data at all
- **B.** Force every asset into the Top Secret level
- **C.** Guarantee the organization never needs encryption
- **D.** Minimize risk while strengthening security in a way that supports operations

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Aligning classification with business needs minimizes risks and strengthens security while supporting business operations.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Over-classifying all assets as Top Secret wastes resources and is not business-aligned.
- A: Classification requires documenting/inventorying data, not avoiding it.
- C: Alignment does not eliminate the need for controls such as encryption.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classifying Data and Assets*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0024 — Asset Classification / Government classification levels  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are government data classification levels described in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Proprietary
- **B.** Top Secret
- **C.** Tier 0 (Essential)
- **D.** Confidential

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** Top Secret and Confidential are government classification levels (along with Secret and Unclassified).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Tier 0 (Essential) is an asset tier, not a data classification level.
- A: Proprietary describes a sensitive data type, not a government classification level.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Classification Levels*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0025 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Data Owner  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which data role holds ultimate responsibility for a data set and typically delegates protection tasks to a custodian?

- **A.** Data Owner
- **B.** Data Custodian
- **C.** Data Subject
- **D.** Data Steward

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The data owner is responsible for the data (often specialized by type, e.g., financial or medical) and delegates implementation to data custodians.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: The custodian implements controls on the owner's behalf.
- D: The steward is a subject-matter expert who assists with classification.
- C: The data subject is the individual the data is about / who uses it.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Roles and Responsibilities*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0026 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Data Custodian  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Who implements the physical, administrative, and logical controls that protect data, working on behalf of the data owner?

- **A.** Data Custodian
- **B.** Data Owner
- **C.** Data Processor
- **D.** Data Steward

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The data custodian implements physical, administrative, and logical controls on behalf of the owner and manages the technical protection of data.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: The owner sets requirements and delegates; it does not necessarily implement controls.
- D: The steward provides subject-matter expertise and assists with classification.
- C: The processor (GDPR) acts on the controller's behalf to process data, a distinct role.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Roles and Responsibilities*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0027 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Data Steward  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which role acts as a subject-matter expert for specific data types and assists in classifying and categorizing the data?

- **A.** Senior Management
- **B.** Data Subject
- **C.** Data Steward
- **D.** Data Custodian

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The data steward is a subject-matter expert for specific data types who assists with classification and works with both owner and custodian.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: The custodian implements technical protection, not SME classification guidance.
- B: The data subject is the individual whose data is processed or a system user.
- A: Senior management is accountable for overall asset management, not data SME work.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Roles and Responsibilities*

---
