### CISSP-D02-Q0028 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Data Subject  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the guide's role model, "data subjects" include which of the following?

- **A.** Human users and non-human entities (applications, scripts) that consume or interact with data
- **B.** Only the physical storage hardware
- **C.** Only external regulators auditing the system
- **D.** Only senior executives who approve budgets

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Data subjects are individuals who consume/interact with data (often "users") and can also include non-human entities such as applications, scripts, or automated processes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Executives are not the definition of data subjects.
- C: Regulators are external oversight, not data subjects in this model.
- B: Hardware is an asset, not a data subject.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Roles and Responsibilities*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0029 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Controller vs Processor  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Under the GDPR-focused roles, who determines the purpose and means of processing personal data and bears ultimate responsibility?

- **A.** Data Processor
- **B.** Data Custodian
- **C.** Data Steward
- **D.** Data Controller

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The data controller determines the purpose and means of processing personal data and holds ultimate responsibility.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The processor acts on the controller's behalf to collect, process, and store data.
- B: The custodian is an internal protection-implementation role, not the GDPR controller.
- C: The steward provides data expertise, not processing-purpose decisions.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Controllers and Processors (GDPR Focused)*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0030 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Controller vs Processor  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A cloud analytics vendor collects and processes customer data strictly according to its client's instructions. Under GDPR roles, the vendor is the:

- **A.** Data Controller
- **B.** Data Processor
- **C.** Data Owner
- **D.** Data Steward

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A party that processes personal data on behalf of, and per the instructions of, the controller is the data processor and is liable for breaches during processing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The controller decides purpose/means; here the vendor follows the client's instructions.
- C: Data owner is an internal accountability role, not the GDPR term for this vendor.
- D: A steward provides data expertise, not bulk processing on instruction.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Controllers and Processors (GDPR Focused)*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0031 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Roles in a network  _(hard, scenario_based)_

On a storage server, an analyst decides which encryption and access requirements apply to a financial dataset, while an administrator actually configures the encryption and monitors access. Which roles do they hold, respectively?

- **A.** Data Owner and Data Custodian
- **B.** Data Custodian and Data Owner
- **C.** Data Processor and Data Controller
- **D.** Data Steward and Data Subject

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Setting the security/access requirements is the data owner's responsibility; configuring encryption and monitoring access is the custodian's implementation role.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: This reverses the roles—the decision-maker is the owner, the implementer is the custodian.
- D: A steward is an SME and a subject is a user; neither matches setting requirements vs implementing them.
- C: Controller/processor are GDPR processing roles, not the owner/custodian split described.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Practical Example of Roles in a Network*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0032 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Role mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each data role (1-4) to its primary responsibility (A-D).

1. Data Owner
2. Data Custodian
3. Data Steward
4. Data Processor

- **A.** Serves as subject-matter expert and assists with classification
- **B.** Implements physical, administrative, and logical controls on the owner's behalf
- **C.** Processes personal data on behalf of the controller
- **D.** Holds ultimate responsibility for the data and sets protection requirements

**Correct answer:** 1-D,2-B,3-A,4-C

**Why correct:** Data Owner → ultimate responsibility/sets requirements (B); Data Custodian → implements controls (A); Data Steward → SME/classification help (D); Data Processor → processes data for the controller (C).

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Roles and Responsibilities*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0033 — Data Roles and Responsibilities / Distinguishing roles  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO statements about data roles are correct? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** The data owner can delegate implementation tasks to a data custodian
- **B.** The data steward assists with classifying and categorizing data
- **C.** The data custodian decides the business purpose for collecting personal data
- **D.** The data subject sets the organization's data classification policy

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** Owners delegate to custodians, and stewards assist with classification/categorization—both correct.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Data subjects are users/individuals; they do not set classification policy.
- C: Deciding the purpose of processing is the controller/owner role, not the custodian.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Data Roles and Responsibilities*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0034 — Handling Requirements / Marking  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Physically identifying an asset with its sensitivity or classification level (for example, a stamped "CONFIDENTIAL" label on a folder) is referred to as:

- **A.** Labeling
- **B.** Marking
- **C.** Tailoring
- **D.** Scoping

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Marking is the physical identification of an asset with its appropriate sensitivity or classification level.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Labeling is the digital/logical identification (e.g., metadata or watermarks).
- D: Scoping selects which controls apply to a system.
- C: Tailoring modifies baseline controls.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Information and Asset Handling Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0035 — Handling Requirements / Labeling  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Embedding a classification level in a file's metadata or as a digital watermark is an example of:

- **A.** Tokenization
- **B.** Degaussing
- **C.** Marking
- **D.** Labeling

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Labeling is the digital or logical identification of an asset with its sensitivity/classification, implemented through metadata or digital watermarks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Marking is the physical identification of an asset.
- B: Degaussing is a magnetic data-destruction method.
- A: Tokenization substitutes sensitive data with non-sensitive tokens.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Information and Asset Handling Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0036 — Handling Requirements / Marking vs labeling  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization stamps "SECRET" on printed reports and embeds a "SECRET" tag in the metadata of the corresponding electronic files. The printed stamp and the metadata tag represent, respectively:

- **A.** Labeling (physical) and marking (logical)
- **B.** Marking (physical) and labeling (logical)
- **C.** Scoping and tailoring
- **D.** Clearing and purging

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The physical stamp is marking; the electronic metadata tag is labeling (logical identification).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: This reverses the definitions—physical is marking, logical is labeling.
- C: Scoping/tailoring are control-selection activities, not handling identification.
- D: Clearing/purging are data-destruction techniques.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Information and Asset Handling Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0037 — Handling Requirements / Consistent processes  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Why should an organization define a consistent process for marking and labeling assets?

- **A.** To make every asset Top Secret regardless of sensitivity
- **B.** To ensure all personnel handle data correctly and support compliance, reducing breach and misuse risk
- **C.** To guarantee assets never need to be inventoried
- **D.** To avoid having to classify data at all

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A consistent marking/labeling process ensures correct, repeatable handling, supports compliance, and minimizes the risk of breaches or asset misuse.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Consistency is about correct handling, not over-classifying everything.
- D: Consistent handling depends on classification, not its absence.
- C: Inventory remains essential to asset management.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Organizational Policies*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0038 — Handling Requirements / Backups and copies  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

When sensitive data is copied or backed up, those copies should be:

- **A.** Treated as public since they are duplicates
- **B.** Handled and stored according to the same sensitivity/classification as the original
- **C.** Exempt from the data retention policy
- **D.** Stored without any access controls to ease recovery

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Copies and backups must be handled and stored according to the data's sensitivity, just like the original.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Copies retain the original's sensitivity and are not public.
- D: Backups of sensitive data still require access controls.
- C: Retention policy applies to copies/backups as well.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Handling Sensitive Data and Assets*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0039 — Asset Management / Provisioning  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In secure asset management, "provisioning" refers to:

- **A.** Auditing financial statements for SOX
- **B.** Permanently deleting all backups
- **C.** Marking assets as public
- **D.** Creating, managing, and destroying assets throughout their lifecycle

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Provisioning involves creating, managing, and destroying assets throughout their lifecycle.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Provisioning covers the full lifecycle, not only deletion of backups.
- C: Marking is a handling activity, not provisioning.
- A: SOX auditing is a compliance activity unrelated to provisioning definition.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Managing System Assets*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0040 — Asset Management / Asset inventory  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An accurate asset inventory is described as crucial primarily because it:

- **A.** Eliminates the need for any security controls
- **B.** Enables effective change control and configuration management by accounting for all assets
- **C.** Replaces the need for data classification
- **D.** Automatically patches all systems

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** An accurate inventory is crucial for implementing change control and configuration management and ensures all assets are accounted for throughout their lifecycle.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Inventory supports, but does not replace, security controls.
- D: Inventory tracks assets; it does not perform patching itself.
- C: Inventory and classification are complementary activities.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Asset Management Components*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0041 — Asset Management / Responsibilities  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Who is described as accountable for overall asset management, including IT assets, hardware procurement, and asset disposal?

- **A.** Senior Management
- **B.** External auditors
- **C.** Data Subjects
- **D.** End users

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Senior management is accountable for overall asset management, including IT assets, hardware, procurement, and disposal.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Data subjects are users/individuals interacting with data.
- B: External auditors verify compliance; they are not accountable for asset management.
- D: End users operate systems but are not accountable for overall asset management.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Responsibilities*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0042 — Asset Management / What to track  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are HARDWARE assets that should be tracked in an inventory? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** A firewall appliance
- **B.** A security awareness policy
- **C.** A network switch
- **D.** An operating system license key

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** Hardware to track includes servers, switches, firewalls, gateways, proxies, and IDS/IPS systems—both a firewall appliance and a network switch qualify.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A license key relates to software, not a hardware asset.
- B: A policy is an administrative document, not a hardware asset.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > What to Track*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0043 — Asset Management / Automated discovery  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which approach uses tools such as Nmap or OpenVAS to discover hosts and identify operating systems for an asset inventory?

- **A.** Degaussing
- **B.** Manual spreadsheet tracking
- **C.** Automated discovery
- **D.** Tokenization

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Automated discovery uses tools like Nessus, Nmap, and OpenVAS for host discovery and OS identification.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Manual tracking uses spreadsheets/databases, not automated scanning tools.
- A: Degaussing is a data-destruction method.
- D: Tokenization is a data-protection/declassification technique.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Tools and Techniques for Asset Tracking*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0044 — Asset Management / Inventory integrity  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

To protect the asset inventory itself, an organization should ensure that:

- **A.** The inventory is publicly editable to encourage updates
- **B.** The inventory is never updated once created
- **C.** Access to view and modify the inventory is restricted to authorized personnel and changes follow change control
- **D.** Anyone may delete entries to keep the list short

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Inventory security and integrity require restricting access/modifications to authorized personnel and managing updates through configuration management and change control.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Public editability destroys integrity and enables tampering.
- B: The inventory must be updated as assets change, via change control.
- D: Uncontrolled deletion undermines accuracy and integrity.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Organizational Policy and Management*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0045 — Asset Management / Asset lifecycle  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The asset lifecycle (per NIST SP 1800-5) includes strategy, planning, design, procurement, operation, maintenance, modification, and:

- **A.** Phishing
- **B.** Marketing
- **C.** Tokenization
- **D.** Disposal

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The asset lifecycle culminates in disposal, completing the cradle-to-grave management of assets.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Marketing is not part of the asset lifecycle.
- C: Tokenization is a data-protection technique, not an asset-lifecycle phase.
- A: Phishing is an attack technique, not a lifecycle phase.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Key Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0046 — Handling Requirements / Regulatory influence  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Regulations such as GDPR and HIPAA most directly influence which aspect of asset security?

- **A.** The brand of laptop employees may purchase
- **B.** The color of classification labels
- **C.** The office floor plan layout
- **D.** How data is stored, accessed, transmitted, and destroyed (data handling)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Regulations like GDPR and HIPAA shape data-handling practices—storage, access control, transmission, and destruction.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Laptop brand selection is procurement, not regulatory data handling.
- C: Floor plans are facilities matters, not data-handling regulation.
- B: Label color is a cosmetic detail, not the substance of handling rules.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Information and Asset Handling Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0047 — Asset Management / Asset details tracked  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which of the following operational details is appropriate to record for a tracked asset?

- **A.** The personal hobbies of the employee who uses it
- **B.** Unrelated marketing slogans
- **C.** Purchase/installation date, licensing, location, and baseline configuration
- **D.** The asset's favorite color

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Operational details to track include purchase/installation dates, licensing, dependencies, location, IP addresses, and baseline configurations.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Personal hobbies are irrelevant to asset tracking.
- D: Assets do not have a "favorite color"; this is not a tracked detail.
- B: Marketing slogans are unrelated to asset records.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > What to Track*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0048 — Data States / Three states  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The guide describes data as existing in three states. Which set correctly lists them?

- **A.** Public, internal, and secret
- **B.** In use, in transit, and at rest
- **C.** Hot, warm, and cold
- **D.** Create, read, and delete

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Data exists in three states—in use, in transit, and at rest—each requiring appropriate protection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Hot/warm/cold describe site recovery strategies, not data states.
- A: Public/internal/secret are classification levels, not data states.
- D: Create/read/delete are operations, not data states.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Understanding Data States*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0049 — Data States / Data at rest  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which protection mechanisms are MOST associated with securing data at rest?

- **A.** Full-disk encryption, Trusted Platform Modules (TPM), and secure storage
- **B.** Memory-only authentication during active processing
- **C.** Network egress monitoring on the perimeter
- **D.** TLS certificates and secure routing

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Data at rest is protected with full-disk encryption, TPMs, and secure storage environments because it is a static target.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: TLS and secure routing protect data in transit.
- B: In-memory protections apply to data in use.
- C: Egress monitoring targets data leaving the network (in transit/exfiltration), not data at rest.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data at Rest*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0050 — Data States / Data in transit  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Data is considered MOST at risk in which state, because it is exposed to the network?

- **A.** At rest
- **B.** Archived
- **C.** In transit
- **D.** In use

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Data in transit is most at risk because it is exposed to the network (wired or wireless); it is protected with encryption, secure routing, and certificates (SSL/TLS).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Data at rest is a static target but is not described as the most-at-risk state.
- D: Data in use is the most volatile but is being processed locally, not exposed across the network.
- B: Archived data is a form of data at rest.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Understanding Data States*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0051 — Data States / Data in use  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which statement best characterizes data in use?

- **A.** It is always encrypted and never at risk
- **B.** It only exists on backup tapes
- **C.** It is data that has been permanently destroyed
- **D.** It is the most volatile state, residing in memory or being processed, and needs authentication, authorization, and monitoring

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Data in use is actively processed (often in memory), is the most volatile state, and requires authentication, authorization, and monitoring to protect integrity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Data in use can be at risk during processing and is not inherently always encrypted.
- B: Backup tapes hold data at rest, not data in use.
- C: Destroyed data is not a live state.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Understanding Data States*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0052 — Data States / Protection by state  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company sends application logs from a web server to a remote analysis platform over the internet. Which protection is MOST appropriate for this data while it travels?

- **A.** Full-disk encryption on the destination server only
- **B.** A Trusted Platform Module on the source server
- **C.** Degaussing the source storage media
- **D.** Encryption with mutual authentication (e.g., TLS) to protect data in motion

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Data in transit is best protected with encryption and mutual authentication (e.g., TLS) to defend confidentiality and integrity while it moves.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Disk encryption at the destination protects data at rest, not while it traverses the network.
- B: A TPM protects keys/data at rest on a device, not data crossing the network.
- C: Degaussing destroys data on magnetic media; it does not protect data in transit.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Security Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0053 — Data Lifecycle / Lifecycle phases  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

Place the data lifecycle phases in their typical order from cradle to grave. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Use
- **B.** Storage
- **C.** Destruction
- **D.** Archiving
- **E.** Creation

**Correct answer:** E,B,A,D,C

**Why correct:** The data lifecycle runs creation → storage → use → archiving → destruction (cradle to grave).

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Managing the Data Lifecycle*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0054 — Data Lifecycle / Creation/classification  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Immediately after data is created, what should occur to ensure it receives the right level of protection?

- **A.** It should be made public by default
- **B.** It should be classified or categorized according to its sensitivity
- **C.** It should be moved straight to archive storage
- **D.** It should be permanently destroyed

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** After creation, data must be classified/categorized by sensitivity so that appropriate protection is applied throughout its life.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Destruction is the final phase, not the step right after creation.
- A: Defaulting to public would expose sensitive data.
- C: Archiving applies to data no longer needed regularly, not freshly created data.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Managing the Data Lifecycle*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0055 — Data Lifecycle / Archiving  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

When data that is no longer needed regularly is moved to archive storage, the guide states that archives must still have:

- **A.** Access controls and encryption applied
- **B.** No retention limits whatsoever
- **C.** Public read access for convenience
- **D.** All security controls removed to save space

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Archived data must have access controls and encryption applied; archiving can be driven by regulatory or organizational requirements.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Removing controls would expose archived sensitive data.
- C: Public access to archives risks unauthorized disclosure.
- B: Retention limits and policies still apply to archived data.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Managing the Data Lifecycle*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0056 — Data Lifecycle / Data value over time  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A dataset that was highly sensitive at launch becomes far less sensitive years later as the information becomes obsolete. According to the guide's "data value over time" consideration, the organization should:

- **A.** Delete the classification policy entirely
- **B.** Immediately make the data public without review
- **C.** Keep the original classification permanently regardless of changes
- **D.** Re-evaluate the data's classification and adjust protection needs as its value changes

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Data value can change over time, affecting classification and protection needs; classification should be re-evaluated accordingly.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Static classification ignores legitimate changes in data value.
- B: Reclassification requires review, not automatic public release.
- A: The policy remains necessary to govern reclassification decisions.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Key Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0057 — Data Lifecycle / Secure destruction  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the destruction phase of the data lifecycle, secure destruction methods are required primarily to:

- **A.** Increase the amount of stored data
- **B.** Avoid the need to follow any regulations
- **C.** Ensure no sensitive data can be recovered by unauthorized parties
- **D.** Make data publicly searchable

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Secure destruction follows policy and legal/regulatory requirements and ensures that no sensitive data can be recovered by unauthorized parties.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Destruction removes data; it does not increase storage.
- D: Destruction prevents access, not public searchability.
- B: Destruction must comply with—not bypass—regulations.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Managing the Data Lifecycle*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0058 — Information System Lifecycle / Initiation phase  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

During the initiation phase of the information system lifecycle, the PRIMARY focus is to:

- **A.** Identify and engage stakeholders and understand requirements and the system's purpose
- **B.** Run the system in full production
- **C.** Conduct the final retirement of the system
- **D.** Securely erase all data and dispose of hardware

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Initiation focuses on identifying/engaging stakeholders (users, managers, customers, auditors, leadership) and understanding requirements and the system's purpose.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Erasure/disposal occurs at the disposal/retirement phase.
- B: Full production occurs in operations and maintenance.
- C: Retirement is the final phase, not initiation.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > The Information System Lifecycle - Part 1*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0059 — Information System Lifecycle / Development vs acquisition  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The "development and acquisition" phase centers on which key decision?

- **A.** Which classification color to use on labels
- **B.** Whether to build the system in-house or buy/acquire a third-party solution, based on capability and security needs
- **C.** How many press releases to issue
- **D.** How to degauss backup tapes

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Development and acquisition addresses the build-vs-buy decision, applying secure development practices internally or vetting third-party solutions for security.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Degaussing is a destruction method, not a build/buy decision.
- A: Label color is a handling cosmetic, not a lifecycle decision.
- C: Press releases are unrelated to the system lifecycle.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > The Information System Lifecycle - Part 1*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0060 — Information System Lifecycle / Disposal phase  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The disposal (retirement) phase of the information system lifecycle requires that:

- **A.** The build-vs-buy decision is made
- **B.** Stakeholder requirements are first gathered
- **C.** All data is securely erased and hardware is disposed of so data cannot be recovered
- **D.** The system is moved into full production use

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Disposal involves secure decommissioning—ensuring all data is securely erased and hardware is disposed of to prevent data recovery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Production use is operations/maintenance, not disposal.
- B: Requirement gathering is part of initiation.
- A: Build-vs-buy is the development/acquisition phase.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Disposal*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0061 — Information System Lifecycle / NIST SP 800-64  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which (now retired but still referenced) NIST publication provides a structured approach to embedding security into the information system lifecycle?

- **A.** NIST SP 800-64
- **B.** NIST SP 800-60
- **C.** NIST SP 800-137
- **D.** NIST SP 800-88

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-64 Rev. 2, though retired, is still referenced for embedding security into the system development lifecycle.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: NIST SP 800-60 covers data types and classification.
- D: NIST SP 800-88 covers media sanitization.
- C: NIST SP 800-137 covers information security continuous monitoring.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > Disposal*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0062 — Information System Lifecycle / Security integration  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A core principle emphasized across the information system lifecycle is that security should be:

- **A.** Added only after a breach occurs
- **B.** Left entirely to the end users
- **C.** Integrated from the initial phases through disposal, at every step
- **D.** Considered only during the disposal phase

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Security must be integrated from the initial phases through disposal—built in at every step rather than bolted on.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Waiting for a breach is reactive and leaves the system exposed.
- D: Security must apply throughout, not only at disposal.
- B: Security is a shared responsibility led by management, not solely end users.

*Source: Domain 2 > Asset Security > The Information System Lifecycle - Part 2*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0063 — Data Retention / Retention principle  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A sound data retention policy holds that data should be retained:

- **A.** Forever, regardless of need or regulation
- **B.** For exactly 24 hours in all cases
- **C.** Only if it is public data
- **D.** Only as long as necessary or legally required, then securely destroyed

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Data should be retained securely and only for as long as necessary per organizational policy and legal/regulatory requirements, then destroyed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Indefinite retention increases liability and breach exposure.
- B: There is no universal 24-hour rule; retention depends on policy/regulation.
- C: Retention applies to many data types, not only public data.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data Retention Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0064 — Data Retention / EOL vs EOS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A vendor announces it will no longer release patches or provide support for a server operating system, although the OS still technically runs. This status is called:

- **A.** End-of-Support (EOS)
- **B.** Data remanence
- **C.** End-of-Life (EOL)
- **D.** Tailoring out

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** End-of-Support (EOS) is when the vendor no longer maintains or supports the hardware/software, even if it still runs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: End-of-Life (EOL) is when the asset can no longer be used or the license expires.
- B: Data remanence is leftover data after deletion attempts.
- D: Tailoring out is removing controls from a baseline.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Retention Best Practices*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0065 — Data Retention / EOL vs EOS  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A critical application server has reached end-of-support, so it no longer receives security patches. From an asset-retention standpoint, the BEST action is to:

- **A.** Remove all monitoring to reduce noise
- **B.** Expose it directly to the internet to extend its usefulness
- **C.** Keep running it indefinitely since it still functions
- **D.** Replace or remove the unsupported asset to avoid security vulnerabilities

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** When assets are no longer supported, they should be replaced or removed because unpatched systems introduce security vulnerabilities.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Running unsupported systems indefinitely leaves unpatched vulnerabilities exposed.
- B: Internet exposure of an unpatched server dramatically increases risk.
- A: Removing monitoring reduces visibility into an already-risky asset.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Asset Retention*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0066 — Data Retention / Retention regulations  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO regulations/standards are cited as governing data retention requirements? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** PCI DSS
- **B.** Wassenaar Arrangement
- **C.** STRIDE
- **D.** HIPAA

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** HIPAA (healthcare) and PCI DSS (payment data) are among the regulations/standards governing data retention.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: The Wassenaar Arrangement governs export controls, not data retention.
- C: STRIDE is a threat-modeling methodology, not a retention regulation.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Regulations Governing Data Retention*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0067 — Data Retention / Retention guidance  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which standard focuses specifically on records management, including the concepts and principles of data retention?

- **A.** ISO 15489
- **B.** NIST SP 800-88
- **C.** ISO 27040
- **D.** PCI DSS

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** ISO 15489 focuses on records management, including data-retention concepts and principles.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: ISO 27040 addresses storage security.
- B: NIST SP 800-88 covers media sanitization.
- D: PCI DSS is a payment-card security standard.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Guidance on Data Retention*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0068 — Data Retention / Chain of custody  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

When data is involved in a legal or security investigation, what must be maintained to ensure its integrity is defensible?

- **A.** An end-user license agreement
- **B.** A public changelog
- **C.** Chain of custody
- **D.** A marketing plan

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Chain of custody is a documented process tracking who accessed data, ensuring its integrity in legal proceedings; such data must be retained until the case concludes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A marketing plan is irrelevant to evidentiary integrity.
- B: A public changelog does not establish evidentiary control.
- A: A EULA is a licensing agreement, not an evidence-integrity process.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Retention and Legal Proceedings*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0069 — Data Retention / Legal hold  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization's policy says to destroy a category of records after 18 months, but those exact records are now the subject of active litigation. The organization should:

- **A.** Make the records public to resolve the dispute faster
- **B.** Destroy them on schedule because policy takes precedence over litigation
- **C.** Retain the records until the legal matter concludes, overriding the routine destruction schedule
- **D.** Move the records off-site and stop tracking who accesses them

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Data involved in legal proceedings must be retained until the case concludes, and chain of custody maintained—this overrides routine destruction.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Destroying records under litigation can constitute spoliation and legal liability.
- A: Public disclosure could violate confidentiality and harm the case.
- D: Ceasing access tracking breaks chain of custody and undermines integrity.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Retention and Legal Proceedings*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0070 — Data Retention / Retention policy contents  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO elements should a well-formed data retention policy define? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** End-of-life and end-of-support requirements for assets
- **B.** The personal opinions of individual employees
- **C.** Retention periods or timelines for the data
- **D.** A list of competitor product prices

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** A retention policy should define retention periods/timelines and EOL/EOS requirements (along with purpose/scope and data covered), aligned with legal/regulatory needs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Personal opinions are not policy elements.
- D: Competitor pricing is irrelevant to a retention policy.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Organizational Data Retention Policy*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0071 — Determining Security Controls / Baseline  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In control selection, a "baseline" is BEST described as:

- **A.** A control that is never modified
- **B.** The maximum number of controls a system can hold
- **C.** The minimum set of controls that serves as a starting point for protection
- **D.** A method for destroying magnetic media

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A baseline is the minimum set of controls for safeguarding security/privacy (often from NIST SP 800-53 or ISO/IEC 27002) and acts as an adaptable starting point.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A baseline is a minimum starting point, not a maximum.
- A: Baselines are adaptable through scoping and tailoring.
- D: Destroying magnetic media is degaussing, unrelated to a control baseline.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Baseline and Tailoring*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0072 — Determining Security Controls / Scoping  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Selecting which controls from a baseline actually apply to a specific system or environment is called:

- **A.** Tailoring in
- **B.** Degaussing
- **C.** Provisioning
- **D.** Scoping

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Scoping determines which baseline controls are applicable to an organization's system or environment.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Tailoring in is adding controls beyond the baseline for new risks.
- B: Degaussing is a data-destruction method.
- C: Provisioning is creating/managing/destroying assets over their lifecycle.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Baseline and Tailoring*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0073 — Determining Security Controls / Tailoring  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Modifying a baseline by ADDING controls to address new risks or heightened data sensitivity is referred to as:

- **A.** Tailoring in
- **B.** Tailoring out
- **C.** Sanitization
- **D.** Scoping

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Tailoring in is adding additional controls based on new risks or data sensitivity; tailoring out is removing controls that are not relevant.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Tailoring out removes controls that do not apply.
- D: Scoping selects which baseline controls apply at all.
- C: Sanitization is a data-destruction concept.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Baseline and Tailoring*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0074 — Determining Security Controls / Tailoring out  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A baseline includes a control requiring secure handling of wireless transmissions, but the target system is fully air-gapped with no wireless capability. The MOST appropriate action is to:

- **A.** Leave the control and force the system to add wireless hardware
- **B.** Tailor in additional wireless controls to be safe
- **C.** Apply the high-watermark principle to add more controls
- **D.** Tailor out the wireless control because it does not apply to this system

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Tailoring out removes baseline controls that are not relevant or applicable—an air-gapped system with no wireless does not need the wireless control.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Adding wireless controls to a system with no wireless wastes effort and is irrelevant.
- C: High watermark addresses co-mingled sensitivity, not inapplicable controls.
- A: Forcing wireless hardware to justify a control is backwards and increases risk.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Baseline and Tailoring*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0075 — Selecting Security Controls / Risk-based selection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

According to the control selection process, controls should be selected based primarily on:

- **A.** Whichever controls are cheapest, regardless of risk
- **B.** The personal preference of the newest administrator
- **C.** The alphabetical order of control names
- **D.** The level of risk and the protection needs of the information

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Controls are selected based on the level of risk and protection needs; over-securing public systems is avoided while sensitive systems receive appropriate controls.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Personal preference is not a valid selection basis.
- A: Cost alone, ignoring risk, leads to under- or over-protection.
- C: Alphabetical order is irrelevant to control selection.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Selection Process*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0076 — Selecting Security Controls / Avoiding over-securing  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A public-facing website serves only non-sensitive marketing content. Applying the control selection principles, the team should:

- **A.** Apply controls proportionate to the low sensitivity and avoid over-securing the public system
- **B.** Classify the marketing content as Top Secret to be safe
- **C.** Remove all controls because the data is public
- **D.** Apply the same controls used for the most sensitive internal database

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Control selection avoids over-securing public systems; controls should be proportionate to the data's low sensitivity and risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Applying maximum internal controls to a public site wastes resources.
- C: Even public systems need baseline protections (e.g., integrity, availability).
- B: Over-classifying non-sensitive content misallocates protection effort.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Selection Process*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0077 — Security Control Frameworks / COBIT  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which framework, managed by ISACA, is designed for IT governance and aligning IT with business objectives?

- **A.** ISO 15489
- **B.** Deming Cycle
- **C.** COBIT
- **D.** NIST SP 800-88

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** COBIT (Control Objectives for Information and Related Technologies), managed by ISACA, focuses on IT governance and aligning IT with business needs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: NIST SP 800-88 covers media sanitization.
- A: ISO 15489 covers records management.
- B: The Deming Cycle (PDCA) is a continuous-improvement model, not a governance framework.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Security Control Frameworks*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0078 — Security Control Frameworks / COSO and SOX  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

COBIT is derived from which framework, which is used to comply with Sarbanes-Oxley (SOX) 404 and focuses on preventing fraudulent financial reporting?

- **A.** ISO/IEC 27002
- **B.** NIST SP 800-53
- **C.** COSO
- **D.** HITRUST

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** COBIT is derived from COSO; COSO is used for SOX 404 compliance and focuses on preventing fraudulent financial reporting.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: ISO/IEC 27002 is an international code of practice for security controls.
- D: HITRUST is a common security framework for health information.
- B: NIST SP 800-53 is the U.S. federal control catalog required by FISMA.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Security Control Frameworks*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0079 — Security Control Frameworks / NIST SP 800-53  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which control framework is widely used by U.S. government organizations, is required by FISMA, and is organized into control families?

- **A.** AACS
- **B.** ISO 15489
- **C.** COBIT
- **D.** NIST SP 800-53

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-53 provides security and privacy controls, is required by FISMA, and is organized into control families (e.g., access control, audit and accountability).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: ISO 15489 is a records-management standard.
- C: COBIT is an IT-governance framework, not the FISMA control catalog.
- A: AACS is a DRM scheme for optical media.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Common Security Control Standards*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0080 — Security Control Frameworks / ISO/IEC 27002  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which international standard, originating from British Standard 7799, provides a widely adopted code of practice broken into control objectives (policies, HR security, cryptography, etc.)?

- **A.** PCI DSS
- **B.** NIST SP 800-37
- **C.** COSO
- **D.** ISO/IEC 27002

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ISO/IEC 27002 originated from BS 7799 (via 17799) and is an international standard of control objectives, widely adopted across industries.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: NIST SP 800-37 is the Risk Management Framework process.
- C: COSO is a financial-reporting/governance framework used for SOX.
- A: PCI DSS is a payment-card security standard.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Security Control Frameworks*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0081 — Security Control Frameworks / HITRUST  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which common security framework is specifically associated with protecting health information?

- **A.** COBIT
- **B.** ISO 15489
- **C.** HITRUST
- **D.** AACS

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** HITRUST (Health Information Trust Alliance) is a common security framework for health information.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: COBIT is a general IT-governance framework.
- B: ISO 15489 is a records-management standard.
- D: AACS is a DRM scheme.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Selecting Security Controls*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0082 — Security Control Frameworks / Framework-to-purpose mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each framework (1-4) to its primary description (A-D).

1. COBIT
2. COSO
3. ISO/IEC 27002
4. NIST SP 800-53

- **A.** International code of practice with security control objectives
- **B.** Financial-reporting framework that drives SOX 404 compliance
- **C.** IT governance framework managed by ISACA
- **D.** U.S. federal security control catalog with control families (required by FISMA)

**Correct answer:** 1-C,2-B,3-A,4-D

**Why correct:** COBIT → IT governance/ISACA (B); COSO → financial reporting/SOX 404 (A); ISO/IEC 27002 → international control objectives (D); NIST SP 800-53 → U.S. federal control families/FISMA (C).

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Important Terms*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0083 — Control Types / Detective control  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Log monitoring and an intrusion detection system are examples of which control type?

- **A.** Deterrent
- **B.** Preventative
- **C.** Detective
- **D.** Recovery

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Detective controls identify incidents after they happen, e.g., log monitoring and intrusion detection systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Preventative controls stop incidents from happening (e.g., firewalls, authentication).
- D: Recovery controls return systems to normal after an incident (e.g., DR plans).
- A: Deterrent controls discourage incidents (e.g., warning banners).

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Types within Defense in Depth Strategy*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0084 — Control Types / Corrective control  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Backup recovery and patch management, used to remedy an incident after detection, are examples of which control type?

- **A.** Corrective
- **B.** Directive
- **C.** Preventative
- **D.** Deterrent

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Corrective controls remedy an incident after detection, e.g., backup recovery and patch management.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Deterrent controls discourage incidents before they occur.
- B: Directive controls mandate behaviors (e.g., policies).
- C: Preventative controls stop incidents from happening in the first place.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Control Types within Defense in Depth Strategy*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0085 — Deming Cycle / PDCA  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the Deming Cycle (PDCA) applied to security controls, which step assesses whether implemented controls are effective and meet security goals?

- **A.** Check
- **B.** Do
- **C.** Act
- **D.** Plan

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** In PDCA, "Check" assesses the effectiveness of controls and whether they meet security goals.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: "Plan" identifies objectives and determines appropriate controls.
- B: "Do" implements the selected controls.
- C: "Act" addresses gaps in effectiveness, especially after incidents or changes.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > The Deming Cycle (PDCA)*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0086 — Data Protection Methods / DLP  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which technology blocks unauthorized data exfiltration by performing egress monitoring and pattern matching against classifications like PII and PHI?

- **A.** Degaussing
- **B.** Digital Rights Management (DRM)
- **C.** Trusted Platform Module (TPM)
- **D.** Data Loss Prevention (DLP)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** DLP blocks unauthorized data exfiltration (egress monitoring) and uses pattern matching to act on classified data such as PII or PHI.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: DRM enforces copyright protection on digital media via encryption.
- C: A TPM secures cryptographic keys/data at rest on a device.
- A: Degaussing is a magnetic data-destruction method.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data Loss Prevention (DLP)*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0087 — Data Protection Methods / DLP types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A DLP deployment that scans local file systems and monitors data leaving individual laptops is which type of DLP?

- **A.** Cloud DLP gateway
- **B.** Perimeter firewall
- **C.** Network DLP
- **D.** Endpoint DLP

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Endpoint DLP scans local file systems and monitors data leaving individual endpoints to detect and block exfiltration.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Network DLP scans outgoing data on the network rather than on the endpoint.
- A: "Cloud DLP gateway" is not the term used; CASB provides cloud DLP functions.
- B: A firewall filters traffic but is not specifically endpoint DLP.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Types of DLP*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0088 — Data Protection Methods / DLP prerequisite  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

For DLP software to reliably detect and act on sensitive data, that data must FIRST be:

- **A.** Encrypted with a key the DLP cannot access
- **B.** Labeled or classified so the DLP policies can recognize it
- **C.** Deleted from all endpoints
- **D.** Made publicly available

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** DLP relies on data being labeled or classified so its policies can detect and act on it; regular updates are also needed for performance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: If DLP cannot inspect the data, it cannot enforce policy on it; this defeats detection.
- C: Deleting data removes the very thing DLP is meant to protect.
- D: Making data public is the opposite of protecting it from exfiltration.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Types of DLP*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0089 — Data Protection Methods / CASB  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Cloud Access Security Broker (CASB) primarily functions as:

- **A.** A records-management retention standard
- **B.** A magnetic device for destroying backup tapes
- **C.** A security policy enforcement point (proxy) between users and cloud services
- **D.** A hardware root of trust embedded in a CPU

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A CASB is a policy enforcement point that acts as a proxy between users and cloud services, applying data security, DLP, threat protection, and URL filtering.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: That describes a degausser.
- A: That describes a standard like ISO 15489.
- D: That describes a TPM.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Cloud Access Security Broker (CASB)*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0090 — Data Protection Methods / DRM  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Digital Rights Management (DRM) primarily uses encryption to:

- **A.** Monitor outbound network traffic for PII
- **B.** Sanitize magnetic media before reuse
- **C.** Track which employees badge into a facility
- **D.** Enforce copyright protection and prevent unauthorized copying/sharing of digital media

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** DRM uses encryption to enforce copyright protection for digital media and prevent copying, piracy, and unauthorized sharing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Monitoring outbound traffic for PII is DLP's role.
- B: Media sanitization is a destruction activity, not DRM.
- C: Badge tracking is a physical access control, not DRM.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data Protection Methods*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0091 — Data Protection Methods / Protection method selection  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company is most worried that employees might email spreadsheets full of customer PII to personal accounts. Which protection method MOST directly addresses this exfiltration risk?

- **A.** A Trusted Platform Module on each laptop
- **B.** Digital Rights Management (DRM) for media files
- **C.** Data Loss Prevention (DLP) with egress monitoring
- **D.** Degaussing of backup tapes

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** DLP with egress monitoring detects and blocks unauthorized transfer of classified data such as PII leaving the organization.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: DRM protects copyrighted media from copying, not general PII exfiltration via email.
- D: Degaussing destroys data on tapes; it does not stop email exfiltration.
- A: A TPM protects keys/data at rest on a device, not data being emailed out.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data Protection Methods*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0092 — Data Protection Methods / Protection methods  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are data protection methods described in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Data Loss Prevention (DLP)
- **B.** Tier 0 (Essential)
- **C.** Unclassified
- **D.** Cloud Access Security Broker (CASB)

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** DLP and CASB are data protection methods; the others are an asset tier and a classification level.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Tier 0 (Essential) is an asset classification tier, not a protection method.
- C: Unclassified is a data classification level, not a protection method.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Important Terms*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0093 — Data Remanence / Definition  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Data remanence refers to:

- **A.** Leftover data that remains after an attempt to delete or remove it
- **B.** Data currently being processed in memory
- **C.** A type of cloud access broker
- **D.** Data that has been classified as Top Secret

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Data remanence is leftover data that remains after deletion attempts; simple deletion often just moves data (e.g., to a recycle bin) rather than destroying it.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Classification level is unrelated to remanence.
- B: Data in memory is data in use, not remanence.
- C: A CASB is a cloud security proxy, not remanence.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Managing Data Remanence*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0094 — Data Destruction / Destruction techniques  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which destruction technique involves REPEATED clearing (overwriting) to ensure data is completely unrecoverable, often used in highly sensitive government or medical environments?

- **A.** Clearing
- **B.** Erasing
- **C.** Labeling
- **D.** Purging

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Purging is repeated clearing to ensure data is completely unrecoverable, common in highly sensitive environments.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Erasing only deletes from the file system and is not effective against remnants.
- A: Clearing overwrites once; purging repeats clearing for stronger assurance.
- C: Labeling is a handling identification activity, not destruction.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data Destruction Techniques*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0095 — Data Destruction / Erasing limitation  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An administrator "deletes" sensitive files and empties the recycle bin before donating the hard drive. Why is this insufficient to prevent data disclosure?

- **A.** Erasing physically shreds the platters, which is unnecessary
- **B.** Erasing removes file-system references but leaves recoverable data remnants on the disk
- **C.** Erasing automatically degausses the drive
- **D.** Erasing encrypts the data so strongly it cannot be read

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Erasing deletes the file-system reference but does not remove the data from the disk, leaving recoverable remnants; clearing/purging/sanitization is required.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Erasing does not physically destroy platters; that would be a separate method.
- D: Erasing does not encrypt data.
- C: Erasing does not perform degaussing.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data Destruction Techniques*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0096 — Data Destruction / Sanitization methods  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which sanitization method uses strong magnetic fields to scramble data and is effective for magnetic media such as backup tapes?

- **A.** Zeroing
- **B.** Tokenization
- **C.** Pseudonymization
- **D.** Degaussing

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Degaussing uses strong magnetic fields to scramble data on magnetic media (e.g., backup tapes), making it unrecoverable.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Zeroing overwrites the medium with zeros and is not magnetic destruction.
- B: Tokenization substitutes data with non-sensitive tokens (a protection method).
- C: Pseudonymization replaces data with aliases (a declassification technique).

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Data Sanitization Methods*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0097 — Data Destruction / Method by media  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A best practice is to choose the sanitization method based on the storage medium. Which pairing follows this guidance?

- **A.** Zeroing for paper documents; marking for SSDs
- **B.** Degaussing for solid-state drives; tokenizing for tapes
- **C.** Degaussing for magnetic tapes; overwriting for hard drives
- **D.** Pseudonymization for hard drives; labeling for tapes

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The guide recommends matching method to medium—e.g., degaussing for tapes and overwriting for hard drives.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Degaussing is for magnetic media (not SSDs), and tokenization is a protection method, not sanitization for tapes.
- D: Pseudonymization and labeling are not media-sanitization methods.
- A: Zeroing applies to digital media, not paper; marking is handling, not SSD sanitization.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Best Practices for Data Remnant Management*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0098 — Data Declassification / Tokenization vs pseudonymization  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which declassification technique substitutes sensitive data with non-sensitive tokens that have no intrinsic value, requiring a separate mapping to recover the original data?

- **A.** Pseudonymization
- **B.** De-identification
- **C.** Tokenization
- **D.** Obfuscation

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Tokenization substitutes sensitive data with meaningless tokens; the tokens must be mapped back to the original for internal use but appear valueless to unauthorized users.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Pseudonymization replaces data with aliases that can still be linked back through separate records, but is not specifically the non-intrinsic-value token mechanism described.
- D: Obfuscation conceals/masks data (e.g., a black box over text).
- B: De-identification removes personal identifiers to anonymize data.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Declassification Techniques*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0099 — Data Declassification / De-identification  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Removing personal identifiers (such as account numbers) from medical records so the data can no longer be linked to a specific person is called:

- **A.** De-identification
- **B.** Scoping
- **C.** Degaussing
- **D.** Tokenization

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** De-identification removes personal identifiers to prevent linking data to a specific subject, anonymizing it.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Tokenization substitutes data with mapped tokens, not identifier removal for anonymization.
- C: Degaussing is magnetic media destruction.
- B: Scoping selects which controls apply to a system.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Declassification Techniques*

---

### CISSP-D02-Q0100 — Data Destruction / NIST SP 800-88  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which NIST Special Publication provides guidelines for media sanitization?

- **A.** NIST SP 800-37
- **B.** NIST SP 800-60
- **C.** NIST SP 800-53
- **D.** NIST SP 800-88

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-88 provides guidelines for media sanitization.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: NIST SP 800-60 covers data types and classification.
- A: NIST SP 800-37 is the Risk Management Framework.
- C: NIST SP 800-53 is the security/privacy control catalog.

*Source: Domain 2 > Data Security Controls > Regulations and Guidelines*

---


## Domain 3: Security Architecture and Engineering

### CISSP-D03-Q0001 — Secure Design Concepts / Confinement (sandboxing)  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Running software in an isolated environment that restricts its access to system resources to avoid unintended interactions is known as:

- **A.** Psychological acceptability
- **B.** Transitive trust
- **C.** Confinement (sandboxing)
- **D.** Open design

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Confinement (sandboxing) runs software in an isolated environment, restricting its access to system resources to prevent unintended interactions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Transitive trust is when trust is passed along a chain of relationships.
- D: Open design means the design can be openly reviewed; security rests on secret keys.
- A: Psychological acceptability concerns making security usable.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0002 — Secure Design Concepts / Bounds  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A control that limits the memory resources available to a process so it cannot affect other areas of the system is referred to as:

- **A.** Assurance
- **B.** Work factor
- **C.** Bounds
- **D.** Complete mediation

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Bounds limit the memory/resources available to a process, ensuring it operates within assigned limits and cannot impact other system areas.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Assurance is the degree of confidence that security controls work as intended.
- D: Complete mediation requires authorization on every access.
- B: Work factor measures the effort required to break a control or cipher.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0003 — Secure Design Concepts / Transitive trust  _(hard, scenario_based)_

System A trusts System B, and System B trusts System C. Because of this chain, System A effectively trusts System C even though it was never directly evaluated. This relationship is an example of:

- **A.** Complete mediation
- **B.** Fail-safe defaults
- **C.** Transitive trust
- **D.** Separation of duties

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Transitive trust is when trust flows along a chain (A→B→C), causing A to implicitly trust C; least common mechanism warns against such transitive trust.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Complete mediation checks authorization on every access, not chained trust.
- D: Separation of duties splits critical functions among people.
- B: Fail-safe defaults deny by default; unrelated to trust chaining.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Trust and Secure Design*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0004 — Secure Design Concepts / Assurance  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In secure design, "assurance" refers to:

- **A.** The act of isolating a process in memory
- **B.** A precomputed table of password hashes
- **C.** The degree of confidence that a system's security controls function as intended
- **D.** A magnetic method of destroying data

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Assurance is the level of confidence that security measures/controls work correctly and as intended.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: That describes memory protection/isolation.
- B: That describes a rainbow table.
- D: That describes degaussing.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Trust and Secure Design*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0005 — Secure Design Principles / Fail-safe defaults  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A design principle stating that default settings should DENY access and that components should fail closed rather than open is:

- **A.** Open design
- **B.** Fail-safe defaults
- **C.** Economy of mechanism
- **D.** Psychological acceptability

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Fail-safe defaults require that default settings deny access and that components fail securely (closed) rather than open.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Open design means the design itself is open to review.
- D: Psychological acceptability concerns usability of security.
- C: Economy of mechanism favors simple, small designs.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0006 — Secure Design Principles / Complete mediation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which principle requires that authorization be checked EVERY time a subject requests access to an object, rather than once and cached indefinitely?

- **A.** Open design
- **B.** Least common mechanism
- **C.** Complete mediation
- **D.** Economy of mechanism

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Complete mediation requires authorization to be verified on each access request, preventing unauthorized access that could arise from stale permissions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Least common mechanism limits shared components/transitive trust.
- D: Economy of mechanism favors simplicity.
- A: Open design concerns openness of the design to review.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0007 — Secure Design Principles / Open design  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The "open design" principle holds that the security of a system should depend on:

- **A.** Using only proprietary, undisclosed algorithms
- **B.** Keeping the entire system design secret from everyone
- **C.** Avoiding any external review of the system
- **D.** Keeping cryptographic keys and passwords secret, not on hiding the design itself

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Open design states the design should be open to external review; security should rest on secret keys/passwords, not obscurity of the design (the opposite of security through obscurity).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Hiding the design is security through obscurity, which open design rejects.
- A: Proprietary undisclosed algorithms contradict open design.
- C: Open design encourages external review.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0008 — Secure Design Principles / Economy of mechanism  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which secure design principle favors keeping a design as simple and small as possible to reduce complexity and potential errors?

- **A.** Complete mediation
- **B.** Separation of privilege
- **C.** Least privilege
- **D.** Economy of mechanism

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Economy of mechanism calls for simple, small designs to reduce complexity and the likelihood of errors/flaws.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Least privilege grants minimum necessary access.
- A: Complete mediation checks every access.
- B: Separation of privilege requires multiple subjects for critical functions.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0009 — Secure Design Principles / Least common mechanism  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A principle that advises LIMITING shared components and avoiding transitive trust between processes to minimize cross-system vulnerabilities is:

- **A.** Psychological acceptability
- **B.** Complete mediation
- **C.** Fail-safe defaults
- **D.** Least common mechanism

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Least common mechanism minimizes shared mechanisms/components and avoids transitive trust, reducing pathways for cross-system compromise.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Psychological acceptability concerns usable security.
- C: Fail-safe defaults deny by default.
- B: Complete mediation checks authorization on each access.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0010 — Secure Design Principles / Psychological acceptability  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Designing security features so users find them simple and are not tempted to bypass them embodies which principle?

- **A.** Psychological acceptability
- **B.** Economy of mechanism
- **C.** Separation of privilege
- **D.** Open design

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Psychological acceptability holds that security should be user-friendly so users do not circumvent it through error or frustration.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Economy of mechanism is about design simplicity, not user experience.
- D: Open design concerns openness of design to review.
- C: Separation of privilege requires multiple parties for critical actions.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0011 — Secure Design Principles / Separation of privilege  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Requiring two or more subjects to execute a critical function, minimizing risk from any single user, is the principle of:

- **A.** Separation of privilege
- **B.** Complete mediation
- **C.** Least privilege
- **D.** Least common mechanism

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Separation of privilege (separation of duties) requires two or more subjects to perform critical functions, reducing single-user risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Least privilege limits each subject to minimum access, but does not require multiple subjects for one function.
- D: Least common mechanism limits shared components.
- B: Complete mediation checks every access.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0012 — Secure Design Principles / Saltzer and Schroeder  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are among Saltzer and Schroeder's secure design principles? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** No write down
- **B.** Birthday attack resistance
- **C.** Least privilege
- **D.** Complete mediation

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** Least privilege and complete mediation are Saltzer & Schroeder design principles.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: "No write down" is the Bell-LaPadula star property, not a Saltzer-Schroeder design principle.
- B: "Birthday attack resistance" is a cryptographic property, not a design principle.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0013 — Zero Trust Architecture / Core concept  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The central premise of Zero Trust Architecture is:

- **A.** Only perimeter firewalls are needed for security
- **B.** Never automatically trust any user or device; verify every access on a per-session basis
- **C.** Trust all devices on the internal/private network by default
- **D.** Trust is granted permanently after the first successful login

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Zero Trust assumes no implicit trust—every access is authenticated/authorized per session regardless of network location.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Zero Trust explicitly rejects implicit trust of private networks.
- D: Access is evaluated continuously per session, not granted permanently.
- A: Zero Trust goes beyond perimeter defenses to per-resource verification.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Zero Trust Architecture*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0014 — Zero Trust Architecture / NIST SP 800-207  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which document defines the Zero Trust Architecture principles referenced in the guide?

- **A.** NIST SP 800-207
- **B.** ISO 11889
- **C.** NIST SP 800-88
- **D.** NIST SP 800-53

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-207 defines Zero Trust Architecture principles.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: NIST SP 800-53 is the security/privacy control catalog.
- B: ISO 11889 is the TPM standard.
- C: NIST SP 800-88 covers media sanitization.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > NIST SP 800-207 Zero Trust Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0015 — Zero Trust Architecture / ZT components  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In a Zero Trust Architecture, which component actually ENFORCES the access decision by acting as a gateway between a subject and a resource?

- **A.** Policy Administrator (PA)
- **B.** Trusted Platform Module (TPM)
- **C.** Policy Engine (PE)
- **D.** Policy Enforcement Point (PEP)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The Policy Enforcement Point (PEP) enforces access control by acting as the gateway/intermediary between subjects and resources.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: The Policy Engine decides whether to grant access (the decision), but does not enforce at the gateway.
- A: The Policy Administrator manages communication and token creation between the PE and PEP.
- B: A TPM is a cryptographic hardware chip, not a ZT policy component.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Zero Trust Architecture Components*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0016 — Zero Trust Architecture / ZT components  _(hard, matching)_

Match each Zero Trust component (1-3) to its role (A-C).

1. Policy Engine (PE)
2. Policy Administrator (PA)
3. Policy Enforcement Point (PEP)

- **A.** Enforces access as a gateway/intermediary between subject and resource
- **B.** Manages subject-resource communication and creates access tokens
- **C.** Decides whether to authorize a subject's access to a resource and logs decisions

**Correct answer:** 1-C,2-B,3-A

**Why correct:** Policy Engine → decides/logs (B); Policy Administrator → manages communication/tokens (A); Policy Enforcement Point → enforces at the gateway (C).

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Zero Trust Architecture Components*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0017 — Zero Trust Architecture / Trust but verify vs ZT  _(medium, scenario_based)_

An organization currently grants broad network access once a device connects to the corporate LAN. To move toward Zero Trust, it should:

- **A.** Grant permanent access tokens that never expire
- **B.** Continue trusting all LAN-connected devices to reduce friction
- **C.** Treat the internal network as untrusted and authenticate/authorize each resource request
- **D.** Rely solely on a stronger perimeter firewall

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Zero Trust removes implicit trust of private networks—each resource request is authenticated/authorized regardless of network location.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Trusting LAN devices by default is exactly what Zero Trust eliminates.
- D: A stronger perimeter still leaves implicit internal trust intact.
- A: Non-expiring tokens contradict per-session, continuous verification.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Zero Trust Architecture*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0018 — Shared Responsibility Model / Inherited controls  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Under the shared responsibility model, an organization that uses a cloud provider typically INHERITS which type of control from the provider?

- **A.** Physical security of the data center
- **B.** Its own user access reviews
- **C.** Its application-layer input validation
- **D.** Classification of its own data

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Customers inherit controls like physical security of the provider's data centers, while retaining responsibility for their own data and access.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Data classification remains the customer's responsibility.
- B: Access reviews of the customer's users remain the customer's job.
- C: Application input validation is the customer's responsibility (especially in IaaS/PaaS).

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Shared Responsibility Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0019 — Privacy by Design / Proactive principle  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Privacy by Design principle "Proactive, not Reactive" means an organization should:

- **A.** Wait for a breach before adding any privacy controls
- **B.** Collect as much data as possible by default
- **C.** Disclose user data publicly for transparency
- **D.** Anticipate and prevent privacy risks before they occur rather than responding after the fact

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Proactive, not reactive focuses on anticipating and preventing privacy risks before they materialize, aiming beyond mere compliance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Waiting for a breach is reactive—the opposite of this principle.
- B: Collecting maximal data violates privacy by default/data minimization.
- C: Transparency means clear policies, not exposing user data publicly.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Privacy By Design*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0020 — Privacy by Design / Privacy by default  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A new app is configured so that, out of the box, it shares the minimum data necessary and keeps the strictest privacy settings unless a user opts in to more sharing. This best reflects which Privacy by Design principle?

- **A.** Privacy by Default
- **B.** Full Functionality (Positive-Sum)
- **C.** Visibility and Transparency
- **D.** End-to-End Security

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Privacy by Default sets the strictest privacy protections as the default, collecting only necessary data and limiting purpose, scope, and retention.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Visibility and Transparency concerns publishing accessible privacy policies.
- B: Full Functionality (Positive-Sum) balances privacy with functionality without trade-offs.
- D: End-to-End Security protects data across its full lifecycle.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Privacy By Design*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0021 — Privacy by Design / Positive-sum  _(medium, single_best_answer)_
