The "Full Functionality – Positive-Sum, Not Zero-Sum" principle asserts that privacy measures should:

- **A.** Replace all business functionality with privacy controls
- **B.** Coexist with business objectives and usability rather than forcing an either/or trade-off
- **C.** Be applied only after the product is decommissioned
- **D.** Always be sacrificed to maximize functionality

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Positive-sum means privacy protections support business processes and user experience without unnecessary trade-offs—not an either/or choice.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Sacrificing privacy for functionality is the zero-sum view this principle rejects.
- A: Privacy does not replace functionality; the two coexist.
- C: Privacy is integrated throughout, not only at decommission.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Full Functionality – Positive-Sum, Not Zero-Sum*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0022 — System Security Capabilities / Memory protection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The PRIMARY goal of memory protection as a system security capability is to:

- **A.** Increase the clock speed of the CPU
- **B.** Provide physical access control to the data center
- **C.** Prevent processes in memory from interacting with each other to avoid data leaks
- **D.** Encrypt all network traffic in transit

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Memory protection prevents processes in memory from interacting, allowing multiple processes (even at different security levels) to run independently without data leakage.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Memory protection is about isolation, not CPU performance.
- D: Encrypting network traffic is a transport-layer control, not memory protection.
- B: Physical access control is a facility control, not memory protection.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > System Security Capabilities*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0023 — System Security Capabilities / Constrained interface  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An application hides admin-only menu items from standard users, exposing only the functions appropriate to each role. This capability is a:

- **A.** Reference monitor
- **B.** Trusted Platform Module
- **C.** Covert channel
- **D.** Restricted/constrained interface

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A restricted/constrained interface limits the objects and functions a user can access based on privilege level, implementing least privilege at the UI.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A TPM is a cryptographic hardware chip.
- A: A reference monitor mediates all access between subjects and objects at a system level.
- C: A covert channel is an unauthorized communication path, not a UI control.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > User Interfaces (Restricted or Constrained Interfaces)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0024 — System Security Capabilities / TPM  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A Trusted Platform Module (TPM) is BEST described as:

- **A.** A software sandbox for running untrusted code
- **B.** A specialized motherboard chip that provides cryptographic services such as key generation and encryption
- **C.** A network appliance that filters egress traffic
- **D.** A cloud service that stores virtual machines

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A TPM is a specialized motherboard chip (ISO 11889) providing cryptographic services such as creating key pairs, signing data, and encryption.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A TPM is local hardware, not a cloud VM service.
- C: Egress filtering is a network DLP/firewall function.
- A: A sandbox isolates code in software, not a cryptographic chip.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Trusted Platform Module (TPM)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0025 — System Security Capabilities / TPM endorsement key  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which TPM component is a private key embedded by the hardware manufacturer that is unique and cannot be altered?

- **A.** Endorsement Key (EK)
- **B.** Storage Root Key (SRK)
- **C.** Attestation Identity Key (AIK)
- **D.** Platform Configuration Register (PCR)

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The Endorsement Key (EK) is a private key embedded by the hardware manufacturer that is unalterable and unique to the TPM.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: The Storage Root Key (SRK) secures keys stored in the TPM.
- D: PCRs track the current software/configuration state.
- C: The AIK is used for attestation of platform identity.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Trusted Platform Module (TPM)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0026 — System Security Capabilities / HSM  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A standalone cryptographic device used to perform encryption/decryption and securely manage cryptographic keys for high-security applications is a:

- **A.** Hardware Security Module (HSM)
- **B.** Policy Enforcement Point (PEP)
- **C.** Sandbox
- **D.** Constrained interface

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** An HSM is a standalone cryptographic device providing encryption/decryption and high-security key management.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A PEP enforces Zero Trust access decisions.
- D: A constrained interface limits UI functions by privilege.
- C: A sandbox isolates running code.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Trusted Platform Module (TPM)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0027 — System Security Capabilities / Virtualization benefits  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

As a system security capability, virtualization is useful for malware analysis primarily because it:

- **A.** Eliminates the need for any access controls
- **B.** Encrypts all data on the network
- **C.** Provides isolated, sandboxed environments where malware can run without affecting the host
- **D.** Permanently prevents all malware from executing

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Virtualization enables isolated, sandboxed environments useful for safely analyzing malware, along with centralized control, scalability, and easy recovery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Virtualization isolates malware but does not prevent its execution within the sandbox.
- B: Virtualization is about emulating systems, not encrypting network traffic.
- A: Access controls are still required in virtualized environments.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > System Security Capabilities*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0028 — Secure Design Principles / Least privilege  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Granting a subject only the minimum access required to perform its job functions is the principle of:

- **A.** Least privilege
- **B.** Complete mediation
- **C.** Economy of mechanism
- **D.** Open design

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Least privilege grants subjects only the minimum access necessary for their functions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Open design concerns openness of the design to review.
- C: Economy of mechanism favors simple design.
- B: Complete mediation checks authorization on each access.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Design Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0029 — Zero Trust Architecture / NIST 800-207 principles  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO statements reflect NIST SP 800-207 Zero Trust principles? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Authentication and authorization are strictly enforced before access is allowed
- **B.** Devices on the private network are implicitly trusted
- **C.** Once authenticated, a session is trusted indefinitely
- **D.** Access to resources is granted on a per-session basis

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Per-session access and strict, real-time authentication/authorization are core NIST SP 800-207 principles.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Zero Trust grants no implicit trust to private-network devices.
- C: Trust is continuously evaluated, not granted indefinitely.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > NIST SP 800-207 Zero Trust Principles*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0030 — Privacy by Design / End-to-end security  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Privacy by Design principle of "End-to-End Security – Full Lifecycle Protection" requires protecting data:

- **A.** Only while it is displayed on screen
- **B.** Throughout its entire lifecycle—creation, processing, storage, and destruction
- **C.** Only after a regulator requests it
- **D.** Only during the initial collection step

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** End-to-end security protects data across its full lifecycle: creation, processing, storage, and destruction, with secure handling and transmission.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Protection must extend beyond on-screen display to all lifecycle stages.
- C: Protection is proactive, not triggered by a regulator request.
- D: Protection spans the whole lifecycle, not only collection.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > End-to-End Security – Full Lifecycle Protection*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0031 — Security Models / Trusted Computing Base  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Trusted Computing Base (TCB) is BEST defined as:

- **A.** The combination of hardware, software, and controls that work together to enforce a security policy
- **B.** A single firewall at the network perimeter
- **C.** A precomputed table of password hashes
- **D.** The list of all users authorized to access a system

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The TCB is the totality of protection mechanisms (hardware, software, firmware, and controls) that together enforce the system's security policy.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A firewall is one control, not the entire trusted computing base.
- D: A user list is not the TCB.
- C: A rainbow table is an attack artifact, unrelated to the TCB.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Understanding Security Models*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0032 — Security Models / Reference monitor  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The reference monitor concept describes a component that:

- **A.** Physically restricts entry to the server room
- **B.** Verifies and enforces access requests between subjects and objects
- **C.** Generates cryptographic key pairs in hardware
- **D.** Stores backup copies of all system data

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The reference monitor mediates (verifies and enforces) all access requests between subjects and objects.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Backups are a recovery function, not the reference monitor.
- C: Key generation is a TPM/HSM function.
- A: Physical entry restriction is a facility control.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Reference Monitor*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0033 — Security Models / Security kernel  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

How does the security kernel relate to the reference monitor concept?

- **A.** The security kernel is the hardware/software implementation that enforces the reference monitor concept
- **B.** The security kernel is an abstract idea while the reference monitor is physical hardware only
- **C.** The security kernel replaces the need for any access control
- **D.** They are unrelated; the kernel handles only memory paging

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The security kernel is the actual implementation (hardware, firmware, software) that realizes and enforces the reference monitor concept, handling access requests and maintaining security integrity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: The reference monitor is the abstract concept; the kernel is its implementation—this option reverses them.
- D: They are directly related, not unrelated.
- C: The kernel enforces access control; it does not eliminate it.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Security Kernel*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0034 — Security Models / Trusted path  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A "trusted path" provides:

- **A.** A precomputed lookup of password hashes
- **B.** A protected communication channel between the user and the security functions of a system
- **C.** A list of all open network ports
- **D.** A magnetic method to destroy data

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A trusted path is a protected channel that assures users they are communicating directly with the TCB/security functions, free from interference.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: That describes a rainbow table.
- D: That describes degaussing.
- C: That describes a port scan result.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Trusted Path*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0035 — Security Models / Lattice model  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A lattice-based access control model assigns security based on:

- **A.** The number of CPU cores in the system
- **B.** The physical color of the device chassis
- **C.** The order in which users logged in
- **D.** Levels (and compartments) where each subject/object sits at a defined position with upper and lower bounds

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Lattice models place subjects and objects at defined security levels with least-upper-bound/greatest-lower-bound relationships, underpinning models like Biba and Bell-LaPadula.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Login order does not define lattice positions.
- B: Chassis color is irrelevant.
- A: CPU core count is irrelevant to a lattice.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Security Perimeter*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0036 — Security Models / Bell-LaPadula  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The Bell-LaPadula model is designed primarily to protect which security property?

- **A.** Integrity
- **B.** Confidentiality
- **C.** Availability
- **D.** Non-repudiation

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Bell-LaPadula is a confidentiality model, especially used in military/government environments.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Integrity is the focus of Biba and Clark-Wilson.
- C: Availability is not the focus of BLP.
- D: Non-repudiation is a cryptographic property, not BLP's focus.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Bell-LaPadula Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0037 — Security Models / Bell-LaPadula  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Bell-LaPadula's Simple Security Rule states "no read up." What does its Star (*) Property state?

- **A.** No read down
- **B.** No write down
- **C.** No read up
- **D.** No write up

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The BLP Star Property is "no write down"—subjects cannot write to a lower classification, preventing leakage of sensitive data to lower levels.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: "No read down" is the Biba Simple Integrity Axiom.
- D: "No write up" is the Biba Star Integrity Axiom.
- C: "No read up" is the BLP Simple Security Rule, not the Star Property.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Bell-LaPadula Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0038 — Security Models / Bell-LaPadula  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A military system must ensure a user cleared only to "Secret" cannot read "Top Secret" documents. Which model rule directly enforces this, and from which model?

- **A.** Simple Integrity Axiom (no read down) — Biba
- **B.** Simple Security Rule (no read up) — Bell-LaPadula
- **C.** Well-formed transactions — Clark-Wilson
- **D.** Star Integrity Axiom (no write up) — Biba

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Preventing a lower-cleared subject from reading higher-classified data is the Bell-LaPadula Simple Security Rule ("no read up"), a confidentiality protection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Biba's simple integrity axiom (no read down) protects integrity, not confidentiality of higher data.
- D: Biba's star integrity axiom (no write up) is an integrity rule.
- C: Clark-Wilson well-formed transactions protect integrity in business apps.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Bell-LaPadula Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0039 — Security Models / Biba  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The Biba model is designed primarily to protect:

- **A.** Anonymity
- **B.** Confidentiality
- **C.** Availability
- **D.** Integrity

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Biba prioritizes integrity, ensuring the accuracy and consistency of data, and is based on the lattice model.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Confidentiality is the focus of Bell-LaPadula.
- C: Availability is not Biba's focus.
- A: Anonymity is not an addressed property.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Biba Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0040 — Security Models / Biba  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Biba's Simple Integrity Axiom is "no read down." What is its Star Integrity Axiom?

- **A.** No write down
- **B.** No write up
- **C.** No read down
- **D.** No read up

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Biba's Star Integrity Axiom is "no write up"—lower-integrity subjects cannot write to higher-integrity objects, protecting data integrity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: "No write down" is the Bell-LaPadula star property.
- D: "No read up" is the Bell-LaPadula simple security rule.
- C: "No read down" is Biba's Simple Integrity Axiom, not the star axiom.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Biba Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0041 — Security Models / BLP vs Biba  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A financial system must ensure low-integrity data entered by untrusted processes cannot corrupt high-integrity records. Which rule applies?

- **A.** Biba Star Integrity Axiom (no write up)
- **B.** Bell-LaPadula Simple Security Rule (no read up)
- **C.** Bell-LaPadula Star Property (no write down)
- **D.** Brewer-Nash conflict-of-interest rule

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Preventing low-integrity subjects from writing to high-integrity objects is Biba's Star Integrity Axiom ("no write up"), protecting record integrity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: BLP no-write-down protects confidentiality, not integrity of high records.
- B: BLP no-read-up is a confidentiality rule.
- D: Brewer-Nash prevents conflicts of interest, not integrity corruption.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Biba Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0042 — Security Models / Model focus mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each security model (1-4) to its primary focus (A-D).

1. Bell-LaPadula
2. Biba
3. Clark-Wilson
4. Brewer-Nash

- **A.** Integrity via well-formed transactions and an access triple
- **B.** Integrity using a lattice (no read down / no write up)
- **C.** Confidentiality (no read up / no write down)
- **D.** Preventing conflicts of interest (Chinese Wall)

**Correct answer:** 1-C,2-B,3-A,4-D

**Why correct:** Bell-LaPadula → confidentiality (B); Biba → integrity via lattice (D); Clark-Wilson → integrity via well-formed transactions/access triple (A); Brewer-Nash → conflict-of-interest prevention (C).

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Security Models - Part 2*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0043 — Security Models / Clark-Wilson  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Clark-Wilson model enforces integrity by requiring subjects to access data only through:

- **A.** A no-read-up confidentiality rule
- **B.** Direct, unrestricted read/write to all objects
- **C.** Well-formed transactions via an access triple (subject, program, object)
- **D.** Conflict-of-interest class separation

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Clark-Wilson restricts subjects from directly manipulating data; access occurs through programs (the subject-program-object access triple) using well-formed transactions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Clark-Wilson specifically restricts direct manipulation of data.
- A: No-read-up is a Bell-LaPadula confidentiality rule.
- D: Conflict-of-interest separation is Brewer-Nash.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Clark-Wilson Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0044 — Security Models / Clark-Wilson terms  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In Clark-Wilson, which element verifies that a Constrained Data Item (CDI) remains in a valid, consistent state after modification?

- **A.** Transformation Procedure (TP)
- **B.** Unconstrained Data Item (UDI)
- **C.** Integrity Verification Procedure (IVP)
- **D.** Access Control Matrix (ACM)

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The Integrity Verification Procedure (IVP) verifies that the integrity of a CDI is maintained after modification.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A Transformation Procedure (TP) is the authorized method that modifies a CDI.
- B: A UDI is data that does not require protection.
- D: An ACM maps subjects to object permissions; it is not the Clark-Wilson IVP.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Key Terms*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0045 — Security Models / Brewer-Nash  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A consulting firm must ensure an analyst who accessed Company A's confidential data cannot then access data from Company A's direct competitor. Which model addresses this?

- **A.** Graham-Denning Model
- **B.** Bell-LaPadula Model
- **C.** Biba Model
- **D.** Brewer-Nash (Chinese Wall) Model

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The Brewer-Nash (Chinese Wall) model dynamically prevents access that would create a conflict of interest, such as accessing competing companies' data.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Bell-LaPadula addresses confidentiality classification levels, not conflicts of interest.
- C: Biba addresses integrity.
- A: Graham-Denning defines subject/object creation and access-rights actions.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Brewer-Nash Model (Chinese Wall Model)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0046 — Security Models / Graham-Denning  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Graham-Denning model focuses on:

- **A.** Preventing low-integrity writes to high-integrity data
- **B.** Suppressing fires in a data center
- **C.** Enforcing no read up for confidentiality
- **D.** Securely creating/deleting subjects and objects and managing access rights via an access control matrix

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Graham-Denning defines eight primary actions for securely creating/deleting subjects and objects and granting/transferring/revoking access rights, using an access control matrix.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: That describes Biba's star integrity axiom.
- C: That describes Bell-LaPadula's simple security rule.
- B: Fire suppression is a physical security control, not a model.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Graham-Denning Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0047 — Security Models / Take-Grant  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The Take-Grant model uses rules such as take, grant, create, and remove primarily to:

- **A.** Detect hash collisions in weak algorithms
- **B.** Define classification levels for military data
- **C.** Manage how access rights are transferred or revoked between subjects and objects
- **D.** Suppress electrical fires in server rooms

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Take-Grant uses take, grant, create, and remove rules to model how rights are assigned, inherited, or removed based on trust relationships.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Hash collisions relate to birthday attacks, not Take-Grant.
- D: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- B: Classification levels relate to data classification/Bell-LaPadula context, not Take-Grant rules.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Biba Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0048 — Security Models / Sutherland / non-interference  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The Sutherland (Non-Deducibility) model aims to prevent which type of security problem?

- **A.** Brute-force cracking of password hashes
- **B.** Information leaks through covert channels and unauthorized inferences about data
- **C.** Physical theft of backup tapes
- **D.** Excessive humidity in a server room

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The Sutherland (Non-Deducibility) model controls information flow to prevent leaks via covert channels and prevent unauthorized inferences about protected data.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Tape theft is a physical security issue.
- A: Hash cracking is a cryptanalytic attack.
- D: Humidity is an environmental control concern.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Sutherland Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0049 — Security Models / Access control matrix  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In an access control matrix, the permissions a specific subject has on a specific object are found:

- **A.** At the cell where the subject's row intersects the object's column
- **B.** In a separate rainbow table
- **C.** In the TPM's endorsement key
- **D.** Only in the matrix header

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** An access control matrix lists subjects as rows and objects as columns; the cell at their intersection holds the granted permissions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Headers label rows/columns; permissions live in the cells.
- B: A rainbow table is an attack artifact, not an ACM.
- C: The EK is a TPM key, unrelated to an ACM.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Access Control Matrix*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0050 — Common Criteria / EAL  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Within Common Criteria, an Evaluation Assurance Level (EAL) expresses:

- **A.** The market price of the product
- **B.** The physical weight of the appliance
- **C.** The depth and rigor of the evaluation/assurance applied to a product (e.g., EAL1 lowest to EAL7 highest)
- **D.** The number of users licensed to use the product

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** EALs (EAL1 through EAL7) express the rigor and depth of the security evaluation, with higher levels indicating more thorough assurance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: EAL is about assurance rigor, not price.
- D: EAL does not denote user count.
- B: EAL has nothing to do with physical weight.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Evaluation Assurance Levels (EALs)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0051 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / ACID  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The ACID properties that ensure reliable database transactions stand for:

- **A.** Authentication, Confidentiality, Integrity, Defense
- **B.** Access, Control, Identity, Directory
- **C.** Availability, Capacity, Interoperability, Density
- **D.** Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ACID = Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability—the properties ensuring reliable database transactions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: These are mixed security terms, not the ACID properties.
- B: These are not the ACID properties.
- C: These are not the ACID properties.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > ACID Framework for Database Transactions*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0052 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / ICS/SCADA  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Industrial Control Systems (ICS) and SCADA environments are especially security-sensitive primarily because:

- **A.** They are never connected to any network
- **B.** They store only public marketing data
- **C.** They automatically patch themselves continuously
- **D.** They manage physical processes (e.g., power, water) where a compromise can cause real-world physical impact

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ICS/SCADA control physical processes, so attacks can have real-world physical and safety impacts; they often run legacy, hard-to-patch components.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Many ICS are networked, increasing exposure.
- B: They handle operational/process data, not just public marketing data.
- C: ICS are often difficult to patch, not continuously self-patching.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Industrial Control Systems*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0053 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / IoT  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A company is deploying hundreds of inexpensive IoT sensors. Which is the MOST appropriate security consideration for this rollout?

- **A.** Place all IoT devices directly on the main corporate LAN for convenience
- **B.** Disable all monitoring to reduce alert volume
- **C.** Assume the devices are secure because they are new
- **D.** Segment IoT devices on isolated networks and change default credentials, since many lack strong built-in security

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** IoT devices often have weak built-in security; segmenting them and removing default credentials reduces the risk they pose to the broader network.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Placing weak IoT devices on the main LAN exposes critical systems.
- C: New does not mean secure; many IoT devices ship with weak defaults.
- B: Disabling monitoring reduces visibility into a high-risk device class.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Internet of Things*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0054 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / Embedded systems  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A common security challenge specific to embedded systems is that they:

- **A.** Cannot be attacked because they have no software
- **B.** Often have limited resources and infrequent updates, making patching difficult
- **C.** Are immune to supply-chain risks
- **D.** Always run a full modern OS with automatic updates

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Embedded systems frequently have constrained resources and are rarely/never updated, making vulnerabilities persistent and patching difficult.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Embedded systems typically run minimal firmware, not full auto-updating OSes.
- A: Embedded systems run software/firmware and can be attacked.
- C: Embedded components are subject to supply-chain risks (e.g., counterfeits, malcode).

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Embedded Systems*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0055 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / API gateway / microservices  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO security functions does an API gateway centralize for calls into a microservices architecture? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Authentication of incoming requests
- **B.** Physically locking the server room door
- **C.** Generating TPM endorsement keys
- **D.** Authorization and rate limiting of traffic

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** An API gateway provides a central point to enforce authentication, authorization, and rate limiting/traffic control for microservice calls.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: EK generation is a TPM hardware function, not an API gateway role.
- B: Door locks are physical controls, unrelated to API gateways.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > API Gateway and Service Mesh Architecture*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0056 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / Database inference/aggregation  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A user without access to classified salary data is able to deduce an executive's salary by combining several unclassified query results. This database vulnerability is BEST described as:

- **A.** A wet-pipe leak
- **B.** Atomicity failure
- **C.** Aggregation of patches
- **D.** Inference

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Inference occurs when a user derives sensitive information from combinations of data they are authorized to see—a classic database confidentiality vulnerability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: "Aggregation of patches" is not a database vulnerability term.
- B: Atomicity failure concerns transaction integrity, not deducing secrets.
- A: A wet-pipe leak is a physical/fire-suppression issue.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Database Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0057 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / SASE  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Secure Access Service Edge (SASE) primarily combines:

- **A.** Only on-premises firewalls with no cloud component
- **B.** Hardware key generation and physical badges
- **C.** Networking and security functions delivered together as a cloud-based service
- **D.** Fire suppression and HVAC controls

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SASE converges network and security capabilities (e.g., SD-WAN, secure web gateway, ZTNA) delivered as a cloud-based service.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: SASE is cloud-delivered, not purely on-prem firewalls.
- D: Those are facility/environmental controls.
- B: Those are unrelated hardware/physical controls.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Secure Access Service Edge*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0058 — Architecture Vulnerabilities / Architecture concepts  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized strategies for mitigating vulnerabilities in modern system architectures? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Traffic isolation and network segmentation
- **B.** Granting every microservice full administrative rights
- **C.** Disabling all logging to improve performance
- **D.** Securing APIs and inter-service communications with encryption

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Segmentation/isolation and securing APIs/inter-service communication with encryption are sound mitigations for modern architecture risks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Granting full admin rights to every service violates least privilege and expands blast radius.
- C: Disabling logging removes detection and forensic capability.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Objective 3.5*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0059 — Security Models / State machine model  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A finite state machine model considers a system secure if:

- **A.** It encrypts all data with symmetric keys
- **B.** It begins in a secure state and every permitted transition leads to another secure state
- **C.** It has the most network ports open
- **D.** It uses the fastest possible CPU

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** In a state machine model, a system is secure if it starts in a secure state and all transitions keep it in secure states.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: CPU speed is irrelevant to the model.
- A: Encryption choice is not what defines a secure state machine.
- C: Open ports do not define security in a state machine model.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Security Perimeter*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0060 — Security Models / Non-interference  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The Non-Interference (Goguen-Meseguer) model ensures that:

- **A.** Fires are suppressed before damaging equipment
- **B.** Data is destroyed when no longer needed
- **C.** Actions of higher-security subjects do not affect or become visible to lower-security subjects
- **D.** All users can read all data regardless of level

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Non-interference ensures that the actions of high-level subjects do not interfere with or leak to low-level subjects, preventing covert information flow.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Unrestricted reads contradict the model's purpose.
- B: Data destruction is a lifecycle/retention concern.
- A: Fire suppression is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Security Models - Part 1*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0061 — Virtualization / Type 1 vs Type 2 hypervisor  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Type 1 hypervisor differs from a Type 2 hypervisor in that a Type 1:

- **A.** Runs directly on the bare-metal hardware without an underlying host OS
- **B.** Always runs as an application on top of a host operating system
- **C.** Is only used on mobile phones
- **D.** Cannot host more than one virtual machine

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A Type 1 (bare-metal) hypervisor runs directly on hardware, typically used for production; a Type 2 (hosted) hypervisor runs as software on a host OS.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Running on a host OS describes a Type 2 hypervisor.
- D: Type 1 hypervisors host many VMs.
- C: Type 1 hypervisors run on servers, not specifically phones.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Types of Hypervisors*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0062 — Virtualization / Hypervisor use cases  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A developer wants to run a few test VMs on their Windows laptop using VMware Workstation. Which hypervisor type is this?

- **A.** Type 1 (bare-metal)
- **B.** A serverless function
- **C.** Type 2 (hosted)
- **D.** A container engine

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** VMware Workstation/VirtualBox run on top of a host OS, making them Type 2 (hosted) hypervisors—suited to desktop/testing use.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Type 1 runs directly on hardware (e.g., ESXi), typically for production servers.
- D: A container engine shares the host kernel rather than virtualizing full machines.
- B: Serverless runs functions in a managed cloud environment, not a desktop VM.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Use Cases for Hypervisor Types*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0063 — Virtualization / VM escape  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An attacker exploits a hypervisor vulnerability to break out of a guest virtual machine and gain control of the underlying host and other VMs. This attack is known as:

- **A.** Pass the hash
- **B.** Tailgating
- **C.** A birthday attack
- **D.** VM escape

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** VM escape is breaking out of a guest VM to reach the hypervisor/host, potentially compromising other VMs—a key virtualization risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Pass the hash reuses stolen password hashes for authentication.
- B: Tailgating is a physical access tactic.
- C: A birthday attack exploits hash collisions.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Drawbacks and Risks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0064 — Containerization / Containers vs VMs  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A key security-relevant difference between containers and virtual machines is that containers:

- **A.** Share the host operating system kernel, whereas each VM runs its own full OS
- **B.** Cannot run application code
- **C.** Each require a separate hypervisor per container
- **D.** Are always more isolated than virtual machines

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Containers share the host OS kernel (lightweight), while VMs each run a full guest OS via a hypervisor; the shared kernel is a notable container risk consideration.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Containers use a container engine and share the kernel, not a hypervisor each.
- B: Containers run application code.
- D: Containers are generally LESS isolated than VMs due to the shared kernel.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Containers vs. Virtual Machines*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0065 — Containerization / Container risks  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized container security risks? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Containers eliminating the need for any access control
- **B.** The impossibility of ever updating a container image
- **C.** Weak isolation/authentication between inter-container communications
- **D.** Vulnerabilities inherited from the host OS or container engine

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** Containers can inherit host/engine vulnerabilities and face risks from inter-container communication and identification/authentication weaknesses.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Container images can and should be rebuilt/updated; the risk is unpatched images, not inability to update.
- A: Containers still require access control.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Containerization Risks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0066 — Containerization / Orchestration  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which tool is primarily used for container ORCHESTRATION (managing many containers across hosts)?

- **A.** Degausser
- **B.** TPM
- **C.** CPTED
- **D.** Kubernetes

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Kubernetes is a container orchestration platform for deploying and managing containers at scale (Docker is the container platform).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A degausser destroys data on magnetic media.
- B: A TPM is a cryptographic hardware chip.
- C: CPTED is a physical site-design strategy.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Containerization*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0067 — Cloud Deployment Models / Community cloud  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Several hospitals jointly use a shared cloud environment built for their common compliance and security needs. This is an example of a:

- **A.** Community cloud
- **B.** Private cloud
- **C.** Public cloud
- **D.** Hybrid cloud

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A community cloud is shared by several organizations with common concerns (e.g., compliance), pooling a tailored environment.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A public cloud is open to the general public/many unrelated tenants.
- B: A private cloud is dedicated to a single organization.
- D: A hybrid cloud combines two or more deployment models.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Cloud Deployment Models*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0068 — Cloud Deployment Models / Hybrid cloud  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A hybrid cloud is BEST described as:

- **A.** An on-premises data center with no cloud at all
- **B.** A cloud open to any member of the public
- **C.** A combination of two or more distinct cloud models (e.g., private and public) that remain unique but work together
- **D.** A cloud used by only one organization with no external connectivity

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A hybrid cloud combines two or more deployment models (e.g., private + public) that remain distinct but are bound together.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: That describes a private cloud.
- B: That describes a public cloud.
- A: That describes purely on-premises infrastructure.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Cloud Deployment Models*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0069 — Cloud Service Models / IaaS responsibility  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company uses Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS). Under the shared responsibility model, the company is MOST responsible for:

- **A.** The hypervisor that virtualizes the hardware
- **B.** The physical security of the provider's data center
- **C.** The operating system, applications, and data it runs on the provided virtual infrastructure
- **D.** The provider's underlying network cabling

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** In IaaS, the provider supplies the virtualized infrastructure (and secures the physical/hypervisor layers), while the customer manages the OS, applications, and data.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Physical data center security is the provider's responsibility.
- A: The hypervisor/virtualization layer is managed by the provider.
- D: Provider network cabling is the provider's responsibility.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Service Models in Cloud Computing*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0070 — Cloud Service Models / SaaS responsibility  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization adopts a SaaS email platform. Even though the provider manages the application and infrastructure, the customer ALWAYS retains responsibility for:

- **A.** Patching the provider's application servers
- **B.** Maintaining the provider's physical facilities
- **C.** Its own data and managing user access/identities
- **D.** Configuring the provider's hypervisors

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Across all cloud service models, the customer remains responsible for its own data and for managing user access/identities; SaaS shifts most other responsibilities to the provider.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Patching the SaaS application servers is the provider's responsibility.
- B: Physical facilities are the provider's responsibility.
- D: Hypervisor configuration is the provider's responsibility.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Shared Responsibility Model*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0071 — Cloud Service Models / Service model mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each cloud service model (1-4) to its description (A-D).

1. IaaS
2. PaaS
3. SaaS
4. SECaaS

- **A.** Provider delivers security services directly from the cloud
- **B.** Provider delivers virtualized infrastructure; customer manages OS, apps, and data
- **C.** Provider delivers a development/runtime platform; customer manages apps and data
- **D.** Provider delivers complete applications; customer manages mainly data and access

**Correct answer:** 1-B,2-C,3-D,4-A

**Why correct:** IaaS → virtualized infrastructure (B); PaaS → platform/runtime (A); SaaS → complete applications (D); SECaaS → cloud-delivered security services (C).

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Service Models in Cloud Computing*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0072 — Cloud Service Models / SECaaS  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Security as a Service (SECaaS) refers to:

- **A.** A bare-metal hypervisor for production VMs
- **B.** A magnetic data-destruction method
- **C.** A physical mantrap at a building entrance
- **D.** Providing security services (e.g., anti-malware, IAM, monitoring) directly from the cloud

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SECaaS delivers security capabilities directly from the cloud as a service.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: That describes a Type 1 hypervisor.
- B: That describes degaussing.
- C: That describes a physical access control.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Service Models in Cloud Computing*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0073 — Cloud Service Models / CASB in cloud  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In a cloud architecture, a Cloud Access Security Broker (CASB) primarily acts as:

- **A.** A proxy/policy enforcement point between users and cloud services
- **B.** A bare-metal hypervisor
- **C.** A fire suppression system
- **D.** A backup generator for the data center

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A CASB functions as a proxy between users and cloud services, enforcing security policies such as DLP and threat protection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A hypervisor virtualizes hardware; it is not a CASB.
- D: A generator provides backup power.
- C: Fire suppression is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Cloud Access Security Broker (CASB)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0074 — Cloud Networking / Serverless risk  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A team is moving many small functions to a serverless (FaaS) platform. Which security concern is MOST characteristic of serverless architectures?

- **A.** Guaranteed immunity to misconfiguration
- **B.** The need to physically rack and patch their own servers
- **C.** Complete elimination of all application vulnerabilities
- **D.** An expanded attack surface with reduced control over the underlying environment

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Serverless expands the attack surface (many small functions/triggers) and reduces customer control over the environment, complicating traditional security approaches.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Serverless removes server management; there is no physical racking for the customer.
- C: Application code can still contain vulnerabilities.
- A: Misconfiguration remains a major serverless risk.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Risks and Vulnerabilities in Serverless Computing*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0075 — Cloud Networking / Serverless mitigation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which is a recommended mitigation for serverless security risk?

- **A.** Disable all monitoring and logging
- **B.** Grant each function unlimited permissions for convenience
- **C.** Store all secrets in plaintext within the function code
- **D.** Minimize code/microservices and limit the use of sensitive data in functions

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Mitigations include clarifying responsibility, minimizing code/microservices, limiting sensitive data use, and designing architecture for CIA.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Unlimited permissions violate least privilege and expand impact.
- A: Disabling monitoring removes detection capability.
- C: Plaintext secrets in code is a severe vulnerability.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Mitigations for Serverless Computing Security*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0076 — Cryptographic Solutions / Symmetric vs asymmetric  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A primary WEAKNESS of symmetric encryption compared to asymmetric encryption is:

- **A.** Secure key distribution and scalability of keys among many parties
- **B.** It requires no keys whatsoever
- **C.** It cannot encrypt data at all
- **D.** It is far too slow for bulk data encryption

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Symmetric encryption is fast but struggles with secure key distribution and scalability (the number of keys grows rapidly as parties increase).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Symmetric encryption is fast and well suited to bulk data—asymmetric is the slower one.
- C: Symmetric encryption does encrypt data.
- B: It requires a shared secret key.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Symmetric Encryption*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0077 — Cryptographic Solutions / Confidentiality with asymmetric  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Alice wants to send a confidential message to Bob using asymmetric encryption so that only Bob can read it. Alice should encrypt the message with:

- **A.** Alice's private key
- **B.** A shared symmetric key sent in the clear
- **C.** Bob's public key (so only Bob's private key can decrypt it)
- **D.** Bob's private key

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** For confidentiality, the sender encrypts with the recipient's public key; only the recipient's matching private key can decrypt it.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Only Bob holds his private key; Alice cannot encrypt with it, and it is not used for inbound confidentiality.
- A: Encrypting with Alice's private key provides a signature, not confidentiality (anyone can decrypt with her public key).
- B: Sending a symmetric key in the clear exposes it to interception.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Asymmetric Encryption*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0078 — Cryptographic Solutions / Digital signature  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

To create a digital signature that provides integrity, authentication, and non-repudiation, the sender signs a hash of the message with:

- **A.** The recipient's public key
- **B.** The sender's public key
- **C.** The sender's private key
- **D.** A shared symmetric session key

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A digital signature is created by hashing the message and encrypting the hash with the sender's private key; anyone can verify with the sender's public key, providing integrity, authentication, and non-repudiation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The recipient's public key is used for confidentiality, not signing.
- D: A symmetric key cannot provide non-repudiation (both parties share it).
- B: The sender's public key is used to verify, not create, the signature.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Digital Signatures*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0079 — Cryptographic Solutions / Hashing  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A cryptographic hash function is primarily used to provide:

- **A.** Physical access control
- **B.** Confidentiality of stored files
- **C.** High-speed bulk encryption
- **D.** Integrity (detecting unauthorized changes to data)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A hash function produces a fixed-length one-way digest used to verify integrity—any change to the input changes the hash.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Hashing is one-way and does not provide confidentiality (it is not reversible encryption).
- C: Hashing is not bulk encryption.
- A: Physical access control is unrelated to hashing.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Hashing*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0080 — Cryptographic Solutions / HMAC  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A Hashed Message Authentication Code (HMAC) combines a hash with a secret key to provide:

- **A.** Physical destruction of media
- **B.** Non-repudiation through a public/private key pair
- **C.** Confidentiality of the entire message body
- **D.** Integrity and authenticity of a message

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** HMAC uses a shared secret key with a hash to provide message integrity and authenticity; because the key is shared, it does not provide non-repudiation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Non-repudiation requires asymmetric keys (a digital signature), not a shared HMAC key.
- C: HMAC authenticates/verifies integrity but does not encrypt the message for confidentiality.
- A: Media destruction is unrelated to HMAC.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Hashed Message Authentication Code (HMAC)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0081 — Key Management / Key escrow  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Storing a copy of cryptographic keys with a trusted third party so they can be recovered if needed is called:

- **A.** Key rotation
- **B.** Split knowledge
- **C.** Key escrow
- **D.** Key revocation

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Key escrow places a copy of keys with a trusted third party for recovery (e.g., for lawful access or business continuity).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Key rotation periodically replaces keys with new ones.
- B: Split knowledge divides key components among multiple people.
- D: Key revocation invalidates a key that should no longer be used.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Cryptographic Key Management*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0082 — Key Management / Split knowledge  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A bank requires that no single administrator can reconstruct the master key alone; two of three custodians must combine their key parts. This control is:

- **A.** Open design
- **B.** Split knowledge (with dual control)
- **C.** Key escrow
- **D.** Key rotation

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Split knowledge divides the key (or its components) among multiple parties so a threshold must cooperate to use/reconstruct it, enforcing dual control.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Key escrow stores a recoverable copy with a third party, not a split-component scheme.
- D: Key rotation replaces keys periodically.
- A: Open design is a secure-design principle, not a key-management control.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Cryptographic Key Management*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0083 — Key Management / Key rotation  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Periodically replacing cryptographic keys with new ones to limit the impact of a compromised key is:

- **A.** Frequency analysis
- **B.** Key escrow
- **C.** Key rotation
- **D.** Split knowledge

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Key rotation periodically replaces keys, reducing the window and data exposed if a key is compromised.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Key escrow stores recoverable copies of keys.
- D: Split knowledge divides key components among parties.
- A: Frequency analysis is a cryptanalytic technique.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Cryptographic Key Management*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0084 — Cryptographic Solutions / PKI  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In Public Key Infrastructure (PKI), which entity issues and digitally signs digital certificates that bind a public key to an identity?

- **A.** Certificate Authority (CA)
- **B.** Hardware Security Module owner
- **C.** Data steward
- **D.** Policy Enforcement Point (PEP)

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A Certificate Authority (CA) issues and signs digital certificates binding a public key to a verified identity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A PEP is a Zero Trust enforcement component.
- B: An HSM stores/protects keys but is not the certificate-issuing authority.
- C: A data steward is a data SME role, not a PKI authority.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Public Key Infrastructure (PKI)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0085 — Cryptographic Solutions / Cryptographic life cycle  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

Order the following stages of the cryptographic key life cycle from first to last. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Key generation
- **B.** Key use (rotation)
- **C.** Key revocation/destruction
- **D.** Key distribution/storage

**Correct answer:** A,D,B,C

**Why correct:** Keys are generated, then distributed/stored securely, used (and rotated) during their validity, and finally revoked/destroyed at end of life.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Cryptographic Life Cycle Steps*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0086 — Cryptographic Solutions / PKI certificate process  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Order the steps by which a subject obtains and uses a PKI certificate. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Relying parties validate the certificate (e.g., via CRL/OCSP) before trusting it
- **B.** Registration Authority verifies the subject's identity
- **C.** Certificate Authority issues and signs the digital certificate
- **D.** Subject generates a key pair and submits a certificate signing request

**Correct answer:** D,B,C,A

**Why correct:** The subject generates keys and submits a CSR, the RA verifies identity, the CA issues/signs the certificate, and relying parties validate it (checking revocation) before trusting it.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > PKI Certificate Creation Process*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0087 — Cryptographic Solutions / Quantum impact  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The PRIMARY concern about large-scale quantum computers for current cryptography is that they could:

- **A.** Eliminate the need for any encryption keys
- **B.** Physically degauss storage media remotely
- **C.** Make all hashing instantly reversible with no effort
- **D.** Break widely used asymmetric algorithms (e.g., RSA) by efficiently solving their underlying math problems

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Quantum computers threaten current asymmetric algorithms (such as RSA/ECC) by efficiently solving the hard math problems they rely on, motivating post-quantum cryptography.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Quantum computing weakens some symmetric/hash strength but does not make hashing trivially reversible.
- A: Encryption still requires keys; quantum changes which algorithms are safe.
- B: Quantum computing does not remotely degauss media.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Quantum Cryptography*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0088 — Cryptographic Solutions / Symmetric algorithms  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are SYMMETRIC encryption algorithms/standards? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** 3DES
- **B.** AES
- **C.** RSA
- **D.** ECC

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** AES and 3DES are symmetric ciphers that use the same key to encrypt and decrypt.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: RSA is an asymmetric algorithm.
- D: ECC (Elliptic Curve Cryptography) is asymmetric.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Symmetric Encryption*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0089 — Key Management / Key management risks  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are genuine key-management risks that weaken a cryptosystem? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Rotating keys on a defined schedule
- **B.** Insecure key storage or usage
- **C.** Weak key strength (e.g., short keys)
- **D.** Revoking keys that are known to be compromised

**Correct answer:** B, C

**Why correct:** Weak/short keys and insecure key storage/usage are key-management risks; rotation and revocation of compromised keys are good practices, not risks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Scheduled key rotation is a protective practice, not a risk.
- D: Revoking compromised keys is a protective practice, not a risk.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Key Management Risks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0090 — Cryptographic Solutions / Non-repudiation limitation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Why does symmetric encryption alone NOT provide non-repudiation?

- **A.** Symmetric keys are too long to manage
- **B.** Symmetric encryption cannot protect confidentiality
- **C.** Symmetric algorithms are always broken by quantum computers
- **D.** Both parties share the same secret key, so neither can prove the other was the sole originator

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Because the same secret key is shared, either party could have produced a message, so symmetric encryption cannot prove sole origin (no non-repudiation); digital signatures (asymmetric) are needed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Key length is not the reason non-repudiation fails.
- B: Symmetric encryption does protect confidentiality.
- C: Quantum impact is separate from the non-repudiation limitation.

*Source: Domain 3 > Virtualization and Cloud Computing > Symmetric Encryption*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0091 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Brute force  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A brute-force attack against a cipher works by:

- **A.** Tricking a user into revealing their password
- **B.** Analyzing the power consumption of the device
- **C.** Trying all possible key combinations until the correct one is found
- **D.** Reusing a captured authentication token

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Brute force tries every possible key; given enough time and resources it is guaranteed to succeed, which is why sufficient key length matters.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Analyzing power consumption is a side-channel attack.
- D: Reusing a captured token is a replay attack.
- A: Tricking a user is social engineering.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Summary of Key Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0092 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Known plaintext  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In a known-plaintext attack, the attacker attempts to recover the key by analyzing:

- **A.** Known plaintext together with its corresponding ciphertext
- **B.** The device's electromagnetic emissions
- **C.** A precomputed table of password hashes
- **D.** Only ciphertext, with no plaintext available

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A known-plaintext attack uses pairs of known plaintext and its resulting ciphertext to deduce the key.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Having only ciphertext describes a ciphertext-only attack.
- B: Electromagnetic emissions are exploited by side-channel attacks.
- C: Precomputed hash tables describe a rainbow table attack.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Cryptanalytic Attacks - Part 1*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0093 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Chosen plaintext  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A chosen-plaintext attack is characterized by the attacker's ability to:

- **A.** Select specific plaintext, have it encrypted, and analyze the resulting ciphertext
- **B.** Physically destroy the encryption hardware
- **C.** Reuse intercepted session tokens
- **D.** Only observe ciphertext that happens to be transmitted

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** In a chosen-plaintext attack, the attacker chooses plaintext, obtains its ciphertext, and analyzes the pair to narrow down the key.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Passive observation of ciphertext is a ciphertext-only attack.
- B: Destroying hardware is not a cryptanalytic chosen-plaintext attack.
- C: Reusing tokens is a replay attack.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Chosen Plaintext Attack*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0094 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Ciphertext-only  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which attack attempts to discover the key by analyzing ONLY ciphertext, with no access to matching plaintext?

- **A.** Chosen-plaintext attack
- **B.** Known-plaintext attack
- **C.** Replay attack
- **D.** Ciphertext-only attack

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A ciphertext-only attack works from ciphertext alone, often aided by techniques like frequency analysis.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Known-plaintext requires matching plaintext-ciphertext pairs.
- A: Chosen-plaintext requires the attacker to encrypt chosen plaintext.
- C: A replay attack reuses captured messages, not pure ciphertext analysis.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Cryptanalytic Attacks - Part 2*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0095 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Differential cryptanalysis  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Differential cryptanalysis attempts to recover key information by:

- **A.** Trying every possible key exhaustively
- **B.** Analyzing how small differences in plaintext (or key) affect the resulting ciphertext
- **C.** Capturing and replaying a valid message
- **D.** Measuring the time each operation takes

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Differential cryptanalysis studies how slight changes (differences) in plaintext or key propagate to ciphertext to infer key information.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Exhaustive key search is brute force.
- D: Measuring operation time is a timing/side-channel attack.
- C: Capturing and replaying is a replay attack.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Chosen Plaintext Attack*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0096 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Meet in the middle  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A meet-in-the-middle attack is particularly effective against ciphers that:

- **A.** Use only a single short key with no rounds
- **B.** Are physically air-gapped
- **C.** Have no key at all
- **D.** Use multiple rounds/stages of encryption (e.g., double encryption)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Meet-in-the-middle encrypts from one end and decrypts from the other to find matching intermediate values, making it effective against multi-stage (e.g., double) encryption.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Single-stage short-key ciphers are attacked by brute force, not meet-in-the-middle.
- B: Air-gapping is a physical control unrelated to this cryptanalytic method.
- C: Encryption inherently involves a key.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Meet in the Middle Attack*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0097 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Birthday attack  _(medium, scenario_based)_

An attacker finds two different inputs that produce the SAME hash value to bypass an integrity check on a weak hashing algorithm. This exploits which attack?

- **A.** Brute force of a symmetric key
- **B.** Replay attack
- **C.** Birthday attack (hash collision)
- **D.** Side-channel attack

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A birthday attack exploits the birthday paradox to find hash collisions (two inputs with the same hash), undermining integrity controls on weaker hash algorithms.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A replay attack reuses captured messages.
- D: A side-channel attack measures physical characteristics.
- A: Brute-forcing a symmetric key is not about hash collisions.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Hash-Based Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0098 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Rainbow table  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A rainbow table attack recovers passwords by:

- **A.** Encrypting chosen plaintext and analyzing the output
- **B.** Reusing an intercepted session cookie
- **C.** Matching a captured password hash against precomputed hash-to-plaintext tables
- **D.** Measuring CPU timing during decryption

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A rainbow table uses precomputed tables of hashes for common passwords; a captured hash matching an entry reveals the plaintext password (salting defends against this).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: That describes a chosen-plaintext attack.
- D: That describes a timing/side-channel attack.
- B: That describes session replay.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Hash-Based Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0099 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Pass the hash  _(medium, scenario_based)_

An attacker steals NTLM password hashes from a Windows host and uses them directly to authenticate to other systems without ever cracking the plaintext password. This is:

- **A.** Pass the hash
- **B.** A timing attack
- **C.** A birthday attack
- **D.** Kerberoasting

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Pass the hash uses stolen password hashes directly for authentication, commonly targeting Windows LM/NTLM, without needing the plaintext password.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Kerberoasting targets Kerberos tickets, not NTLM hash reuse.
- C: A birthday attack finds hash collisions, not credential reuse.
- B: A timing attack measures operation duration.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Hash-Based Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0100 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Kerberoasting  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Kerberoasting specifically targets:

- **A.** TLS certificate revocation lists
- **B.** The power consumption of a smart card
- **C.** Physical badge readers at a facility
- **D.** Kerberos authentication by harvesting and cracking service ticket hashes

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Kerberoasting targets Kerberos by requesting and harvesting service-ticket hashes and attempting to crack them offline to obtain credentials.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Power consumption is a side-channel target.
- A: CRLs relate to PKI certificate validation, not Kerberoasting.
- C: Badge readers are physical controls.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Hash-Based Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0101 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Replay attack  _(medium, scenario_based)_

An attacker intercepts an encrypted authentication message and resends it later to gain access. Which countermeasure BEST defends against this replay attack?

- **A.** Increasing the CPU clock speed
- **B.** Longer office fences
- **C.** A wet-pipe fire suppression system
- **D.** Timestamps, expiration periods, or nonces/timeouts on messages

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Replay attacks reuse captured valid messages; timestamps, expiration windows, and nonces/timeouts ensure a captured message cannot be reused later.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Fences are physical perimeter controls, irrelevant to message replay.
- C: Fire suppression is a facility control.
- A: CPU speed has no effect on replay defense.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Replay Attack*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0102 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Side-channel / timing  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An attacker measures the exact time and power a smart card uses during cryptographic operations to infer the secret key, without attacking the algorithm mathematically. This is a:

- **A.** Side-channel attack (including timing analysis)
- **B.** Man-in-the-middle attack
- **C.** Chosen-ciphertext attack
- **D.** Brute-force attack

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Side-channel attacks exploit physical operational characteristics (timing, power consumption, emissions) rather than the math of the algorithm; timing analysis is one variant.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A chosen-ciphertext attack analyzes selected ciphertext and its decryption, not physical characteristics.
- D: Brute force tries all keys mathematically.
- B: A man-in-the-middle intercepts communications between parties.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Side Channel Attack*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0103 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Work factor  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In cryptanalysis, "work factor" refers to:

- **A.** The humidity level in the server room
- **B.** The time, effort, and resources required to break a cryptosystem
- **C.** The physical weight of the cryptographic hardware
- **D.** The number of employees who manage the system

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Work factor measures the effort/time/resources needed to defeat a cryptosystem, helping assess whether an attack is feasible.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Staff count is unrelated to work factor.
- C: Physical weight is irrelevant.
- A: Humidity is an environmental control concern.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Cryptanalytic Attacks - Part 3*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0104 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Attack-to-description mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each cryptanalytic attack (1-4) to its description (A-D).

1. Brute force
2. Birthday attack
3. Side-channel attack
4. Replay attack

- **A.** Reuses a captured valid message to gain access
- **B.** Exploits physical characteristics such as timing or power use
- **C.** Tries all possible keys until one works
- **D.** Finds two inputs producing the same hash (collision)

**Correct answer:** 1-C,2-D,3-B,4-A

**Why correct:** Brute force → tries all keys (B); Birthday → hash collision (A); Side-channel → physical characteristics (D); Replay → reuses captured message (C).

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Summary of Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0105 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Hash-based attacks  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO attacks specifically target password HASHES? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** CPTED bypass
- **B.** Pass the hash
- **C.** Brownout exploitation
- **D.** Rainbow table attack

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** Rainbow table attacks and pass-the-hash both target password hashes (matching precomputed hashes, and reusing stolen hashes respectively).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: CPTED is a physical site-design strategy, not a hash attack.
- C: A brownout is a power anomaly, not a hash attack.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Hash-Based Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0106 — Cryptanalytic Attacks / Ciphertext attacks  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO techniques are associated with analyzing ciphertext to break encryption? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Mantrap installation
- **B.** Generator maintenance
- **C.** Related-key attack
- **D.** Frequency analysis

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** Frequency analysis (identifying repeating patterns) and related-key attacks (comparing ciphertexts under related keys) are ciphertext-focused cryptanalytic techniques.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A mantrap is a physical access control.
- B: Generator maintenance is a power-availability practice.

*Source: Domain 3 > Cryptanalytic Attacks > Cryptanalytic Attacks - Part 2*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0107 — Physical Security / First line of defense  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Physical security is often described as the ________ in protecting sensitive data, systems, and personnel.

- **A.** First line of defense
- **B.** Least important control
- **C.** Last resort only
- **D.** Same thing as encryption

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Physical security is the first line of defense, protecting facilities, systems, and people before logical controls are even reached.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: It is a primary, front-line defense, not merely a last resort.
- B: It is foundational, not least important.
- D: Encryption is a logical control; physical security is distinct.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Physical Security*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0108 — Physical Security / CPTED  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design (CPTED) focuses on:

- **A.** Designing the physical environment to influence behavior and deter crime
- **B.** Generating cryptographic key pairs
- **C.** Defining cloud shared-responsibility boundaries
- **D.** Encrypting data at rest on servers

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** CPTED uses environmental design (natural access control, surveillance, and territorial reinforcement) to influence behavior and deter crime.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Encryption is a logical control, not CPTED.
- B: Key generation is a cryptographic function.
- C: Shared responsibility is a cloud concept.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design (CPTED)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0109 — Physical Security / CPTED elements  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are key elements of CPTED? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Pre-action sprinkler systems
- **B.** Natural access control
- **C.** Rainbow tables
- **D.** Natural surveillance

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** CPTED elements are natural access control, natural surveillance, and natural territorial reinforcement.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Pre-action sprinklers are a fire suppression system, not a CPTED element.
- C: Rainbow tables are a cryptanalytic attack technique.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design (CPTED)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0110 — Physical Security / Natural surveillance  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Designing a parking lot with clear sightlines and good lighting so that activity is easily observed is an example of which CPTED element?

- **A.** Mantrap deployment
- **B.** Natural access control
- **C.** Natural territorial reinforcement
- **D.** Natural surveillance

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Natural surveillance designs spaces (lighting, sightlines) so legitimate users can readily observe activity, deterring crime.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Natural access control guides movement via design (paths, entrances).
- C: Natural territorial reinforcement uses design to express ownership of space.
- A: A mantrap is a specific physical access control device, not a CPTED element.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design (CPTED)*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0111 — Physical Security / Fencing heights  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

To deter a DETERMINED intruder, the guide recommends fencing of approximately:

- **A.** 3-4 feet
- **B.** 8+ feet topped with barbed wire
- **C.** 1 foot decorative edging
- **D.** No fence at all

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Fencing of 8+ feet with barbed wire is intended to deter determined intruders; 3-4 feet only deters casual trespassers.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: 3-4 feet deters only casual intruders.
- C: 1 foot edging provides no meaningful deterrence.
- D: No fence provides no perimeter deterrence.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Types of Physical Security Controls*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0112 — Physical Security / Mantrap  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A data center wants to prevent an unauthorized person from slipping in behind an authorized employee (tailgating). Which control DIRECTLY addresses this?

- **A.** A mantrap (double-door interlock allowing one person at a time)
- **B.** A taller perimeter fence
- **C.** A backup generator
- **D.** A wet-pipe sprinkler

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A mantrap uses interlocking doors that admit one person at a time, directly preventing tailgating/piggybacking into secure areas.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A fence addresses perimeter intrusion, not tailgating at a door.
- D: A sprinkler is fire suppression.
- C: A generator provides backup power.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Types of Physical Security Controls*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0113 — Physical Security / Bollards  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Sturdy posts installed in front of a building entrance to stop a vehicle from ramming into it are called:

- **A.** Turnstiles
- **B.** Bollards
- **C.** PTZ cameras
- **D.** Mantraps

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Bollards are posts/barriers that prevent vehicles from ramming into a structure or pedestrian area.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Turnstiles control pedestrian flow, not vehicle ramming.
- D: A mantrap prevents tailgating at a door.
- C: PTZ cameras are surveillance devices.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Detection*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0114 — Physical Security / Facility control categories  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are categories of facility security controls described in the guide's layered (defense-in-depth) approach? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Deterrence
- **B.** Kerberoasting
- **C.** Brute force
- **D.** Delay

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Facility controls follow a layered model of deterrence, denial, detection, delay, and response; deterrence and delay are two of these categories.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Kerberoasting is a cryptanalytic credential attack.
- C: Brute force is a cryptanalytic attack.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Types of Facility Security Controls*

---
