### CISSP-D03-Q0115 — Physical Security / Detection sensors  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A Passive Infrared (PIR) sensor detects intruders by sensing:

- **A.** Changes in infrared (heat) energy from a moving body
- **B.** Hash collisions
- **C.** Power voltage fluctuations
- **D.** Radio frequency interference from lights

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A PIR sensor detects the infrared (heat) energy changes caused by a moving person, a common motion-detection method.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: RFI relates to electrical noise, not PIR motion detection.
- C: Voltage fluctuations are power anomalies, not PIR triggers.
- B: Hash collisions are a cryptographic concept.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Detection*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0116 — Physical Security / Data center tiers  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Among the four data center tiers, which provides the HIGHEST uptime and redundancy?

- **A.** Tier 1
- **B.** Tier 4
- **C.** Tier 2
- **D.** Tier 0

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Data center Tier 4 provides the highest level of uptime and redundancy; Tier 1 is the lowest.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Tier 1 has the lowest uptime/redundancy.
- C: Tier 2 is below Tier 3 and Tier 4.
- D: "Tier 0" is not a data center tier.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Facility Power Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0117 — Physical Security / Environmental humidity  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Server room humidity is generally kept around 50% because excessively LOW humidity can cause:

- **A.** Static electricity (electrostatic discharge)
- **B.** Hash collisions
- **C.** Power surges
- **D.** Metal corrosion

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Low humidity increases static electricity (ESD risk), while high humidity causes condensation and metal corrosion—so ~50% is targeted.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Corrosion results from HIGH humidity, not low.
- C: Power surges are an electrical anomaly, not a humidity effect.
- B: Hash collisions are a cryptographic concept.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > HVAC Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0118 — Physical Security / Power anomalies  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An EXTENDED period of low voltage (not a complete outage) is called a:

- **A.** Blackout
- **B.** Spike
- **C.** Sag
- **D.** Brownout

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A brownout is an extended period of low voltage; a sag is a temporary (brief) low voltage.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A sag is a temporary (short) low voltage, not extended.
- A: A blackout is a complete power loss.
- B: A spike is a temporary high voltage.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Power Issues*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0119 — Physical Security / Power anomalies  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A momentary (very brief) increase in voltage is a spike, whereas an EXTENDED period of high voltage is a:

- **A.** Sag
- **B.** Surge
- **C.** Brownout
- **D.** Fault

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A surge is an extended period of high voltage; a spike is a temporary (momentary) high voltage.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A sag is a temporary low voltage.
- C: A brownout is an extended low voltage.
- D: A fault is a temporary power loss.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Power Issues*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0120 — Physical Security / Power anomalies  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO terms describe a COMPLETE or temporary LOSS of power (rather than a voltage level change)? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Fault (temporary loss)
- **B.** Surge (extended high voltage)
- **C.** Blackout (complete loss)
- **D.** Brownout (extended low voltage)

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** A blackout is a complete power loss and a fault is a temporary power loss; surge and brownout are voltage-level anomalies, not losses.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A surge is extended HIGH voltage, not a loss.
- D: A brownout is extended LOW voltage, not a complete loss.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Power Issues*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0121 — Physical Security / Fire triangle  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The fire triangle consists of heat, fuel, and:

- **A.** Voltage
- **B.** Water
- **C.** Humidity
- **D.** Oxygen

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The fire triangle is oxygen, heat, and fuel; removing any one component disrupts the fire.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Water is a suppression agent, not a fire-triangle component.
- C: Humidity is an environmental factor, not a triangle component.
- A: Voltage is a power concept, not part of the fire triangle.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Fire Triangle Components*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0122 — Physical Security / Fire class C  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A fire breaks out in energized electrical equipment (Class C). Which suppression agent is appropriate, and which must be AVOIDED?

- **A.** Use carbon dioxide or a halon substitute; avoid water
- **B.** Use soda acid; avoid halon substitutes
- **C.** Use water; avoid carbon dioxide
- **D.** Use dry powder only; avoid all gases

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Class C (electrical) fires require non-conductive agents like carbon dioxide or a halon substitute; water is conductive and dangerous on energized equipment.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Water is conductive and hazardous on electrical fires—this is reversed.
- D: Dry powder is for Class D (metal) fires, not the recommended Class C agent.
- B: Soda acid (water-based) is for Class A, not energized electrical fires.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Fire Extinguisher Classes and Materials*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0123 — Physical Security / Fire class D  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which extinguishing agent is appropriate for a Class D (combustible metals such as magnesium or lithium) fire?

- **A.** Water
- **B.** Carbon dioxide
- **C.** Dry powder
- **D.** Soda acid

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Class D combustible-metal fires require dry powder agents; water and common agents can react dangerously with burning metals.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Water can react violently with burning metals.
- B: CO2 is for Class B/C, not Class D metals.
- D: Soda acid is a water-based Class A agent.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Fire Extinguisher Classes and Materials*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0124 — Physical Security / Pre-action suppression  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A data center wants a sprinkler system that minimizes the risk of accidental water discharge onto equipment by requiring two separate triggers before water is released. Which system fits BEST?

- **A.** Deluge system
- **B.** Wet pipe system
- **C.** Pre-action system
- **D.** Open-head system

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A pre-action system combines dry and wet pipe behavior and requires two activation conditions (e.g., heat/smoke detection plus sprinkler activation), reducing accidental discharge—suited to computer rooms/data centers.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A deluge system releases large volumes quickly and is not recommended for electronics.
- B: A wet-pipe system is always water-filled, raising accidental-discharge risk.
- D: An open-head (wet) system does not provide the dual-trigger protection.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Facility-Level Fire Suppression Systems*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0125 — Physical Security / Wet vs dry pipe  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A dry-pipe fire suppression system differs from a wet-pipe system in that the dry-pipe system:

- **A.** Is always filled with water ready to discharge immediately
- **B.** Requires no water source at all
- **C.** Uses only carbon dioxide gas
- **D.** Holds compressed air in the pipes and fills with water only upon activation

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A dry-pipe system keeps compressed air in the pipes and admits water only when activated, reducing the risk of leaks/freezing compared to an always-charged wet-pipe system.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Being always water-filled describes a wet-pipe (closed-head) system.
- C: Dry-pipe systems still use water, not only CO2.
- B: A water source is still required; it just fills on activation.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Facility-Level Fire Suppression Systems*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0126 — Physical Security / Fire detection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A photoelectric smoke detector triggers based on:

- **A.** Disruption of an electrical plate by ionized particles from radioactive material
- **B.** Disruption of a light source/beam by smoke particles
- **C.** A fixed temperature threshold being reached
- **D.** A rapid rate of temperature increase

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A photoelectric smoke detector triggers when smoke disrupts a light source/beam; ionization detectors instead use radioactive material affecting an electrical plate.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: That describes an ionization smoke detector.
- D: That describes a rate-of-rise heat detector.
- C: That describes a fixed-temperature detector.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Fire Detection Methods*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0127 — Physical Security / Fire safety priority  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In fire safety planning, the TOP priority is always to:

- **A.** Avoid triggering any alarms to prevent panic
- **B.** Protect system assets and equipment above all else
- **C.** Preserve the building's aesthetic appearance
- **D.** Protect human life by enabling safe exit from the facility

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Protecting human life (safe egress) is the top fire-safety priority; protecting assets is secondary.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Asset protection is secondary to life safety.
- C: Aesthetics are irrelevant to fire safety priorities.
- A: Alarms are essential for safe evacuation, not to be suppressed.

*Source: Domain 3 > Physical Security > Fire Prevention, Detection, and Suppression*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0128 — Information System Lifecycle / Security integration  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Managing the information system lifecycle securely means that security should be:

- **A.** Added only when an auditor requests it
- **B.** Limited to the disposal phase
- **C.** Integrated from initiation through disposal, at every phase
- **D.** Considered only after the system goes live

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Secure lifecycle management integrates security at every phase—from initiation/requirements through development, operations, and secure disposal.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Bolting on security after go-live is reactive and leaves gaps.
- A: Security is proactive, not auditor-triggered.
- B: Security spans the whole lifecycle, not only disposal.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > System Security Capabilities*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0129 — Information System Lifecycle / Secure disposal  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A server holding sensitive data is being retired at the end of the system lifecycle. Which action is MOST important during this disposal phase?

- **A.** Donate the drives immediately without any wiping
- **B.** Leave the data intact in case it is needed later
- **C.** Publish the data publicly to clear the storage
- **D.** Securely sanitize/destroy the data so it cannot be recovered before disposing of the hardware

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Secure disposal requires sanitizing or destroying data so it cannot be recovered, then disposing of hardware appropriately.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Leaving sensitive data intact on retired hardware risks disclosure.
- A: Donating un-wiped drives exposes sensitive data (remanence).
- C: Publishing the data is a severe confidentiality breach.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > System Security Capabilities*

---

### CISSP-D03-Q0130 — Information System Lifecycle / Verification and validation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

During the verification and validation stage of the system lifecycle, the goal is to:

- **A.** Negotiate the hardware procurement contract
- **B.** Securely destroy all of the system's data
- **C.** Identify and engage stakeholders for the first time
- **D.** Confirm through testing/assessment that the system meets its technical, security, and user requirements

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Verification and validation use testing, code reviews, and security assessments to confirm the system meets all requirements before full-scale implementation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Data destruction occurs at disposal/retirement.
- C: Stakeholder engagement is part of initiation.
- A: Procurement negotiation is part of acquisition.

*Source: Domain 3 > Secure Design Principles > Objective 3.5*

---


## Domain 4: Communication and Network Security

### CISSP-D04-Q0001 — Network Components / Switch  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A network switch primarily forwards frames to the correct port based on:

- **A.** The cable color
- **B.** The destination IP subnet only
- **C.** The destination MAC address (Layer 2)
- **D.** The application port number only

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A switch operates at Layer 2, directing frames to the appropriate port based on destination MAC address (multilayer switches can also do Layer 3).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Routing by IP subnet is a Layer 3 router function (though multilayer switches can also route).
- D: Port-number filtering is a transport/application-layer firewall function.
- A: Cable color is irrelevant to switching decisions.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0002 — Network Components / Router  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A router operates primarily at which OSI layer to forward packets between networks?

- **A.** Layer 1 (Physical)
- **B.** Layer 7 (Application)
- **C.** Layer 3 (Network)
- **D.** Layer 2 (Data Link)

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A router operates at Layer 3, using a route table/policies to forward packets between different networks based on IP addresses.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Layer 1 devices (repeaters/hubs) deal with raw bits/signals.
- D: Layer 2 devices (switches/bridges) forward by MAC address.
- B: Layer 7 is the application layer, not routing.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0003 — Network Components / Hub vs switch  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Why is a switch generally more secure and efficient than a hub?

- **A.** A switch encrypts all traffic while a hub does not
- **B.** A hub performs deep packet inspection
- **C.** A switch forwards frames only to the destination port, while a hub repeats traffic to all ports
- **D.** A hub operates at Layer 3 while a switch operates at Layer 1

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A switch directs frames only to the intended port (reducing exposure and collisions), whereas a hub repeats incoming signals out all ports.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Neither device encrypts traffic by default.
- D: A hub is a Layer 1 device and a switch is Layer 2 (this is reversed).
- B: Deep packet inspection is an NGFW capability, not a hub function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0004 — Network Components / Gateway  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which device connects network segments that use DIFFERENT protocols, translating between them?

- **A.** Repeater
- **B.** Hub
- **C.** Gateway
- **D.** Bridge

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A gateway connects networks using different protocols, performing translation between them.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A repeater regenerates signals on the same medium (Layer 1).
- B: A hub repeats signals to all ports (Layer 1).
- D: A bridge connects two segments using the SAME protocol (Layer 2).

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0005 — Network Components / Brouter  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A "brouter" is best described as a device that:

- **A.** Only repeats electrical signals at Layer 1
- **B.** Combines bridge and router functions—routing at Layer 3 when possible, otherwise bridging at Layer 2
- **C.** Assigns IP addresses dynamically to hosts
- **D.** Encrypts all traffic at the application layer

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A brouter combines a bridge and a router, routing using Layer 3 when possible and defaulting to Layer 2 bridging otherwise.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Signal repetition at Layer 1 describes a repeater.
- D: Application-layer encryption is unrelated to a brouter.
- C: Dynamic IP assignment is DHCP's function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0006 — Network Components / Repeater  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A repeater is a Layer 1 device whose primary purpose is to:

- **A.** Translate between different protocols
- **B.** Route packets between subnets
- **C.** Regenerate and extend a signal to overcome distance/attenuation
- **D.** Filter traffic by application content

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A repeater regenerates/amplifies signals at the physical layer to extend transmission distance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Routing between subnets is a Layer 3 router function.
- D: Application-content filtering is an application-layer proxy/NGFW function.
- A: Protocol translation is a gateway function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0007 — Network Components / Device-to-layer mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each network device (1-4) to the OSI layer at which it primarily operates (A-D).

1. Hub/Repeater
2. Switch/Bridge
3. Router
4. Application-level proxy

- **A.** Layer 7 (Application)
- **B.** Layer 1 (Physical)
- **C.** Layer 3 (Network)
- **D.** Layer 2 (Data Link)

**Correct answer:** 1-B,2-D,3-C,4-A

**Why correct:** Hub/Repeater → Layer 1 (B); Switch/Bridge → Layer 2 (A); Router → Layer 3 (D); Application-level proxy → Layer 7 (C).

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0008 — Network Components / Proxy  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A proxy that makes requests to the internet on behalf of internal clients primarily helps security by:

- **A.** Encrypting all data at rest
- **B.** Assigning MAC addresses to hosts
- **C.** Increasing the broadcast domain size
- **D.** Hiding internal client details and allowing inspection/filtering of requests

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A proxy acts as an intermediary, concealing internal hosts and enabling content inspection/filtering of outbound/inbound requests.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Proxies do not exist to enlarge broadcast domains.
- A: Data-at-rest encryption is unrelated to a forwarding proxy.
- B: MAC assignment is not a proxy function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0009 — Network Infrastructure / UPS types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which UPS type continuously powers equipment from its battery/inverter, providing the cleanest, most consistent power with no transfer time?

- **A.** Standby (offline) UPS
- **B.** A diesel generator
- **C.** Line-interactive UPS
- **D.** Online (double-conversion) UPS

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An online/double-conversion UPS always powers the load through the battery/inverter, delivering consistent, clean power with effectively no transfer delay.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A standby/offline UPS only switches to battery when power fails, with a brief transfer time.
- C: A line-interactive UPS adds automatic voltage regulation but is not full double-conversion.
- B: A generator provides long-term backup power but is not a UPS and has startup delay.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Network Infrastructure Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0010 — Network Infrastructure / Line-interactive UPS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A line-interactive UPS adds which capability over a basic standby UPS?

- **A.** Automatic Voltage Regulation (AVR) to stabilize power fluctuations
- **B.** Routing packets between subnets
- **C.** Wireless access point functionality
- **D.** Deep packet inspection of traffic

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A line-interactive UPS includes AVR to stabilize voltage fluctuations (sags/surges) without switching to battery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Routing is a router function.
- D: Deep packet inspection is an NGFW feature.
- C: Wireless access is a WAP function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Network Infrastructure Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0011 — Network Infrastructure / Generators  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A data center needs to maintain operations through a multi-hour utility outage. Beyond a UPS, what provides the LONG-TERM backup power, and what does it require?

- **A.** A generator, which requires a steady fuel supply and regular maintenance
- **B.** A proxy server, which requires more bandwidth
- **C.** A larger broadcast domain, which requires more switches
- **D.** A line-interactive UPS alone, which needs no fuel

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A generator provides long-term backup power for extended outages but requires a steady fuel supply and regular maintenance/testing; the UPS bridges the gap until the generator starts.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Broadcast domains are unrelated to power continuity.
- D: A UPS provides only short-duration power and cannot sustain multi-hour outages.
- B: A proxy is a network-traffic intermediary, not a power source.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Network Infrastructure Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0012 — Network Infrastructure / EOL vs EOS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

When a vendor announces End of Service (EOS) for a network appliance, it means:

- **A.** The product gains new premium features
- **B.** The product's warranty is automatically extended indefinitely
- **C.** The vendor will no longer provide support and updates for the product
- **D.** The product instantly stops functioning

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** End of Service/Support means the vendor terminates support and updates; running such gear risks unpatched vulnerabilities and should be planned for replacement.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: The device may still run; the risk is lack of support/patches.
- A: EOS removes support; it does not add features.
- B: EOS typically coincides with the end of warranty/support, not extension.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Network Infrastructure Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0013 — Transmission Media / Fiber optic  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A key SECURITY advantage of fiber-optic cabling over copper twisted pair is that fiber:

- **A.** Requires no physical protection at all
- **B.** Does not emit electromagnetic signals that can be tapped via emanations and resists EMI
- **C.** Is always cheaper and easier to install
- **D.** Automatically encrypts the data it carries

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Fiber transmits light through a glass core, producing no electromagnetic emanations to intercept and being immune to EMI—harder to tap passively than copper.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Fiber is typically more expensive and harder to terminate than copper.
- D: Fiber does not encrypt data; it is just a medium.
- A: Fiber still requires physical protection from damage and intrusion.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Fiber Optic Cabling*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0014 — Transmission Media / Plenum cable  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Cabling must be run through a building's air-handling (plenum) spaces above the ceiling. Which cable property is MOST important for safety here?

- **A.** Plenum-rated cabling that emits less toxic smoke when burning
- **B.** The lowest-cost cable regardless of fire rating
- **C.** A cable with the highest possible bandwidth only
- **D.** The brightest-colored jacket available

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Plenum spaces circulate air; plenum-rated cable uses fire-resistant jacketing that emits less toxic smoke if it burns, a life-safety requirement.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Jacket color does not affect fire safety in plenum spaces.
- B: Using non-plenum cable in plenum spaces violates fire codes and endangers life.
- C: Bandwidth alone does not address the plenum fire-safety requirement.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Fiber Optic Cabling*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0015 — Transmission Media / Twisted pair shielding  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Shielded Twisted Pair (STP) is preferable to Unshielded Twisted Pair (UTP) primarily in environments with:

- **A.** Only wireless devices
- **B.** Exclusively fiber endpoints
- **C.** No power at all
- **D.** High electromagnetic interference (EMI)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** STP adds shielding to reduce the effects of EMI/crosstalk, making it preferable in electrically noisy environments; UTP is cheaper where EMI is low.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Cabling choice is unrelated to the absence of power.
- A: Wireless devices do not use twisted-pair cabling.
- B: Fiber endpoints would use fiber, not twisted pair.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Twisted Pair Cables*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0016 — Transmission Media / Network topology  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which network topology provides the HIGHEST redundancy because every node connects to every other node?

- **A.** Single point-to-point link
- **B.** Bus topology
- **C.** Mesh topology
- **D.** Star topology

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A (full) mesh topology connects every node to every other node, maximizing redundancy and fault tolerance at higher cost.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A bus topology shares a single backbone—a single break can disrupt the segment.
- D: A star topology depends on a central device whose failure affects all nodes.
- A: A single link offers no redundancy.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Transmission Media - Part 1*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0017 — Transmission Media / Star topology  _(hard, scenario_based)_

In a star topology where all hosts connect to a single central switch, what is the PRIMARY availability concern?

- **A.** It cannot support more than two devices
- **B.** Every host can read every other host's traffic by default
- **C.** The central device is a single point of failure for the whole segment
- **D.** Adding a new host requires rewiring all existing hosts

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** In a star topology, all nodes depend on the central device; its failure takes down the entire segment, making it a single point of failure.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A switch (unlike a hub) does not flood all traffic to every host by default.
- D: Adding a host only requires connecting it to the central device, not rewiring others.
- A: A star can support many devices, limited by the central device's capacity.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Transmission Media - Part 1*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0018 — Network Types / LAN/WAN/MAN/PAN  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A network connecting devices across a large geographic area, such as between cities or countries, is a:

- **A.** Wide Area Network (WAN)
- **B.** Personal Area Network (PAN)
- **C.** Metropolitan Area Network (MAN)
- **D.** Local Area Network (LAN)

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A WAN spans large geographic areas (e.g., between cities/countries), often using carrier links.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A LAN covers a small area like a single building or campus.
- B: A PAN covers a very short range around a person (e.g., Bluetooth).
- C: A MAN spans a city/metropolitan area—larger than a LAN but smaller than a WAN.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Network Types*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0019 — Network Types / PAN  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Bluetooth connecting a phone to nearby wireless earbuds is an example of a:

- **A.** Wide Area Network (WAN)
- **B.** Metropolitan Area Network (MAN)
- **C.** Storage Area Network
- **D.** Personal Area Network (PAN)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A PAN is a very short-range network around an individual, commonly using Bluetooth.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A WAN spans large geographic distances.
- B: A MAN spans a metropolitan area.
- C: A SAN is a specialized storage network, not a personal Bluetooth link.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Network Types*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0020 — Communications / Baseband vs broadband  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which statement correctly distinguishes baseband from broadband transmission?

- **A.** Baseband uses the entire medium for a single channel; broadband divides the medium into multiple channels
- **B.** Broadband can carry only one signal at a time
- **C.** Baseband always uses fiber; broadband always uses copper
- **D.** Baseband is wireless and broadband is wired

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Baseband dedicates the whole medium to one signal/channel at a time, while broadband splits the medium into multiple simultaneous channels/frequencies.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Neither is tied to a specific cable type by definition.
- B: Broadband carries multiple signals simultaneously, not just one.
- D: Both can be wired; the distinction is single vs. multiple channels.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Types of Communications*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0021 — Communications / Duplex  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A communication method that allows both parties to send and receive data SIMULTANEOUSLY is:

- **A.** Simplex
- **B.** Full duplex
- **C.** Baseband
- **D.** Half duplex

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Full duplex permits simultaneous two-way communication; half duplex allows two-way but only one direction at a time; simplex is one-way only.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Half duplex allows only one direction at a time.
- A: Simplex is strictly one-way.
- C: Baseband refers to channel usage, not directionality.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Communication Methods*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0022 — Network Monitoring / SNMP  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which protocol is widely used to monitor and manage the status of network devices (e.g., routers, switches)?

- **A.** SMTP
- **B.** FTP
- **C.** SNMP (Simple Network Management Protocol)
- **D.** ARP

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SNMP is used to monitor and manage network devices, collecting status and performance data (use SNMPv3 for security).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: SMTP routes email.
- D: ARP resolves IP addresses to MAC addresses.
- B: FTP transfers files.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Monitoring Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0023 — Network Monitoring / ICMP  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which protocol is used by the ping and traceroute utilities to report communication status, and has no assigned port?

- **A.** HTTP
- **B.** SNMP
- **C.** DHCP
- **D.** ICMP

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ICMP provides communication status and error messages and underlies ping and traceroute; it has no port number.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: SNMP manages devices and uses ports 161/162.
- A: HTTP serves web content on port 80.
- C: DHCP assigns IP addresses on ports 67/68.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Monitoring Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0024 — Network Monitoring / Monitoring process  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

Order the network monitoring process steps as described in the guide. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Action
- **B.** Collection
- **C.** Detection
- **D.** Monitoring

**Correct answer:** B,D,C,A

**Why correct:** The monitoring process flows from collecting data, to monitoring it, to detecting anomalies, to taking action.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Process of Network Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0025 — Network Monitoring / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A primary security-related purpose of network monitoring is to:

- **A.** Observe network performance and detect anomalies that may indicate a security issue
- **B.** Permanently prevent any device from ever failing
- **C.** Replace the need for firewalls entirely
- **D.** Encrypt all data at rest automatically

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Network monitoring focuses on performance and visibility, enabling detection of anomalies that can indicate attacks or failures.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Monitoring detects issues; it cannot prevent all hardware failures.
- D: Monitoring does not perform data-at-rest encryption.
- C: Monitoring complements, not replaces, firewalls.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Purposes of Network Monitoring*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0026 — Network Components / Device selection  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO devices make forwarding decisions using Layer 2 (MAC) information? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Bridge
- **B.** Switch
- **C.** Repeater
- **D.** Router

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** Bridges and switches are Layer 2 devices that forward based on MAC addresses.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A router forwards at Layer 3 using IP addresses.
- C: A repeater operates at Layer 1, regenerating signals without MAC decisions.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0027 — Network Infrastructure / Redundancy  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO measures improve the AVAILABILITY of network infrastructure during power problems? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Using a hub instead of a switch
- **B.** Disabling all monitoring
- **C.** Uninterruptible Power Supplies (UPS)
- **D.** Backup generators

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** UPS units cover short outages/fluctuations and generators sustain long outages—both improve availability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Disabling monitoring reduces visibility and does not aid power availability.
- A: Replacing a switch with a hub harms performance/security and is unrelated to power.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Network Infrastructure Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0028 — Transmission Media / Coaxial cable  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Coaxial cable carries signals over which medium?

- **A.** A copper core surrounded by shielding
- **B.** Fiber strands bundled together
- **C.** Open-air radio waves only
- **D.** A glass core using light

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Coaxial cable uses a central copper conductor surrounded by insulation and metallic shielding to carry electrical signals.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A glass core using light describes fiber optic cable.
- C: Open-air radio waves describe wireless transmission.
- B: Bundled fiber strands describe fiber cabling, not coax.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Coaxial Cable*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0029 — Network Components / WAP  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A Wireless Access Point (WAP) primarily:

- **A.** Routes packets between autonomous systems on the internet
- **B.** Generates backup power for the data center
- **C.** Sanitizes magnetic media
- **D.** Bridges wireless client devices onto a wired network

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A WAP connects wireless clients to a wired LAN, acting as a bridge between the wireless and wired segments.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Inter-AS routing on the internet is a BGP/router function.
- B: Backup power is a generator/UPS function.
- C: Media sanitization is a data-destruction activity.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Components > Hardware Operations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0030 — Communications / CSMA  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Wireless networks use CSMA/CA rather than CSMA/CD primarily because:

- **A.** Wireless media never experience any contention
- **B.** Stations cannot reliably detect collisions on wireless media, so they try to AVOID them
- **C.** Collision detection is illegal on wireless networks
- **D.** CSMA/CA encrypts all wireless frames

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** On shared wireless media, a transmitting station cannot reliably hear collisions (hidden-node problem), so CSMA/CA focuses on avoidance rather than detection (CSMA/CD, used on wired Ethernet).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Wireless media do experience contention; that is why a MAC method is needed.
- C: There is no legal prohibition; it is a technical limitation.
- D: CSMA/CA is a media-access method, not an encryption scheme.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Collision Management*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0031 — OSI Model / Layer functions  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Routing and logical addressing for packet delivery between networks occur at which OSI layer?

- **A.** Layer 3 (Network)
- **B.** Layer 2 (Data Link)
- **C.** Layer 7 (Application)
- **D.** Layer 4 (Transport)

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Layer 3 (Network) manages routing and logical (IP) addressing for packet transfer between networks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Layer 2 handles MAC addressing and frames within a segment.
- D: Layer 4 manages end-to-end logical connections (TCP/UDP).
- C: Layer 7 provides the application interface.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > OSI Model Layers*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0032 — OSI Model / Transport layer  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The OSI Transport layer (Layer 4) is responsible for:

- **A.** Providing the human user interface
- **B.** Controlling logical end-to-end connections (e.g., TCP connection-oriented, UDP connectionless)
- **C.** Resolving IP addresses to MAC addresses
- **D.** Converting frames into bits for the physical medium

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Layer 4 controls end-to-end logical connections—TCP (connection-oriented) and UDP (connectionless).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Bit conversion for the medium is Layer 1 (Physical).
- C: IP-to-MAC resolution (ARP) is a Layer 2/3 function.
- A: The user interface is Layer 7 (Application).

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > OSI Model Layers*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0033 — OSI Model / PDU per layer  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

At the OSI Network layer, the protocol data unit (PDU) is called a:

- **A.** Packet
- **B.** Segment
- **C.** Bit
- **D.** Frame

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The Network layer PDU is the packet; Transport = segment, Data Link = frame, Physical = bits.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A segment is the Transport-layer PDU.
- D: A frame is the Data Link-layer PDU.
- C: Bits are the Physical-layer PDU.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Protocol Data Unit (PDU)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0034 — OSI Model / Layer-to-PDU mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each OSI layer (1-4) to its protocol data unit (A-D).

1. Transport layer
2. Network layer
3. Data Link layer
4. Physical layer

- **A.** Segment
- **B.** Packet
- **C.** Frame
- **D.** Bits

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-B,3-C,4-D

**Why correct:** Transport → Segment (B); Network → Packet (A); Data Link → Frame (D); Physical → Bits (C).

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Protocol Data Unit (PDU)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0035 — OSI Model / Encapsulation order  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

During encapsulation, data is wrapped as it moves DOWN the stack from the sending application to the wire. Order the PDUs from first formed to last. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Frame (Data Link)
- **B.** Packet (Network)
- **C.** Segment (Transport)
- **D.** Data (Application)

**Correct answer:** D,C,B,A

**Why correct:** Encapsulation proceeds Data → Segment → Packet → Frame (then bits on the wire) as each lower layer adds its header.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Encapsulation Process*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0036 — TCP/IP / TCP three-way handshake  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

Order the messages of the TCP three-way handshake. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** SYN-ACK
- **B.** ACK
- **C.** SYN

**Correct answer:** C,A,B

**Why correct:** The TCP handshake is SYN (client initiates), SYN-ACK (server acknowledges and responds), ACK (client confirms), after which data transfer begins.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > TCP Three-Way Handshake*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0037 — TCP/IP / TCP vs UDP  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A live video-conferencing application prioritizes low latency and can tolerate occasional lost frames. Which transport protocol is the BETTER fit, and why?

- **A.** TCP, because guaranteed delivery is essential for real-time video
- **B.** UDP, because it is connectionless and avoids handshake/retransmission overhead
- **C.** ICMP, because it carries the video payload efficiently
- **D.** ARP, because it resolves addresses quickly

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** UDP is connectionless and lightweight (no handshake/retransmission), favoring low-latency real-time media that tolerates minor loss; TCP's reliability adds latency.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: TCP's retransmission/ordering adds latency that harms real-time media.
- D: ARP resolves IP-to-MAC; it does not carry application data.
- C: ICMP carries control/status messages, not video payloads.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 4 - Transport Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0038 — TCP/IP / Connection-oriented  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which transport protocol is connection-oriented and provides reliable, ordered delivery using a handshake?

- **A.** UDP
- **B.** TCP
- **C.** ICMP
- **D.** IP

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** TCP is connection-oriented, establishing a session via the three-way handshake and providing reliable, ordered delivery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: UDP is connectionless and does not guarantee delivery.
- D: IP is a connectionless Layer 3 routing protocol.
- C: ICMP carries status/error messages, not reliable data streams.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 4 - Transport Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0039 — IP Addressing / RFC 1918 private ranges  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which of the following is a private (RFC 1918) IPv4 address range?

- **A.** 224.0.0.0 – 239.255.255.255
- **B.** 8.0.0.0 – 8.255.255.255
- **C.** 1.0.0.0 – 1.255.255.255
- **D.** 10.0.0.0 – 10.255.255.255

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** RFC 1918 private ranges are 10.0.0.0/8, 172.16.0.0–172.31.255.255 (/12), and 192.168.0.0/16; 10.x is private.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: 8.0.0.0/8 is public address space.
- C: 1.0.0.0/8 is public address space.
- A: 224.0.0.0–239.255.255.255 is Class D multicast, not private unicast.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IPv4 Private Address Ranges (RFC 1918)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0040 — IP Addressing / NAT/PAT  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Network Address Translation (NAT) primarily enables:

- **A.** Encryption of all packets leaving the network
- **B.** Private internal IP addresses to be translated to public addresses for internet routing
- **C.** Resolution of domain names to IP addresses
- **D.** Automatic assignment of MAC addresses

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** NAT translates private (RFC 1918) addresses to public addresses (and back) so internal hosts can communicate over the internet; PAT maps many hosts to one public IP via ports.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: NAT does not encrypt traffic.
- D: MAC assignment is done by hardware, not NAT.
- C: Name-to-IP resolution is DNS's job.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IPv4 Private Address Ranges (RFC 1918)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0041 — IP Addressing / PAT  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A small office has 50 internal hosts but only one public IP address from its ISP, and all hosts need internet access. Which technique makes this possible?

- **A.** Port Address Translation (PAT), mapping many private hosts to one public IP using unique ports
- **B.** Disabling NAT entirely
- **C.** Assigning each host a Class A public block
- **D.** Using DNSSEC to share the address

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** PAT (NAT overload) maps many internal hosts to a single public IP by tracking unique source ports per connection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A single ISP-assigned public IP cannot provide a public block per host.
- B: Disabling NAT would leave private hosts unable to route to the internet.
- D: DNSSEC authenticates DNS responses; it does not share an IP among hosts.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IPv4 Private Address Ranges (RFC 1918)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0042 — IP Addressing / CIDR  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The CIDR notation /24 corresponds to which subnet mask?

- **A.** 255.255.255.0
- **B.** 255.255.255.255
- **C.** 255.0.0.0
- **D.** 255.255.0.0

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** /24 means 24 network bits, i.e., the mask 255.255.255.0 (the traditional Class C default).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: 255.0.0.0 is /8.
- D: 255.255.0.0 is /16.
- B: 255.255.255.255 is /32 (a single host).

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > CIDR Notation for Subnet Masks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0043 — IP Addressing / Multicast class  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

IPv4 addresses in the range 224.0.0.0 – 239.255.255.255 (Class D) are reserved for:

- **A.** Loopback testing
- **B.** Private internal addressing
- **C.** Multicasting
- **D.** Experimental use

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Class D (224.0.0.0–239.255.255.255) is reserved for multicast.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Private addressing uses RFC 1918 ranges in Classes A/B/C.
- A: Loopback is 127.0.0.0/8.
- D: Experimental use is Class E (240.0.0.0+).

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IPv4 Classes*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0044 — IP Addressing / IPv6 benefits  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A benefit of IPv6 over IPv4 highlighted in the guide is that IPv6:

- **A.** Uses shorter 32-bit addresses than IPv4
- **B.** Cannot support quality of service
- **C.** Has a vast address space and built-in autoconfiguration that reduces the need for NAT
- **D.** Eliminates the need for any routing

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** IPv6 provides an enormous address space and built-in configuration (reducing reliance on NAT/DHCP), plus features like scoped addresses and QoS.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: IPv6 uses 128-bit addresses, longer than IPv4's 32-bit.
- B: IPv6 supports QoS for traffic prioritization.
- D: IPv6 still relies on routing between networks.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Benefits of IPv6*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0045 — IP Addressing / Private vs public  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO address ranges are RFC 1918 private ranges? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** 200.1.0.0 – 200.1.255.255
- **B.** 192.168.0.0 – 192.168.255.255
- **C.** 11.0.0.0 – 11.255.255.255
- **D.** 172.16.0.0 – 172.31.255.255

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** 172.16.0.0–172.31.255.255 and 192.168.0.0/16 are RFC 1918 private ranges (along with 10.0.0.0/8).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: 11.0.0.0/8 is public address space (only 10.0.0.0/8 is private in Class A).
- A: 200.1.x is public address space.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IPv4 Private Address Ranges (RFC 1918)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0046 — Network Domains / Broadcast vs collision domain  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which statement about broadcast and collision domains is correct?

- **A.** A hub separates both collision and broadcast domains per port
- **B.** Switches and hubs are functionally identical for collision domains
- **C.** A router places all connected networks in one broadcast domain
- **D.** A switch separates collision domains per port, while a router separates broadcast domains

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Each switch port is its own collision domain; routers bound broadcast domains (broadcasts do not cross a router by default).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A hub is a single shared collision domain (no separation per port).
- C: Routers separate broadcast domains rather than merging them.
- B: A hub is one collision domain; a switch gives each port its own—so they differ.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Network Domains*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0047 — Routing / Static vs dynamic routes  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A dynamic route differs from a static route in that a dynamic route is:

- **A.** Manually configured and never changes
- **B.** Only usable on wireless networks
- **C.** Automatically learned and updated by routing protocols exchanging information with neighbors
- **D.** Always more secure than any static route

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Dynamic routes are learned/updated automatically via routing protocols that share information with neighboring routers, adapting to topology changes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Manual, unchanging configuration describes a static route.
- D: Dynamic routing is not inherently more secure; it can introduce protocol risks.
- B: Dynamic routing is used on wired and wireless networks alike.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Types of Routes*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0048 — Routing / Distance vector vs link state  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A link-state routing protocol differs from a distance-vector protocol in that link-state protocols:

- **A.** Cannot operate within an autonomous system
- **B.** Build a map of the overall network state to select the most reliable/fastest routes
- **C.** Choose paths based only on hop count and direction
- **D.** Never converge after a topology change

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Link-state protocols compute routes from a complete view of network topology/state (e.g., OSPF), often yielding faster, more reliable paths than hop-count-based distance-vector protocols.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Choosing by hop count/direction describes distance-vector protocols (e.g., RIP).
- A: Link-state protocols (like OSPF) commonly run as interior gateway protocols within an AS.
- D: Link-state protocols do converge; they are often faster to converge than distance-vector.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Routing Protocol Types*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0049 — Routing / BGP / EGP  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) is used to exchange routing information:

- **A.** Between different autonomous systems (as an exterior gateway protocol)
- **B.** Between USB devices on a host
- **C.** To assign IP addresses to clients
- **D.** Only within a single switch

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** BGP is the exterior gateway protocol used to exchange routing information between autonomous systems (e.g., across the internet).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Switch-internal forwarding is not BGP's role.
- B: USB device communication is unrelated to routing.
- C: IP assignment is DHCP's function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Types of Routing Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0050 — Routing / IGP  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Interior Gateway Protocols (IGPs) such as OSPF are used to exchange routing information:

- **A.** Only between competing internet service providers
- **B.** To translate private IPs to public IPs
- **C.** Within the same autonomous system
- **D.** Exclusively over Bluetooth links

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** IGPs (e.g., OSPF, EIGRP, RIP) exchange routing data within a single autonomous system; EGPs (e.g., BGP) operate between autonomous systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Routing between different systems/ISPs is the role of EGPs like BGP.
- D: IGPs are not limited to Bluetooth and typically run over wired/enterprise links.
- B: IP translation is NAT, not a routing protocol.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Types of Routing Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0051 — Routing / Autonomous system  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

An Autonomous System (AS) is best described as:

- **A.** A single laptop with no network connection
- **B.** A type of fire suppression system
- **C.** A wireless encryption protocol
- **D.** A collection of network devices managed under a single routing policy

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An AS is a set of networks/devices under a common administration and routing policy, identified by an AS number for inter-AS routing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A standalone laptop is not an AS.
- B: Fire suppression is a physical control, unrelated to routing.
- C: Wireless encryption (e.g., WPA2) is not an AS.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Types of Routing Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0052 — IP Addressing / MAC vs IP address  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which statement correctly contrasts a MAC address with an IP address?

- **A.** A MAC address is logical and an IP address is physical
- **B.** Both are assigned by DHCP at boot time
- **C.** Both operate only at Layer 7
- **D.** A MAC address is a physical Layer 2 hardware address; an IP address is a logical Layer 3 address

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A MAC address is a hardware (physical) address used at Layer 2; an IP address is a logical Layer 3 address that can change (e.g., via DHCP).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: This reverses the definitions—MAC is physical, IP is logical.
- B: MAC addresses are burned into hardware, not assigned by DHCP (which assigns IPs).
- C: They operate at Layers 2 and 3, not Layer 7.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IP Networking*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0053 — Routing / Routing protocol types  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are distance-vector routing protocols? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** BGP
- **B.** TLS
- **C.** OSPF
- **D.** RIP

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** RIP and BGP are categorized as distance-vector (BGP is a path-vector variant) in the guide; OSPF is link-state and TLS is not a routing protocol.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: OSPF is a link-state protocol.
- B: TLS is a transport-layer security protocol, not a routing protocol.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Key Routing Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0054 — OSI Model / Data Link layer  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

MAC addressing and frame delivery within a local segment occur at which OSI layer?

- **A.** Layer 4 (Transport)
- **B.** Layer 2 (Data Link)
- **C.** Layer 5 (Session)
- **D.** Layer 3 (Network)

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Layer 2 (Data Link) handles hardware (MAC) addressing and frame delivery within a network segment.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Layer 3 handles logical IP addressing and routing between networks.
- A: Layer 4 handles end-to-end transport (TCP/UDP).
- C: Layer 5 manages sessions.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > OSI Model Layers*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0055 — OSI Model / Session layer  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Establishing, maintaining, and terminating communication sessions between applications is the role of which OSI layer?

- **A.** Layer 5 (Session)
- **B.** Layer 2 (Data Link)
- **C.** Layer 3 (Network)
- **D.** Layer 1 (Physical)

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Layer 5 (Session) establishes, maintains, and terminates sessions between communicating systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Layer 1 transmits raw bits.
- C: Layer 3 routes packets.
- B: Layer 2 delivers frames within a segment.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > OSI Model Layers*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0056 — IP Addressing / IPv4 class A  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A Class A IPv4 network has which default subnet mask?

- **A.** 255.255.255.255 (/32)
- **B.** 255.255.255.0 (/24)
- **C.** 255.255.0.0 (/16)
- **D.** 255.0.0.0 (/8)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Class A uses a default /8 mask (255.0.0.0), providing a large number of hosts per network.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: /16 (255.255.0.0) is the Class B default.
- B: /24 (255.255.255.0) is the Class C default.
- A: /32 denotes a single host, not a class default.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IPv4 Classes*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0057 — TCP/IP / TCP/IP model layers  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The four-layer TCP/IP model consists of the Application, Transport, Internet, and ________ layers.

- **A.** Session
- **B.** Data Center
- **C.** Link (Network Access)
- **D.** Presentation

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The TCP/IP model has four layers: Application, Transport, Internet, and Link (Network Access).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Session is an OSI layer, not a separate TCP/IP model layer.
- D: Presentation is an OSI layer, folded into Application in TCP/IP.
- B: "Data Center" is not a TCP/IP model layer.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > TCP Three-Way Handshake*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0058 — Communications / Converged protocols  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

iSCSI is an example of a converged protocol because it:

- **A.** Merges the SCSI storage protocol with IP networking to allow remote storage access
- **B.** Replaces the need for any cabling
- **C.** Routes traffic only within a single VLAN
- **D.** Encrypts all wireless traffic using AES

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** iSCSI converges the SCSI storage command protocol with TCP/IP networking, enabling block storage access over standard IP networks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: AES wireless encryption (WPA2/3) is unrelated to iSCSI.
- B: iSCSI runs over IP networks, which require cabling/infrastructure.
- C: iSCSI is not limited to a single VLAN.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Converged Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0059 — Communications / Covert channels  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An attacker hides data exfiltration inside the unused fields and timing of an otherwise legitimate protocol so it blends with normal traffic. This abuse of encapsulation is known as:

- **A.** A three-way handshake
- **B.** A covert channel (storage or timing)
- **C.** A default route
- **D.** A broadcast storm

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Covert channels (storage- or timing-based) abuse encapsulation/protocol features to hide unauthorized communication within legitimate-looking traffic, often bypassing detection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A broadcast storm is excessive broadcast traffic, not hidden exfiltration.
- A: A three-way handshake establishes a TCP connection.
- C: A default route is a routing configuration, not a hidden channel.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Multilayer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0060 — IP Addressing / Loopback  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The IPv4 address 127.0.0.1 is commonly used as the:

- **A.** Standard public DNS server
- **B.** Broadcast address of every subnet
- **C.** Loopback (localhost) address for testing the local TCP/IP stack
- **D.** Default gateway for all networks

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** 127.0.0.1 is the loopback/localhost address used to test the local TCP/IP stack on the host itself.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: The default gateway is a router interface address, not loopback.
- B: A broadcast address is the highest address in a subnet, not 127.0.0.1.
- A: 127.0.0.1 is internal to the host, not a public DNS server.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > IP Networking*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0061 — TCP/IP / Encapsulation concept  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

During encapsulation, what happens to data as it moves down the OSI stack?

- **A.** Each layer adds its own header (and sometimes trailer) information
- **B.** Each layer removes a header to expose the payload
- **C.** The data is converted to a single packet with no changes
- **D.** The data is encrypted at every layer automatically

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Encapsulation adds headers (and trailers) at each descending layer, forming segments, packets, frames, and finally bits; the reverse (de-encapsulation) strips them on receipt.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Removing headers is de-encapsulation, which occurs on the receiving side moving up the stack.
- D: Encapsulation itself does not automatically encrypt at every layer.
- C: The data changes form (segment/packet/frame/bits) as headers are added.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Encapsulation Process*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0062 — Communication Protocols / Secure remote access  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which protocol should REPLACE Telnet for remote command-line administration because it encrypts the session?

- **A.** HTTP
- **B.** FTP
- **C.** SSH
- **D.** SNMPv1

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SSH provides encrypted remote access and should replace Telnet, which transmits credentials and commands in cleartext.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: HTTP is for unencrypted web traffic, not remote shell access.
- D: SNMPv1 monitors devices and is itself insecure; it is not a remote shell.
- B: FTP transfers files in cleartext and is not a secure remote shell.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 7 - Application Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0063 — Communication Protocols / Secure web  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

HTTPS provides secure web communication primarily by using:

- **A.** ARP to resolve addresses
- **B.** NAT to translate addresses
- **C.** ICMP to confirm delivery
- **D.** TLS (formerly SSL) to encrypt the session

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** HTTPS secures web traffic by layering HTTP over TLS (which replaced SSL), providing confidentiality and integrity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: ARP resolves IP-to-MAC addresses; it does not encrypt web traffic.
- B: NAT translates addresses; it does not provide encryption.
- C: ICMP carries status/error messages, not web encryption.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 7 - Application Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0064 — Communication Protocols / Cleartext protocols  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A security review finds employees logging into a device management interface over Telnet across the corporate LAN. What is the PRIMARY risk and best remediation?

- **A.** Credentials travel in cleartext and can be sniffed; replace Telnet with SSH
- **B.** Telnet encrypts too strongly; downgrade to HTTP
- **C.** Telnet uses too much bandwidth; switch to UDP
- **D.** There is no risk because it is on the internal LAN

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Telnet sends credentials and commands in cleartext, exposing them to sniffing (including internal threats); SSH provides an encrypted replacement.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: The risk is confidentiality (cleartext), not bandwidth.
- B: Telnet does not encrypt at all; HTTP is also cleartext.
- D: Internal networks are not inherently trusted (insider/lateral-movement risk).

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 7 - Application Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0065 — Communication Protocols / DNP3 / ICS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

DNP3 is a protocol most commonly associated with:

- **A.** Assigning IP addresses to laptops
- **B.** Resolving domain names
- **C.** Encrypting email messages
- **D.** Industrial control systems (e.g., electric, water, oil and gas)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** DNP3 (Distributed Network Protocol v3) is widely used in industrial control systems such as electric utilities, water/wastewater, and oil and gas.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Email encryption uses S/MIME or PGP, not DNP3.
- B: Name resolution is DNS.
- A: IP assignment is DHCP.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Important Protocol to Know for the Exam*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0066 — Firewalls / Stateful vs stateless  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

How does a stateful inspection firewall improve on a static (stateless) packet-filtering firewall?

- **A.** It only inspects the source IP and nothing else
- **B.** It operates exclusively at Layer 1
- **C.** It cannot block any traffic
- **D.** It tracks the state of active connections to decide which packets belong to a legitimate session

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A stateful firewall maintains a state table of active connections (Layers 3-4), allowing return traffic that belongs to established sessions and blocking unsolicited packets.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Inspecting only source IP describes basic stateless filtering.
- B: Stateful inspection works at Layers 3-4, not Layer 1.
- C: Stateful firewalls do block traffic based on policy and state.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Stateful Inspection Firewall*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0067 — Firewalls / Static packet filter  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A static packet-filtering firewall makes allow/deny decisions based on:

- **A.** Source/destination IP addresses, protocols, and port numbers
- **B.** The user's biometric data
- **C.** The full application payload content
- **D.** The physical temperature of the device

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A static (stateless) packet filter operates at Layer 3 and filters on IPs, protocols, and ports without tracking connection state.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Deep application-payload inspection is done by application-level gateways/NGFWs.
- B: Biometrics are an authentication factor, not a packet-filter criterion.
- D: Device temperature is irrelevant to filtering.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Static Packet Filtering Firewall*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0068 — Firewalls / Application-level gateway  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

An application-level gateway (proxy firewall) provides stronger content filtering than a packet filter because it:

- **A.** Ignores the application data entirely
- **B.** Operates faster with no performance impact
- **C.** Inspects and manages application-layer (Layer 7) data, not just headers
- **D.** Only looks at MAC addresses

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** An application-level gateway operates at Layer 7, inspecting and filtering application data for detailed content control (at some performance cost).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: MAC-only inspection is a Layer 2 concern, not an application gateway.
- B: Deeper inspection typically reduces performance, not improves it.
- A: Inspecting application data is precisely its purpose.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Proxy Firewall*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0069 — Firewalls / NGFW  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Next-Generation Firewall (NGFW) typically adds which capabilities beyond traditional firewalls?

- **A.** Physical fire suppression
- **B.** Only stateless port filtering
- **C.** Deep packet inspection, intrusion prevention, and application awareness
- **D.** Magnetic media sanitization

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** NGFWs combine traditional filtering with deep packet inspection, integrated IPS, application awareness, and threat intelligence.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Stateless port filtering is the most basic firewall capability, not an NGFW addition.
- A: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- D: Media sanitization is a data-destruction activity.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Next Generation Firewall (NGFW)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0070 — Firewalls / Firewall-type mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each firewall type (1-4) to its description (A-D).

1. Static packet filter
2. Stateful inspection
3. Application-level gateway
4. NGFW

- **A.** Filters on IP/protocol/port with no state (Layer 3)
- **B.** Adds IPS, deep packet inspection, and application awareness
- **C.** Tracks connection state across Layers 3-4
- **D.** Inspects application-layer (Layer 7) content as a proxy

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-C,3-D,4-B

**Why correct:** Static packet filter → stateless L3 (B); Stateful inspection → tracks state L3-4 (A); Application-level gateway → L7 proxy (D); NGFW → IPS/DPI/app-aware (C).

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Types of Firewalls*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0071 — Firewall Architecture / Screened subnet (DMZ)  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company wants to host public web servers while keeping its internal network isolated, using two firewalls to create a buffer zone. This architecture is a:

- **A.** Flat network with all hosts in one zone
- **B.** Single bastion host with no firewall
- **C.** Screened subnet (DMZ)
- **D.** A mesh topology of switches

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A screened subnet (DMZ) uses two firewalls to place public-facing servers in a buffer zone between the internet and the internal network, limiting exposure.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A lone bastion host without firewalls does not create the two-firewall buffer described.
- A: A flat network provides no isolation between public and internal hosts.
- D: A switch mesh is a topology, not a firewall security architecture.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Screened Subnet (DMZ)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0072 — Firewall Architecture / Bastion host  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A bastion host is:

- **A.** A device that assigns IP addresses to clients
- **B.** A backup generator for the firewall
- **C.** A wireless access point for guests
- **D.** A hardened, exposed system specifically built to withstand attacks at a network boundary

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A bastion host is a hardened, often internet-facing system designed and locked down to resist attacks, frequently placed in or at the edge of a DMZ.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- B: A generator provides backup power.
- C: A guest WAP is a wireless access device, not a bastion host.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Bastion Host*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0073 — IPsec / Transport vs tunnel mode  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company is building a site-to-site VPN across the internet and wants the ENTIRE original IP packet (header and payload) protected. Which IPsec mode should it use?

- **A.** Tunnel mode (encrypts the full packet, including the original header)
- **B.** Transport mode (encrypts only the payload, leaving the header exposed)
- **C.** Half-duplex mode
- **D.** Cleartext mode for efficiency

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** IPsec tunnel mode encrypts the entire original packet (header + payload) and is typical for site-to-site/gateway VPNs; transport mode protects only the payload.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Transport mode leaves the original header exposed—less suited to full site-to-site protection.
- D: There is no "cleartext mode" for a secure VPN.
- C: Half-duplex is a communication directionality, not an IPsec mode.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > IPsec Operational Modes*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0074 — IPsec / AH vs ESP  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which IPsec component provides CONFIDENTIALITY (encryption) of the data, in addition to integrity and authentication?

- **A.** Security Association (SA)
- **B.** Authentication Header (AH)
- **C.** ISAKMP
- **D.** Encapsulating Security Payload (ESP)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ESP provides confidentiality (encryption) plus integrity and authentication; AH provides integrity/authentication/non-repudiation but does NOT encrypt.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: AH provides integrity and authentication but no encryption (no confidentiality).
- C: ISAKMP manages security associations/keys; it is not the data-encrypting component.
- A: An SA defines the parameters of a connection, not the encryption mechanism itself.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Components of IPsec*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0075 — IPsec / ISAKMP / SA  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In IPsec, a Security Association (SA) defines:

- **A.** The office building floor plan
- **B.** The physical cable type used
- **C.** The DNS server addresses
- **D.** The agreed security protocols, algorithms, and keys for a connection

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An SA specifies the security protocols, algorithms, and key material for an IPsec connection; ISAKMP negotiates and manages SAs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Cable type is a physical-media concern, not an SA.
- A: Floor plans are facility design, unrelated to SAs.
- C: DNS server addresses are unrelated to IPsec SAs.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Security Associations and ISAKMP*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0076 — Secure Channels / VPN purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A Virtual Private Network (VPN) primarily provides:

- **A.** A secure, encrypted tunnel for data across an untrusted network
- **B.** Faster CPU performance on endpoints
- **C.** Physical fire suppression in the data center
- **D.** Automatic assignment of MAC addresses

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A VPN creates an encrypted tunnel (e.g., via IPsec or TLS) so data can traverse untrusted networks (like the internet) securely.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: MAC addresses are hardware-assigned, not VPN-provided.
- C: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- B: A VPN does not increase CPU speed.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Endpoint Security*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0077 — Secure Channels / L2TP/IPsec  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

L2TP is often paired with IPsec for VPNs because L2TP by itself:

- **A.** Cannot create a tunnel at all
- **B.** Is a physical-layer cabling standard
- **C.** Provides tunneling but no encryption, so IPsec adds the confidentiality
- **D.** Encrypts data too strongly to be practical

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** L2TP creates the tunnel but does not encrypt; IPsec is added to provide encryption/confidentiality (L2TP/IPsec).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: L2TP provides no encryption on its own, so it is not "too strong."
- A: L2TP does create tunnels; it just lacks encryption.
- B: L2TP is a tunneling protocol, not a physical cabling standard.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 2 - Data Link Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0078 — Network Access Control / NAC purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Network Access Control (NAC) primarily enforces security by:

- **A.** Encrypting data at rest on file servers
- **B.** Replacing the need for any authentication
- **C.** Checking a device's posture/compliance before (and during) granting it network access
- **D.** Assigning public IP addresses to all devices

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** NAC assesses device identity and posture (e.g., patch level, configuration) before admitting it, and can monitor compliance afterward, quarantining non-compliant devices.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Data-at-rest encryption is a separate control.
- D: NAC controls access; it is not a public IP assignment service.
- B: NAC complements authentication; it does not eliminate it.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Network Access Control*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0079 — Network Access Control / Pre vs post-admission  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A laptop fails its health check (missing patches) when it connects. The NAC system places it on a restricted remediation network until it updates. Which NAC concepts are demonstrated?

- **A.** Static NAT translation
- **B.** Post-admission control that ignores device health
- **C.** Agentless DNS resolution
- **D.** Pre-admission control with a quarantine/remediation network

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Pre-admission NAC evaluates device posture before granting full access; non-compliant devices are sent to a quarantine/remediation network to be fixed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Ignoring device health contradicts the health-check described.
- C: Agentless DNS resolution is unrelated to posture-based quarantine.
- A: Static NAT is an address-translation method, not NAC posture control.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Types of NAC Implementations*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0080 — Network Access Control / 802.1X  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

IEEE 802.1X is used to provide:

- **A.** A method to assign multicast addresses
- **B.** A fire suppression standard for data centers
- **C.** A wireless cable category rating
- **D.** Port-based network access control requiring authentication before a device is allowed on the network

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** 802.1X provides port-based network access control, requiring a device/user to authenticate (often via RADIUS) before the switch port or WLAN grants access.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Fire suppression standards are unrelated to 802.1X.
- C: Cable categories (Cat5/6) are unrelated to 802.1X.
- A: Multicast addressing is Class D IP, not 802.1X.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Network Access Control*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0081 — Secure Channels / Email security  _(medium, scenario_based)_

Which approach BEST provides confidentiality and integrity for email message content?

- **A.** Sending email over plain SMTP with no protections
- **B.** Disabling spam filters
- **C.** End-to-end message encryption/signing (e.g., S/MIME or PGP)
- **D.** Using a longer display name

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** S/MIME or PGP provide message-level encryption and digital signatures, protecting confidentiality and integrity (and enabling sender authentication).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Plain SMTP transmits messages in cleartext without protection.
- B: Disabling spam filters weakens, not strengthens, email security.
- D: A display name does not secure message content.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Email Security*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0082 — Secure Channels / VoIP security  _(medium, scenario_based)_

Because VoIP carries voice over the IP network, a key security consideration is that it:

- **A.** Requires a separate copper telephone line per call
- **B.** Inherits IP network threats (e.g., eavesdropping, DoS) and should be segmented/encrypted
- **C.** Is immune to network attacks because it carries voice
- **D.** Cannot be encrypted under any circumstances

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** VoIP runs over IP and is exposed to network threats like eavesdropping and DoS; segmentation (e.g., voice VLANs) and encryption help protect it.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Carrying voice does not exempt VoIP from IP-based attacks.
- D: VoIP can be encrypted (e.g., SRTP/TLS).
- A: VoIP uses the existing IP infrastructure, not a copper line per call.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Voice Communications*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0083 — Communication Protocols / Secure protocol selection  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO protocols provide built-in encryption/security for their function? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** FTP
- **B.** HTTPS
- **C.** SSH
- **D.** Telnet

**Correct answer:** B, C

**Why correct:** SSH (secure remote access) and HTTPS (TLS-encrypted web) provide built-in security; Telnet and FTP transmit data in cleartext.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Telnet is an unencrypted remote-access protocol.
- A: FTP transmits files and credentials in cleartext.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Communication Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0084 — Firewalls / Firewall deployment  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Web Application Firewall (WAF) differs from a traditional network firewall in that it:

- **A.** Inspects and filters HTTP/HTTPS application traffic to protect web apps (e.g., from injection/XSS)
- **B.** Provides backup power to web servers
- **C.** Routes traffic between autonomous systems
- **D.** Only filters based on MAC addresses

**Correct answer:** A
