**Why correct:** A WAF focuses on application-layer web traffic, protecting web applications from attacks like SQL injection and cross-site scripting.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: MAC filtering is a Layer 2 control, not a WAF function.
- B: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- C: Inter-AS routing is a BGP/router function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Deployment Strategies*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0085 — IPsec / AH non-repudiation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The IPsec Authentication Header (AH) provides integrity, authentication, and non-repudiation, but notably does NOT provide:

- **A.** Confidentiality (encryption of the data)
- **B.** Data integrity
- **C.** Source authentication
- **D.** Protection against replay

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** AH provides integrity, authentication, non-repudiation, and anti-replay, but it does not encrypt the data (no confidentiality)—ESP is needed for that.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: AH does help protect against replay attacks.
- B: AH does provide data integrity.
- C: AH does provide source authentication.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Components of IPsec*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0086 — Network Access Control / NAC deployment models  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are NAC deployment models described in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Multicast NAC
- **B.** Agentless NAC
- **C.** Tunnel-mode NAC
- **D.** Agent-based NAC

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** NAC can be deployed agent-based (software on the endpoint) or agentless (no installed agent); these are the two models described.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: "Tunnel-mode" is an IPsec mode, not a NAC deployment model.
- A: "Multicast NAC" is not a defined NAC deployment model.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Deployment Models*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0087 — Communication Protocols / ARP  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Address Resolution Protocol (ARP) is used to:

- **A.** Encrypt web traffic
- **B.** Assign dynamic IP addresses to clients
- **C.** Resolve a domain name to an IP address
- **D.** Resolve a known IP address to its corresponding MAC address

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ARP maps a known IP (Layer 3) address to a MAC (Layer 2) address for local frame delivery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Name-to-IP resolution is DNS.
- A: Web traffic encryption is TLS/HTTPS.
- B: Dynamic IP assignment is DHCP.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 2 - Data Link Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0088 — Firewalls / Firewall as first line  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In network defense, firewalls are commonly described as:

- **A.** A backup power source for the network
- **B.** A first line of defense that controls traffic between trust zones
- **C.** A method of physically destroying data
- **D.** A protocol for resolving domain names

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Firewalls form a first line of network defense, enforcing policy on traffic between zones of differing trust.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- C: Data destruction is sanitization, not a firewall.
- D: Name resolution is DNS.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Firewalls Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0089 — Secure Channels / Remote access auth  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

To strengthen remote-access security for a VPN, the BEST practice among these is to:

- **A.** Share a single VPN password across all employees
- **B.** Allow any device to connect without checks
- **C.** Require multi-factor authentication for remote users
- **D.** Disable logging of remote connections

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Requiring MFA significantly reduces the risk of compromised remote-access credentials being used by attackers.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Allowing unchecked devices invites compromised endpoints onto the network.
- A: Shared passwords destroy accountability and are easily leaked.
- D: Disabling logging removes visibility and forensic capability.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Objective 4.3*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0090 — Firewall Architecture / Multi-homed firewall  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A multi-homed firewall is characterized by:

- **A.** Having exactly one network interface
- **B.** Being unable to enforce any policy
- **C.** Operating only at the physical layer
- **D.** Having multiple network interfaces connecting different network segments/zones

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A multi-homed firewall has two or more network interfaces, each connected to a different segment/zone, allowing it to control traffic between them.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A single interface would be single-homed, not multi-homed.
- B: Multi-homed firewalls do enforce policy between their interfaces.
- C: Firewalls operate at Layers 3-7, not only the physical layer.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Firewall Architecture*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0091 — Communication Protocols / DNS  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The Domain Name System (DNS) primarily:

- **A.** Provides backup power to servers
- **B.** Encrypts all email in transit
- **C.** Resolves human-readable domain names to IP addresses
- **D.** Assigns MAC addresses to network cards

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** DNS translates domain names (e.g., example.com) into IP addresses so traffic can be routed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Email encryption is handled by S/MIME/PGP/TLS, not DNS.
- D: MAC addresses are hardware-assigned, not by DNS.
- A: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.

*Source: Domain 4 > Networking Concepts > Layer 7 - Application Layer Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0092 — Firewalls / Screened host  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A screened-host architecture combines a packet-filtering router with:

- **A.** A bastion host that the router directs external traffic to
- **B.** A wireless mesh of access points
- **C.** A magnetic degausser
- **D.** Two separate internet connections

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A screened host uses a screening router plus a bastion host; the router filters traffic and directs permitted external traffic to the hardened bastion host.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Dual internet connections describe redundancy, not the screened-host design.
- B: A wireless mesh is a topology, not this firewall architecture.
- C: A degausser destroys data; it is unrelated to firewall architecture.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Screened Host*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0093 — Reconnaissance Attacks / Passive vs active  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Gathering information about a target using publicly available sources (OSINT) without directly interacting with the target systems is:

- **A.** Passive reconnaissance
- **B.** A denial-of-service attack
- **C.** ARP poisoning
- **D.** Active reconnaissance

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Passive reconnaissance collects information from public sources (OSINT) without touching the target, making it harder to detect.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Active reconnaissance directly probes the target (e.g., port scanning), which can be detected.
- B: A DoS attack disrupts availability, not information gathering.
- C: ARP poisoning is a spoofing attack, not reconnaissance via OSINT.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Reconnaissance Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0094 — Reconnaissance Attacks / Active recon  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An attacker runs a port scan against a company's public IP range to find open services. This activity is BEST classified as:

- **A.** A teardrop attack
- **B.** Passive reconnaissance
- **C.** A replay attack
- **D.** Active reconnaissance

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Port scanning directly interacts with the target to enumerate open services, which is active reconnaissance (and is detectable by monitoring).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Passive recon uses public sources without touching the target; scanning is direct interaction.
- A: A teardrop attack sends overlapping fragments to crash a host, not enumerate services.
- C: A replay attack reuses captured messages, not scanning.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Reconnaissance Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0095 — Reconnaissance Attacks / Recon types  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO activities are forms of reconnaissance? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Port and service scanning
- **B.** Open-source intelligence (OSINT) gathering
- **C.** Flooding a server with traffic
- **D.** Reinstalling encryption keys

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** OSINT gathering (passive) and port/service scanning (active) are reconnaissance activities used to profile a target.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Reinstalling encryption keys describes a KRACK attack, not reconnaissance.
- C: Flooding a server is a denial-of-service attack.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Reconnaissance Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0096 — Spoofing and Poisoning / ARP poisoning  _(hard, scenario_based)_

On a LAN, an attacker sends forged ARP replies so that traffic destined for the gateway is instead sent to the attacker's machine, enabling interception. This attack is:

- **A.** DNS poisoning
- **B.** A Smurf attack
- **C.** War driving
- **D.** ARP spoofing/poisoning (enabling a man-in-the-middle)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ARP poisoning sends forged ARP replies that map the gateway's IP to the attacker's MAC, letting the attacker intercept traffic (man-in-the-middle) on the local segment.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: DNS poisoning corrupts name resolution, not ARP/MAC mappings on the LAN.
- B: A Smurf attack is an ICMP amplification DoS, not interception.
- C: War driving is searching for wireless networks while moving.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Key Attack Methods*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0097 — Spoofing and Poisoning / DNS poisoning  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

An attacker alters DNS records so users typing a legitimate bank URL are redirected to a malicious look-alike site. This is DNS poisoning, and the BEST defense is:

- **A.** Lengthening the office fence
- **B.** Disabling all logging
- **C.** DNSSEC, which authenticates DNS responses
- **D.** Using a hub instead of a switch

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** DNS poisoning redirects traffic via forged DNS data; DNSSEC adds cryptographic authentication of DNS responses to detect/prevent tampering.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Disabling logging reduces visibility and does not defend DNS integrity.
- D: A hub worsens security and does not protect DNS.
- A: A taller fence is a physical control unrelated to DNS poisoning.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Key Attack Methods*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0098 — Spoofing and Poisoning / IP spoofing  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

IP spoofing involves:

- **A.** Changing a device's MAC address to bypass filtering
- **B.** Flooding a target with ICMP echo replies
- **C.** Forging the source IP address in packet headers to appear as a trusted source
- **D.** Redirecting DNS lookups to malicious servers

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** IP spoofing manipulates the source IP in packet headers to impersonate a trusted host and bypass IP-based controls.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Changing a MAC address is MAC spoofing.
- D: Redirecting DNS lookups is DNS spoofing/poisoning.
- B: Flooding with ICMP replies relates to a Smurf/amplification DoS.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Key Attack Methods*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0099 — Spoofing and Poisoning / MAC spoofing defense  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which control helps defend against MAC spoofing at the data link layer by restricting which devices may use a switch port?

- **A.** Port security / MAC filtering
- **B.** A wet-pipe sprinkler
- **C.** A backup generator
- **D.** DNSSEC

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Port security/MAC filtering restricts switch ports to authorized MAC addresses, reducing the risk of spoofing at Layer 2.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: DNSSEC protects DNS responses, not switch-port MAC access.
- C: A generator provides power, not MAC access control.
- B: A sprinkler is a fire-suppression control.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Defensive Measures*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0100 — Spoofing and Poisoning / Spoofing types  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are spoofing/poisoning attacks? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Teardrop attack
- **B.** DNS spoofing
- **C.** War chalking
- **D.** ARP poisoning

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** ARP poisoning and DNS spoofing falsify mappings/records to redirect or intercept traffic.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A teardrop attack is a DoS using overlapping fragments, not spoofing.
- C: War chalking is marking locations of wireless networks, not spoofing.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Key Attack Methods*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0101 — Denial of Service / Smurf attack  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A Smurf attack amplifies traffic against a victim by:

- **A.** Marking sidewalks with network symbols
- **B.** Sending oversized packets that exceed the MTU
- **C.** Reinstalling WPA2 encryption keys
- **D.** Spoofing the victim's IP and broadcasting ICMP echo requests so many hosts reply to the victim

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A Smurf attack spoofs the victim's source IP and sends ICMP echo requests to a broadcast address; all responders flood the victim with replies (amplification).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Oversized packets exceeding MTU describe a Ping of Death.
- C: Reinstalling WPA2 keys describes KRACK.
- A: Marking locations is war chalking.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Reflection and Amplification Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0102 — Denial of Service / Fraggle attack  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A Fraggle attack is similar to a Smurf attack but uses which protocol for its echo flood?

- **A.** UDP
- **B.** ARP
- **C.** ICMP
- **D.** TCP

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A Fraggle attack is the UDP-echo analog of the Smurf attack (which uses ICMP echo).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: ICMP echo is used by the Smurf attack.
- D: TCP is not the protocol used in a Fraggle attack.
- B: ARP is not used for the Fraggle echo flood.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Reflection and Amplification Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0103 — Denial of Service / Ping of Death  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Ping of Death attack crashes vulnerable systems by:

- **A.** Forging DNS records
- **B.** Sending malformed or oversized packets that exceed the maximum allowed size (MTU)
- **C.** Cracking WPA2 handshakes
- **D.** Spoofing ARP replies on the LAN

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A Ping of Death sends oversized/malformed packets exceeding the MTU that some systems mishandle, causing crashes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Forging ARP replies is ARP poisoning.
- C: Cracking WPA2 handshakes relates to KRACK/wireless attacks.
- A: Forging DNS records is DNS poisoning.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Legacy Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0104 — Denial of Service / Land attack  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A Land attack causes a target to malfunction by sending SYN packets where:

- **A.** The DNS record points to a malicious site
- **B.** The ARP table is flooded with random MACs
- **C.** The payload is encrypted with the target's public key
- **D.** The source and destination IP are both set to the target's own address

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A Land attack spoofs SYN packets with the target's own IP as both source and destination, causing the machine to attempt to connect to itself and hang/crash.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Encryption with a public key is not how a Land attack works.
- A: Pointing DNS to a malicious site is DNS poisoning.
- B: Flooding the ARP table is a different (CAM/ARP) attack.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Legacy Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0105 — Denial of Service / Teardrop attack  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A Teardrop attack crashes a system by sending:

- **A.** Forged ARP replies
- **B.** A single oversized ping packet
- **C.** Unsolicited Bluetooth messages
- **D.** Fragmented packets with overlapping payloads that fail during reassembly

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A Teardrop attack sends overlapping/malformed IP fragments that crash systems unable to correctly reassemble them.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A single oversized ping is the Ping of Death.
- A: Forged ARP replies are ARP poisoning.
- C: Unsolicited Bluetooth messages are bluejacking.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Legacy Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0106 — Denial of Service / DoS vs DDoS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Distributed Denial of Service (DDoS) attack differs from a basic DoS attack in that a DDoS:

- **A.** Encrypts the victim's files for ransom
- **B.** Relies on a single host with a slow connection
- **C.** Always targets only wireless networks
- **D.** Uses many compromised systems (a botnet) to flood the target simultaneously

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A DDoS leverages many distributed, often compromised hosts (a botnet) to overwhelm the target, making it harder to block than a single-source DoS.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: DDoS can target any network service, not only wireless.
- A: Encrypting files for ransom is ransomware, not DDoS.
- B: A single slow host describes a limited DoS, not a distributed attack.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Denial of Service Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0107 — Denial of Service / DoS protection  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO strategies help mitigate denial-of-service attacks? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Rate limiting and traffic analysis
- **B.** Disabling all firewalls
- **C.** Load balancing and network redundancy
- **D.** Sharing admin passwords widely

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** Rate limiting/traffic analysis and load balancing/redundancy help absorb and detect DoS traffic, reducing impact on any single point.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Disabling firewalls removes a key filtering defense.
- D: Sharing admin passwords harms security and does not mitigate DoS.

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Protection Strategies*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0108 — Network Attacks / Attack-to-description mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each network attack (1-4) to its description (A-D).

1. Smurf attack
2. Land attack
3. ARP poisoning
4. DNS poisoning

- **A.** Forges ARP replies to intercept LAN traffic (MITM)
- **B.** Alters DNS records to redirect users to malicious sites
- **C.** Sends SYN packets with the target's IP as both source and destination
- **D.** Spoofs the victim IP and floods it via ICMP broadcast replies

**Correct answer:** 1-D,2-C,3-A,4-B

**Why correct:** Smurf → ICMP broadcast amplification (B); Land → self-addressed SYN (D); ARP poisoning → forged ARP/MITM (A); DNS poisoning → altered DNS records (C).

*Source: Domain 4 > Network Security and Attacks > Legacy Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0109 — Wireless Security / WEP weakness  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Wired Equivalent Privacy (WEP) is considered insecure largely because it:

- **A.** Requires multi-factor authentication for every packet
- **B.** Uses 256-bit AES with strong key management
- **C.** Cannot be used on any wireless network
- **D.** Uses the weak RC4 cipher with flawed key handling

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** WEP relies on the RC4 stream cipher with weak IV/key handling, making it easily cracked; it should be replaced by WPA2/WPA3.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: WEP does not use AES; that describes WPA2/WPA3.
- A: WEP does not use per-packet MFA.
- C: WEP can technically run on wireless networks but is insecure.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Encryption Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0110 — Wireless Security / WPA2  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

WPA2 improved wireless security over WPA primarily by introducing:

- **A.** WPS PIN-only authentication
- **B.** The RC4 stream cipher
- **C.** Cleartext transmission
- **D.** CCMP with AES encryption

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** WPA2 uses CCMP (based on AES), providing stronger encryption than WPA's TKIP and WEP's RC4.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: RC4 is the weak cipher used by WEP.
- C: WPA2 encrypts traffic; it is not cleartext.
- A: WPS is a convenience feature with security weaknesses, not WPA2's core improvement.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Encryption Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0111 — Wireless Security / WPA3  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

WPA3 strengthens key exchange against offline password-guessing attacks by using:

- **A.** The RC4 cipher
- **B.** Simultaneous Authentication of Equals (SAE)
- **C.** WEP-style shared keys
- **D.** Cleartext PSK exchange

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** WPA3 introduces SAE (Simultaneous Authentication of Equals) for a more secure handshake that resists offline dictionary attacks, plus stronger AES key sizes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: RC4 is the weak WEP cipher.
- C: WEP shared keys are insecure and not used by WPA3.
- D: WPA3 does not exchange the PSK in cleartext.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Encryption Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0112 — Wireless Security / Encryption protocol order  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO wireless security protocols are considered STRONG/current choices (versus deprecated ones)? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** WPA2
- **B.** WPA3
- **C.** Open (no encryption)
- **D.** WEP

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** WPA2 (CCMP/AES) and WPA3 (SAE/AES) are the strong, current choices; WEP and open networks are insecure/deprecated.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: WEP is deprecated and easily broken.
- C: An open network provides no encryption.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Encryption Protocols*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0113 — Wireless Security / WPS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Why is Wi-Fi Protected Setup (WPS) generally NOT recommended?

- **A.** It uses 256-bit AES that is too strong to manage
- **B.** Its PIN-based setup is vulnerable to brute-force attacks
- **C.** It only works on wired networks
- **D.** It requires fiber-optic cabling

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** WPS uses short PINs that are susceptible to brute-force attacks, so it is discouraged despite its convenience.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: WPS is weak, not too strong.
- D: WPS is a wireless feature unrelated to fiber cabling.
- C: WPS is for wireless setup, not wired networks.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Wi-Fi Protected Setup (WPS)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0114 — Wireless Attacks / Evil twin  _(hard, scenario_based)_

At an airport, an attacker sets up a rogue access point named "Airport_Free_WiFi" that mimics the legitimate network to capture victims' credentials. This is BEST described as:

- **A.** An evil twin attack
- **B.** A teardrop attack
- **C.** A Smurf attack
- **D.** War chalking

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** An evil twin is a fake access point impersonating a legitimate SSID to lure users and capture their credentials/traffic.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: War chalking marks the locations of wireless networks.
- B: A teardrop attack is a fragmentation DoS.
- C: A Smurf attack is an ICMP amplification DoS.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Key Wireless Network Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0115 — Wireless Attacks / Rogue AP  _(medium, scenario_based)_

An employee plugs an unauthorized wireless access point into the corporate LAN to get better Wi-Fi at their desk. The security concern is that this is a:

- **A.** Rogue access point that bypasses network security controls
- **B.** A required part of 802.1X
- **C.** Form of DNSSEC
- **D.** Legitimate redundancy improvement

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A rogue access point is an unauthorized AP connected to the network that bypasses security controls and creates an uncontrolled entry point.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Unauthorized APs are a security risk, not sanctioned redundancy.
- C: DNSSEC authenticates DNS responses; it is unrelated.
- B: 802.1X is port-based access control, not an unauthorized AP.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Key Wireless Network Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0116 — Wireless Attacks / KRACK  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A Key Reinstallation Attack (KRACK) exploits a weakness in:

- **A.** The DNS resolution process
- **B.** The WPA2 four-way handshake, forcing reuse of encryption keys
- **C.** The ARP cache on a switch
- **D.** The TCP three-way handshake

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** KRACK manipulates the WPA2 four-way handshake to force nonce/key reinstallation, enabling decryption/replay of wireless traffic.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: DNS resolution is targeted by DNS poisoning, not KRACK.
- C: ARP cache manipulation is ARP poisoning.
- D: The TCP handshake is unrelated to WPA2 key reinstallation.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Key Wireless Network Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0117 — Wireless Attacks / War driving  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Driving around an area with a device to locate and identify vulnerable wireless networks is called:

- **A.** War driving
- **B.** Bluesnarfing
- **C.** Bluejacking
- **D.** War chalking

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** War driving is moving through an area to discover and map wireless networks (often to find vulnerable ones).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: War chalking marks the discovered networks' locations.
- C: Bluejacking sends unsolicited Bluetooth messages.
- B: Bluesnarfing steals data over Bluetooth.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Key Wireless Network Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0118 — Wireless Attacks / War chalking  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Marking physical locations with symbols to indicate the presence of open or vulnerable wireless networks is:

- **A.** Signal jamming
- **B.** An evil twin
- **C.** War driving
- **D.** War chalking

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** War chalking uses symbols/markings to advertise the location and characteristics of nearby wireless networks for others to exploit.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: War driving is the act of moving around to find networks.
- A: Signal jamming is a wireless DoS.
- B: An evil twin is an impostor access point.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Key Wireless Network Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0119 — Wireless Attacks / Signal jamming  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Wireless signal jamming is a form of which attack category?

- **A.** Spoofing
- **B.** Denial of service (DoS)
- **C.** Reconnaissance
- **D.** Privilege escalation

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Signal jamming floods the radio frequency to disrupt wireless communications—a denial-of-service attack on availability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Spoofing falsifies identity/records, not RF disruption.
- C: Reconnaissance gathers information rather than disrupting service.
- D: Privilege escalation gains higher access, not RF disruption.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Key Wireless Network Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0120 — Bluetooth Security / Bluesnarfing  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Forcing a Bluetooth connection to access and steal stored data from a device is known as:

- **A.** Bluejacking
- **B.** Bluebugging
- **C.** Bluesnarfing
- **D.** War driving

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Bluesnarfing forces a Bluetooth connection to access/steal data stored on the target device.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Bluejacking sends unsolicited messages, not data theft.
- B: Bluebugging takes control of the device to execute commands.
- D: War driving is wireless-network discovery.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Bluetooth Security Threats*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0121 — Bluetooth Security / Bluebugging  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Gaining control of a device over Bluetooth to execute commands without the user's knowledge is:

- **A.** War chalking
- **B.** Bluejacking
- **C.** Bluesnarfing
- **D.** Bluebugging

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Bluebugging gives an attacker control of a device to execute commands covertly over Bluetooth.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Bluejacking only sends unsolicited messages.
- C: Bluesnarfing steals stored data but does not necessarily control the device.
- A: War chalking marks wireless network locations.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Bluetooth Security Threats*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0122 — Bluetooth Security / Bluetooth attacks  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are Bluetooth-specific security threats? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Bluejacking
- **B.** DNS poisoning
- **C.** Bluesnarfing
- **D.** Smurf attack

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** Bluejacking (unsolicited messages) and bluesnarfing (data theft) are Bluetooth-specific attacks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A Smurf attack is an ICMP amplification DoS on IP networks.
- B: DNS poisoning targets name resolution, not Bluetooth.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Bluetooth Security Threats*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0123 — Wireless Concepts / SSID types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The BSSID in a wireless network refers to:

- **A.** The human-readable network name only
- **B.** The encryption key used by clients
- **C.** The MAC address of a specific access point
- **D.** The IP address of the default gateway

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The BSSID (Basic SSID) is the MAC address of a specific access point; the ESSID is the network name shared across multiple APs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The network name is the SSID/ESSID, not the BSSID.
- B: The encryption key is separate from the BSSID.
- D: The gateway IP is unrelated to the BSSID.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Service Set Identifiers (SSID)*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0124 — Wireless Concepts / Wireless modes  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In wireless infrastructure mode, client devices communicate:

- **A.** Only directly device-to-device with no access point
- **B.** Exclusively over fiber-optic cable
- **C.** Through a central access point rather than directly peer-to-peer
- **D.** Without any radio frequencies

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Infrastructure mode routes client traffic through a central access point; ad-hoc mode is the peer-to-peer alternative with no AP.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Direct device-to-device with no AP describes ad-hoc mode.
- B: Wireless uses radio, not fiber cabling.
- D: Wireless inherently uses radio frequencies.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Wireless Network Modes*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0125 — Mobile Device Management / Remote wipe  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An employee's smartphone containing corporate data is lost. Which MDM control BEST protects the data on that device?

- **A.** Disabling the company VPN for everyone
- **B.** Removing the screen lock to ease recovery
- **C.** Increasing the office Wi-Fi signal strength
- **D.** Remote device sanitization (remote wipe), ideally combined with device encryption

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** MDM remote wipe lets administrators sanitize a lost/stolen device remotely; with data-at-rest encryption, the data is protected even before the wipe completes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Disabling the VPN does not protect data already on the lost device.
- C: Wi-Fi signal strength is irrelevant to protecting a lost device.
- B: Removing the screen lock weakens protection of the device.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Mobile Device Management Controls*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0126 — Mobile Device Management / Geofencing  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

An MDM "geofencing" control enforces security policies based on:

- **A.** The color of the device case
- **B.** The physical location of the device
- **C.** The brand of the device charger
- **D.** The number of installed apps

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Geofencing applies location-based access and permissions, e.g., restricting features when a device leaves an approved area.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Case color is irrelevant to geofencing.
- D: App count is not the basis of geofencing.
- C: Charger brand is irrelevant.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Mobile Device Management Controls*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0127 — Mobile Device Management / MDM controls  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are MDM controls that protect enterprise mobile devices? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Allowing untrusted app installation
- **B.** Configuring inactivity/session timeouts
- **C.** Disabling all device passcodes
- **D.** Enforcing encryption for data at rest

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** Encryption of data at rest and inactivity/session timeouts are MDM controls that reduce data exposure on mobile devices.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Disabling passcodes weakens device security.
- A: Allowing untrusted apps increases malware risk.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Mobile Device Management Controls*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0128 — Wireless Security / General Wi-Fi hardening  _(medium, scenario_based)_

Which is a recommended general measure to harden a wireless network?

- **A.** Keep the factory default admin password
- **B.** Broadcast the admin credentials in the SSID
- **C.** Change default passwords/SSIDs and use WPA2 or WPA3 encryption
- **D.** Leave the network open to maximize compatibility

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Changing default credentials/SSIDs and using strong encryption (WPA2/WPA3) are core wireless hardening steps, ideally with a defense-in-depth approach.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: An open network exposes traffic to anyone in range.
- A: Default admin passwords are widely known and easily abused.
- B: Broadcasting credentials in the SSID exposes them to attackers.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > General Wi-Fi Security Measures*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0129 — Wireless Attacks / Evil twin vs rogue AP  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

What primarily distinguishes an evil twin from a generic rogue access point?

- **A.** A rogue AP always uses WPA3 encryption
- **B.** An evil twin is always authorized by IT
- **C.** An evil twin only affects wired networks
- **D.** An evil twin deliberately impersonates a legitimate SSID to deceive users into connecting

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An evil twin is a rogue AP specifically configured to mimic a legitimate network's SSID to trick users; a generic rogue AP is simply an unauthorized AP, not necessarily impersonating one.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: An evil twin is malicious and unauthorized, not IT-sanctioned.
- A: A rogue AP's encryption is attacker-chosen and often weak/open, not guaranteed WPA3.
- C: Evil twins target wireless clients, not wired-only networks.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Key Wireless Network Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D04-Q0130 — Wireless Concepts / OFDM  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Orthogonal Frequency Division Multiplexing (OFDM) improves wireless throughput by:

- **A.** Eliminating the need for any access point
- **B.** Sending all data on a single narrow frequency
- **C.** Encrypting frames with RC4
- **D.** Dividing data into multiple substreams sent over separate subchannels

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** OFDM splits data into multiple substreams across separate orthogonal subchannels, increasing throughput and resilience to interference.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: OFDM uses multiple subchannels, not a single narrow frequency.
- C: OFDM is a modulation/multiplexing method, not an encryption cipher.
- A: OFDM does not remove the need for an access point.

*Source: Domain 4 > Wireless Networking Introduction > Orthogonal Frequency Division Multiplexing (OFDM)*

---


## Domain 5: Identity and Access Management (IAM)

### CISSP-D05-Q0001 — Identity Proofing / Proofing steps  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The identity proofing process consists of which three core steps?

- **A.** Plan, do, check
- **B.** Encrypt, hash, and sign
- **C.** Resolution, validation, and verification
- **D.** Detect, respond, and recover

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Identity proofing consists of resolution (uniquely identifying the applicant), validation (confirming the evidence is genuine), and verification (confirming the evidence belongs to the applicant).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Encrypt/hash/sign are cryptographic operations, not proofing steps.
- D: Detect/respond/recover are incident/CSF functions.
- A: Plan/do/check is the Deming cycle.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Identity Proofing Process*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0002 — Identity Proofing / Proofing sequence  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

Order the identity proofing process steps as described in the guide. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Validation
- **B.** Enrollment
- **C.** Applicant request
- **D.** Resolution
- **E.** Verification

**Correct answer:** C,D,A,E,B

**Why correct:** The flow runs from the applicant request, to resolution, validation, verification, and finally enrollment (creating the credential).

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Proofing Process Steps*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0003 — Identity Proofing / Resolution  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In identity proofing, the "resolution" step specifically aims to:

- **A.** Encrypt the applicant's biometric template
- **B.** Revoke the applicant's prior credentials
- **C.** Uniquely distinguish the applicant from all other individuals
- **D.** Grant the applicant access to all systems

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Resolution uniquely identifies/distinguishes the applicant as a single, specific person before evidence is validated and verified.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Encrypting a biometric template is a storage/protection step, not resolution.
- D: Granting access is authorization, which follows enrollment.
- B: Revoking credentials is part of deprovisioning, not proofing resolution.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Proofing Process Steps*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0004 — Identity Proofing / Verification  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

During proofing, the "verification" step confirms that:

- **A.** The applicant has paid for the service
- **B.** The network connection is encrypted
- **C.** The validated identity evidence actually belongs to the applicant presenting it
- **D.** The password meets complexity requirements

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Verification confirms that the (already validated) identity evidence genuinely relates to the applicant—linking the person to the evidence.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Payment is unrelated to identity verification.
- B: Connection encryption is a transport concern, not proofing verification.
- D: Password complexity is a credential policy, not identity verification.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Proofing Process Steps*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0005 — Assurance Levels / IAL/AAL  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In identity management, increasing assurance levels (Level 1 to Level 3) generally indicate:

- **A.** Progressively weaker security requirements
- **B.** Progressively stronger confidence/rigor in the identity proofing and authentication
- **C.** The physical size of the data center
- **D.** The number of users in the system

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Higher assurance levels require more rigorous proofing and stronger authentication, providing greater confidence in the claimed identity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Higher levels are stronger, not weaker.
- D: Assurance levels measure confidence, not user counts.
- C: They are unrelated to data center size.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Assurance Levels*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0006 — Identity Management / Credential Service Provider  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Credential Service Provider (CSP) is responsible for:

- **A.** Routing packets between autonomous systems
- **B.** Physically guarding the data center entrance
- **C.** Issuing and managing credentials for subscribers after enrollment
- **D.** Suppressing fires in server rooms

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A CSP registers identities and issues/manages credentials for subscribers as part of the identity lifecycle.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Physical guarding is a facility security function.
- D: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- A: Inter-AS routing is a BGP/router function.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Establishing Identity*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0007 — Provisioning Lifecycle / Lifecycle steps  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

Order the identity and access provisioning lifecycle steps as described in the guide. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Review
- **B.** Revocation
- **C.** Provisioning

**Correct answer:** C,A,B

**Why correct:** The lifecycle runs from provisioning (granting access), to periodic review (re-certifying access), to revocation (removing access when no longer needed).

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Identity and Access Lifecycle*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0008 — Provisioning Lifecycle / Deprovisioning  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An employee resigns. From an access-management standpoint, what is the MOST time-critical deprovisioning action?

- **A.** Archive their desk furniture
- **B.** Add them to additional security groups for record-keeping
- **C.** Schedule their next access review for next quarter
- **D.** Promptly revoke the departing employee's access to systems and data

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Access should be revoked quickly on departure to minimize the window for misuse of lingering credentials.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A future review does nothing to close the immediate access window.
- A: Furniture handling is not an access concern.
- B: Adding groups expands access—the opposite of deprovisioning.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Deprovisioning*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0009 — Provisioning Lifecycle / Access review  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Periodic access reviews (recertification) during the identity lifecycle primarily help to:

- **A.** Increase the number of administrators
- **B.** Replace the need for authentication
- **C.** Encrypt all network traffic
- **D.** Detect and remove excessive or no-longer-needed access (privilege creep)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Regular access reviews identify accumulated/unnecessary access and adjust it, countering privilege creep and unauthorized use.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Reviews right-size access; they do not add administrators.
- C: Traffic encryption is unrelated to access review.
- B: Reviews complement, not replace, authentication.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Lifecycle Steps*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0010 — Provisioning Lifecycle / Automation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Automating the provisioning and deprovisioning process primarily improves security by:

- **A.** Ensuring access is granted and removed consistently and promptly, reducing human error
- **B.** Eliminating the need for any access policy
- **C.** Guaranteeing no account is ever disabled
- **D.** Allowing users to grant themselves any access

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Automation makes provisioning/deprovisioning consistent and timely, reducing the manual errors and delays that leave dangling access.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Automation enforces policy; it does not remove the need for one.
- D: Self-granting access violates least privilege and separation of duties.
- C: Automation should ensure accounts ARE disabled when appropriate.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Key Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0011 — Federated Identity / FIM concept  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Federated Identity Management (FIM) primarily enables:

- **A.** Users to access multiple organizations' systems using a single, shared identity across trust domains
- **B.** Physical access control to buildings
- **C.** Encryption of all data at rest
- **D.** Automatic patching of operating systems

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** FIM lets a user's identity be trusted across organizational boundaries, so one identity can access resources in multiple trust domains (e.g., via SAML).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Data-at-rest encryption is a separate control.
- D: OS patching is unrelated to federation.
- B: Physical access control is a facility function.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Federated Identity Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0012 — Federated Identity / SAML  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) is built on XML and is primarily used to:

- **A.** Encrypt files on a hard drive
- **B.** Suppress electrical fires
- **C.** Exchange authentication and authorization assertions between an identity provider and a service provider
- **D.** Assign IP addresses to clients

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SAML uses XML (and SOAP) to exchange authentication/authorization assertions between an identity provider (IdP) and service provider (SP), enabling web SSO/federation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- A: File encryption is unrelated to SAML.
- B: Fire suppression is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0013 — Federated Identity / IdP vs SP  _(hard, scenario_based)_

In a SAML web-SSO flow, a user tries to access a partner web application. The application redirects to a trusted party that authenticates the user and asserts their identity. The application is the ____ and the authenticating party is the ____.

- **A.** KDC; TGS
- **B.** Service Provider (SP); Identity Provider (IdP)
- **C.** Identity Provider (IdP); Service Provider (SP)
- **D.** Resource Owner; Resource Server

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The application consuming the assertion is the Service Provider (SP); the party that authenticates the user and issues the assertion is the Identity Provider (IdP).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: This reverses the roles—the app is the SP, the authenticator is the IdP.
- D: Resource Owner/Server are OAuth roles, not SAML SP/IdP.
- A: KDC/TGS are Kerberos components, not SAML roles.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Operational Flow of SAML*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0014 — Federated Identity / SPML  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Service Provisioning Markup Language (SPML) is used primarily to:

- **A.** Exchange user, resource, and service provisioning information between organizations/systems
- **B.** Encrypt email messages
- **C.** Route packets between subnets
- **D.** Authenticate users with biometrics

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** SPML is an XML-based standard for exchanging provisioning information (creating/managing accounts and resources) across systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Biometric authentication is unrelated to SPML.
- B: Email encryption uses S/MIME/PGP, not SPML.
- C: Packet routing is a network-layer function.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Service Provisioning Markup Language (SPML)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0015 — Federated Identity / XACML  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Extensible Access Control Markup Language (XACML) is primarily used to:

- **A.** Provide a backup power source
- **B.** Resolve domain names to IP addresses
- **C.** Carry voice over IP networks
- **D.** Define and evaluate attribute-based access control policies

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** XACML is a standard for expressing and evaluating access control policies, commonly underpinning attribute-based access control (ABAC/PBAC).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Voice over IP is VoIP, unrelated to XACML.
- A: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- B: Name resolution is DNS.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Extensible Access Control Markup Language (XACML)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0016 — Federated Identity / Standards purpose  _(hard, matching)_

Match each identity/access standard (1-4) to its primary purpose (A-D).

1. SAML
2. SPML
3. XACML
4. OAuth

- **A.** Defining attribute-based access control policies
- **B.** Exchanging authentication/authorization assertions for SSO
- **C.** Exchanging account/resource provisioning information
- **D.** Delegated authorization using access tokens

**Correct answer:** 1-B,2-C,3-A,4-D

**Why correct:** SAML → authn/authz assertions/SSO (B); SPML → provisioning exchange (A); XACML → access-policy definition (D); OAuth → token-based delegated authorization (C).

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Federated Identity Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0017 — Federated Identity / Delegated identity  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A key BENEFIT of delegated identity management is that it:

- **A.** Removes all logging of access
- **B.** Eliminates the need for any authentication
- **C.** Allows identity/access administration to be distributed to appropriate parties while maintaining central policy
- **D.** Forces every user to share one password

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Delegated identity management distributes administrative responsibility (e.g., to departments or partners) while central governance/policy is maintained, improving scalability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Delegation does not remove authentication.
- D: Shared passwords destroy accountability and are not delegation.
- A: Logging remains essential; delegation does not remove it.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Delegated Identity Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0018 — Identity Management / Centralized vs decentralized  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A multinational company wants consistent access policy and a single point of administration for identities across all regions. Which access-control administration approach BEST fits?

- **A.** Centralized access control
- **B.** No access control at all
- **C.** Per-user self-administration of all rights
- **D.** Fully decentralized control with no coordination

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Centralized access control provides a single administration point and consistent policy enforcement across the organization, ideal for uniform governance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Fully decentralized control yields inconsistent policy and weaker governance for this goal.
- B: No access control is never acceptable.
- C: Self-administration of rights violates least privilege and separation of duties.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Identity Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0019 — Identity Management / Core IAM functions  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which sequence correctly orders the core IAM concepts for a subject interacting with a system?

- **A.** Authorization, identification, accountability, authentication
- **B.** Accountability, authorization, authentication, identification
- **C.** Authentication, accountability, identification, authorization
- **D.** Identification, authentication, authorization, accountability

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A subject is first identified, then authenticated (proves the identity), then authorized (granted access), with accountability (auditing) tracking actions throughout.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Authorization cannot precede identification/authentication.
- B: This reverses the logical order.
- C: Identification must come before authentication.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Identity Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0020 — Identity Proofing / Validation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The "validation" step of identity proofing confirms that:

- **A.** The submitted identity evidence is authentic and accurate
- **B.** The data center temperature is within range
- **C.** The applicant has logged out of all sessions
- **D.** The firewall rules are correctly ordered

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Validation confirms the identity evidence itself is genuine/accurate (e.g., a real, unexpired ID document), before verification links it to the applicant.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Logging out sessions is unrelated to proofing validation.
- B: Temperature is an environmental control concern.
- D: Firewall ordering is a network control, not proofing.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Proofing Process Steps*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0021 — Provisioning Lifecycle / Joiner-mover-leaver  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An employee transfers from Finance to Marketing. From an access-management standpoint, the BEST practice during this "mover" event is to:

- **A.** Disable the account permanently
- **B.** Grant the new role's access and remove the old role's access that is no longer needed
- **C.** Grant administrator rights for convenience
- **D.** Keep all Finance access and simply add Marketing access

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** On role change, access should be re-baselined to the new role—adding what is needed and removing now-unneeded access—to avoid privilege creep.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Retaining old access while adding new access causes privilege creep.
- A: A transferring employee still needs an active account for the new role.
- C: Granting admin rights violates least privilege.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Identity and Access Lifecycle*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0022 — Federated Identity / SAML components  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are core building blocks of SAML? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Bindings
- **B.** Fire classes
- **C.** Assertions
- **D.** Rainbow tables

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** SAML is structured around assertions (statements about a subject), protocols, and bindings (how messages are transported).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Rainbow tables are a cryptanalytic attack artifact.
- B: Fire classes are a physical-security concept.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > SAML Assertions, Protocols, and Bindings*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0023 — Identity Management / Distributed access control  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A drawback of a decentralized/distributed access control approach is that it:

- **A.** Eliminates the need for any identity store
- **B.** Always provides a single point of administration
- **C.** Guarantees uniform configuration everywhere
- **D.** Can lead to inconsistent policy enforcement across the organization

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Decentralized control distributes administration closer to resources but risks inconsistent policy and configuration across the organization.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A single administration point is a feature of centralized, not decentralized, control.
- A: An identity store is still required.
- C: Decentralization tends to produce variation, not guaranteed uniformity.

*Source: Domain 5 > Identity Management Introduction > Identity Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0024 — Authentication Factors / Type 1 (know)  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A password is an example of which authentication factor type?

- **A.** Type 4 — Somewhere you are
- **B.** Type 1 — Something you know
- **C.** Type 3 — Something you are
- **D.** Type 2 — Something you have

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A password is knowledge-based (Type 1 — something you know).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Type 2 is a possession such as a token or smart card.
- C: Type 3 is a biometric trait.
- A: Location is a contextual factor, not a primary Type.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Authentication Factor Types*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0025 — Authentication Factors / Type 2 (have)  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A hardware token or smart card represents which authentication factor type?

- **A.** Type 2 — Something you have
- **B.** Type 1 — Something you know
- **C.** A context-based factor
- **D.** Type 3 — Something you are

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A token or smart card is a possession-based factor (Type 2 — something you have).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Type 1 is knowledge (e.g., a password/PIN).
- D: Type 3 is a biometric.
- C: Context-based factors (e.g., location) are not Type 2.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Authentication Factor Types*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0026 — Authentication Factors / Type 3 (are)  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A fingerprint scan is which authentication factor type?

- **A.** Type 3 — Something you are
- **B.** Type 1 — Something you know
- **C.** Something you do
- **D.** Type 2 — Something you have

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A fingerprint is a biometric trait (Type 3 — something you are).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Type 1 is knowledge-based.
- D: Type 2 is possession-based.
- C: "Something you do" (e.g., keystroke dynamics) is a behavioral/contextual factor, not the physiological Type 3 fingerprint.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Authentication Factor Types*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0027 — Authentication Factors / MFA definition  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

For authentication to qualify as TRUE multi-factor authentication (MFA), the factors used must:

- **A.** Be entered on the same screen
- **B.** All be biometrics from the same finger
- **C.** Come from two or more DIFFERENT factor types (e.g., something you know + something you have)
- **D.** All be passwords of different lengths

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** MFA requires factors from different categories (knowledge, possession, inherence); combining two of the same type (e.g., two passwords) is not true MFA.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Two passwords are both Type 1—still single-factor in nature.
- B: Two biometrics are both Type 3, not multi-factor.
- A: Where factors are entered is irrelevant to whether they are different types.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Types of Authentication Factors*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0028 — Authentication Factors / 2FA scenario  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A login requires a password AND a one-time code from a hardware token. How many DISTINCT factor types are in use, and what is this called?

- **A.** Two types (know + have) — two-factor authentication
- **B.** Zero types — anonymous access
- **C.** Three types — multi-factor authentication
- **D.** One type — single-factor authentication

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A password (Type 1, know) plus a token code (Type 2, have) uses two different factor types—two-factor authentication.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Two different types is not single-factor.
- C: Only two types are used, not three.
- B: Authentication is occurring, so it is not anonymous.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Authentication Process Examples*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0029 — Authentication Factors / Factor-type mapping  _(medium, matching)_

Match each authentication factor category (1-4) to an example (A-D).

1. Something you know
2. Something you have
3. Something you are
4. Somewhere you are

- **A.** A password or PIN
- **B.** A login restricted to a trusted geographic location
- **C.** A fingerprint or iris scan
- **D.** A smart card or hardware token

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-D,3-C,4-B

**Why correct:** Know → password/PIN (B); Have → smart card/token (A); Are → fingerprint/iris (D); Somewhere you are → geographic/location-based (C).

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Authentication Factor Types*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0030 — Authentication Factors / Context-based  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Evaluating a login's geographic location and the time of day as part of the access decision is an example of:

- **A.** Single-factor knowledge authentication
- **B.** Context-based authentication
- **C.** Static packet filtering
- **D.** Key escrow

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Context-based authentication factors in attributes like location ("somewhere you are"), time, and behavior to strengthen or adapt access decisions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Knowledge-only authentication ignores contextual signals.
- C: Static packet filtering is a firewall technique.
- D: Key escrow is a key-management control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Context-Based Authentication*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0031 — Authentication Factors / MFA strength  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A bank wants to strengthen logins against stolen passwords. Which change provides the GREATEST improvement to authentication assurance?

- **A.** Add a second factor of a different type (e.g., a possession-based token or biometric)
- **B.** Require a longer password only
- **C.** Display the password as the user types it
- **D.** Ask the same security question twice

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Adding a different factor type (possession or inherence) means a stolen password alone is insufficient—materially raising assurance versus knowledge-only changes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A longer password is still a single knowledge factor that can be phished/stolen.
- D: Repeating a knowledge question adds no new factor type.
- C: Revealing the typed password weakens, not strengthens, security.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Key Exam Tips*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0032 — Biometrics / Physiological vs behavioral  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which of the following is a BEHAVIORAL biometric rather than a physiological one?

- **A.** Retina pattern
- **B.** Keystroke dynamics
- **C.** Iris pattern
- **D.** Fingerprint

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Keystroke dynamics (how a person types) is a behavioral biometric; fingerprints, iris, and retina are physiological traits.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A fingerprint is a physiological trait.
- C: Iris pattern is physiological.
- A: Retina pattern is physiological.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Behavioral Attributes*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0033 — Biometrics / FRR (Type 1 error)  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In a biometric system, the False Rejection Rate (FRR), also called a Type 1 error, occurs when the system:

- **A.** Incorrectly REJECTS a legitimate, authorized user
- **B.** Incorrectly ACCEPTS an unauthorized impostor
- **C.** Stores templates in plaintext
- **D.** Crashes during enrollment

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** FRR (Type 1 error) is a false rejection—an authorized user is wrongly denied; it harms usability/availability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Falsely accepting an impostor is the False Acceptance Rate (FAR, Type 2 error).
- D: A crash is a reliability failure, not FRR.
- C: Plaintext template storage is a protection issue, not FRR.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Biometric System Errors*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0034 — Biometrics / FAR (Type 2 error)  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which biometric error is generally the MOST dangerous from a security standpoint?

- **A.** False Rejection Rate (FRR / Type 1) — rejecting a valid user
- **B.** False Acceptance Rate (FAR / Type 2) — accepting an impostor
- **C.** Enrollment time
- **D.** Crossover Error Rate (CER)

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A false acceptance (FAR, Type 2) admits an unauthorized impostor, which is the more serious security failure; FRR mainly hurts usability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: FRR denies legitimate users—an inconvenience, not a breach.
- D: CER is a tuning metric (where FRR=FAR), not itself an error event.
- C: Enrollment time is a usability metric, not a security error.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Biometric System Errors*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0035 — Biometrics / CER/EER  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The Crossover Error Rate (CER), also called the Equal Error Rate (EER), is:

- **A.** The encryption strength of stored templates
- **B.** The time required to scan a fingerprint
- **C.** The maximum number of users a system can enroll
- **D.** The point where the False Rejection Rate equals the False Acceptance Rate; a lower CER indicates a more accurate system

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** CER/EER is the rate at which FRR equals FAR; a lower CER means the biometric system is more accurate overall and is used to compare/tune systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: CER is an accuracy metric, not an enrollment capacity.
- B: Scan time is a usability metric, not CER.
- A: Template encryption is a protection measure, not CER.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Crossover Error Rate (CER)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0036 — Biometrics / Tuning trade-off  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A high-security facility tunes its biometric scanner to be more strict. What is the LIKELY trade-off?

- **A.** Both FAR and FRR drop to zero automatically
- **B.** FAR decreases (fewer impostors accepted) but FRR increases (more legitimate users rejected)
- **C.** FAR increases and FRR decreases
- **D.** The CER is no longer relevant after tuning

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Tightening the threshold reduces false acceptances (lower FAR) at the cost of more false rejections (higher FRR)—the inherent biometric trade-off.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: No tuning eliminates both error types simultaneously.
- C: Stricter settings lower FAR, not raise it.
- D: CER remains a useful comparison metric regardless of tuning.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Crossover Error Rate (CER)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0037 — Biometrics / Behavioral biometrics  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are behavioral biometrics? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Voice pattern recognition
- **B.** Signature dynamics
- **C.** Fingerprint
- **D.** Iris scan

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** Signature dynamics and voice pattern recognition are behavioral biometrics; iris and fingerprint are physiological.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: An iris scan is a physiological biometric.
- C: A fingerprint is a physiological biometric.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Behavioral Attributes*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0038 — Directory Services / LDAP purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

LDAP (Lightweight Directory Access Protocol) is primarily used to:

- **A.** Encrypt files on a laptop
- **B.** Query and manage directory services that store identity/account information
- **C.** Provide backup power during outages
- **D.** Suppress fires in a server room

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** LDAP is used to access and manage directory services (e.g., Active Directory), which store users, groups, and other identity data.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: File encryption is unrelated to LDAP.
- D: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- C: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > LDAP (Lightweight Directory Access Protocol)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0039 — Directory Services / LDAP DN components  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In an LDAP Distinguished Name (DN), what does the "OU" component represent?

- **A.** Open Utility
- **B.** Object Username
- **C.** Outbound URL
- **D.** Organizational Unit

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** In an LDAP DN, OU = Organizational Unit (a container/subdivision), alongside CN (Common Name) and DC (Domain Component).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: "Object Username" is not an LDAP DN component.
- C: "Outbound URL" is not an LDAP DN component.
- A: "Open Utility" is not an LDAP DN component.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > LDAP (Lightweight Directory Access Protocol)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0040 — Directory Services / LDAP DN components  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In an LDAP Distinguished Name, the "CN" component typically identifies the:

- **A.** Country Number
- **B.** Common Name (e.g., a specific user or object)
- **C.** Cache Node
- **D.** Cipher Name

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** CN = Common Name, which identifies a specific entry such as a user or object within the directory.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: "Country Number" is not an LDAP DN component.
- D: "Cipher Name" is unrelated to LDAP DNs.
- C: "Cache Node" is not an LDAP DN component.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > LDAP (Lightweight Directory Access Protocol)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0041 — AAA / AAA framework  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The "AAA" framework in access management stands for:

- **A.** Authentication, Authorization, and Accounting
- **B.** Access, Allocation, and Accountability
- **C.** Availability, Assurance, and Auditing
- **D.** Authentication, Availability, and Anonymity

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** AAA stands for Authentication (who you are), Authorization (what you can do), and Accounting (recording what you did).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: These are not the AAA components.
- B: These are not the AAA components.
- D: These are not the AAA components.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > AAA (Authentication, Authorization, and Accounting)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0042 — AAA / RADIUS vs TACACS+  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A notable difference between TACACS+ and RADIUS is that TACACS+:

- **A.** Encrypts the entire payload and separates authentication, authorization, and accounting
- **B.** Is identical to RADIUS in every respect
- **C.** Sends all data in cleartext with no encryption
- **D.** Cannot be used for network device administration

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** TACACS+ encrypts the full payload and treats authentication, authorization, and accounting as separate functions; RADIUS encrypts only the password and combines authentication/authorization.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: TACACS+ encrypts the entire payload, not nothing.
- D: TACACS+ is commonly used for administering network devices.
- B: They differ in encryption scope and AAA separation.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > AAA (Authentication, Authorization, and Accounting)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0043 — AAA / Diameter  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Diameter is best described as:

- **A.** A biometric scanner type
- **B.** A physical security barrier around a facility
- **C.** A successor AAA protocol designed to improve upon RADIUS
- **D.** A fire suppression agent

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Diameter is an AAA protocol developed as an evolution/successor to RADIUS with enhanced capabilities.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Physical barriers are facility controls.
- A: Diameter is not a biometric scanner.
- D: It is not a fire suppression agent.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > AAA (Authentication, Authorization, and Accounting)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0044 — AAA / Accounting  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The "Accounting" component of AAA is most directly associated with which security goal?

- **A.** Confidentiality of data at rest
- **B.** Physical perimeter defense
- **C.** Accountability (recording and auditing user actions)
- **D.** Load balancing

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Accounting records user activities (e.g., session start/stop, commands), supporting accountability and auditing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Data-at-rest confidentiality is a separate control.
- B: Perimeter defense is a physical control.
- D: Load balancing is a performance/availability technique.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > AAA (Authentication, Authorization, and Accounting)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0045 — Authentication Factors / SFA risk  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Single-factor authentication (SFA) using only a password is considered weak primarily because:

- **A.** It cannot be used on modern systems
- **B.** It requires too many devices to log in
- **C.** It always locks out legitimate users
- **D.** A single stolen or guessed credential grants full access

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** With SFA, compromising the single factor (e.g., a phished password) is enough to gain access; MFA mitigates this.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: SFA requires fewer, not more, devices.
- C: SFA does not inherently lock out legitimate users.
- A: SFA is still widely (if unwisely) used on modern systems.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Types of Authentication Factors*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0046 — Biometrics / Physiological biometrics  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which of the following is a PHYSIOLOGICAL biometric?

- **A.** Keystroke dynamics
- **B.** Mouse-movement pattern
- **C.** Signature dynamics
- **D.** Retina scan

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A retina scan measures a physical (physiological) characteristic; the others are behavioral biometrics.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Keystroke dynamics is behavioral.
- C: Signature dynamics is behavioral.
- B: Mouse-movement patterns are behavioral.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Biometric Authentication*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0047 — Directory Services / LDAP DN concept  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In LDAP, a Distinguished Name (DN) serves to:

- **A.** Encrypt the directory database
- **B.** Provide backup power to the directory server
- **C.** Uniquely identify an entry's full path within the directory hierarchy
- **D.** Assign IP addresses to directory clients

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A DN uniquely identifies a directory entry by specifying its complete hierarchical path (e.g., CN=user,OU=Sales,DC=example,DC=com).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A DN names an entry; it does not encrypt the database.
- D: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- B: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > LDAP (Lightweight Directory Access Protocol)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0048 — Authentication Factors / Something you do  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Analyzing the unique rhythm and timing of how a user types their password reflects which contextual/behavioral factor?

- **A.** Somewhere you are
- **B.** Something you do (keystroke dynamics)
- **C.** Something you have
- **D.** Something you know

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Keystroke dynamics is a "something you do" behavioral factor based on how the user performs an action.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: "Something you have" is a possession factor.
- A: "Somewhere you are" is a location factor.
- D: "Something you know" is the password content itself, not the typing rhythm.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Context-Based Authentication*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0049 — Authentication Factors / Factor combinations  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO combinations qualify as TRUE multi-factor authentication? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Password + hardware token code
- **B.** Password + PIN
- **C.** Two different fingerprints
- **D.** Fingerprint + smart card

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Password (know) + token (have) and fingerprint (are) + smart card (have) each combine different factor types—true MFA.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Password + PIN are both knowledge factors (Type 1)—not MFA.
- C: Two fingerprints are both Type 3—not MFA.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Types of Authentication Factors*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0050 — Directory Services / Directory protocols  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Directory services (such as those queried by LDAP) primarily provide:

- **A.** A method to physically destroy hard drives
- **B.** A fire detection sensor array
- **C.** A centralized store of identity and resource information for authentication/authorization
- **D.** A backup generator control panel

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Directory services centrally store identities, groups, and resource information, supporting authentication and authorization decisions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Drive destruction is a sanitization activity.
- D: Generator control is a power-management function.
- B: Fire detection is a physical safety control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Authentication Systems*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0051 — AAA / RADIUS use  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

RADIUS is commonly used to provide centralized AAA for:

- **A.** Encrypting data at rest on servers
- **B.** Suppressing fires in data centers
- **C.** Translating private IPs to public IPs
- **D.** Remote/network access (e.g., VPN, Wi-Fi 802.1X) authentication

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** RADIUS centralizes authentication/authorization/accounting for remote and network access such as VPN and 802.1X Wi-Fi.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Data-at-rest encryption is a separate control.
- B: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- C: IP translation is NAT.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Key Terms to Know*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0052 — Single Sign-On / SSO concept  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Single Sign-On (SSO) primarily allows a user to:

- **A.** Use a different password for every application
- **B.** Avoid authenticating at all
- **C.** Encrypt all of their files automatically
- **D.** Authenticate once and gain access to multiple systems without re-entering credentials

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SSO lets a user authenticate a single time and then access multiple connected systems without repeatedly entering credentials.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Separate passwords per app is the opposite of SSO.
- B: SSO still requires authentication—just once.
- C: File encryption is unrelated to SSO.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Single Sign On*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0053 — Single Sign-On / SSO drawback  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company implements SSO across all internal applications. What is the PRIMARY security drawback the team must mitigate?

- **A.** Users must remember dozens of separate passwords
- **B.** It makes auditing user activity impossible
- **C.** Applications can no longer be accessed at all
- **D.** If the single credential is compromised, the attacker can reach all connected systems

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SSO concentrates risk: a compromised single credential unlocks every connected system, so it must be paired with strong authentication (e.g., MFA) and monitoring.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: SSO reduces the number of passwords—it does not increase them.
- C: SSO enables, not prevents, application access.
- B: SSO does not make auditing impossible; centralized logging is still possible.
