*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Single Sign On*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0054 — Single Sign-On / SSO mitigation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which control BEST mitigates the concentrated risk of SSO?

- **A.** Using one shared SSO account for the whole team
- **B.** Requiring multi-factor authentication for the SSO login
- **C.** Removing session timeouts entirely
- **D.** Disabling logging on the identity provider

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Because SSO concentrates access behind one login, protecting that login with MFA substantially reduces the impact of a stolen password.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Disabling logging removes the visibility needed to detect abuse.
- A: A shared account destroys accountability and worsens risk.
- C: Removing timeouts leaves sessions open to hijacking.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Single Sign On*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0055 — Kerberos / KDC components  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Kerberos Key Distribution Center (KDC) is composed of which two services?

- **A.** Authentication Server (AS) and Ticket Granting Service (TGS)
- **B.** Policy Engine and Policy Enforcement Point
- **C.** Resource Owner and Resource Server
- **D.** Identity Provider and Service Provider

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The KDC contains the Authentication Server (AS), which issues TGTs, and the Ticket Granting Service (TGS), which issues service tickets.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: IdP/SP are SAML federation roles.
- C: Resource Owner/Server are OAuth roles.
- B: PE/PEP are Zero Trust components.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Kerberos*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0056 — Kerberos / Operation flow  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Order the steps of the Kerberos authentication flow. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** TGS issues a Service Ticket
- **B.** Client uses the Service Ticket to access the service
- **C.** Client presents the TGT to the Ticket Granting Service (TGS)
- **D.** AS issues a Ticket Granting Ticket (TGT)
- **E.** Client authenticates to the Authentication Server (AS)

**Correct answer:** E,D,C,A,B

**Why correct:** The client authenticates to the AS, receives a TGT, presents the TGT to the TGS, receives a Service Ticket, and uses it to access the target service.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Kerberos Operation Flow*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0057 — Kerberos / TGT  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In Kerberos, a Ticket Granting Ticket (TGT) is used by the client to:

- **A.** Directly decrypt the target server's files
- **B.** Permanently disable the KDC
- **C.** Request service tickets from the Ticket Granting Service without re-entering the password
- **D.** Assign IP addresses to clients

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** After authenticating, the client holds a TGT that it presents to the TGS to obtain service tickets, enabling SSO without re-authenticating each time.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A TGT does not decrypt server files directly.
- D: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- B: A TGT does not disable the KDC.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Tickets*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0058 — Kerberos / KDC single point of failure  _(hard, scenario_based)_

In a Kerberos deployment, the KDC becomes unavailable. What is the MOST direct consequence, and what design lesson does it illustrate?

- **A.** Authentication fails (Kerberos fails secure); the KDC is a single point of failure that needs redundancy
- **B.** Existing files are permanently deleted
- **C.** Passwords are exposed in cleartext to all users
- **D.** All users automatically gain administrator rights

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** If the KDC is down/compromised, Kerberos fails secure (access is denied), making the KDC a critical single point of failure that should be made redundant and time-synchronized.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: KDC failure denies access; it does not grant admin rights.
- B: KDC failure does not delete files.
- C: KDC failure does not expose passwords in cleartext.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Kerberos Operation Flow*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0059 — Kerberos / Time synchronization  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Why must all systems in a Kerberos realm keep their clocks synchronized?

- **A.** It is required to assign MAC addresses
- **B.** Kerberos tickets are time-stamped; significant clock skew causes valid tickets to be rejected
- **C.** It encrypts the directory database
- **D.** Synchronization increases network bandwidth

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Kerberos uses time-stamped tickets (and tolerates only small skew) to resist replay; if clocks drift too far, authentication fails.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Time sync does not increase bandwidth.
- A: MAC assignment is hardware-based, not time-driven.
- C: Time sync does not encrypt databases.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Kerberos Operation Flow*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0060 — Kerberos / Kerberoasting  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A Kerberoasting attack works by:

- **A.** Sending unsolicited Bluetooth messages
- **B.** Physically stealing a smart card
- **C.** Requesting service tickets and cracking their hashes offline to recover service-account passwords
- **D.** Flooding the network with ICMP echo replies

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Kerberoasting requests service tickets (encrypted with the service account's key) and cracks the hashes offline to recover the service account's password.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: ICMP echo flooding is a Smurf/DoS attack.
- B: Stealing a smart card is a possession-factor theft, not Kerberoasting.
- A: Unsolicited Bluetooth messages are bluejacking.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Kerberos Operation Flow*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0061 — Kerberos / Component mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each Kerberos element (1-4) to its role (A-D).

1. Authentication Server (AS)
2. Ticket Granting Service (TGS)
3. Ticket Granting Ticket (TGT)
4. Service Ticket

- **A.** Issues service tickets when presented a valid TGT
- **B.** Verifies initial credentials and issues a TGT
- **C.** Proves the client authenticated, used to request service tickets
- **D.** Presented to a target service to gain access

**Correct answer:** 1-B,2-A,3-C,4-D

**Why correct:** AS → verifies credentials/issues TGT (B); TGS → issues service tickets (A); TGT → proves authentication, requests service tickets (D); Service Ticket → presented to the target service (C).

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Kerberos*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0062 — Federation Protocols / OAuth vs OIDC  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which statement correctly distinguishes OAuth 2.0 from OpenID Connect (OIDC)?

- **A.** OAuth 2.0 is for authentication; OIDC is only for encryption
- **B.** OIDC is older than OAuth and replaced it
- **C.** OAuth 2.0 is for delegated authorization; OIDC adds an identity (authentication) layer on top of OAuth 2.0
- **D.** They are unrelated and never used together

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** OAuth 2.0 handles delegated authorization (granting access via tokens); OIDC builds an authentication/identity layer on top of OAuth 2.0 (using REST/JSON, JWTs).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: OAuth is authorization-focused; OIDC adds authentication, not encryption only.
- D: OIDC is built directly on OAuth 2.0—they are closely related.
- B: OIDC came after and extends OAuth 2.0; it did not replace it.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > OAuth and OIDC*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0063 — Federation Protocols / OAuth purpose  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A user lets a photo-printing app access their cloud photos WITHOUT sharing their cloud password, by granting a scoped access token. Which standard enables this delegated access?

- **A.** WEP
- **B.** Kerberoasting
- **C.** CPTED
- **D.** OAuth 2.0

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** OAuth 2.0 enables delegated authorization: the app receives a scoped access token to act on the user's behalf without seeing the user's password.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: WEP is a wireless encryption protocol.
- B: Kerberoasting is an attack technique.
- C: CPTED is a physical site-design strategy.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > OAuth and OIDC*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0064 — Federation Protocols / OpenID  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

OpenID allows users to:

- **A.** Access multiple websites using a single set of credentials managed by a third-party provider
- **B.** Physically enter a secured facility
- **C.** Encrypt files on their local disk
- **D.** Route packets between autonomous systems

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** OpenID lets a third-party provider manage authentication credentials so users can sign in to many sites with one identity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: File encryption is unrelated to OpenID.
- B: Physical entry is a facility control.
- D: Packet routing is a network function.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > OpenID*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0065 — Federation Protocols / OAuth components  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In the OAuth framework, the application requesting access to protected resources on the user's behalf is the:

- **A.** Authorization Server
- **B.** Resource Server
- **C.** Resource Owner
- **D.** Client

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The OAuth client is the application requesting access; the resource owner is the user, the resource server hosts the data, and the authorization server issues tokens.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: The resource owner is the user who owns the data and grants access.
- B: The resource server hosts the protected resources.
- A: The authorization server issues access tokens.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > OAuth Workflow*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0066 — Federation Protocols / SSO via SAML  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In enterprise web SSO, SAML is commonly used to:

- **A.** Encrypt files at rest on endpoints
- **B.** Suppress fires in server rooms
- **C.** Provide backup power during outages
- **D.** Pass authentication/authorization assertions from an identity provider to web applications

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SAML conveys authentication/authorization assertions from the IdP to service providers (web apps), enabling federated web SSO.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: File encryption is a separate control.
- B: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- C: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0067 — Federation Protocols / JWT/OIDC  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

OpenID Connect commonly conveys identity claims using which token format?

- **A.** JSON Web Token (JWT)
- **B.** An LDAP DN
- **C.** A magnetic stripe
- **D.** A SYN-ACK packet

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** OIDC uses REST/JSON and conveys identity information in JSON Web Tokens (JWTs).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A magnetic stripe is a physical card medium, not a token format.
- B: An LDAP DN names a directory entry, not an OIDC token.
- D: A SYN-ACK is a TCP handshake message.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > OpenID Connect*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0068 — Credential Management / CMS purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A credential management system (password manager) primarily helps users by:

- **A.** Physically guarding the data center
- **B.** Assigning IP addresses to devices
- **C.** Securely storing and retrieving credentials so users can use strong, unique passwords
- **D.** Encrypting the entire corporate WAN

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A CMS securely stores credentials (often in an encrypted vault), enabling strong, unique passwords without users having to memorize them all.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A CMS does not encrypt the WAN.
- B: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- A: Physical guarding is a facility control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Credential Management Systems*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0069 — Credential Management / Credential vault  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A "credential vault" within a CMS is best described as:

- **A.** A physical safe for paper documents only
- **B.** A backup generator enclosure
- **C.** A secured, encrypted store that holds credentials and secrets
- **D.** A network switch port

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A credential vault is the secured, encrypted repository where a CMS stores credentials/secrets, protecting them from unauthorized access.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A generator enclosure is a power-equipment housing.
- A: A credential vault is digital, not a paper safe.
- D: A switch port is a network connection, not a secret store.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Attributes Stored in Credential Management Systems*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0070 — Credential Management / CMS risk  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization adopts a centralized credential vault. What is the KEY risk that must be tightly controlled?

- **A.** Users can no longer use any passwords
- **B.** It eliminates the need to ever rotate credentials
- **C.** The vault becomes a high-value single target; its master access must be strongly protected
- **D.** It prevents administrators from auditing access

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A centralized vault concentrates many secrets, making it a high-value target; access to it must be strongly protected (e.g., MFA, strict access controls, monitoring).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The vault enables, not prevents, password use.
- B: Credential rotation is still needed.
- D: A vault supports auditing rather than preventing it.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Credential Management Systems*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0071 — Privileged Access / JIT access  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Just-In-Time (JIT) access reduces standing privilege by:

- **A.** Disabling all access reviews
- **B.** Giving every user permanent administrator rights
- **C.** Granting elevated access only for the time it is needed, then automatically revoking it
- **D.** Sharing one admin account among the team

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** JIT access provisions elevated rights only when needed and for a limited window (often via ephemeral accounts/temporary elevation), shrinking the attack surface of standing privilege.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Permanent admin rights are exactly what JIT avoids.
- A: JIT complements, not eliminates, access reviews.
- D: Shared admin accounts destroy accountability.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Just-In-Time Access*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0072 — Privileged Access / Ephemeral accounts  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

An "ephemeral account" used in JIT access is one that:

- **A.** Has permanent domain administrator rights
- **B.** Can never be audited
- **C.** Is shared by all employees indefinitely
- **D.** Exists only temporarily for a specific task and is removed afterward

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Ephemeral accounts are created for a specific, short-lived purpose and removed when no longer needed, limiting standing privileged access.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Permanent admin rights contradict the ephemeral concept.
- B: Ephemeral accounts can and should be audited.
- C: They are single-purpose and temporary, not shared indefinitely.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Components of JIT Access*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0073 — Privileged Access / PAM  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Privileged Access Management (PAM) focuses primarily on:

- **A.** Controlling, monitoring, and securing the use of accounts with elevated privileges
- **B.** Assigning IP addresses to endpoints
- **C.** Suppressing fires in data centers
- **D.** Encrypting wireless traffic

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** PAM secures, controls, and monitors privileged/administrative accounts, which are high-value targets, often combined with JIT elevation and session recording.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- D: Wireless encryption is WPA2/WPA3.
- C: Fire suppression is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Privileged Access Management (PAM)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0074 — Privileged Access / PAM scenario  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

A security team wants admins to have NO standing privileged access. Which TWO practices BEST help achieve zero standing privilege with accountability? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Sharing a single root password among the team
- **B.** PAM with privileged session monitoring/recording
- **C.** Giving every admin a permanent domain-admin account
- **D.** Just-In-Time elevation granted only on request for a limited time

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** JIT elevation removes standing privilege by granting time-bound access on request, and PAM with session monitoring provides control and accountability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Permanent domain-admin accounts are exactly the standing privilege to eliminate.
- A: A shared root password destroys accountability and is a severe risk.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Implementation and Best Practices*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0075 — Privileged Access / Temporary elevation  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO practices align with Just-In-Time privileged access? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Permanent standing admin rights for all
- **B.** Automatic revocation after the task completes
- **C.** Sharing privileged credentials broadly
- **D.** Time-bound temporary elevation of privileges

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** JIT access grants temporary, time-bound elevation and revokes it automatically when the task is done.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Permanent standing rights are the opposite of JIT.
- C: Sharing privileged credentials undermines accountability and least privilege.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Components of JIT Access*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0076 — Single Sign-On / SSO technologies  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which of the following is a common technology used to implement SSO?

- **A.** Kerberos
- **B.** Wet-pipe sprinklers
- **C.** CPTED
- **D.** Degaussing

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Kerberos is a common SSO technology (along with SAML and OIDC); it issues tickets so users authenticate once.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Degaussing destroys magnetic media.
- C: CPTED is a physical site-design strategy.
- B: Wet-pipe sprinklers are fire suppression.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Common SSO Technologies*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0077 — Federation Protocols / SAML vs OAuth use  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An enterprise needs employees to SSO into internal SaaS web apps with assertions of who they are. A separate consumer app needs to let users authorize limited access to their data via tokens. Which pairing of standards fits BEST?

- **A.** Kerberoasting for SSO; CPTED for authorization
- **B.** RADIUS for SSO; degaussing for authorization
- **C.** SAML for enterprise web SSO; OAuth 2.0 for delegated authorization
- **D.** OAuth 2.0 for enterprise SSO; WEP for delegated authorization

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SAML is well-suited to enterprise web SSO (identity assertions), while OAuth 2.0 is designed for delegated authorization via access tokens.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: WEP is a wireless cipher, not an authorization framework.
- A: Kerberoasting is an attack and CPTED is physical design.
- B: RADIUS is network AAA and degaussing destroys media—neither fits.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > OAuth and OIDC*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0078 — Kerberos / Mutual authentication  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A security strength of Kerberos is that it:

- **A.** Transmits passwords in cleartext for speed
- **B.** Stores all credentials on every client
- **C.** Provides mutual authentication and avoids sending passwords across the network
- **D.** Eliminates the need for any time source

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Kerberos supports mutual authentication and uses tickets/keys so user passwords are not transmitted across the network in the clear.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Kerberos specifically avoids sending cleartext passwords.
- D: Kerberos depends on synchronized time.
- B: Credentials are managed by the KDC, not stored on every client.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Kerberos*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0079 — Credential Management / CMS best practice  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which is a sound security practice when deploying a credential management system?

- **A.** Allow anonymous access to the vault
- **B.** Store the master password on a public web page
- **C.** Disable encryption to speed up retrieval
- **D.** Protect the vault with strong authentication (e.g., MFA) and restrict/monitor access

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The vault holds many secrets, so it must be protected with strong authentication, least-privilege access, encryption, and monitoring.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Publishing the master password defeats the vault entirely.
- C: Disabling encryption exposes all stored secrets.
- A: Anonymous access would expose every stored credential.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Credential Management Systems*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0080 — Federation Protocols / OAuth token  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

OAuth 2.0 authorizes a client to access resources by issuing it a(n):

- **A.** WPA2 pre-shared key
- **B.** Kerberos service ticket
- **C.** LDAP distinguished name
- **D.** Access token with a defined scope

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** OAuth issues scoped access tokens that the client presents to the resource server to access only the permitted resources.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Service tickets belong to Kerberos, not OAuth.
- C: An LDAP DN names a directory entry.
- A: A WPA2 PSK is a wireless key.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > OAuth and OIDC*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0081 — Single Sign-On / SSO expense  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Besides the security risk, which is a noted practical DRAWBACK of implementing SSO?

- **A.** Users must memorize more passwords
- **B.** It cannot be combined with MFA
- **C.** Integration complexity and expense across many applications
- **D.** It removes the need for an identity provider

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SSO can be costly and complex to integrate across diverse applications, which is a practical drawback noted in the guide.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: SSO reduces the number of passwords.
- B: SSO can and should be combined with MFA.
- D: SSO relies on an identity provider, not removes it.

*Source: Domain 5 > Authentication Mechanisms > Single Sign On*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0082 — Access Control Models / DAC  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In Discretionary Access Control (DAC), access decisions are based on:

- **A.** Predefined firewall port/protocol rules
- **B.** The object OWNER granting permissions to subjects, typically via an ACL
- **C.** Security labels and clearances enforced by a central authority
- **D.** A calculated real-time risk score

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** In DAC, the object owner determines who may access the object, usually through an Access Control List (ACL); it is flexible but offers weaker, identity-based control.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Labels/clearances enforced centrally describe MAC.
- D: A real-time risk score describes risk-based access control.
- A: Port/protocol rules describe rule-based access control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Discretionary Access Control (DAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0083 — Access Control Models / MAC  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Mandatory Access Control (MAC) enforces access using:

- **A.** A list of allowed TCP ports
- **B.** The discretion of the individual data owner
- **C.** Security labels on subjects and objects compared against the subject's clearance
- **D.** Group/role membership only

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** MAC is non-discretionary: access is determined by comparing security labels/classifications on objects against the subject's clearance, common in military/highly sensitive environments.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Owner discretion describes DAC.
- D: Role/group membership describes RBAC.
- A: Allowed ports describe rule-based access control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Mandatory Access Control (MAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0084 — Access Control Models / RBAC  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) grants access based on:

- **A.** A subject's assigned role or group, helping reduce privilege creep
- **B.** The object owner's personal discretion
- **C.** A real-time geolocation risk score
- **D.** Security labels and clearances

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** RBAC assigns permissions to roles/groups and users inherit them via role membership, streamlining administration and reducing privilege creep—useful for high-turnover environments.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Labels/clearances describe MAC.
- B: Owner discretion describes DAC.
- C: A geolocation risk score describes risk-based access control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0085 — Access Control Models / ABAC  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) makes decisions based on:

- **A.** Multiple attributes of the subject, object, action, and environment
- **B.** Only the subject's single role
- **C.** Only the destination port number
- **D.** Only the object owner's preference

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** ABAC (also called policy-based access control) evaluates combinations of subject, object, action, and environmental attributes (e.g., clearance, department, time, IP), enabling fine-grained policies often via XACML.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Owner-only preference describes DAC.
- B: A single role describes RBAC, which is coarser than ABAC.
- C: Port-only decisions describe rule-based access control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0086 — Access Control Models / Rule-based  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Rule-based access control, as used in devices like firewalls, grants or denies access based on:

- **A.** A user's job title alone
- **B.** The subject's biometric template
- **C.** The object owner's discretion
- **D.** Predefined rules such as port number and protocol

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Rule-based access control applies predefined rules/restrictions (e.g., allow HTTP on port 80) regardless of user identity, common in firewalls and IPS.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Biometric templates relate to authentication, not rule-based ACLs.
- C: Owner discretion describes DAC.
- A: Job-title/role-based access is RBAC.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Rule-Based Access Control*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0087 — Access Control Models / Risk-based (RAdAC)  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A user logs in from an unrecognized IP on an untrusted network, so the system requires additional authentication factors before granting access. Which access control model is this?

- **A.** Mandatory access control
- **B.** Rule-based access control
- **C.** Discretionary access control
- **D.** Risk-based (risk-adaptive) access control

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Risk-based (RAdAC) access control dynamically adjusts requirements based on a calculated risk level (device, location, sensitivity), e.g., demanding MFA for higher-risk logins.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: DAC relies on owner-set permissions, not dynamic risk.
- A: MAC uses static labels/clearances.
- B: Rule-based uses fixed rules, not adaptive risk scoring.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Risk-Based Access Control*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0088 — Access Control Models / Model selection  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A defense agency must enforce that data classified "Top Secret" is only accessible to subjects with Top Secret clearance, with no owner discretion allowed. Which model is MOST appropriate?

- **A.** Discretionary Access Control (DAC)
- **B.** Mandatory Access Control (MAC)
- **C.** Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)
- **D.** Rule-Based Access Control

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** MAC enforces non-discretionary access using labels/clearances—ideal for classified environments where owners cannot override the policy.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: DAC allows owner discretion, which the requirement forbids.
- C: RBAC groups by role but does not enforce clearance/label dominance like MAC.
- D: Rule-based controls (e.g., port rules) do not enforce classification clearance.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Mandatory Access Control (MAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0089 — Access Control Models / RBAC turnover  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A call center with very high staff turnover wants to simplify granting and revoking access as employees join and leave. Which access control model BEST fits?

- **A.** MAC — assign clearances and labels to every file
- **B.** DAC — let each employee set their own permissions
- **C.** RBAC — assign access to roles, then add/remove users from roles
- **D.** Risk-based — recompute risk on every keystroke

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** RBAC streamlines administration in high-turnover environments: access is tied to roles, so onboarding/offboarding is just role assignment/removal.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: DAC self-set permissions would be chaotic and inconsistent at scale.
- A: MAC labeling every file is heavy and aimed at classified data, not call-center turnover.
- D: Per-keystroke risk scoring is unnecessary overhead for this need.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0090 — Access Control Models / Model-to-basis mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each access control model (1-4) to the basis for its access decisions (A-D).

1. DAC
2. MAC
3. RBAC
4. ABAC

- **A.** Owner discretion via ACLs (identity-based)
- **B.** Multiple attributes (subject, object, action, environment)
- **C.** Role or group membership
- **D.** Security labels/clearances set by a central authority

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-D,3-C,4-B

**Why correct:** DAC → owner discretion/ACLs (B); MAC → labels/clearances (A); RBAC → role/group membership (D); ABAC → multiple attributes (C).

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Control Models*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0091 — Access Control Models / DAC weakness  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A noted security weakness of DAC compared to MAC is that DAC:

- **A.** Is only usable on wireless networks
- **B.** Cannot use access control lists at all
- **C.** Relies on individual owners' discretion, making consistent, fine-grained control harder to guarantee
- **D.** Always requires a security clearance

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Because DAC delegates access decisions to object owners, enforcement can be inconsistent and harder to control centrally—weaker than label-driven MAC for sensitive data.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: DAC specifically uses ACLs.
- D: Clearances are a MAC concept, not required by DAC.
- A: DAC is not limited to wireless networks.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Discretionary Access Control (DAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0092 — Access Control Models / Non-discretionary  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In non-discretionary access control, access rights are determined by:

- **A.** A central authority or policy, not the individual object owner
- **B.** The destination MAC address only
- **C.** The current temperature of the server room
- **D.** Each user for their own files

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Non-discretionary models (e.g., MAC, RBAC) have a central authority/policy set access rights, rather than leaving them to object owners (DAC).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Per-user discretion is DAC, which is discretionary.
- B: MAC-address-only decisions are a Layer 2 filter, not this concept.
- C: Room temperature is an environmental control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Non-Discretionary Access Control*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0093 — Access Control Models / ABAC attributes  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are categories of attributes that ABAC can evaluate? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Subject attributes (e.g., clearance, department)
- **B.** The font used in the document
- **C.** The color of the user's desk
- **D.** Environmental attributes (e.g., time, IP address)

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** ABAC evaluates subject, object, action, and environmental attributes; clearance/department (subject) and time/IP (environmental) are valid examples.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Document font is not an access-control attribute.
- C: Desk color is irrelevant to access decisions.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0094 — Authorization / Implicit deny  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The principle of "implicit deny" means that:

- **A.** All users get administrator access
- **B.** Access is allowed by default unless explicitly denied
- **C.** Logging is turned off by default
- **D.** Access is denied by default unless it is explicitly granted

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Implicit deny ("permit by exception, deny by default") denies any access not explicitly allowed, a secure default.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Allow-by-default is the opposite (and insecure) approach.
- A: Implicit deny restricts, not grants, broad access.
- C: Implicit deny concerns access, not logging configuration.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0095 — Authorization / Permissions/rights/privileges  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In the guide's authorization terminology, "privileges" are best defined as:

- **A.** Only the subject's ability to access an object
- **B.** The combination of a subject's permissions and rights
- **C.** A list of all denied users
- **D.** Only the subject's authority to perform specific actions

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Privileges are the combination of permissions (ability to access an object) and rights (authority to perform actions).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Ability to access an object alone is a permission.
- D: Authority to perform actions alone is a right.
- C: A deny list is not the definition of privileges.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0096 — Authorization / Access control matrix vs capability table  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

How does a capability table differ from an access control matrix?

- **A.** A capability table is object-focused and a matrix is subject-focused
- **B.** They are identical in every way
- **C.** A capability table is a physical security barrier
- **D.** A capability table is subject-focused (lists what a subject can access), while an ACL/matrix is object-focused

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A capability table lists a subject's capabilities (subject-focused); an access control list/matrix is organized by object (object-focused). They are complementary views.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: This reverses the focus of each.
- B: They present different (subject vs object) views.
- C: A capability table is a logical construct, not a physical barrier.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0097 — Authorization / Content vs context-dependent  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A database hides specific salary columns from users not in HR based on the data being viewed. This is an example of which control?

- **A.** Physical access control
- **B.** Context-dependent control (based on the sequence/situation of actions)
- **C.** Content-dependent control (based on the type/content of the object)
- **D.** Rule-based firewall control

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Content-dependent controls restrict access based on the actual content/type of the object (e.g., specific sensitive columns), independent of the request sequence.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Context-dependent controls depend on the situation/sequence of actions, not the data content itself.
- D: A firewall rule filters network traffic, not database column content.
- A: Physical access control governs facility entry, not data columns.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Content-Dependent vs. Context-Dependent Controls*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0098 — Authorization / Constrained interface  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A constrained interface restricts access by:

- **A.** Encrypting the network connection
- **B.** Physically locking the server rack
- **C.** Limiting the actions/options available based on the subject's privilege level
- **D.** Assigning IP addresses to users

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A constrained interface only exposes the functions/options a subject is privileged to use, implementing least privilege at the interface.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Connection encryption is a transport control, not interface constraint.
- D: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- B: Locking a rack is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0099 — Authorization / Separation of duties  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Separation of duties as an authorization principle is designed to:

- **A.** Divide critical tasks among multiple people to prevent any one person having excessive control
- **B.** Remove the need for least privilege
- **C.** Allow users to approve their own requests
- **D.** Give one administrator complete control for efficiency

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Separation of duties splits sensitive tasks so no single individual can complete a critical process alone, reducing fraud and error.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Concentrating control in one person defeats separation of duties.
- C: Self-approval undermines the control.
- B: SoD complements, not replaces, least privilege.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Authorization Principles*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0100 — Authorization / Least privilege  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Least privilege in authorization means providing subjects with:

- **A.** Administrator rights by default
- **B.** The maximum access in case it is needed later
- **C.** Access to all data in the organization
- **D.** Only the access rights necessary to perform their role

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Least privilege grants only the access necessary for a subject's duties, limiting the impact of compromise or misuse.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Granting extra "just in case" access violates least privilege.
- A: Default admin rights are excessive.
- C: All-data access ignores need-to-know and least privilege.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Authorization Principles*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0101 — Authorization / Need to know  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A user has the technical permission to open a shared folder but should not see one specific file unrelated to their duties. Restricting that file enforces:

- **A.** Open design
- **B.** Implicit allow
- **C.** Mandatory vacation
- **D.** Need to know

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Need to know restricts access to specific information to only those who require it for their duties, even where broader permissions exist.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: "Implicit allow" is insecure and not the principle here.
- C: Mandatory vacation is a fraud-detection control.
- A: Open design is a secure-design principle, not access scoping.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Authorization Principles*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0102 — Authorization / Authorization principles  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are authorization principles emphasized in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Separation of duties
- **B.** Implicit allow
- **C.** Least privilege
- **D.** Security through obscurity

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** Separation of duties and least privilege (with need-to-know) are core authorization principles.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Security through obscurity is discouraged, not an authorization principle.
- B: "Implicit allow" is insecure; the secure default is implicit deny.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Authorization Principles*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0103 — Accountability / Definition  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Accountability in access management ensures that:

- **A.** Passwords are stored in plaintext
- **B.** Subjects can be held responsible for their actions, supporting non-repudiation
- **C.** Networks never experience downtime
- **D.** All data is encrypted at rest

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Accountability tracks and verifies subjects' actions so they can be held responsible, supporting non-repudiation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Data-at-rest encryption is a separate control.
- C: Accountability does not guarantee uptime.
- A: Plaintext password storage is an insecure practice, not accountability.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Accountability*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0104 — Accountability / Implementation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which mechanism is MOST fundamental to implementing accountability?

- **A.** Removing the identity provider
- **B.** Turning off encryption
- **C.** Disabling all user accounts
- **D.** System logging/auditing of subject actions

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Logging and auditing of actions are fundamental to accountability—recording who did what, when, to support investigation and non-repudiation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Disabling accounts prevents work, not enables accountability.
- A: Removing the identity provider would undermine authentication and tracking.
- B: Disabling encryption is unrelated and insecure.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Implementing Accountability*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0105 — Accountability / Log protection  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An attacker who gains admin access tries to delete the audit logs to hide their actions. Which control BEST preserves accountability?

- **A.** Disabling logging during maintenance windows
- **B.** Storing logs on the same account the admin controls
- **C.** Allowing all users to edit logs
- **D.** Protecting logs from tampering (e.g., write-once/remote log storage with restricted access)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Protecting logs from tampering—e.g., forwarding to a secured/append-only remote log store with strict access—preserves accountability even if an endpoint is compromised.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Storing logs where the admin can alter them defeats accountability.
- A: Disabling logging creates blind spots attackers exploit.
- C: Allowing anyone to edit logs destroys their integrity.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Implementing Accountability*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0106 — Accountability / Non-repudiation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Accountability supports non-repudiation by:

- **A.** Assigning dynamic IP addresses
- **B.** Suppressing fires in the data center
- **C.** Encrypting the corporate WAN
- **D.** Linking actions to authenticated subjects so they cannot credibly deny performing them

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** By tying logged actions to authenticated identities, accountability prevents subjects from denying actions they performed (non-repudiation).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: WAN encryption is unrelated to non-repudiation linkage.
- A: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- B: Fire suppression is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Key Benefits*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0107 — Session Management / Session timeout  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A session timeout improves security by:

- **A.** Automatically ending an inactive session and requiring re-authentication
- **B.** Encrypting the user's files
- **C.** Permanently disabling the user account
- **D.** Assigning a new IP address

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Session timeouts end inactive sessions after a set period, reducing the risk of an unattended, still-authenticated session being hijacked.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A timeout ends a session; it does not disable the account.
- B: Timeouts do not encrypt files.
- D: Timeouts do not assign IP addresses.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Session Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0108 — Session Management / Session ID  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A session ID is used to:

- **A.** Bind a user's authenticated credentials to an active session
- **B.** Provide backup power
- **C.** Resolve domain names
- **D.** Encrypt the hard drive

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A session ID ties the user's credentials/state to an active session, and must be protected (e.g., against hijacking/fixation).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Disk encryption is a separate control.
- B: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- C: Name resolution is DNS.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Session Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0109 — Session Management / Session hijacking defense  _(hard, scenario_based)_

To reduce the risk of session hijacking on a web application, which combination is MOST effective?

- **A.** Reuse the same session ID for all users
- **B.** Embed the user password in every URL
- **C.** Disable HTTPS to improve speed
- **D.** Use unpredictable session IDs over TLS and enforce idle/absolute session timeouts

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Strong, random session IDs transmitted over TLS, combined with idle/absolute timeouts, significantly reduce hijacking/fixation risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Putting passwords in URLs exposes them in logs/history.
- A: Reusing one session ID for all users is catastrophic.
- C: Disabling HTTPS exposes session tokens to interception.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Session Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0110 — Logical Access / Subject vs object  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In access control terminology, a "subject" is:

- **A.** A backup generator
- **B.** A passive resource being accessed
- **C.** A fire suppression system
- **D.** An active entity (user, program, or process) that requests access to an object

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A subject is the active entity (user, program, script, service) that acts on an object (the passive resource being accessed).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A passive resource being accessed is the object, not the subject.
- C: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- A: A generator provides backup power.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Subjects and Objects in Logical Access*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0111 — Logical Access / Roles interchange  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A web server requests data from a database, then returns it to a user's browser. Regarding the database transaction, the web server is acting as the ____.

- **A.** Object (it is the passive resource)
- **B.** Subject (it is the active entity requesting the object/data)
- **C.** Reference monitor
- **D.** Identity provider

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Subject/object roles are relative: when the web server requests data from the database, the server is the active subject and the database is the object. Roles interchange based on the access.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: In this transaction the database (not the web server) is the passive object.
- C: A reference monitor mediates access; it is not the requesting server here.
- D: An identity provider issues identity assertions, unrelated to this DB request.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Subjects and Objects in Logical Access*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0112 — Logical Access / Logical access goals  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The goals of controlling logical access align directly with which security model?

- **A.** The CIA triad (confidentiality, integrity, availability)
- **B.** The fire triangle (oxygen, heat, fuel)
- **C.** The TCP three-way handshake
- **D.** The OSI model layers

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Logical access control aims to prevent unauthorized disclosure (confidentiality), maintain authorized-only changes (integrity), and ensure availability for authorized subjects—the CIA triad.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: The fire triangle is a physical-safety concept.
- D: The OSI model describes networking layers.
- C: The TCP handshake establishes a connection.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Goals in Controlling Access*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0113 — Access Control Models / DAC ACL  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

DAC commonly tracks which subjects may access an object using a(n):

- **A.** Fire suppression plan
- **B.** Access Control List (ACL)
- **C.** UPS battery
- **D.** Subnet mask

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** DAC uses Access Control Lists (ACLs) to record which subjects have which permissions on an object.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A fire suppression plan is a physical-safety document.
- D: A subnet mask defines network ranges.
- C: A UPS battery provides backup power.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Discretionary Access Control (DAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0114 — Access Control Models / MAC environments  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

MAC is most commonly used in environments handling:

- **A.** Highly sensitive data such as national security, PII, or PHI
- **B.** Anonymous guest Wi-Fi
- **C.** Disposable test data with no value
- **D.** Public marketing brochures only

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** MAC's rigid label/clearance enforcement suits highly sensitive environments (military/government, regulated PII/PHI).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Public brochures need little access control.
- B: Guest Wi-Fi does not require MAC labeling.
- C: Disposable test data does not warrant MAC overhead.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Mandatory Access Control (MAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0115 — Authorization / Object-focused control  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An access control list (ACL) attached to a file is an example of which type of access view?

- **A.** Object-focused (lists which subjects can access the object)
- **B.** Biometric enrollment
- **C.** Risk-based scoring
- **D.** Subject-focused (lists a subject's capabilities)

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** An ACL is organized by the object, listing which subjects have which permissions on it (object-focused); a capability table is subject-focused.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A subject-focused list is a capability table, not an ACL.
- C: Risk-based scoring is a dynamic model, not an ACL view.
- B: Biometric enrollment is an authentication process.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0116 — Accountability / Audit value  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Besides assigning responsibility, robust accountability/auditing can also:

- **A.** Replace the need for authentication
- **B.** Reveal design vulnerabilities that allow unauthorized actions
- **C.** Encrypt all stored data automatically
- **D.** Eliminate the need for backups

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Reviewing accountability data can expose gaps/vulnerabilities in system design that permit unauthorized actions, informing improvements.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Auditing complements, not replaces, authentication.
- C: Auditing does not encrypt data.
- D: Backups remain necessary for availability.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Key Benefits*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0117 — Access Control Models / Privilege creep  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

A long-tenured employee has accumulated access from every past role (privilege creep). Which TWO practices BEST prevent this? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Periodic access reviews/recertification
- **B.** Granting permanent administrator rights to all staff
- **C.** Disabling all audit logging
- **D.** Role-based access tied to current duties, removing old access on transfer

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Periodic access reviews and RBAC tied to current roles (removing old access at transfer) prevent the accumulation of unneeded access (privilege creep).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Disabling logging removes visibility and worsens security.
- B: Permanent admin rights for all is excessive standing privilege, the opposite of the goal.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0118 — Authorization / Rights definition  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In authorization terms, a "right" refers to a subject's:

- **A.** Choice of web browser
- **B.** Network bandwidth allocation
- **C.** Physical office location
- **D.** Authority to conduct specific actions (e.g., shut down a system)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A right is the authority to perform specific actions; permissions concern access to objects, and privileges combine the two.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Office location is not an authorization right.
- A: Browser choice is irrelevant to authorization rights.
- B: Bandwidth allocation is a network resource matter.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0119 — Access Control Models / Rule-based use  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Rule-based access control is MOST commonly seen in which type of device?

- **A.** Biometric fingerprint readers
- **B.** Firewalls and intrusion prevention systems
- **C.** Backup generators
- **D.** HVAC units

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Rule-based access control (allow/deny by predefined rules like port/protocol) is characteristic of firewalls and IPS.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Fingerprint readers perform biometric authentication.
- C: Generators provide backup power.
- D: HVAC units manage environment, not access rules.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Rule-Based Access Control*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0120 — Authorization / Deny by default scenario  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A new application is configured so that any access not explicitly permitted is blocked. A user with no assigned permissions tries to open a record and is denied. This reflects which principle?

- **A.** Implicit allow
- **B.** Mandatory vacation
- **C.** Implicit deny (deny by default)
- **D.** Privilege creep

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Blocking anything not explicitly permitted is implicit deny ("permit by exception, deny by default")—a secure default.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Implicit allow would have granted the access.
- B: Mandatory vacation is a fraud-detection control.
- D: Privilege creep is accumulation of excess access, not a default-deny outcome.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0121 — Access Control Models / ABAC vs RBAC  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Compared to RBAC, ABAC provides FINER-grained control primarily because it can:

- **A.** Eliminate the need for any policy
- **B.** Only ever consider the user's role
- **C.** Ignore the object's sensitivity
- **D.** Factor in many attributes (e.g., time, location, device) beyond a single role

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** ABAC evaluates multiple attributes (subject, object, action, environment), enabling more granular, context-aware decisions than role-only RBAC.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Considering only a role describes RBAC, which is coarser.
- C: ABAC can use object sensitivity as an attribute.
- A: ABAC is policy-driven; it does not remove the need for policy.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0122 — Accountability / Accountability example  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which activity BEST demonstrates accountability in action?

- **A.** Logs that record which user accessed and deleted a sensitive file
- **B.** A larger UPS battery
- **C.** A faster internet connection for the office
- **D.** A brighter parking-lot light

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Recording who accessed/modified/deleted sensitive data is accountability in action—enabling investigation and non-repudiation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Bandwidth is unrelated to accountability.
- D: Lighting is a physical/CPTED control.
- B: A UPS supports availability, not action attribution.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Accountability*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0123 — Session Management / Re-authentication  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

After a session times out due to inactivity, the user must:

- **A.** Wait for the network to reboot
- **B.** Reinstall the operating system
- **C.** Re-authenticate to start a new session
- **D.** Change their MAC address

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A timed-out session requires re-authentication before a new session is established, limiting unauthorized use of an abandoned session.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The network does not need to reboot for re-authentication.
- B: No OS reinstall is required.
- D: MAC address changes are unrelated to session re-authentication.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Session Management*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0124 — Access Control Models / Model overview  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The overall purpose of any access control model is to govern:

- **A.** The color scheme of the user interface
- **B.** The brand of network cabling used
- **C.** How a subject is permitted to interact with an object
- **D.** The office seating arrangement

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Access control models define how subjects (users, devices, processes) are authorized to interact with objects (files, services, functions).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: UI color is a cosmetic choice.
- B: Cabling brand is a procurement detail.
- D: Seating arrangement is unrelated to logical access control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Control Models*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0125 — Authorization / Privilege definition  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A "permission," in authorization terms, refers to a subject's:

- **A.** Authority to perform any action anywhere
- **B.** Biometric template
- **C.** Physical clearance badge
- **D.** Ability to access an object

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A permission is the ability to access an object; combined with rights (authority to act), they form privileges.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Authority to perform actions is a "right," not a permission.
- C: A clearance badge is a physical credential, not the logical permission concept.
- B: A biometric template is authentication data, not a permission.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Access Authorization*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0126 — Access Control Models / Risk-based factors  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO contextual factors might a risk-based access control system evaluate? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Geolocation of the request
- **B.** Device type/trust
- **C.** The office wall color
- **D.** The user's favorite food

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** Risk-based access control uses contextual data such as device trust and geolocation (plus network attributes and object sensitivity) to calculate risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Personal food preferences are irrelevant to access risk.
- C: Wall color is irrelevant to access decisions.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Risk-Based Access Control*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0127 — Logical Access / Access definition  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Logical access control manages a subject's ability to:

- **A.** Park in the company garage
- **B.** Physically enter the building lobby
- **C.** Adjust the thermostat in the lobby
- **D.** Open, modify, execute, or delete data and resources

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Logical access control governs privileges to open, modify, execute, or delete data/resources, protecting confidentiality, integrity, and availability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Physical entry is a physical access control.
- C: Thermostat control is an environmental matter.
- A: Parking is a physical/facility concern.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Controlling Logical Access*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0128 — Authorization / SoD example  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A finance process is designed so the person who creates a vendor payment cannot also approve it. This control is:

- **A.** Implicit allow
- **B.** Session timeout
- **C.** Separation of duties
- **D.** Single sign-on

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Requiring different people to create and approve a payment is separation of duties, preventing one individual from completing a sensitive transaction alone.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: SSO is an authentication convenience, not a duty split.
- A: Implicit allow is an insecure default, unrelated here.
- B: A session timeout ends inactive sessions; it is not duty separation.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Authorization Principles*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0129 — Access Control Models / Identity-based  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

DAC is often described as "identity-based" access control because access depends on:

- **A.** The current network risk score
- **B.** The classification label of the data only
- **C.** The firewall rule set
- **D.** The identity of the individual subject and the owner's granted permissions

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** DAC ties access to the subject's identity and the owner-granted permissions (via ACLs), hence "identity-based."

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Classification-label enforcement describes MAC.
- A: Network risk scoring describes risk-based access control.
- C: Firewall rule sets describe rule-based access control.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Discretionary Access Control (DAC)*

---

### CISSP-D05-Q0130 — Accountability / Strong access controls  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

To support accountability, access to logs and sensitive files should be governed by:

- **A.** No authentication during business hours
- **B.** Anonymous, unrestricted access
- **C.** A single shared account for convenience
- **D.** Strong authentication and least-privilege access controls

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Strong authentication and least privilege over logs/sensitive files protect their integrity and ensure actions are attributable—supporting accountability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Anonymous access destroys attribution.
- C: Shared accounts eliminate individual accountability.
- A: Removing authentication breaks accountability.

*Source: Domain 5 > Access and Authorization > Implementing Accountability*

---


## Domain 6: Security Assessment and Testing

### CISSP-D06-Q0001 — Assessment Strategy / Test vs assessment vs audit  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which statement BEST distinguishes a security audit from a security test?

- **A.** A test is performed only by external regulators
- **B.** A test always checks compliance while an audit attacks the system
- **C.** An audit focuses on verifying compliance with governance/regulations and reporting on posture; a test exercises a control to see if it works
- **D.** An audit and a test are identical activities

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A security audit evaluates compliance with governance, regulations, and overall security posture (reporting status and risk impact); a test exercises specific controls to verify they function.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: This reverses the roles—audits emphasize compliance verification.
- D: Audits and tests serve different purposes.
- A: Tests can be performed internally or by hired testers, not only regulators.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Security Test and Assessment Overview*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0002 — Security Audits / Internal audit  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An internal security audit is characterized by being:

- **A.** Performed by an outside firm and reported to regulators only
- **B.** Prohibited before an external audit
- **C.** Performed by internal personnel and reported to senior management
- **D.** Always more objective than an external audit

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Internal audits are conducted by internal staff and reported to senior management, often as a self-audit before an external audit.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: That describes an external/third-party audit.
- D: Internal audits are generally LESS objective than independent external audits.
- B: Internal self-audits are commonly performed before external audits.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Types of Security Audits*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0003 — Security Audits / External vs third-party  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A business partner hires an outside firm to audit a vendor and have the results reported back to the partner (not the vendor) for an acquisition decision. This is BEST described as a:

- **A.** Internal audit
- **B.** Penetration test
- **C.** Third-party audit
- **D.** Self-assessment

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A third-party audit is performed by an outside team on behalf of another organization, with results reported to that organization—common for compliance or acquisition due diligence.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Internal audits are run by the audited org's own staff and report to its management.
- D: A self-assessment is performed by the org on itself.
- B: A penetration test simulates attacks, not a compliance audit reported to a third party.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Types of Security Audits*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0004 — Security Audits / External audit value  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The PRIMARY value of an external audit (conducted by an outside specialist firm) is that it:

- **A.** Is always cheaper than an internal audit
- **B.** Requires no scope definition
- **C.** Provides an unbiased, independent perspective
- **D.** Eliminates the need for any internal controls

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** External audits provide an independent, unbiased perspective, reporting to executive officers.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: External audits are often more costly, not cheaper.
- B: Scope must still be defined for any audit.
- D: Audits assess controls; they do not remove the need for them.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Types of Security Audits*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0005 — Performance Indicators / KRI  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A Key Risk Indicator (KRI) differs from a Key Performance Indicator (KPI) in that a KRI:

- **A.** Measures only past financial profit
- **B.** Measures potential failure or ineffectiveness in security controls (forward-looking risk)
- **C.** Can only be expressed as a color code
- **D.** Is identical to a KPI in every way

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A KRI measures the potential for failure/ineffectiveness of controls—signaling rising risk—whereas a KPI measures performance against objectives.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: KRIs concern security-control risk, not just financial profit.
- D: KRIs and KPIs measure different things (risk vs performance).
- C: KRIs are not limited to color codes.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Key Risk Indicators (KRIs)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0006 — Performance Indicators / KPI  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Key Performance Indicator (KPI) in security assessment is BEST used to:

- **A.** Define the office floor plan
- **B.** Predict the exact date of the next breach
- **C.** Measure how well security activities are meeting defined objectives/targets
- **D.** Replace all logging and monitoring

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** KPIs measure performance against goals/targets (e.g., percentage of systems patched on time), helping gauge program effectiveness.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: KPIs measure performance, not predict exact breach dates.
- D: KPIs complement, not replace, monitoring.
- A: Floor plans are unrelated to KPIs.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Performance and Risk Indicators*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0007 — Measurement Standards / NIST 800-55 / ISO 27004  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which pair of standards provides guidance on security measurement and metrics?

- **A.** PCI DSS and HIPAA
- **B.** NIST SP 800-88 and ISO 11889
- **C.** WPA2 and WPA3
- **D.** NIST SP 800-55 and ISO 27004

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-55 and ISO 27004 both address security measurement, metrics, and evaluation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: NIST SP 800-88 is media sanitization; ISO 11889 is TPM.
- A: PCI DSS/HIPAA are compliance regulations, not measurement guides.
- C: WPA2/WPA3 are wireless encryption protocols.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Measurement Standards and Guides*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0008 — Data Collection / Triple constraint  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A team must collect assessment data but has limited time and budget. The guide frames this trade-off using the triple constraint of:

- **A.** Confidentiality, integrity, and availability
- **B.** Red, blue, and purple teams
- **C.** People, process, and technology
- **D.** Scope, time, and cost (which together affect data quality)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The triple constraint of scope, time, and cost governs the quality of collected assessment data—expanding one usually pressures the others.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: People/process/technology is a different model, not the triple constraint here.
- A: CIA is the security triad, not the project triple constraint.
- B: Red/blue/purple are testing teams, not constraints.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Collecting Security Data*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0009 — Data Collection / Account management data  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Reviewing account management data during an assessment primarily helps verify that:

- **A.** The data center temperature is correct
- **B.** The office has enough parking
- **C.** The wireless encryption is WPA3
- **D.** Account creation, modification, and removal follow policy (e.g., no orphaned or excessive accounts)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Account management data (identities and authenticators across the account lifecycle) lets assessors verify proper creation, modification, and auditing of accounts—catching orphaned or over-privileged accounts.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Temperature is an environmental control concern.
- C: Wireless encryption is a network control, not account data.
- B: Parking is unrelated to account management.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Account Management Data*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0010 — Data Collection / Management approval  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Before conducting security assessment data collection, the team should FIRST obtain:

- **A.** Management approval and support for the assessment
- **B.** A new set of office furniture
- **C.** A larger UPS battery
- **D.** Permission from external attackers

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Assessments require management approval and support to authorize scope, access, and resources.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Furniture is irrelevant to assessment authorization.
- D: Attackers do not authorize assessments.
- C: A UPS is a power device, not assessment authorization.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Collecting Security Data*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0011 — DR Testing / Test types order  _(medium, sequence_ordering)_

Order the disaster recovery test types from LEAST disruptive/intensive to MOST disruptive. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Checklist (read-through) test
- **B.** Parallel test
- **C.** Structured walkthrough (tabletop)
- **D.** Full interruption test
- **E.** Simulation test

**Correct answer:** A,C,E,B,D

**Why correct:** DR tests escalate from a checklist review, to a tabletop walkthrough, to a simulation, to a parallel test (no impact to live ops), to a full interruption test (production is impacted).

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Disaster Recovery Test Types and Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0012 — DR Testing / Parallel test  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A company wants to validate that its recovery site can run production workloads WITHOUT taking the live production environment offline. Which DR test fits BEST?

- **A.** Checklist (read-through) test
- **B.** Structured walkthrough (tabletop)
- **C.** Parallel test
- **D.** Full interruption test

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A parallel test runs recovery procedures/site in parallel without impacting live operations—validating recovery capability with low risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A full interruption test deliberately impacts production, which the requirement forbids.
- A: A checklist test only reviews documents, not actual recovery operations.
- B: A tabletop exercise discusses the plan but does not run the recovery site.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Disaster Recovery Test Types and Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0013 — DR Testing / Full interruption test  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which DR test is the MOST thorough but also the RISKIEST, because production operations are actually moved to the backup site?

- **A.** Checklist test
- **B.** Full interruption test
- **C.** Parallel test
- **D.** Simulation test

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A full interruption test moves production to the backup site, impacting live operations—most realistic and thorough but highest risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A parallel test does not impact live operations.
- D: A simulation walks through a scenario without full production cutover.
- A: A checklist test only reviews documentation.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Disaster Recovery Test Types and Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0014 — DR Testing / Tabletop exercise  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A structured walkthrough (tabletop) DR test consists of:

- **A.** Team members reviewing the DR plan together in a meeting to identify issues
- **B.** Sending invalid data to an application to find bugs
- **C.** Scanning the network for open ports
- **D.** Actually failing over production to a cloud site

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A structured walkthrough/tabletop exercise gathers the team to review the DR plan and identify gaps—discussion-based, no live failover.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Failing over production is a full interruption test.
- B: Sending invalid data is fuzz testing.
- C: Port scanning is a vulnerability-scanning activity.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Disaster Recovery Test Types and Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0015 — Backups / Differential backup  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A differential backup copies:

- **A.** The entire system every time
- **B.** Nothing; it only verifies existing backups
- **C.** Only data modified since the last full OR incremental backup
- **D.** All data modified since the last FULL backup

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A differential backup copies everything changed since the last full backup (so differentials grow until the next full backup).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Copying changes since the last full OR incremental describes an incremental backup.
- A: Backing up everything each time describes a full backup.
- B: A differential backup copies changed data; it is not merely a verification.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Backup Types and Testing Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0016 — Backups / Incremental backup  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization runs a full backup on Sunday and incremental backups each weeknight. To FULLY restore data after a Thursday failure, which sets are needed?

- **A.** Any single incremental backup
- **B.** Only Thursday's incremental backup
- **C.** The Sunday full backup plus every incremental from Monday through Thursday
- **D.** Only the Sunday full backup

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Incrementals capture only changes since the last full or incremental, so a full restore needs the full backup plus the complete chain of incrementals up to the failure.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A single incremental lacks all prior changes since the full backup.
- D: The full backup alone misses changes made during the week.
- A: One incremental is insufficient; the entire chain is required.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Backup Types and Testing Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0017 — Backups / Full backup  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A full backup is:

- **A.** A scan for open network ports
- **B.** A copy of only the files changed since yesterday
- **C.** A complete copy of all system and user data
- **D.** A read-through review of the backup plan

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A full backup captures a complete copy of all objects and user data, enabling a complete restore from one set.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Copying only recent changes describes incremental/differential backups.
- D: A read-through review is a checklist DR test.
- A: Port scanning is a vulnerability-assessment activity.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Backup Types and Testing Requirements*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0018 — Backups / Backup verification  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

During a security assessment, the MOST important thing to verify about backups is that they:

- **A.** Can actually be restored, and are retained/encrypted/archived per policy
- **B.** Are never tested to avoid wear
- **C.** Use the brightest-colored tapes
- **D.** Are stored on the same server as production data

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Backups must be tested for successful restoration and verified for retention, encryption, and proper archiving—an untested backup is unreliable.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Storing backups with production defeats their disaster-recovery purpose.
- B: Backups must be tested; untested backups may fail when needed.
- C: Tape color is irrelevant to backup integrity.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Backup Data Testing and Verification*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0019 — Data Collection / Training verification  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Collecting security training and awareness data during an assessment helps the organization:

- **A.** Eliminate the need for any technical controls
- **B.** Guarantee no employee will ever click a phishing link
- **C.** Replace the disaster recovery plan
- **D.** Track compliance with training requirements and identify gaps for emerging risks
