**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Training/awareness data verifies who has completed required training, tracks compliance, and reveals gaps relative to emerging risks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Training complements, not replaces, technical controls.
- B: Training reduces but cannot guarantee zero risky behavior.
- C: Training data does not replace DR planning.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Verifying Training and Awareness*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0020 — DR Testing / Checklist test  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A checklist (read-through) DR test involves:

- **A.** Distributing the DR checklist to team members to review and provide feedback
- **B.** Exploiting vulnerabilities with Metasploit
- **C.** Cutting over production to the backup site
- **D.** Encrypting all backup tapes

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A checklist/read-through test distributes the DR plan/checklist for review and feedback—the least disruptive DR test.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Production cutover is a full interruption test.
- B: Exploitation with Metasploit is a penetration-testing activity.
- D: Encrypting tapes is a backup-handling control, not a DR test type.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Disaster Recovery Test Types and Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0021 — Data Collection / Sources of data  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are valid sources of measurement data for a security assessment? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** The CEO's favorite color
- **B.** Risk assessment/analysis results
- **C.** The cafeteria menu
- **D.** Vulnerability assessment outcomes categorized by severity

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** Risk assessment results and severity-categorized vulnerability outcomes (plus incident/recovery timelines) are valid measurement-data sources.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Personal color preference is not assessment data.
- C: The cafeteria menu is irrelevant.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Sources of Measurement Data*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0022 — Security Audits / Audit report content  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A security audit report typically includes:

- **A.** Only the source code of every application
- **B.** The office Wi-Fi password
- **C.** Compliance status and the perceived risk impact
- **D.** A list of employee salaries

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Audit reports communicate compliance status against governance/regulations and the perceived risk impact of findings.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Audits report on controls/compliance, not full source code.
- D: Salaries are not audit-report content.
- B: Publishing the Wi-Fi password would be a security risk, not audit content.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Security Audit*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0023 — Assessment Strategy / Define scope  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A critical FIRST step when building a security test and assessment strategy is to:

- **A.** Delete all existing logs
- **B.** Immediately exploit production systems
- **C.** Disable monitoring to reduce noise
- **D.** Define the scope of what will be assessed

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Defining the scope establishes what systems, controls, and objectives the assessment covers, guiding all subsequent activities.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Exploiting production without scope/authorization is reckless and unauthorized.
- A: Deleting logs destroys evidence and accountability.
- C: Disabling monitoring reduces visibility during assessment.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Security Test and Assessment Strategy*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0024 — DR Testing / Simulation test  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A simulation DR test is BEST described as:

- **A.** A scan of all open TCP ports
- **B.** A structured walkthrough applied to a specific disaster scenario, evaluating results and corrective actions
- **C.** A static analysis of application source code
- **D.** A complete production cutover to the cloud

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A simulation test applies a structured walkthrough to a specific scenario (e.g., natural or man-made disaster), then evaluates results and corrective actions—without full production impact.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Full production cutover is a full interruption test.
- C: Static source-code analysis is SAST, not a DR test.
- A: Port scanning is a vulnerability-assessment activity.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Disaster Recovery Test Types and Considerations*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0025 — Data Collection / Account lifecycle audit  _(hard, scenario_based)_

During an assessment, an auditor finds several active accounts belonging to employees who left months ago. This finding MOST directly indicates a weakness in:

- **A.** The account deprovisioning/auditing part of the account management lifecycle
- **B.** The data center cooling system
- **C.** The fire suppression system
- **D.** The wireless encryption protocol

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Lingering accounts for departed staff indicate failures in account auditing/deprovisioning within the account management lifecycle—an access risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Cooling is an environmental control, unrelated to orphaned accounts.
- D: Wireless encryption does not govern account removal.
- C: Fire suppression is a physical safety control.

*Source: Domain 6 > Security Test and Assessment > Account Management Lifecycle*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0026 — Vulnerability Assessment / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The PRIMARY purpose of a vulnerability assessment is to:

- **A.** Identify and categorize weaknesses so they can be prioritized and remediated
- **B.** Exploit weaknesses to gain full control of systems
- **C.** Encrypt all data on the network
- **D.** Permanently eliminate all risk

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A vulnerability assessment identifies and categorizes weaknesses (often by severity) to reduce risk exposure—it does not exploit them (that is penetration testing).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Exploiting weaknesses is penetration testing, not a vulnerability assessment.
- D: No assessment eliminates all risk.
- C: Assessment finds weaknesses; it does not perform encryption.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Vulnerability Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0027 — Vulnerability Assessment / Assessment vs pen test  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Management wants to know which weaknesses exist on its servers but does NOT want testers to actually break in or disrupt operations. Which activity fits BEST?

- **A.** A breach and attack simulation against production
- **B.** A full interruption DR test
- **C.** A vulnerability assessment/scan
- **D.** A full-scope penetration test with exploitation

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A vulnerability assessment/scan identifies and reports weaknesses without exploiting them, matching the requirement to avoid intrusion/disruption.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A penetration test actively exploits weaknesses, which management excluded.
- B: A full interruption test disrupts production—out of scope here.
- A: BAS exercises attack techniques, which may be more intrusive than wanted.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Vulnerability Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0028 — Vulnerability Sources / CVE/NVD  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The MITRE CVE list and the National Vulnerability Database (NVD) primarily provide:

- **A.** Standardized identifiers and details for publicly known vulnerabilities
- **B.** Physical access badges for the data center
- **C.** Encryption keys for production systems
- **D.** A backup power supply for scanners

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** CVE provides unique identifiers for publicly known vulnerabilities, and the NVD enriches them with details/scoring—both are key vulnerability information sources.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- C: CVE/NVD do not provide encryption keys.
- B: Badges are physical access controls.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Sources for Vulnerability Information*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0029 — SCAP / Purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Security Content Automation Protocol (SCAP), created by NIST, primarily exists to:

- **A.** Provide backup power to scanners
- **B.** Replace the need for any vulnerability scanning
- **C.** Encrypt wireless traffic
- **D.** Standardize how vulnerability and configuration information is expressed and reported

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SCAP standardizes the format/exchange of security content (vulnerabilities, configuration checks, scoring) so tools interoperate and reporting is consistent.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Backup power is unrelated to SCAP.
- B: SCAP standardizes scanning content; it does not replace scanning.
- C: Wireless encryption is WPA2/WPA3, not SCAP.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Security Content Automation Protocol (SCAP)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0030 — SCAP / CVSS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Within SCAP, the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) is used to:

- **A.** Uniquely name a hardware/software platform
- **B.** Assign a severity score to a vulnerability to help prioritize remediation
- **C.** List the open ports on a host
- **D.** Encrypt the scan results

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** CVSS provides a numeric severity score (e.g., 0–10) for vulnerabilities, helping teams prioritize which to fix first.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Naming a platform is the role of CPE (Common Platform Enumeration).
- C: Listing open ports is a function of a port scan, not CVSS.
- D: CVSS scores severity; it does not encrypt results.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > SCAP Identification Schemes*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0031 — SCAP / CPE  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Common Platform Enumeration (CPE) within SCAP is used to:

- **A.** Provide standardized names for hardware, operating systems, and applications
- **B.** Score the severity of a vulnerability
- **C.** Map a network's physical cabling
- **D.** Define interactive checklist questions

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** CPE is a standardized naming scheme for platforms (hardware, OS, applications), enabling consistent identification across tools.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Severity scoring is CVSS.
- D: Interactive checklist questions are OCIL.
- C: CPE does not map physical cabling.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > SCAP Identification Schemes*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0032 — SCAP / Components  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO of the following are SCAP components? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** SYN flood
- **B.** XCCDF
- **C.** WPA3
- **D.** OVAL

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** XCCDF (configuration checklists) and OVAL (vulnerability/assessment language) are SCAP components, along with OCIL, CPE, CVE, and CVSS.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: WPA3 is a wireless encryption protocol.
- A: A SYN flood is a DoS attack technique.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Key SCAP Components*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0033 — Vulnerability Scanning / Credentialed vs non-credentialed  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A team needs to verify exact patch levels and detailed configurations on servers. Which scan type provides this deeper insight, and what does it require?

- **A.** A credentialed (authenticated) scan, which requires privileged login access
- **B.** A non-credentialed scan, which needs no access at all
- **C.** A SYN scan, which only checks port state
- **D.** A discovery scan, which only finds live hosts

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A credentialed/authenticated scan logs in with privileged access to reveal patch levels and detailed configurations—deeper than a non-credentialed scan.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A non-credentialed scan only checks general settings/communications with limited depth.
- D: A discovery scan finds active hosts, not detailed patch levels.
- C: A SYN scan determines port status, not configuration/patch detail.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Scans*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0034 — Vulnerability Scanning / Non-credentialed scan  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A non-credentialed vulnerability scan is characterized by:

- **A.** Logging in as root to read every configuration file
- **B.** Checking general settings/communications with read-only access and limited depth
- **C.** Requiring physical access to each server
- **D.** Always taking longer than a credentialed scan

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A non-credentialed scan inspects externally visible settings/communications with read-only access—faster but shallower than a credentialed scan.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Root login for deep config reading describes a credentialed scan.
- D: Non-credentialed scans are typically faster, not slower.
- C: Scans are network-based; they do not require physical access.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Scans*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0035 — Vulnerability Scanning / Discovery scan  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A discovery scan is primarily used to:

- **A.** Encrypt host communications
- **B.** Score vulnerabilities with CVSS
- **C.** Exploit a vulnerability to gain shell access
- **D.** Detect active hosts across an IP range/subnet to maintain an inventory

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A discovery scan targets an IP address/range/subnet to find live hosts, useful for maintaining an accurate host inventory.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Exploiting for shell access is penetration testing.
- B: CVSS scoring is a separate severity step.
- A: Discovery scans identify hosts; they do not encrypt communications.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Discovery Scan*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0036 — Vulnerability Scanning / Port status  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In scan results, a port reported as "filtered" generally means:

- **A.** The port is definitely open and accepting connections
- **B.** A firewall or filter is blocking the scanner from determining the port's true state
- **C.** The port is physically disconnected
- **D.** The host does not exist

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A filtered (unknown/filtered) result indicates a firewall/filter is preventing the scanner from confirming whether the port is open or closed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: An open port would be reported as "open," not filtered.
- C: Filtering is a logical block, not a physical disconnection.
- D: A non-existent host would not return port results at all.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Port Status Results in Discovery Scans*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0037 — Packet Scans / XMAS scan  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

An XMAS scan sends packets with which characteristic to probe a target's response?

- **A.** A completed three-way handshake on every port
- **B.** Multiple TCP flags set (e.g., FIN, PSH, URG) "lighting up" the packet
- **C.** No flags and an empty payload only
- **D.** Only the SYN flag set

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** An XMAS scan sets several TCP flags (commonly FIN, PSH, URG) at once—"lit up like a Christmas tree"—to elicit responses that reveal port state while sometimes evading basic logging.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A completed handshake on every port describes a TCP Connect scan.
- D: Only the SYN flag set describes a SYN scan.
- C: A no-flags packet describes a NULL scan, not XMAS.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Packet Scans in Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0038 — Packet Scans / SYN scan  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A SYN (half-open) scan is often preferred over a full TCP Connect scan because it:

- **A.** Always completes the full handshake and logs every connection
- **B.** Does not complete the three-way handshake, making it stealthier and faster
- **C.** Encrypts the scan traffic end to end
- **D.** Requires privileged credentials on the target

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A SYN scan sends SYN and interprets the response without completing the handshake (sending RST instead of ACK), making it faster and less likely to be fully logged than a TCP Connect scan.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Completing the handshake/logging describes a TCP Connect scan.
- D: A SYN scan does not require target credentials.
- C: A SYN scan does not encrypt its traffic.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Packet Scans in Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0039 — Packet Scans / TCP Connect scan  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A TCP Connect scan differs from a SYN scan in that it:

- **A.** Requires the XMAS flag combination
- **B.** Never receives any response from the target
- **C.** Can only scan UDP services
- **D.** Completes the full three-way handshake, which is more easily logged

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A TCP Connect scan fully establishes the connection (completes the handshake), which is reliable but more likely to be logged than a half-open SYN scan.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: It does receive handshake responses; that is how it confirms open ports.
- C: TCP Connect scans target TCP services, not only UDP.
- A: XMAS is a different scan technique.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Packet Scans in Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0040 — Vulnerability Scanning / Best practices  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A vulnerability scanning best practice is to:

- **A.** Run scans once at install and never again
- **B.** Keep scanners updated with the latest signatures/plugins and schedule scans appropriately
- **C.** Use an outdated scanner to avoid false positives
- **D.** Disable scanning during business hours permanently

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Scanners must be kept updated (new signatures/plugins) and scans planned/scheduled (e.g., incremental scanning) to remain effective against current threats.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: One-time scanning misses newly disclosed vulnerabilities.
- D: Scans should be scheduled thoughtfully, not permanently disabled.
- C: Outdated scanners miss new vulnerabilities; updates are essential.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Best Practices for Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0041 — Vulnerability Scanning / Signature-based  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A signature-based vulnerability scanner detects issues by:

- **A.** Comparing system characteristics against a database of known vulnerability signatures
- **B.** Encrypting files to test resilience
- **C.** Randomly guessing passwords until one works
- **D.** Physically inspecting cabling

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Signature-based scanners match observed system attributes against a database of known vulnerability signatures—so the signature database must stay current.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Password guessing is a brute-force attack, not signature scanning.
- B: Encrypting files is not how scanners detect vulnerabilities.
- D: Cabling inspection is a physical activity, not a scan.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Vulnerability Scanning Process*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0042 — Vulnerability Scanning / Scan scope risk  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A vulnerability scan against a fragile legacy system causes it to crash. Which practice would BEST reduce this risk in the future?

- **A.** Plan/schedule scans and test scan intensity in a non-production environment first
- **B.** Remove all logging so crashes are not recorded
- **C.** Always run the most aggressive scan settings on production
- **D.** Stop scanning the environment entirely

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Careful planning/scheduling and validating scan intensity against test/non-production systems reduces the chance of crashing fragile production hosts.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Aggressive settings on production increase crash risk.
- D: Stopping all scanning leaves the environment unassessed.
- B: Removing logging hides problems and harms accountability.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Best Practices for Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0043 — Vulnerability Assessment / Vulnerability management process  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

After a scan identifies vulnerabilities, the NEXT logical step in a vulnerability management process is to:

- **A.** Delete the scanner
- **B.** Immediately ignore all findings
- **C.** Prioritize the findings (e.g., by severity) and remediate or mitigate them
- **D.** Publish all findings publicly with full exploit details

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Vulnerability management continues by prioritizing findings (often by severity/CVSS) and remediating or mitigating them, then re-scanning to verify.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Ignoring findings leaves the organization exposed.
- A: Deleting the scanner removes a needed capability.
- D: Publicly releasing exploit details before remediation is reckless and unethical.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Vulnerability Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0044 — Packet Scans / UDP scan  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

UDP scans are often slower and less reliable than TCP scans because UDP:

- **A.** Always completes a three-way handshake
- **B.** Encrypts every packet by default
- **C.** Is connectionless, so the absence of a reply is ambiguous (open or filtered)
- **D.** Cannot be scanned at all

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** UDP is connectionless—no handshake—so a lack of response is ambiguous (open|filtered), making UDP scans slower and harder to interpret.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: UDP has no handshake (that is TCP).
- B: UDP does not encrypt by default.
- D: UDP can be scanned, just less reliably.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Packet Scans in Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0045 — SCAP / OVAL  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Within SCAP, OVAL (Open Vulnerability and Assessment Language) is primarily used to:

- **A.** Carry voice traffic over IP
- **B.** Score the severity of a vulnerability numerically
- **C.** Provide a standardized platform name
- **D.** Express system states/tests to assess for vulnerabilities and configuration issues

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** OVAL provides a standardized language to describe system states and tests used to assess machines for vulnerabilities and configuration issues.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Numeric severity scoring is CVSS.
- C: Standardized platform naming is CPE.
- A: Voice over IP is VoIP, unrelated to OVAL.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Key SCAP Components*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0046 — Vulnerability Sources / US-CERT/vendor  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are valid sources of vulnerability information? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** The MITRE CVE database
- **B.** The company holiday calendar
- **C.** The office cafeteria menu
- **D.** Vendor security advisories

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Vendor security advisories and the MITRE CVE database (plus the NVD and US-CERT) are key sources of vulnerability information.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A cafeteria menu is irrelevant to vulnerabilities.
- B: A holiday calendar is unrelated.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Sources for Vulnerability Information*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0047 — Vulnerability Scanning / Incremental scanning  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Incremental scanning is a best practice that helps by:

- **A.** Scanning the entire enterprise in one pass during peak hours
- **B.** Scanning only printers and nothing else
- **C.** Breaking large scans into manageable segments to reduce load and improve coverage over time
- **D.** Never updating the scanner

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Incremental scanning divides scanning into smaller segments, reducing impact and making thorough coverage more manageable.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A single massive peak-hours scan risks disruption.
- D: Scanners must be updated regularly.
- B: Limiting scans to printers would miss most systems.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Best Practices for Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0048 — Vulnerability Scanning / Open port result  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A scan result of "open" for a port indicates that:

- **A.** The host is powered off
- **B.** A service is listening and accepting connections on that port
- **C.** The port is blocked by a firewall
- **D.** The cable is unplugged

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** An "open" port means a service is actively listening/accepting connections, which may represent an attack surface to evaluate.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A firewall-blocked port would show as filtered.
- A: A powered-off host would not return open ports.
- D: An unplugged cable is a physical-layer issue, not an "open" result.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Port Status Results in Discovery Scans*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0049 — SCAP / OCIL  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

OCIL (Open Checklist Interactive Language) within SCAP is used for:

- **A.** Expressing checks that require human interaction/questionnaire responses
- **B.** Naming platforms uniquely
- **C.** Routing packets between subnets
- **D.** Numerically scoring vulnerability severity

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** OCIL standardizes interactive checks (e.g., questionnaire-style items requiring human input) within SCAP content.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Severity scoring is CVSS.
- B: Platform naming is CPE.
- C: Packet routing is a network function.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Key SCAP Components*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0050 — Vulnerability Scanning / ACK scan  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

An ACK scan is most useful for:

- **A.** Completing a full three-way handshake to log connections
- **B.** Assigning IP addresses to hosts
- **C.** Determining whether a firewall is stateful and how it filters (mapping rule sets)
- **D.** Encrypting traffic to the target

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** An ACK scan sends ACK packets to probe firewall behavior—helping determine whether a firewall is stateful and how ports are filtered—rather than simply finding open services.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Completing the handshake describes a TCP Connect scan.
- D: An ACK scan does not encrypt traffic.
- B: IP assignment is DHCP's function.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Packet Scans in Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0051 — Vulnerability Assessment / Assessment cadence  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO practices improve the value of an ongoing vulnerability scanning program? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Scheduling scans to balance coverage and impact
- **B.** Disabling authentication so scans run faster
- **C.** Ignoring all medium/low findings forever
- **D.** Regularly updating scanner signatures

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Keeping signatures current and scheduling scans to balance coverage with operational impact strengthen a scanning program.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Disabling authentication reduces depth and harms security; credentialed scans are valuable.
- C: Permanently ignoring lower-severity findings lets risk accumulate.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Best Practices for Vulnerability Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0052 — SCAP / Tool interoperability  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A key benefit of using SCAP-compatible tools is that they:

- **A.** Eliminate the need for any patching
- **B.** Guarantee zero false positives
- **C.** Provide physical security for the data center
- **D.** Can exchange and consume standardized security content consistently

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SCAP compatibility lets tools share standardized content (CVE, CPE, CVSS, OVAL, etc.), improving consistency and interoperability across the security toolchain.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: SCAP supports identifying issues; patching is still required.
- B: No tool guarantees zero false positives.
- C: SCAP is about content standards, not physical security.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > SCAP Identification Schemes*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0053 — Vulnerability Assessment / Severity prioritization  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A scan returns hundreds of findings. With limited remediation capacity, which approach is BEST?

- **A.** Fix findings in alphabetical order by hostname
- **B.** Do nothing until every finding can be fixed at once
- **C.** Patch only the lowest-severity issues first
- **D.** Prioritize remediation by severity and exposure (e.g., critical, internet-facing first)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** With limited capacity, prioritize by severity (e.g., CVSS) and exposure—remediating critical, internet-facing vulnerabilities first to reduce the greatest risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Alphabetical order ignores risk.
- C: Fixing low-severity first leaves critical risks open.
- B: Waiting to fix everything at once leaves critical risks unaddressed indefinitely.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Vulnerability Assessments*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0054 — Penetration Testing / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

How does a penetration test differ from a vulnerability assessment?

- **A.** A penetration test never requires authorization
- **B.** A penetration test actively exploits vulnerabilities to demonstrate real risk
- **C.** A penetration test only reviews documentation
- **D.** A penetration test only lists weaknesses without testing them

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A penetration test simulates an attack and exploits vulnerabilities to demonstrate actual, achievable risk—going beyond merely identifying weaknesses.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Listing weaknesses without exploiting is a vulnerability assessment.
- A: Pen tests require authorization (rules of engagement).
- C: Document review is an audit/checklist activity, not exploitation.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0055 — Penetration Testing / Rules of engagement  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Before any penetration test begins, the single MOST important document to establish is the:

- **A.** Rules of engagement (defining scope, methods, objectives, and authorization)
- **B.** Office seating chart
- **C.** Marketing brochure
- **D.** Employee cafeteria schedule

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The rules of engagement define scope, methods, and objectives and—critically—provide legal authorization, ensuring the test is performed ethically and lawfully.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A cafeteria schedule is irrelevant to a pen test.
- C: A marketing brochure has no bearing on test authorization.
- B: A seating chart does not authorize or scope a test.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Rules of Engagement*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0056 — Penetration Testing / Knowledge types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In a BLACK box penetration test, the testers:

- **A.** Are employees of the target with admin access
- **B.** Are given partial information about the system
- **C.** Have full knowledge of all system details
- **D.** Have no prior knowledge of the target, increasing discovery effort and cost

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Black box testers start with no internal knowledge, requiring more discovery effort and typically costing more, while simulating an external attacker.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Full knowledge describes white box testing.
- B: Partial information describes grey box testing.
- A: Insider admin access is not the black-box premise.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Penetration Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0057 — Penetration Testing / Knowledge types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A WHITE box penetration test is typically more cost-effective than black box because testers:

- **A.** Must discover everything from scratch
- **B.** Cannot use any tools
- **C.** Have full system knowledge, reducing time spent on discovery
- **D.** Are forbidden from seeing any documentation

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** White box testers receive full system details, so they spend less time on discovery—often making the engagement more efficient/cost-effective.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Discovering everything from scratch describes black box testing.
- D: White box testers are given documentation, not denied it.
- B: Testers may use tools in any test type.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Penetration Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0058 — Penetration Testing / Grey box  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A grey box penetration test provides testers with:

- **A.** Partial knowledge of the system (a mix of white and black box)
- **B.** Complete administrator credentials to everything
- **C.** Only the physical building address
- **D.** No information at all

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Grey box testing gives testers some information while keeping other elements hidden—a blend of white and black box approaches.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: No information describes black box testing.
- B: Full admin access goes beyond the partial-knowledge grey box premise.
- C: Only an address would be closer to black box, not grey box.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Penetration Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0059 — Testing Teams / Red team  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In security testing, the RED team plays which role?

- **A.** Combines attack and defense to improve posture
- **B.** Simulates the attacker
- **C.** Writes the financial audit report
- **D.** Defends against the simulated attack

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The red team simulates adversaries, attempting to breach defenses; the blue team defends; the purple team integrates both.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Defending is the blue team's role.
- A: Combining both is the purple team.
- C: Financial audit reporting is unrelated to red teaming.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Testing Teams*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0060 — Testing Teams / Blue team  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The BLUE team in a security exercise is responsible for:

- **A.** Writing application source code
- **B.** Approving the budget
- **C.** Defending against the simulated attacks
- **D.** Simulating the attacker

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The blue team defends systems and responds to the red team's simulated attacks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Simulating attacks is the red team's role.
- B: Budget approval is a management function.
- A: Writing source code is a development task.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Testing Teams*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0061 — Testing Teams / Purple team  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A PURPLE team primarily exists to:

- **A.** Combine red and blue team efforts to improve overall security posture
- **B.** Replace both the red and blue teams permanently
- **C.** Manage the data center HVAC
- **D.** Conduct only financial audits

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** The purple team integrates red (offense) and blue (defense) so findings translate directly into improved detection and defense.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Purple teaming complements, not permanently replaces, red/blue functions.
- D: Financial audits are unrelated to purple teaming.
- C: HVAC management is a facilities function.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Testing Teams*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0062 — Testing Teams / Team-to-role mapping  _(medium, matching)_

Match each security testing team (1-3) to its role (A-C).

1. Red team
2. Blue team
3. Purple team

- **A.** Simulates the attacker
- **B.** Defends against simulated attacks
- **C.** Integrates offense and defense to improve posture

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-B,3-C

**Why correct:** Red → simulates the attacker (B); Blue → defends (A); Purple → integrates offense/defense (C).

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Testing Teams*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0063 — Breach and Attack Simulation / BAS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Breach and Attack Simulation (BAS) tools primarily:

- **A.** Permanently remove all vulnerabilities
- **B.** Replace the need for any monitoring
- **C.** Provide physical access control to facilities
- **D.** Automatically and continuously test whether security controls detect and respond to threats

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** BAS automates simulated attacks to validate whether controls detect and respond effectively, highlighting gaps for remediation—often continuously.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: BAS validates controls; it does not remove vulnerabilities by itself.
- B: BAS complements, not replaces, monitoring.
- C: Physical access control is unrelated to BAS.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Breach and Attack Simulation (BAS)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0064 — Penetration Testing / Phases order  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Order the penetration testing phases as described in the guide. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Exploitation
- **B.** Discovery (passive reconnaissance)
- **C.** Post-exploitation
- **D.** Scanning (active reconnaissance)
- **E.** Reporting

**Correct answer:** B,D,A,C,E

**Why correct:** Pen testing proceeds: Discovery (passive recon/OSINT), Scanning (active recon), Exploitation, Post-exploitation (lateral movement/persistence/covering tracks), and Reporting.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing Phases*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0065 — Penetration Testing / Discovery phase  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The discovery (reconnaissance) phase of a penetration test focuses on:

- **A.** Writing the executive summary
- **B.** Moving laterally across compromised systems
- **C.** Exploiting vulnerabilities with Metasploit
- **D.** Passive information gathering (OSINT/footprinting) without interacting with the target

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The discovery phase uses passive reconnaissance—OSINT and footprinting—to gather information without touching the target.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Exploitation occurs in a later phase.
- B: Lateral movement is part of post-exploitation.
- A: The executive summary belongs to the reporting phase.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing Phases*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0066 — Penetration Testing / Scanning phase  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The scanning phase of a penetration test involves:

- **A.** Passive OSINT only, with no system interaction
- **B.** Covering tracks by deleting log files
- **C.** Negotiating the rules of engagement
- **D.** Active reconnaissance such as ping sweeps, port scans, and banner grabbing

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The scanning (active reconnaissance) phase interacts with the target—ping sweeps, port scans, banner grabbing, vulnerability scans—to identify weaknesses.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Passive-only OSINT is the discovery phase.
- B: Covering tracks occurs in post-exploitation.
- C: Rules of engagement are agreed before testing begins.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing Phases*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0067 — Penetration Testing / Post-exploitation  _(hard, scenario_based)_

After gaining a foothold on one server, a tester pivots to other systems, maintains access, and removes evidence of activity. Which phase does this represent?

- **A.** Post-exploitation
- **B.** Scanning
- **C.** Discovery
- **D.** Reporting

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Post-exploitation uses initial access to move laterally, maintain access/persistence, and cover tracks (evading detection).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Discovery is passive pre-attack information gathering.
- B: Scanning is active reconnaissance before exploitation.
- D: Reporting documents findings after testing.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing Phases*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0068 — Penetration Testing / Reporting phase  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The reporting phase of a penetration test should include:

- **A.** No documentation, to keep results secret from the client
- **B.** Live exploit code released publicly before remediation
- **C.** An executive summary and a technical report, mapping findings to the rules of engagement
- **D.** Only a single CVSS number with no context

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The reporting phase documents findings, vulnerabilities, and remediation for different audiences (executive summary + technical report), mapping results to the rules of engagement.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A bare score without context does not help remediation.
- B: Releasing exploits publicly before fixes is reckless and unethical.
- A: The client must receive documented findings—that is the deliverable.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing Phases*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0069 — Penetration Testing / Post-test cleanup  _(hard, scenario_based)_

After completing an authorized penetration test, the team should perform post-test cleanup, which means:

- **A.** Permanently deleting the client's production data
- **B.** Leaving backdoors in place for future tests
- **C.** Recovering affected hosts, removing tools/scripts, and restoring systems to their original state
- **D.** Publishing the client's vulnerabilities online

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Post-test cleanup restores systems to their original state—recovering hosts and removing any tools, scripts, or artifacts introduced during testing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Leaving backdoors is unethical and dangerous.
- A: Deleting production data is destructive and out of scope.
- D: Publishing client vulnerabilities violates confidentiality and ethics.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing Phases*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0070 — Penetration Testing / Physical scope  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Penetration testing can target which of the following?

- **A.** Only wireless networks
- **B.** Only printed documents
- **C.** Both logical controls (networks/software) and physical controls (locks/cameras)
- **D.** Only the HVAC system

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Pen tests can assess both logical controls (network/software systems) and physical controls (locks, cameras), depending on scope.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Pen tests are not limited to wireless.
- B: They are not limited to printed documents.
- D: They are not limited to HVAC.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0071 — Log Reviews / NTP clock sync  _(hard, scenario_based)_

During an incident investigation, logs from different servers show inconsistent timestamps, making it hard to correlate events. Which control would have PREVENTED this problem?

- **A.** Storing logs only on the compromised host
- **B.** Disabling logging on half the servers
- **C.** Synchronizing all systems to a common time source via NTP
- **D.** Using a different time zone per server with no reference

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Synchronizing clocks via NTP ensures consistent timestamps across systems, which is essential for correlating events during log review and investigations.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Disabling logging removes the evidence entirely.
- A: Storing logs only on the compromised host risks tampering and loss.
- D: Unreferenced per-server time zones worsen correlation problems.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Log Reviews*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0072 — Log Reviews / Log protection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

To protect log integrity, a recommended practice is to:

- **A.** Keep logs only on the originating host with no copies
- **B.** Allow all users to edit logs freely
- **C.** Delete logs daily to save space
- **D.** Store logs on a remote/centralized server and periodically hash them

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Storing logs on remote/centralized servers (limiting local tampering) and periodically hashing them to verify integrity are sound log-protection practices.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Local-only logs can be altered or destroyed by an attacker on that host.
- B: Freely editable logs lose integrity.
- C: Deleting logs daily can violate retention policy and destroy evidence.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Log Protection and Security Best Practices*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0073 — Log Reviews / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The PRIMARY purpose of reviewing system logs is to:

- **A.** Track system events and provide an audit trail for detecting/investigating issues
- **B.** Increase network bandwidth
- **C.** Provide backup power to servers
- **D.** Encrypt data at rest automatically

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Logs record system events and form audit trails that support detection, investigation, and accountability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Logs do not increase bandwidth.
- D: Logging is not data-at-rest encryption.
- C: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Log Reviews*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0074 — Penetration Testing / Exploitation phase  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the exploitation phase, testers attempt to:

- **A.** Synchronize all server clocks
- **B.** Gather only public OSINT data
- **C.** Write the final executive summary
- **D.** Bypass security controls (manually or with tools like Metasploit) to compromise the target

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The exploitation phase actively bypasses security controls—using manual techniques or tools such as Metasploit—to compromise the target while minimizing indicators of compromise.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Gathering public OSINT is the discovery phase.
- C: The executive summary belongs to reporting.
- A: Clock synchronization is a log-review/operations concern.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing Phases*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0075 — Breach and Attack Simulation / BAS vs pen test  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Compared to a periodic manual penetration test, a key advantage of BAS is that it:

- **A.** Eliminates the need to ever patch systems
- **B.** Requires no security controls to be present
- **C.** Provides legal authorization automatically
- **D.** Can run continuously/automatically to repeatedly validate control effectiveness

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** BAS automates simulated attacks so control effectiveness can be validated continuously, complementing point-in-time manual pen tests.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: BAS validates existing controls; it does not remove the need for them.
- A: Patching is still required regardless of BAS.
- C: Authorization still comes from rules of engagement, not BAS itself.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Breach and Attack Simulation (BAS)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0076 — Penetration Testing / Authorization importance  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Why is documented authorization (in the rules of engagement) essential before testing?

- **A.** It provides legal cover so testers are not committing unauthorized access
- **B.** It guarantees no vulnerabilities will be found
- **C.** It encrypts the network automatically
- **D.** It makes the test run faster

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Without documented authorization, the same actions a pen tester performs would be illegal unauthorized access; the rules of engagement provide the necessary legal cover.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Authorization is about legality, not speed.
- C: It does not encrypt the network.
- B: It does not affect whether vulnerabilities exist.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Rules of Engagement*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0077 — Testing Teams / Red vs blue distinction  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO statements about testing teams are correct? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** The red team approves the corporate budget
- **B.** The blue team writes the marketing plan
- **C.** The red team emulates real-world attackers
- **D.** The blue team focuses on detection and defense

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** The red team emulates attackers and the blue team handles detection/defense; together (purple) they improve posture.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Budget approval is a management function, not a red-team task.
- B: Marketing plans are unrelated to the blue team.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Testing Teams*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0078 — Penetration Testing / Tools  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are Linux distributions commonly used for penetration testing? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Parrot OS
- **B.** Adobe Photoshop
- **C.** Kali Linux
- **D.** Microsoft Excel

**Correct answer:** A, C

**Why correct:** Kali Linux and Parrot OS (along with BlackArch and BackBox) are distributions bundling penetration-testing tools.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Excel is a spreadsheet application.
- B: Photoshop is image-editing software.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Popular Tools/Distributions for Penetration Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0079 — Log Reviews / Periodic assessment  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Conducting periodic log assessments helps an organization to:

- **A.** Assign IP addresses to hosts
- **B.** Increase the CPU clock speed
- **C.** Replace the disaster recovery plan
- **D.** Meet policy/compliance requirements and detect issues that automated alerts may miss

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Periodic log reviews verify compliance with policy and can surface anomalies/trends that real-time alerts alone might not catch.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Log review does not change CPU speed.
- C: Log review does not replace DR planning.
- A: IP assignment is DHCP's function.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Log Protection and Security Best Practices*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0080 — Penetration Testing / Pen test value  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A board asks why a clean vulnerability scan is not sufficient and why a penetration test was also commissioned. The BEST justification is that a pen test:

- **A.** Guarantees the network can never be breached
- **B.** Is cheaper and faster than a scan in every case
- **C.** Removes the need to ever scan again
- **D.** Demonstrates whether identified weaknesses are actually exploitable and what real impact they have

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A scan finds potential weaknesses; a pen test validates which are truly exploitable and demonstrates real business impact, informing prioritization.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Pen tests are usually more involved/costly than scans, not cheaper in every case.
- C: Scanning remains a regular, complementary practice.
- A: No test guarantees an unbreachable network.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Penetration Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0081 — Software Testing / Unit testing  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Unit testing focuses on:

- **A.** How the entire integrated system meets all requirements
- **B.** How two or more technologies work together
- **C.** Individual components, scripts, or features in isolation
- **D.** Whether a change broke previously working functionality

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Unit testing targets specific applications, scripts, or components in isolation, assessing a feature's security/performance/usability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Testing the entire system against requirements is system/acceptance testing.
- B: Testing how technologies work together is integration testing.
- D: Checking that changes did not break existing functionality is regression testing.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0082 — Software Testing / Integration testing  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Integration testing verifies:

- **A.** The speed of the office network
- **B.** How two or more components/technologies work together
- **C.** A single isolated function only
- **D.** That the entire system meets acceptance criteria

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Integration testing checks that combined components/technologies interact correctly.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A single isolated function is unit testing.
- D: Whole-system acceptance is system testing.
- A: Network speed is unrelated to integration testing.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0083 — Software Testing / Regression testing  _(medium, scenario_based)_

After patching a bug, a team re-runs tests to confirm the fix did not break any previously working features. This is:

- **A.** Fuzz testing
- **B.** Unit testing
- **C.** Regression testing
- **D.** Smoke testing

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Regression testing ensures recent changes have not adversely affected existing functionality.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Unit testing checks an isolated component, not the impact of a change on the whole.
- D: Smoke testing is a quick basic-functionality check after a build.
- A: Fuzz testing sends invalid input to find flaws.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0084 — Software Testing / System/acceptance testing  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

System testing (also called acceptance testing or a reasonableness check) verifies that:

- **A.** The software meets its functional and security requirements as a whole
- **B.** Invalid inputs cause crashes
- **C.** Two modules exchange data
- **D.** A single function behaves correctly in isolation

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** System/acceptance testing validates the complete software against its functional and security requirements.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Isolated single-function testing is unit testing.
- B: Driving crashes via invalid input is fuzz testing.
- C: Module-to-module data exchange is integration testing.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0085 — Software Testing / Smoke testing  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Smoke testing is BEST described as:

- **A.** A quick check that the basic functionality works after a new build
- **B.** A penetration test of the network perimeter
- **C.** A review of the disaster recovery plan
- **D.** An exhaustive test of every code path

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Smoke testing quickly verifies that the core functionality of a build works before deeper testing proceeds.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Exhaustive path testing is coverage-focused, not a quick smoke test.
- B: A perimeter pen test is unrelated to build smoke testing.
- C: Reviewing a DR plan is a checklist DR test.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0086 — Software Testing / Fuzz testing  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Fuzz testing discovers vulnerabilities by:

- **A.** Synchronizing system clocks
- **B.** Reviewing source code line by line without running it
- **C.** Encrypting the application's database
- **D.** Sending invalid, unexpected, or random data to an application to see how it responds

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Fuzz testing injects invalid/unexpected/random inputs to uncover crashes, vulnerabilities, or performance issues.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Reviewing code without running it is static testing (SAST).
- C: Encrypting a database is unrelated to fuzzing.
- A: Clock synchronization is NTP, not fuzzing.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0087 — Software Testing / Mutation vs generational fuzzing  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which statement correctly distinguishes mutation fuzzing from generational fuzzing?

- **A.** Both are identical and use no input data
- **B.** Neither produces invalid inputs
- **C.** Mutation fuzzing builds inputs from a model; generational fuzzing alters valid data
- **D.** Mutation (dumb) fuzzing alters valid data to create invalid inputs; generational (smart) fuzzing builds inputs from a model

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Mutation (dumb) fuzzing modifies existing valid data into invalid inputs; generational (smart) fuzzing synthesizes inputs using a model of the expected format.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: This reverses the two techniques.
- A: They differ in how inputs are produced.
- B: Both produce invalid inputs to probe the target.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Mutation Fuzzing (Dumb Fuzzing)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0088 — Code Review / SAST vs DAST  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A security team wants to find insecure code patterns early by analyzing source code WITHOUT executing the application. Which approach fits BEST?

- **A.** Dynamic Application Security Testing (DAST)
- **B.** Static Application Security Testing (SAST)
- **C.** Fuzz testing in production
- **D.** A full interruption DR test

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** SAST analyzes source code without running it, catching insecure patterns early in development.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: DAST analyzes the application while it runs, not the static source.
- C: Fuzzing runs the application with malformed input; it is dynamic.
- D: A full interruption test is a DR exercise, unrelated to code analysis.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Static Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0089 — Code Review / DAST  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Dynamic Application Security Testing (DAST) analyzes an application:

- **A.** By degaussing the storage media
- **B.** Only by reading source code in a text editor
- **C.** While it is running, often when code cannot be taken out of production
- **D.** By reviewing the disaster recovery plan

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** DAST tests a running application (often in production-like conditions), observing behavior rather than reading static source.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Reading static source is SAST.
- A: Degaussing destroys magnetic media.
- D: DR-plan review is a DR test, not code analysis.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Static Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0090 — Code Review / Fagan inspection  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In the Fagan inspection process, which phase immediately FOLLOWS the Inspection phase?

- **A.** Planning
- **B.** Overview
- **C.** Follow-up
- **D.** Rework

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The Fagan inspection order is Planning, Overview, Inspection, Rework, Follow-up—so Rework directly follows Inspection (remediating the flaws found).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Planning is the first phase, before Overview and Inspection.
- B: Overview precedes Inspection.
- C: Follow-up is the final phase, after Rework.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Fagan Inspection Process (Phased Code Review Process)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0091 — Code Review / Fagan inspection purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The Fagan inspection is BEST described as a:

- **A.** A disaster recovery test
- **B.** Automated penetration test
- **C.** Structured, phased formal code review process to find defects/vulnerabilities
- **D.** A type of wireless encryption

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A Fagan inspection is a formal, phased code-review process (planning through follow-up) used to identify defects and vulnerabilities.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: It is a manual code review, not an automated pen test.
- D: It is unrelated to wireless encryption.
- A: It is not a DR test.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Fagan Inspection Process (Phased Code Review Process)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0092 — Code Review / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The purpose of a software code review is to:

- **A.** Identify flaws, defects, and security vulnerabilities before deployment
- **B.** Encrypt the production database
- **C.** Provide backup power to the build server
- **D.** Increase the application's marketing appeal

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Code reviews analyze developed code to find flaws, defects, and security vulnerabilities before the software is deployed.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Marketing appeal is not the goal of a code review.
- B: Database encryption is a separate control.
- C: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Software Code Reviews*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0093 — Misuse Testing / Definition  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Misuse (abuse case) testing evaluates software by:

- **A.** Simulating improper or malicious use from an attacker's perspective
- **B.** Measuring the office network bandwidth
- **C.** Reviewing the data retention schedule
- **D.** Confirming the software meets only its happy-path requirements

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Misuse/abuse-case testing models how an attacker or careless user might misuse the system, uncovering vulnerabilities the normal use cases miss.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Testing only the happy path is standard functional testing, not misuse testing.
- B: Bandwidth measurement is unrelated.
- C: Retention scheduling is a data-management task.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Misuse Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0094 — Misuse Testing / UML  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In misuse testing, UML diagrams are used to:

- **A.** Score vulnerabilities with CVSS
- **B.** Encrypt the source code automatically
- **C.** Synchronize system clocks
- **D.** Map legitimate user and attacker (misuse) interactions to highlight vulnerabilities

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** UML diagrams in misuse testing visually map both legitimate use and potential misuse by attackers, highlighting vulnerabilities and security requirements.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: UML diagrams do not encrypt code.
- A: CVSS scoring is a separate severity activity.
- C: Clock synchronization is NTP, not UML.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Misuse Test Visualization - UML Diagram*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0095 — Interface Testing / Purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Interface testing focuses on:

- **A.** Only the visual color scheme of the UI
- **B.** The connections between components/systems and how data is exchanged securely
- **C.** The financial audit of the company
- **D.** The physical temperature of the server room

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Interface testing evaluates connections (API, UI, physical interfaces) and validates secure, correct data exchange, including error handling and recovery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: UI color is a cosmetic detail, not the focus of interface testing.
- D: Server room temperature is an environmental concern.
- C: Financial audits are unrelated to interface testing.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Interface Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0096 — Interface Testing / Key areas  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are key areas evaluated during interface testing? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Secure session management
- **B.** The CEO's travel itinerary
- **C.** The cafeteria menu rotation
- **D.** Error handling for unexpected inputs

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Interface testing examines error handling, security controls, session management, and recovery/resilience of the interface.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A travel itinerary is unrelated to interface testing.
- C: The cafeteria menu is irrelevant.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Key Areas for Interface Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0097 — Interface Testing / Interface types  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are types of interfaces that interface testing evaluates? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** A parking permit
- **B.** User Interface (UI)
- **C.** Application Programming Interface (API)
- **D.** A fire extinguisher

**Correct answer:** B, C

**Why correct:** Interface testing covers APIs (software-to-software), user interfaces (UI), and physical interfaces.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A fire extinguisher is a safety device, not a software interface.
- A: A parking permit is unrelated.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Interface Testing*

### CISSP-D06-Q0098 — Compliance Testing / SOC reports  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A SaaS provider wants a report attesting to its SECURITY and privacy controls that it can share (under NDA) with prospective customers' auditors. Which SOC report fits BEST?

- **A.** SOC 1
- **B.** PCI ROC
- **C.** SOC 2
- **D.** SOC 3

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SOC 2 reports on security, availability, processing integrity, confidentiality, and privacy controls—appropriate for detailed sharing with customers/auditors under NDA.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: SOC 1 focuses on controls relevant to financial reporting.
- D: SOC 3 is a high-level public summary, lacking the detail auditors need.
- B: A PCI ROC concerns payment-card compliance, not general security-control attestation.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Compliance Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0099 — Compliance Testing / SOC report mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each SOC report (1-3) to its focus (A-C).

1. SOC 1
2. SOC 2
3. SOC 3

- **A.** A high-level summary suitable for public/general use
- **B.** Controls relevant to financial reporting
- **C.** Security and privacy controls (detailed, for restricted distribution)

**Correct answer:** 1-B,2-C,3-A

**Why correct:** SOC 1 → financial-reporting controls (B); SOC 2 → security/privacy controls (A); SOC 3 → high-level public summary (C).

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Compliance Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0100 — Compliance Testing / SOC 3  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A SOC 3 report is intended to be:

- **A.** A penetration test report
- **B.** A detailed financial-controls report for auditors
- **C.** A disaster recovery checklist
- **D.** A high-level summary suitable for public/general distribution

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SOC 3 provides a high-level, general-use summary that can be shared publicly, unlike the detailed SOC 1/SOC 2 reports.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Detailed financial-controls reporting is SOC 1.
- A: A pen test report is a different artifact.
- C: A DR checklist is a continuity document.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Compliance Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0101 — Compliance Testing / CSA STAR  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the CSA STAR program for cloud security, Level 1 versus Level 2 differ in that Level 1 is:

- **A.** A financial report, while Level 2 is a privacy report
- **B.** A third-party audit, while Level 2 is a self-assessment
- **C.** A penetration test, while Level 2 is a vulnerability scan
- **D.** A self-assessment, while Level 2 involves a third-party audit

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** CSA STAR Level 1 is a self-assessment (suited to lower-risk environments); Level 2 involves an independent third-party audit (for higher-risk environments).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: This reverses the two levels.
- C: STAR levels are assurance tiers, not pen test vs scan.
- A: STAR is about cloud security assurance, not financial vs privacy reports.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Compliance Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0102 — Compliance Testing / Purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Compliance testing helps an organization demonstrate:

- **A.** Due care and ongoing due diligence in meeting required standards
- **B.** That the office Wi-Fi is fast
- **C.** That it never needs any security controls
- **D.** That its marketing campaigns are effective

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Compliance testing (via assessments and audits) verifies controls are properly implemented, demonstrating due care and ongoing due diligence.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Compliance requires controls, not their absence.
- D: Marketing effectiveness is unrelated.
- B: Wi-Fi speed is not a compliance outcome.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Compliance Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0103 — Compliance Testing / SSAE 18  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

SSAE 18 is the standard under which which type of reports are produced?

- **A.** Fire safety certificates
- **B.** SOC reports
- **C.** CVE identifiers
- **D.** WPA3 certificates

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** SSAE 18 is the attestation standard under which SOC 1/2/3 reports are produced.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: WPA3 is a wireless encryption protocol.
- C: CVE identifiers come from MITRE.
- A: Fire safety certificates are physical-safety documents.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Compliance Testing*
