### CISSP-D06-Q0104 — Test Coverage / Branch coverage  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Branch coverage ensures that:

- **A.** Each program function has been called
- **B.** Every loop has been exercised
- **C.** Every line of code has been executed at least once
- **D.** Each decision branch (e.g., both true and false paths) in the code has been tested

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Branch coverage verifies that each decision branch (each outcome of a conditional) has been tested.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Executing every line is statement coverage.
- A: Calling each function is function coverage.
- B: Exercising loops is loop coverage.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Branch Coverage*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0105 — Test Coverage / Statement coverage  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Statement coverage measures whether:

- **A.** Every loop iteration boundary was tested
- **B.** Each decision branch outcome was tested
- **C.** Every interface was fuzzed
- **D.** Each individual code statement has been executed during testing

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Statement coverage tracks whether each executable statement in the code has been run at least once during testing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Testing each branch outcome is branch coverage.
- A: Loop-boundary testing is loop coverage.
- C: Fuzzing interfaces is a different activity.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Statement Coverage*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0106 — Test Coverage / Coverage calculation  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A test suite exercises 30 of a module's 60 executable statements. The statement coverage and the implied confidence level are:

- **A.** 30% coverage
- **B.** Cannot be determined from the numbers given
- **C.** 200% coverage
- **D.** 50% coverage (tested items divided by total items)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Coverage = items tested / total items = 30/60 = 50%; higher coverage generally implies greater confidence in testing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: 200% is impossible; coverage cannot exceed 100%.
- A: 30% would require 18 of 60, not 30 of 60.
- B: The ratio is directly computable from the figures.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Test Coverage Analysis*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0107 — Test Coverage / Loop coverage  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Loop coverage is important for security/reliability because it helps prevent:

- **A.** Service outages caused by improperly terminating (continuous) loops
- **B.** Physical tailgating into the building
- **C.** Weak wireless encryption
- **D.** DNS poisoning

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Loop coverage tests loop behavior (including boundaries), helping catch loops that never terminate and could cause outages.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Wireless encryption is unrelated to loop coverage.
- B: Tailgating is a physical-security issue.
- D: DNS poisoning is a network attack, not a loop defect.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Loop Coverage*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0108 — Test Coverage / Function coverage  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Function (functional) coverage ensures that:

- **A.** Each program function has been tested/called for verification
- **B.** Every statement is executed
- **C.** Each decision branch is tested
- **D.** Each loop boundary is exercised

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Function coverage verifies that each program function has been exercised during testing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Loop boundaries are loop coverage.
- B: Executing every statement is statement coverage.
- C: Testing each branch is branch coverage.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Functional Coverage (Function Coverage)*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0109 — Reporting / Executive summary  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The executive summary of a test/assessment report is primarily written for:

- **A.** Attackers, with full exploit code
- **B.** Developers, with line-by-line code fixes
- **C.** The facilities team, with HVAC settings
- **D.** Senior management, focusing on critical findings and business impact

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The executive summary targets senior management, summarizing critical findings, recommendations, and business impacts in non-technical terms.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Line-by-line fixes belong in the technical report for developers.
- A: Reports are not written for attackers.
- C: HVAC settings are unrelated to the executive summary.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Executive Summary*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0110 — Reporting / Assessment vs audit report  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Which statement BEST distinguishes an assessment report from an audit report?

- **A.** They are identical documents with different titles
- **B.** An assessment report is only for attackers
- **C.** An audit report contains no findings at all
- **D.** An assessment report conveys security findings/recommendations, while an audit report attests to compliance status against criteria

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An assessment report communicates security findings and recommendations; an audit report formally attests to compliance status against defined criteria.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Reports are for stakeholders, not attackers.
- C: Audit reports do contain findings (compliance status/exceptions).
- A: They serve distinct purposes.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Reports*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0111 — Reporting / Ethical disclosure  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Ethical disclosure of test findings primarily ensures:

- **A.** That vulnerabilities are sold to the highest bidder
- **B.** Transparency and adherence to the Code of Ethics when reporting vulnerabilities
- **C.** That findings are hidden from the affected organization
- **D.** That exploit code is posted publicly before any fix

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Ethical disclosure reports findings transparently and responsibly, consistent with the Code of Ethics—giving the organization the chance to remediate.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Selling vulnerabilities is unethical and harmful.
- C: Hiding findings from the affected org defeats the purpose of testing.
- D: Public exploit release before a fix is reckless.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Ethical Disclosure*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0112 — Reporting / Report components  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are key components typically included in a security test report? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Identified threats and vulnerabilities
- **B.** Criticality and likelihood of findings
- **C.** The office floor cleaning schedule
- **D.** The CEO's lunch order

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** Reports include identified threats/vulnerabilities and their criticality/likelihood (and exposure) to support prioritized remediation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A lunch order is irrelevant.
- C: A cleaning schedule is unrelated to the security report.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Key Report Components*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0113 — Reporting / Remediation recommendations  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Remediation recommendations in a report typically suggest:

- **A.** That all findings be ignored
- **B.** Corrective actions such as patches, updates, or additional security controls
- **C.** That the report be deleted immediately
- **D.** That the company stop logging events

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Remediation recommendations propose corrective actions (patches, updates, controls) to address findings, sometimes noting exceptions where remediation is deferred.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Ignoring findings leaves risk unaddressed.
- D: Stopping logging reduces visibility and accountability.
- C: Deleting the report destroys the deliverable and audit trail.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Remediation Recommendations*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0114 — Software Testing / Sanity check  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A sanity check in software testing is:

- **A.** A quick validation of a feature's viability early in development
- **B.** A full penetration test of the network
- **C.** A complete disaster recovery failover
- **D.** An exhaustive test of every code branch

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A sanity check quickly validates whether a feature/idea is viable early on before investing in deeper testing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Exhaustive branch testing is coverage-focused, not a quick sanity check.
- B: A network pen test is unrelated.
- C: A DR failover is a continuity test.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0115 — Code Review / Static vs dynamic  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The KEY difference between static and dynamic testing is that static testing:

- **A.** Analyzes code WITHOUT executing it, while dynamic testing runs the code
- **B.** Is only used for hardware
- **C.** Requires the application to be in production
- **D.** Runs the code, while dynamic testing only reads it

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Static testing (SAST) examines code without running it; dynamic testing (DAST) analyzes the running application.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: This reverses static and dynamic.
- C: Static testing examines source and does not require production.
- B: These methods apply to software, not only hardware.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Static Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0116 — Software Testing / Knowledge-based testing  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized software testing approaches based on tester knowledge of the system? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Black box testing
- **B.** White box testing
- **C.** Degaussing testing
- **D.** Fire-drill testing

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** White box (full knowledge), black box (no knowledge), and gray box (partial) are knowledge-based testing approaches.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Degaussing destroys magnetic media; it is not a software testing approach.
- D: "Fire-drill testing" is not a software testing knowledge category.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Software Testing Methods*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0117 — Compliance Testing / SOC 1  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A SOC 1 report focuses on controls relevant to:

- **A.** Financial reporting
- **B.** Office furniture inventory
- **C.** Fire suppression systems
- **D.** Wireless encryption strength

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** SOC 1 addresses controls relevant to a service organization's impact on its customers' financial reporting.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Wireless encryption is unrelated to SOC 1.
- C: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- B: Furniture inventory is unrelated.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Compliance Testing*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0118 — Test Coverage / Condition coverage  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Condition coverage differs from branch coverage in that condition coverage evaluates:

- **A.** Only whether each statement executed
- **B.** Only whether each loop ran once
- **C.** Only whether each function was called
- **D.** Each individual boolean sub-condition within a decision

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Condition coverage checks each boolean sub-condition (operand) within a decision, which is finer-grained than branch coverage (which checks decision outcomes).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Function calls are function coverage.
- A: Statement execution is statement coverage.
- B: Loop execution is loop coverage.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Condition Coverage*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0119 — Reporting / Audience targeting  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Effective test reports tailor content to different audiences by:

- **A.** Writing one identical report for everyone in deep technical jargon
- **B.** Providing an executive summary for leadership and a detailed technical report for practitioners
- **C.** Omitting all findings to avoid alarm
- **D.** Sending only raw scanner output with no analysis

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Reports should serve their audiences: an executive summary (business impact) for leadership and a technical report (detailed findings/remediation) for practitioners.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Jargon-only reports impair leadership decision-making.
- C: Omitting findings defeats the report's purpose.
- D: Raw output without analysis is not actionable.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Analyzing Test Results*

---

### CISSP-D06-Q0120 — Misuse Testing / Combined use  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Misuse testing is often conducted ALONGSIDE which other activities?

- **A.** Payroll processing and invoicing
- **B.** HVAC maintenance and landscaping
- **C.** Risk analysis, threat modeling, and penetration testing
- **D.** Marketing and sales outreach

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Misuse testing is frequently combined with risk analysis, threat modeling, security assessments, and penetration testing to map possible attack vectors.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Payroll/invoicing are financial operations.
- B: HVAC/landscaping are facilities tasks.
- D: Marketing/sales are unrelated to misuse testing.

*Source: Domain 6 > Conduct Security Testing > Application of Misuse Testing*

## Domain 7: Security Operations

### CISSP-D07-Q0001 — IDS/IPS / IDS vs IPS  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The KEY functional difference between an IDS and an IPS is that an IPS:

- **A.** Is a physical lock on the server room
- **B.** Only logs events and never alerts
- **C.** Can actively block/stop detected malicious traffic, not just alert on it
- **D.** Cannot detect any intrusions

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** An IDS detects and alerts on suspicious activity; an IPS can additionally take preventive action (block/drop) inline to stop the threat.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: An IDS does alert; the distinction is the IPS's ability to actively block.
- D: Both detect intrusions; the IPS also prevents.
- A: IDS/IPS are logical controls, not physical locks.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > IDS/IPS Systems*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0002 — IDS/IPS / Signature vs anomaly detection  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An organization wants its IDS to catch brand-new, never-before-seen attacks (not just known ones). Which detection method is MOST suitable, and what is its main drawback?

- **A.** Signature-based detection; it cannot be updated
- **B.** Signature-based detection; it never needs updates
- **C.** Behavior/anomaly-based detection; it is prone to false positives and needs a baseline learning period
- **D.** Behavior-based detection; it can only detect known signatures

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Behavior/anomaly-based detection compares activity to a learned baseline of "normal," so it can flag novel attacks—but it is prone to false positives and requires a learning period.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Signature-based detection only catches known patterns and does require updates.
- D: Behavior-based detection is precisely what catches unknown (non-signature) activity.
- B: Signature-based detection requires constant updates for new threats.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Detection Types*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0003 — IDS/IPS / Signature-based  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Knowledge-based (signature-based) intrusion detection works by:

- **A.** Learning a baseline of normal behavior over time
- **B.** Encrypting all network packets
- **C.** Matching activity against a database of known attack patterns/signatures
- **D.** Randomly blocking traffic

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Signature/knowledge-based detection (pattern matching) compares observed activity to known attack signatures and requires constant updates to detect emerging threats.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Learning a baseline of normal is behavior/anomaly-based detection.
- D: Signature detection is targeted, not random.
- B: It detects patterns; it does not encrypt packets.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Detection Types*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0004 — IDS/IPS / NIDS vs HIDS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A limitation of a Host-Based IDS (HIDS) compared to a Network-Based IDS (NIDS) is that the HIDS:

- **A.** Cannot monitor any host activity
- **B.** Is unable to detect file changes
- **C.** Cannot analyze network-wide threats; it only monitors activity on its own host
- **D.** Only works on wireless networks

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A HIDS monitors a specific host's activity but cannot see network-level threats or misconfigurations across the network—that is the NIDS's role.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Monitoring host activity is exactly what a HIDS does.
- B: HIDS can detect host file/integrity changes.
- D: HIDS is not limited to wireless networks.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Host-Based IDS (HIDS) and Host-Based IPS (HIPS)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0005 — IDS/IPS / Stateful protocol analysis  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Stateful protocol analysis detection identifies threats by:

- **A.** Only matching simple string signatures
- **B.** Comparing observed events against expected protocol states using vendor signature profiles
- **C.** Blocking all traffic by default
- **D.** Learning a statistical baseline of user behavior

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Stateful protocol analysis evaluates events against the known/expected states of a communication protocol (using vendor-provided profiles) to detect protocol anomalies.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Simple string matching describes basic signature detection.
- D: Statistical baselining of behavior is anomaly-based detection.
- C: It analyzes protocol state; it does not block everything by default.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Stateful Protocol Analysis*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0006 — Honeypots / Purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A honeypot is primarily deployed to:

- **A.** Replace the corporate firewall
- **B.** Provide backup power to the data center
- **C.** Lure and observe attackers using a decoy system, diverting them from real assets
- **D.** Encrypt production databases

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A honeypot is a decoy system designed to attract attackers so their methods can be studied and they are diverted from real production systems (a honeynet is a network of honeypots).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- D: A honeypot is a decoy, not a database-encryption tool.
- A: A honeypot complements, not replaces, a firewall.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Honeypots and Honeynets*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0007 — Preventative Controls / Whitelisting vs blacklisting  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A high-security environment wants to allow ONLY explicitly approved applications to run and block everything else. Which approach fits, and what is it also called?

- **A.** Blacklisting — "permit by exception"
- **B.** Blacklisting — "deny by exception" (allow by default)
- **C.** Whitelisting — "allow everything by default"
- **D.** Whitelisting — "permit by exception" (deny by default)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Whitelisting permits only specified actions/applications and denies everything else by default ("permit by exception")—stricter and more secure, ideal for high-security needs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Blacklisting allows everything except listed items (less strict).
- A: Blacklisting is "deny by exception," not "permit by exception."
- C: Whitelisting denies by default, not allows everything.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Whitelisting*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0008 — Preventative Controls / Blacklisting  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Blacklisting ("deny by exception") is characterized by:

- **A.** Blocking all traffic with no exceptions
- **B.** Encrypting all data at rest
- **C.** Allowing only explicitly approved items
- **D.** Denying specific listed items and allowing everything else by default

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Blacklisting denies specific actions/items while allowing everything else by default—less secure than whitelisting but favors availability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Allowing only approved items is whitelisting.
- A: Blacklisting allows non-listed items; it does not block everything.
- B: Encryption is unrelated to blacklisting.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Blacklisting*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0009 — Preventative Controls / Sandboxing  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Sandboxing (confinement) protects systems by:

- **A.** Providing redundant power to servers
- **B.** Issuing digital certificates
- **C.** Routing packets between subnets
- **D.** Running code in an isolated environment so it cannot affect the rest of the system

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Sandboxing/confinement isolates code execution so untrusted or suspicious software cannot harm the host or other systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Redundant power is a UPS/generator function.
- C: Packet routing is a network function.
- B: Certificate issuance is a PKI/CA function.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Sandboxing*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0010 — Security Operations Concepts / Separation of duties  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Separation (segregation) of duties in security operations primarily:

- **A.** Prevents any single individual from compromising security controls by dividing responsibilities
- **B.** Gives one administrator full control for efficiency
- **C.** Allows users to approve their own changes
- **D.** Eliminates the need for logging

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Separation of duties divides responsibilities so no single person can both perform and conceal a malicious or erroneous action—reducing fraud and conflict of interest.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Concentrating control in one person defeats SoD.
- D: SoD does not remove logging requirements.
- C: Self-approval undermines the control.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Separation or Segregation of Duties*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0011 — Security Operations Concepts / Job rotation  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A finance department rotates staff through different roles every few months. Besides cross-training, what security BENEFIT does job rotation provide?

- **A.** It can expose fraud/errors that a single person could otherwise hide over time
- **B.** It encrypts the payroll database
- **C.** It permanently removes the need for separation of duties
- **D.** It guarantees no employee will ever leave

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Job rotation makes it harder for one person to conceal ongoing fraud/errors (a successor may discover it) and reduces dependence on any single individual.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Job rotation complements, not replaces, separation of duties.
- D: Rotation does not control attrition.
- B: Rotation does not perform database encryption.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Job Rotation*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0012 — Security Operations Concepts / Privileged account management  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Privileged account management in security operations focuses on:

- **A.** Providing fire suppression in the data center
- **B.** Encrypting wireless traffic
- **C.** Controlling and monitoring the use of elevated/administrative accounts
- **D.** Assigning IP addresses to endpoints

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Privileged account management controls, restricts, and monitors elevated accounts because they are high-value targets and high-impact if misused.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- A: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- B: Wireless encryption is WPA2/WPA3.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Privileged Account Management*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0013 — Security Operations Concepts / Need to know vs least privilege  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In the guide's framing, how does least privilege differ in emphasis from need-to-know?

- **A.** Need-to-know applies only to wireless networks
- **B.** Least privilege focuses on functional access (what you can do); need-to-know focuses on data access (what you can see)
- **C.** They are identical concepts
- **D.** Least privilege grants maximum access; need-to-know grants none

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** The guide notes least privilege emphasizes functional access rights, while need-to-know restricts access to specific data/information required for duties.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: They are related but distinct in emphasis (function vs data).
- D: Least privilege grants minimum, not maximum, access.
- A: Need-to-know is not limited to wireless networks.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Security Operation Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0014 — Preventative Controls / Control categorization  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are PREVENTATIVE controls (designed to stop violations before they occur)? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Application whitelisting
- **B.** Reviewing IDS alert logs after an event
- **C.** Analyzing logs during an investigation
- **D.** A firewall blocking unauthorized traffic

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Whitelisting and a blocking firewall prevent violations before they happen; reviewing logs/alerts after the fact is detective.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Reviewing alert logs after an event is a detective activity.
- C: Analyzing logs during an investigation is detective/forensic.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Detective and Preventative Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0015 — IDS/IPS / Detection-method mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each IDS detection method (1-3) to its description (A-C).

1. Signature-based detection
2. Anomaly-based detection
3. Stateful protocol analysis

- **A.** Matches activity against a database of known attack patterns
- **B.** Evaluates events against expected protocol states
- **C.** Compares activity to a learned baseline of normal behavior

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-C,3-B

**Why correct:** Signature-based → known attack patterns (B); Anomaly-based → baseline of normal (A); Stateful protocol analysis → expected protocol states (C).

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Detection Types*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0016 — IDS/IPS / NIPS deployment  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Network-Based IPS (NIPS) is BEST positioned to:

- **A.** Issue user credentials
- **B.** Monitor only a single host's file integrity
- **C.** Monitor and block malicious traffic across network segments inline
- **D.** Provide backup power

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A NIPS sits inline on the network to monitor and actively block malicious traffic across segments network-wide.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Single-host file integrity monitoring is a HIDS/HIPS function.
- D: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- A: Credential issuance is an IAM function.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > IDS and IPS Deployment Types*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0017 — Third-Party Security / Integration  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

When using third-party security services, a key best practice is to:

- **A.** Assume they need no contractual security requirements
- **B.** Give them unrestricted access with no oversight
- **C.** Integrate their roles into the incident response plan, covering both detection and prevention
- **D.** Exclude them entirely from any incident planning

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Third-party providers should be integrated into the incident response plan with defined roles in detection and prevention, and governed by appropriate contracts/SLAs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Unrestricted, unmonitored access is a major risk.
- D: Excluding them from planning leaves gaps if they are involved operationally.
- A: Contractual security requirements (SLAs) are essential for third parties.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Third-Party Involvement in Incident Response*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0018 — Honeypots / Padded cell  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A "padded cell" works alongside an IDS by:

- **A.** Transferring a detected attacker into an isolated, simulated environment in real time
- **B.** Encrypting all production data
- **C.** Providing redundant power
- **D.** Permanently deleting the attacker's device

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A padded cell is a protected, isolated environment that an IDS redirects an attacker into once detected, containing them away from real systems.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: It isolates, not destroys, the attacker's device.
- B: It is a containment environment, not an encryption tool.
- C: Redundant power is unrelated to a padded cell.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Advanced Honeypot Deployment*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0019 — IDS/IPS / False positives  _(medium, scenario_based)_

After deploying an anomaly-based IDS, the SOC is flooded with alerts for legitimate-but-unusual activity. The MOST likely cause is:

- **A.** The IDS only uses known signatures
- **B.** The network has no traffic at all
- **C.** The IDS is a physical access control
- **D.** False positives from an immature/insufficient baseline of normal behavior

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Anomaly-based detection is prone to false positives, especially before the baseline of normal behavior is well established (the learning period).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Signature-only detection would not flag unusual-but-unknown activity as anomalies.
- B: Alerts indicate there is traffic to analyze.
- C: An IDS is a logical control, not a physical access control.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Behavior-Based Detection (Anomaly-Based)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0020 — Security Operations Concepts / Least privilege  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Applying least privilege in security operations means:

- **A.** Granting subjects only the access required to perform their job functions
- **B.** Allowing anonymous access to systems
- **C.** Giving all operators administrator rights
- **D.** Removing all access controls

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Least privilege grants only the minimum access necessary, limiting the damage from compromise or misuse.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Universal admin rights violate least privilege.
- B: Anonymous access undermines accountability.
- D: Removing access controls is the opposite of least privilege.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Security Operation Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0021 — Detective Controls / Detective vs preventative  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which is an example of a DETECTIVE control?

- **A.** Application whitelisting that prevents unapproved software
- **B.** An IDS that alerts on suspicious activity
- **C.** A locked door that stops entry
- **D.** A firewall that blocks unauthorized ports

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Detective controls identify/announce that an event has occurred (e.g., an IDS alert); the others prevent events from occurring.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A blocking firewall is preventative.
- A: Whitelisting is preventative.
- C: A locked door is a preventative physical control.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Detective and Preventative Concepts*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0022 — IDS/IPS / WIDS  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Wireless IDS (WIDS) is specifically designed to:

- **A.** Monitor only wired host file integrity
- **B.** Encrypt email messages
- **C.** Monitor wireless network traffic for protocol-related anomalies (e.g., rogue APs)
- **D.** Provide backup generator power

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A WIDS monitors wireless traffic for anomalies such as rogue access points and wireless-specific attacks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Wired host file integrity is a HIDS concern.
- D: Generator power is unrelated.
- B: Email encryption is S/MIME/PGP.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Wireless IDS and Wireless IPS*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0023 — Preventative Controls / Defense in depth  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Layered security (defense in depth) against malware means:

- **A.** Disabling all security to improve performance
- **B.** Relying on one antivirus product alone
- **C.** Encrypting only the company logo
- **D.** Using multiple, overlapping controls so no single failure leads to compromise

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Defense in depth layers multiple controls (e.g., anti-malware, awareness training, filtering) so the failure of one does not result in compromise.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A single control is the opposite of layered defense.
- A: Disabling security increases risk.
- C: Encrypting a logo provides no meaningful protection.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Defense Mechanisms Against Malware*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0024 — Security Operations Concepts / SoD scenario  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A single administrator can create user accounts AND approve their own access requests AND clear the audit logs. Which principle is violated, and what is the risk?

- **A.** Fail-secure; the system fails open
- **B.** Separation of duties; one person could grant and hide unauthorized access
- **C.** Least common mechanism; too many shared components
- **D.** Defense in depth; the network is too segmented

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Concentrating account creation, approval, and log-clearing in one person violates separation of duties—enabling that person to grant and conceal unauthorized access.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Defense in depth concerns layered controls, not this concentration of duties.
- C: Least common mechanism concerns shared components, not duty concentration.
- A: Fail-secure concerns failure behavior, not duty separation.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Separation or Segregation of Duties*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0025 — Malware / Worm vs virus  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The KEY difference between a worm and a virus is that a worm:

- **A.** Self-propagates across networks without needing a host file or user action
- **B.** Always requires a user to open an infected file
- **C.** Only encrypts files for ransom
- **D.** Cannot spread between systems

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A worm self-replicates and spreads across networks on its own, whereas a virus typically needs to attach to a host file and rely on user action to spread.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Requiring a user to open a file describes a virus, not a worm.
- D: Worms spread between systems—that is their hallmark.
- C: Encrypting files for ransom describes ransomware.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Malware*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0026 — Malware / Logic bomb  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A logic bomb is malware that:

- **A.** Executes its malicious payload when a specific condition or trigger is met
- **B.** Self-propagates across networks immediately
- **C.** Displays unwanted advertisements
- **D.** Provides remote control of a device

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A logic bomb lies dormant until a defined condition/trigger (date, event, action) is met, then executes its payload.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Immediate self-propagation describes a worm.
- D: Remote control describes a RAT.
- C: Unwanted ads describe adware.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Malware*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0027 — Malware / RAT  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Remote Access Trojan (RAT) primarily enables an attacker to:

- **A.** Provide backup power
- **B.** Synchronize system clocks
- **C.** Gain covert remote control over an infected device
- **D.** Display advertisements to generate revenue

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A RAT gives an attacker covert remote control of the infected system, often for surveillance, data theft, or lateral movement.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Revenue-generating ads describe adware.
- B: Clock synchronization is NTP.
- A: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Malware*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0028 — Malware / Polymorphic virus  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A polymorphic virus is difficult for signature-based antivirus to detect because it:

- **A.** Changes its code/appearance with each infection to evade signature matching
- **B.** Only runs on wireless networks
- **C.** Cannot replicate at all
- **D.** Always uses the exact same byte pattern

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A polymorphic virus mutates its code/signature on each infection, defeating static signature matching and requiring heuristic/behavioral detection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A constant byte pattern would be easy to signature-match.
- C: Polymorphic viruses do replicate (while mutating).
- B: They are not limited to wireless networks.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Viruses by Behavior*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0029 — Malware / Macro virus  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A macro virus typically spreads through:

- **A.** The master boot record only
- **B.** Wireless access points exclusively
- **C.** Physical door locks
- **D.** Malicious macros embedded in documents (e.g., word processor/spreadsheet files)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A macro virus is written in the macro language of applications and embeds in documents, executing when the document is opened with macros enabled.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: MBR infection describes a master boot record virus.
- B: Macro viruses spread via documents, not only wireless.
- C: Door locks are physical controls.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Virus Types by Infection Method*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0030 — Anti-Malware / On-access vs on-demand  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An antivirus product scans every file the moment it is opened or executed. Which scanning mode is this, and how does it differ from the alternative?

- **A.** On-demand scanning; it only runs once a year
- **B.** On-access scanning; it checks files in real time as they are used, vs on-demand which runs on a schedule/manually
- **C.** On-access scanning; it never checks files in real time
- **D.** On-demand scanning; it scans every file as it opens

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** On-access scanning inspects files in real time as they are accessed/executed; on-demand scanning runs on a schedule or when manually initiated.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: On-demand is not limited to yearly; and the scenario describes real-time access.
- C: On-access does check in real time—that defines it.
- D: Real-time-as-opened is on-access, not on-demand.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Malware Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0031 — Anti-Malware / Heuristic analysis  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Heuristic (behavior-based) malware analysis improves on pure signature detection by:

- **A.** Identifying suspicious behavior/characteristics, helping catch new or unknown malware
- **B.** Only matching exact known signatures
- **C.** Encrypting the infected files
- **D.** Requiring no analysis at all

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Heuristic analysis evaluates code behavior/characteristics (statically or dynamically) to flag previously unseen malware that lacks a known signature.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Exact-signature matching is the limitation heuristics overcome.
- D: Heuristics involve analysis, not its absence.
- C: Detection does not encrypt the files.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Anti-Malware*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0032 — Anti-Malware / Functions  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

When antivirus software isolates an infected file so it cannot execute or spread, but does not delete it, this action is called:

- **A.** Encrypt
- **B.** Quarantine
- **C.** Delete
- **D.** Disinfect

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Quarantine isolates the suspicious/infected file (preventing execution/spread) while preserving it for review or recovery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Disinfect attempts to clean the malware from the file.
- C: Delete removes the file entirely.
- A: Encrypt is not an anti-malware response function here.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Anti-Malware Functions*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0033 — Malware / Malware-type mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each malware type (1-4) to its description (A-D).

1. Ransomware
2. Keylogger
3. Trojan horse
4. Botnet

- **A.** Records keystrokes to capture credentials/data
- **B.** Encrypts data and demands payment for the key
- **C.** Malicious software disguised as a legitimate program
- **D.** A network of compromised devices controlled by an attacker

**Correct answer:** 1-B,2-A,3-C,4-D

**Why correct:** Ransomware → encrypts for ransom (B); Keylogger → records keystrokes (A); Trojan horse → disguised malware (D); Botnet → network of compromised devices (C).

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Malware*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0034 — Logging and Monitoring / SIEM  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A Security Information and Event Management (SIEM) system primarily:

- **A.** Provides backup power to the data center
- **B.** Aggregates and correlates logs/events from many sources for centralized analysis and alerting
- **C.** Encrypts wireless traffic
- **D.** Physically guards the building entrance

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A SIEM centralizes log/event collection, correlation, and alerting across the environment, supporting detection and investigation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- D: Physical guarding is a facility control.
- C: Wireless encryption is WPA2/WPA3.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Security Information and Event Management (SIEM)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0035 — Logging and Monitoring / Cyber Kill Chain  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Order these stages of the Cyber Kill Chain as an intrusion progresses. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Delivery
- **B.** Actions on Objectives
- **C.** Weaponization
- **D.** Installation
- **E.** Command and Control (C2)
- **F.** Exploitation
- **G.** Reconnaissance

**Correct answer:** G,C,A,F,D,E,B

**Why correct:** The Cyber Kill Chain runs: Reconnaissance, Weaponization, Delivery, Exploitation, Installation, Command and Control, and Actions on Objectives.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Cyber Kill Chain*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0036 — Logging and Monitoring / MITRE ATT&CK  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The MITRE ATT&CK framework is BEST described as:

- **A.** A wireless encryption standard
- **B.** A knowledge base of adversary tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs)
- **C.** A backup power configuration
- **D.** A type of fire suppression system

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** MITRE ATT&CK catalogs real-world adversary tactics, techniques, and procedures, used for threat modeling, detection engineering, and defense.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Wireless encryption standards are WPA2/WPA3.
- C: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- D: Fire suppression is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > MITRE ATT&CK Framework*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0037 — Logging and Monitoring / SOAR  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A SOC is overwhelmed by repetitive alert triage. Which technology automates and orchestrates response workflows (runbooks) to reduce manual effort?

- **A.** A cold recovery site
- **B.** SOAR (Security Orchestration, Automation, and Response)
- **C.** A UPS
- **D.** A padded cell

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** SOAR orchestrates and automates response workflows (runbooks/playbooks) across tools, reducing repetitive manual work and speeding response.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A cold site is a DR recovery facility.
- D: A padded cell isolates attackers, not automates SOC workflows.
- C: A UPS provides backup power.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Security Orchestration, Automation and Response (SOAR)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0038 — Logging and Monitoring / UBA  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

User Behavior Analytics (UBA) helps detect threats by:

- **A.** Encrypting all data at rest
- **B.** Establishing baselines of normal user activity and flagging anomalous behavior (e.g., insider threats)
- **C.** Providing backup generator power
- **D.** Routing packets between subnets

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** UBA models normal user behavior and flags deviations (e.g., unusual access patterns), aiding detection of compromised accounts and insider threats.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Data-at-rest encryption is a separate control.
- D: Packet routing is a network function.
- C: Generator power is unrelated.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Behavior Analytics*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0039 — Logging and Monitoring / Log protection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

To preserve the integrity and usefulness of logs for monitoring, an organization should:

- **A.** Disable logging to save disk space
- **B.** Allow every user to edit log files
- **C.** Store logs only on the system that generated them
- **D.** Centralize logs on protected servers and restrict who can modify them

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Centralizing logs on protected/remote servers with restricted modification protects against tampering and supports reliable monitoring and investigation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Universal edit rights destroy log integrity.
- C: Local-only logs can be altered/destroyed by an attacker on that host.
- A: Disabling logging eliminates the audit trail.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Protecting Log Files*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0040 — Logging and Monitoring / Threat intelligence  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Threat intelligence primarily helps an organization to:

- **A.** Provide redundant power during outages
- **B.** Make informed, proactive security decisions using analyzed information about threats and TTPs
- **C.** Encrypt the company website
- **D.** Assign IP addresses to hosts

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** Threat intelligence gathers and analyzes information about threats/TTPs to support proactive, informed defensive decisions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Redundant power is a UPS/generator function.
- D: IP assignment is DHCP's function.
- C: Website encryption is TLS/HTTPS.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Threat Intelligence*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0041 — Change Management / Change control purpose  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The PRIMARY security purpose of a formal change control process is to:

- **A.** Ensure changes are reviewed/approved so they do not introduce misconfigurations or security incidents
- **B.** Eliminate the need for backups
- **C.** Allow any administrator to change production without review
- **D.** Speed up changes by skipping testing

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Change control provides structured review/approval of updates and configuration changes, maintaining security and preventing misconfigurations or incidents.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Unreviewed production changes are exactly what change control prevents.
- B: Change control does not remove the need for backups.
- D: Skipping testing increases the risk of incidents.

*Source: Domain 7 > Configuration Management > Change Control*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0042 — Configuration Management / Baseline  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In configuration management, a secure baseline configuration serves as:

- **A.** A documented reference for consistent, secure deployment of configuration items
- **B.** A wireless encryption key
- **C.** A list of employees authorized to take vacation
- **D.** A backup generator schedule

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A secure baseline defines a known-good configuration for each configuration item (CI), used as a reference for consistent, secure deployments and drift detection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Vacation lists are unrelated to configuration baselines.
- D: Generator schedules are power-management items.
- B: A wireless key is a network secret, not a config baseline.

*Source: Domain 7 > Configuration Management > Implementation Phase*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0043 — Configuration Management / NIST 800-128 phases  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Order the four main phases of security-focused configuration management (NIST SP 800-128). (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Controlling configuration changes
- **B.** Identifying and implementing configurations
- **C.** Planning
- **D.** Monitoring

**Correct answer:** C,B,A,D

**Why correct:** NIST SP 800-128 configuration management proceeds: Planning, Identifying/Implementing configurations, Controlling changes, and Monitoring.

*Source: Domain 7 > Configuration Management > Configuration Management*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0044 — Patch Management / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Patch management primarily reduces risk by:

- **A.** Encrypting all network traffic
- **B.** Providing backup power
- **C.** Applying vendor updates to fix known vulnerabilities in a controlled way
- **D.** Assigning IP addresses

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Patch management deploys updates to remediate known vulnerabilities, ideally tested and rolled out through change control to avoid disruption.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Traffic encryption is a separate control.
- B: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- D: IP assignment is DHCP's function.

*Source: Domain 7 > Configuration Management > System Patch Management*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0045 — Patch Management / Test before deploy  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A critical patch is released. Before deploying it broadly to production, the BEST practice is to:

- **A.** Disable patching entirely to avoid downtime
- **B.** Push it to all production systems immediately with no testing
- **C.** Ignore it until the next annual review
- **D.** Test the patch in a non-production environment and deploy via change control

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Patches should be tested in a representative non-production environment and rolled out through change control to balance security with stability.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Untested mass deployment risks outages from a faulty patch.
- C: Delaying critical patches leaves known vulnerabilities exposed.
- A: Disabling patching leaves systems vulnerable indefinitely.

*Source: Domain 7 > Configuration Management > System Patch Management*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0046 — Configuration Management / Media management  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Media management as part of configuration/operations involves:

- **A.** Designing marketing campaigns
- **B.** Routing BGP between autonomous systems
- **C.** Scheduling employee lunches
- **D.** Protecting and controlling physical and digital media (e.g., drives, tapes, cloud storage)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Media management secures and controls physical and digital media—hard drives, backup tapes, and cloud storage—across their lifecycle.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Marketing campaigns are unrelated.
- C: Lunch scheduling is unrelated.
- B: BGP routing is a network function.

*Source: Domain 7 > Configuration Management > Media Management*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0047 — Malware / Ransomware response  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Ransomware has encrypted files on several endpoints. Which combination BEST supports recovery WITHOUT paying the ransom?

- **A.** Paying immediately to guarantee data return
- **B.** Disabling all backups to save space
- **C.** Isolating affected systems and restoring from clean, tested offline backups
- **D.** Connecting all systems together to share the decryptor

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Containing the spread (isolation) and restoring from clean, tested, offline backups enables recovery without funding criminals or trusting attacker-provided keys.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Paying does not guarantee recovery and encourages further attacks.
- D: Interconnecting systems risks spreading the ransomware further.
- B: Disabling backups removes the very means of recovery.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Malware*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0048 — Anti-Malware / Scanning modes  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized anti-malware scanning modes? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** On-vacation scanning
- **B.** On-access scanning
- **C.** On-demand scanning
- **D.** On-paper scanning

**Correct answer:** B, C

**Why correct:** On-access (real-time) and on-demand (scheduled/manual) are the two anti-malware scanning modes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: "On-vacation scanning" is not a real mode.
- D: "On-paper scanning" is not an anti-malware mode.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Types of Malware Scanning*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0049 — Logging and Monitoring / Log entry components  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO elements are essential components of a useful log entry for investigations? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** An accurate timestamp of when the event occurred
- **B.** The marketing slogan of the company
- **C.** The color of the server chassis
- **D.** The source/identity associated with the event

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Accurate timestamps (synchronized time) and the source/identity tied to the event are essential so activity can be correlated and attributed during investigations.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A slogan is irrelevant to log analysis.
- C: Chassis color is irrelevant.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Essential Components of Log Entries*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0050 — EOL/EOS / End of life  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Running software that has reached End of Life (EOL)/End of Support (EOS) is a security risk because:

- **A.** It automatically gains new security features
- **B.** It encrypts itself for protection
- **C.** The vendor no longer issues security patches, leaving known vulnerabilities unfixed
- **D.** It cannot run on any hardware

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** EOL/EOS software stops receiving vendor security updates, so newly discovered vulnerabilities remain unpatched—such systems should be replaced or isolated.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: EOL/EOS removes support; it does not add features.
- D: EOL software may still run; the risk is lack of patches.
- B: EOL software does not self-encrypt for protection.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > End of Life (EOL) and End of Support (EOS)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0051 — Logging and Monitoring / Kill chain disruption  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The defensive value of the Cyber Kill Chain model is that:

- **A.** It guarantees attacks can never succeed
- **B.** It is a wireless encryption protocol
- **C.** It only applies to physical security
- **D.** Disrupting any single stage can break the attack before the adversary achieves their objective

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Because an intrusion must progress through ordered stages, defenders who detect and disrupt any stage can stop the attack before its final objective.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: No model guarantees prevention of all attacks.
- B: It is an intrusion model, not wireless encryption.
- C: It applies to cyber intrusions broadly, not only physical security.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Cyber Kill Chain*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0052 — Malware / Spyware vs adware  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Spyware is distinguished from adware in that spyware primarily:

- **A.** Displays unwanted advertisements to generate revenue
- **B.** Encrypts files for ransom
- **C.** Provides backup power
- **D.** Covertly gathers information about a user without consent

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Spyware covertly collects user information/activity; adware focuses on displaying unwanted advertisements.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Displaying ads describes adware.
- B: Encrypting for ransom describes ransomware.
- C: Backup power is unrelated.

*Source: Domain 7 > Detective and Preventative Measures > Additional Malware Types*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0053 — Configuration Management / Provisioning/automation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Using automation to create, manage, or destroy configuration items consistently helps security by:

- **A.** Removing the need for any baseline
- **B.** Disabling change control
- **C.** Allowing untracked ad-hoc changes
- **D.** Reducing manual errors and enforcing consistent, repeatable secure configurations

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Automated provisioning enforces consistent, repeatable configurations (from baselines), reducing manual error and configuration drift.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Automation relies on baselines as references.
- C: Untracked ad-hoc changes undermine configuration management.
- B: Automation should operate within change control, not replace it.

*Source: Domain 7 > Configuration Management > Provisioning and Automation*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0054 — Incident Management / CBK steps order  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Order the incident management steps as listed in the (ISC)² CBK. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Mitigation
- **B.** Remediation
- **C.** Detection
- **D.** Recovery
- **E.** Lessons Learned
- **F.** Reporting
- **G.** Response

**Correct answer:** C,G,A,F,D,B,E

**Why correct:** The CBK incident management steps are: Detection, Response, Mitigation, Reporting, Recovery, Remediation, and Lessons Learned.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Incident Management Steps (CBK)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0055 — Incident Management / Preparation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the NIST incident response model, the Preparation phase includes:

- **A.** Establishing the IR policy, forming the incident response team, and deploying tools (IDS, DLP, etc.)
- **B.** Restoring systems from backup
- **C.** Writing the final lessons-learned report
- **D.** Eradicating malware from infected hosts

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Preparation establishes the incident response policy, defines incidents/priorities, forms the IR team with roles/contacts, and deploys necessary tools before incidents occur.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Eradication occurs during containment/eradication/recovery, not preparation.
- C: The lessons-learned report is post-incident activity.
- B: Restoring from backup is part of recovery.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Incident Management Steps (NIST Mapping)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0056 — Incident Management / Detection and triage  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A SOC analyst receives many alerts and must determine which represent genuine incidents requiring response. This analysis/prioritization activity is called:

- **A.** Provisioning
- **B.** Lessons learned
- **C.** Remediation
- **D.** Triage

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Triage analyzes and prioritizes events to confirm genuine incidents and determine response urgency during the detection/analysis phase.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Remediation addresses root causes after response.
- B: Lessons learned is the post-incident review.
- A: Provisioning is an identity/configuration activity.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Detection and Analysis*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0057 — Incident Management / Lessons learned  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The PRIMARY purpose of the lessons-learned (post-incident) phase is to:

- **A.** Assign blame to a single employee
- **B.** Pay any ransom demanded
- **C.** Analyze how the incident was handled and improve future response plans/controls
- **D.** Immediately delete all incident logs

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Lessons learned reviews incident handling, identifies gaps and root causes, and drives improvements to plans, controls, and processes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The goal is improvement, not blame assignment.
- D: Logs should be retained, not deleted, for analysis and evidence.
- B: Paying ransom is not the purpose of lessons learned.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Lessons Learned*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0058 — Incident Management / Containment priority  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Active malware is spreading laterally across the network during an incident. After detection/analysis, what is the MOST immediate priority?

- **A.** Contain/mitigate the spread (e.g., isolate affected systems) to limit damage
- **B.** Conduct the annual security awareness training
- **C.** Schedule next year's penetration test
- **D.** Write the executive summary

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Once an incident is confirmed and spreading, containment/mitigation (isolating affected systems) is the immediate priority to limit damage before eradication and recovery.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Reporting/executive summaries come after stabilizing the situation.
- B: Awareness training is not an immediate incident-response action.
- C: Scheduling future tests does not address the active incident.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Detection and Analysis*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0059 — Investigations / Investigation types  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An inquiry into whether an organization complied with a specific industry regulator's rules is which type of investigation?

- **A.** Criminal
- **B.** Regulatory
- **C.** Civil
- **D.** Administrative

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** A regulatory investigation examines compliance with a regulator's rules/standards for an industry.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Administrative investigations address internal policy matters.
- A: Criminal investigations pursue violations of criminal law.
- C: Civil investigations concern disputes between private parties.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Investigations*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0060 — Investigations / Burden of proof  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A cyber case may be pursued criminally (risking imprisonment) or civilly (seeking damages). Which correctly pairs each with its standard of proof?

- **A.** Both use preponderance of the evidence
- **B.** Both use beyond a reasonable doubt
- **C.** Criminal = beyond a reasonable doubt; Civil = preponderance of the evidence
- **D.** Criminal = preponderance of the evidence; Civil = beyond a reasonable doubt

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Criminal cases require the higher "beyond a reasonable doubt" standard; civil cases use the lower "preponderance of the evidence" standard.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: This reverses the two standards.
- B: Civil cases use the lower preponderance standard, not beyond a reasonable doubt.
- A: Criminal cases use the higher beyond-a-reasonable-doubt standard.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Burden of Proof Standards*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0061 — Investigations / Administrative investigation  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

An internal investigation into a suspected violation of company policy, used to support an HR decision, is a(n):

- **A.** Administrative investigation
- **B.** Criminal investigation
- **C.** Civil investigation
- **D.** Regulatory investigation

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Administrative investigations address internal policy violations and inform organizational/HR decisions.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Criminal investigations involve law enforcement and criminal law.
- D: Regulatory investigations concern compliance with a regulator.
- C: Civil investigations involve disputes between parties.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Investigations*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0062 — Investigations / MO analysis  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Modus Operandi (MO) analysis in an investigation focuses on:

- **A.** The brand of the victim's laptop
- **B.** The company's marketing budget
- **C.** The attacker's characteristic methods/patterns of operation
- **D.** The office floor plan

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** MO analysis studies the attacker's characteristic methods and patterns, which can help attribute or anticipate behavior.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Laptop brand is not the focus of MO analysis.
- D: Floor plans are unrelated to MO.
- B: Marketing budgets are irrelevant.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Investigations*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0063 — Evidence / Best evidence rule  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

The "best evidence rule" generally requires that:

- **A.** Only photocopies are ever admissible
- **B.** Verbal testimony always outweighs documents
- **C.** Evidence be destroyed after the case
- **D.** Original documents/evidence be used whenever possible (copies are secondary and may be inadmissible)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The best evidence rule favors original documents/evidence; copies are secondary and can be challenged for authenticity.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: The rule prefers originals over copies, not the reverse.
- B: The rule is about document originals, not testimony precedence.
- C: Evidence must be preserved, not destroyed.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Key Evidence Rules*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0064 — Evidence / Hearsay  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Hearsay evidence is:

- **A.** Information relayed from someone who did not directly witness the event
- **B.** An expert's professional opinion
- **C.** A witness's account of something they personally observed
- **D.** Physical evidence collected at the scene

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Hearsay is secondhand information from someone who did not directly witness the event, and is often subject to admissibility limits.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: A firsthand account is direct evidence.
- B: An expert opinion is opinion evidence.
- D: Physical scene evidence is not testimonial hearsay.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Testimonial Evidence*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0065 — Evidence / Circumstantial vs corroborative  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Video footage that supports and verifies the access logs already collected is an example of which evidence type?

- **A.** Opinion evidence
- **B.** Hearsay evidence
- **C.** Circumstantial evidence
- **D.** Corroborative evidence

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Corroborative evidence supports or verifies other evidence (e.g., video footage alongside access logs), strengthening a case.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Circumstantial evidence implies a fact indirectly rather than verifying existing evidence.
- B: Hearsay is secondhand information.
- A: Opinion evidence is an expert's professional judgment.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Additional Evidence Types*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0066 — Evidence / Direct evidence  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Direct evidence is BEST described as:

- **A.** Secondhand information from a non-witness
- **B.** An expert's opinion
- **C.** Facts that only imply an event occurred
- **D.** Witness testimony based on firsthand observation

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Direct evidence is testimony from a witness who personally observed the event.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Secondhand non-witness information is hearsay.
- B: An expert opinion is opinion evidence.
- C: Indirectly implied facts are circumstantial evidence.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Testimonial Evidence*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0067 — Evidence / Chain of custody  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A laptop seized as evidence is later challenged in court because no one can prove who handled it between seizure and analysis. Which control was inadequately maintained?

- **A.** Chain of custody
- **B.** Whitelisting
- **C.** Load balancing
- **D.** Quality of Service (QoS)

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Chain of custody documents who handled evidence, when, and how—an unbroken, documented chain is required to preserve evidentiary integrity and admissibility.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Load balancing distributes traffic; it is unrelated to evidence.
- B: Whitelisting is a preventative software control.
- D: QoS prioritizes network traffic, unrelated to evidence handling.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Chain of Custody*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0068 — Evidence / Evidence types  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized types of testimonial evidence? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Whitelisted evidence
- **B.** Failover evidence
- **C.** Direct evidence
- **D.** Opinion evidence

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** Direct evidence (firsthand observation) and opinion evidence (expert judgment) are testimonial evidence types, along with hearsay.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: "Failover evidence" is not an evidence type (failover is a recovery technique).
- A: "Whitelisted evidence" is not an evidence type.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Testimonial Evidence*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0069 — Digital Forensics / Forensic process order  _(hard, sequence_ordering)_

Order the core digital forensic process stages within incident response. (Provide the option labels in order.)

- **A.** Analysis
- **B.** Examination
- **C.** Collection
- **D.** Reporting

**Correct answer:** C,B,A,D

**Why correct:** The forensic process proceeds: Collection (acquire evidence), Examination (extract relevant data), Analysis (interpret findings), and Reporting (document conclusions).

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Forensic Process in Incident Response*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0070 — Digital Forensics / Order of volatility  _(hard, scenario_based)_

During live evidence collection on a running system, which data should generally be captured FIRST to avoid losing it?

- **A.** Archived backup tapes in off-site storage
- **B.** Printed documents in a filing cabinet
- **C.** Volatile data such as RAM contents and active network connections
- **D.** Data on powered-off hard drives

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The order of volatility dictates collecting the most volatile data first (e.g., RAM, running processes, network connections) before it is lost, then less-volatile media.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Off-site backup tapes are non-volatile and can be collected later.
- B: Printed documents are static and not volatile.
- D: Powered-off drives retain data and are less volatile than RAM.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Forensic Process in Incident Response*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0071 — Digital Forensics / eDiscovery  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Digital forensics, when applied to identifying electronic evidence for legal proceedings, is also referred to as:

- **A.** Whitelisting
- **B.** Load balancing
- **C.** eDiscovery (electronic data discovery)
- **D.** Quality of Service

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Digital forensics for legal evidence is also called eDiscovery/electronic data discovery, following a structured reference model (EDRM).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Load balancing distributes traffic.
- A: Whitelisting is a preventative control.
- D: QoS prioritizes network traffic.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Digital Forensics*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0072 — Digital Forensics / Preserve evidence  _(hard, scenario_based)_

To analyze a compromised hard drive forensically while keeping the evidence admissible, an investigator should:

- **A.** Boot and use the original drive directly to save time
- **B.** Reformat the drive before analysis
- **C.** Share the drive freely without documentation
- **D.** Work from a forensic image/copy and protect the original, maintaining chain of custody

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Forensic best practice analyzes a verified bit-for-bit image (often with write-blocking), preserving the original and documenting chain of custody to maintain integrity/admissibility.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Using the original directly risks altering evidence and breaking admissibility.
- B: Reformatting destroys the evidence.
- C: Undocumented sharing breaks chain of custody.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Purpose of Digital Forensics*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0073 — Digital Forensics / Reporting  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The reporting stage of a digital forensic investigation emphasizes:

- **A.** Accuracy and authenticity, presenting admissible evidence with clear explanations
- **B.** Speed over correctness
- **C.** Deleting the underlying evidence
- **D.** Omitting how conclusions were reached

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Forensic reporting focuses on accuracy/authenticity, documenting how conclusions were reached and presenting admissible evidence clearly (e.g., for court).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Sacrificing correctness undermines admissibility.
- D: The methodology must be documented, not omitted.
- C: Evidence must be preserved, not deleted.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Digital Forensic Incident Response*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0074 — Incident Management / IR team  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Forming an Incident Response Team (IRT) with defined roles and contacts BEFORE an incident occurs is part of which phase?

- **A.** Remediation
- **B.** Recovery
- **C.** Lessons learned
- **D.** Preparation

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Establishing the IR team, roles, responsibilities, and contacts is a Preparation activity, done before incidents occur.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Recovery restores normal operations after an incident.
- C: Lessons learned is a post-incident review.
- A: Remediation fixes root causes after response.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Incident Management Steps (NIST Mapping)*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0075 — Investigations / Evidence collection techniques  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized evidence collection techniques in investigations? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Disabling all logging
- **B.** Surveillance
- **C.** Deleting suspect files
- **D.** Interviewing witnesses

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** Interviewing and surveillance (along with interrogation and digital forensics) are recognized evidence collection techniques.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Disabling logging destroys potential evidence.
- C: Deleting suspect files destroys evidence.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Evidence Collection Techniques*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0076 — Digital Forensics / Investigator competence  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A key consideration for forensic investigations is that investigators should:

- **A.** Be competent, follow consistent policy/process, and maintain chain of custody
- **B.** Work alone with no accountability
- **C.** Avoid documenting any of their steps
- **D.** Improvise without documented procedures

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Forensic investigators should be competent, aware of data sources, and follow consistent policies/processes while maintaining chain of custody for admissibility.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Improvising without procedures undermines repeatability and admissibility.
- C: Steps must be documented for the evidence to hold up.
- B: Accountability and documented handling are essential.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Considerations for Forensic Investigations*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0077 — Incident Management / Investigation trigger  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the guide's model, a security investigation is typically INITIATED by:

- **A.** The annual budget approval
- **B.** A scheduled office cleaning
- **C.** The incident response process identifying an event that warrants investigation
- **D.** The marketing department's campaign launch

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Incident response initiates the investigation when an event is detected/confirmed that requires deeper inquiry (administrative, civil, criminal, or regulatory).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Marketing campaigns do not trigger security investigations.
- A: Budget approval is unrelated.
- B: Office cleaning is unrelated.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Investigation Process*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0078 — Evidence / Opinion evidence  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Opinion evidence is provided by:

- **A.** A witness who saw the event firsthand
- **B.** A network load balancer
- **C.** Subject-matter experts offering professional opinions on the evidence
- **D.** A non-witness relaying secondhand information

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Opinion evidence comes from qualified experts offering professional opinions to help interpret technical evidence.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Firsthand observation is direct evidence.
- D: Secondhand information is hearsay.
- B: A load balancer is a network device, not an evidence source.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Testimonial Evidence*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0079 — Investigations / Criminal investigation  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A criminal investigation differs from the other investigation types in that it:

- **A.** Uses no evidence at all
- **B.** Only addresses internal company policy
- **C.** Cannot involve law enforcement
- **D.** Concerns potential violations of criminal law and can lead to prosecution

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A criminal investigation addresses potential violations of criminal law, may involve law enforcement, and can lead to prosecution under the highest standard of proof.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Internal policy matters are administrative investigations.
- C: Criminal investigations commonly involve law enforcement.
- A: Evidence is central to a criminal investigation.

*Source: Domain 7 > Incident Management > Types of Investigations*

---

### CISSP-D07-Q0080 — Incident Management / Remediation vs recovery  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In incident handling, how does remediation differ from recovery?

- **A.** Recovery deletes all evidence
- **B.** They are the same step with different names
- **C.** Remediation restores systems; recovery assigns blame
- **D.** Recovery restores normal operations; remediation addresses the root cause to prevent recurrence

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Recovery returns affected systems to normal operation; remediation fixes the underlying root cause/vulnerability so the incident does not recur.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: They are distinct steps with different goals.
- C: This reverses the definitions; remediation addresses root cause.
