A KEY difference between compiled and interpreted languages is that compiled languages:

- **A.** Are always executed line-by-line from source at runtime
- **B.** Require no translation to run
- **C.** Cannot be used to build secure software
- **D.** Are translated to machine code before execution, so the source is not distributed to run it

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Compiled languages are translated to machine code ahead of execution (the distributed artifact is the binary), while interpreted languages are translated/executed at runtime from source.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Line-by-line runtime execution describes interpreted languages.
- C: Both compiled and interpreted languages can build secure software.
- B: Compiled languages require compilation; interpreted require an interpreter.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Compiled vs. Interpreted Languages*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0047 — Programming Languages / Interpreted security note  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An interpreted application is distributed as human-readable source. From a security standpoint, what is a notable consideration?

- **A.** It is impossible to reverse engineer in any way
- **B.** It cannot contain vulnerabilities
- **C.** The source code (and any embedded secrets) is readable by anyone who obtains it
- **D.** It automatically hides all logic from users

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Interpreted code is distributed as source, so its logic—and any embedded secrets or vulnerabilities—are directly visible to anyone with the files; secrets must not be hardcoded.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Even compiled binaries can be reverse engineered; interpreted source is trivially readable.
- D: Distributing source exposes, rather than hides, the logic.
- B: Any code can contain vulnerabilities.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Compiled vs. Interpreted Languages*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0048 — Programming Languages / Generations  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

In the programming language generations, which generation corresponds to machine language (binary)?

- **A.** Fourth generation (4GL)
- **B.** First generation (1GL)
- **C.** Third generation (3GL)
- **D.** Fifth generation (5GL)

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** First-generation languages (1GL) are machine language (binary) executed directly by hardware; higher generations are progressively more abstract/human-readable.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: 3GLs are high-level languages (e.g., C, Java), not binary.
- A: 4GLs are higher-level, often domain-specific languages.
- D: 5GLs are associated with AI/constraint-based programming.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Programming Language Generations*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0049 — Programming Languages / Code libraries  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Reusable code libraries enhance efficiency and consistency, but from a security perspective they must be:

- **A.** Properly managed and kept updated, since vulnerable libraries introduce supply chain risk
- **B.** Trusted blindly regardless of source
- **C.** Avoided entirely in all software
- **D.** Never updated once included

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Libraries speed development but can carry vulnerabilities; they must be vetted, kept updated, and tracked (supply chain risk management) to remain secure.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Outdated libraries accumulate known vulnerabilities.
- B: Blind trust ignores supply chain risk (e.g., malicious or vulnerable components).
- C: Libraries are valuable; the goal is secure management, not avoidance.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Code Libraries*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0050 — Programming Languages / SDK/IDE  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

A Software Development Kit (SDK) typically provides:

- **A.** Backup power for build servers
- **B.** A wireless encryption key
- **C.** Tools, libraries, and resources to build applications for a platform (sometimes including third-party resources)
- **D.** Physical access control to the office

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** An SDK bundles tools, libraries, documentation, and sometimes third-party resources to develop for a given platform; an IDE is the integrated coding environment.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Backup power is a UPS/generator function.
- D: Physical access control is a facility control.
- B: A wireless key is a network secret.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Software Development Kit (SDK)*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0051 — Programming Languages / SDK supply chain  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

A security risk specific to SDKs that include third-party resources is that:

- **A.** They make all code automatically secure
- **B.** They cannot be scanned for vulnerabilities
- **C.** They eliminate the need for testing
- **D.** Bundled third-party components can introduce vulnerabilities or malicious code (supply chain risk)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SDKs may include third-party libraries/components that carry their own vulnerabilities or malicious code, so they must be vetted and tracked (supply chain risk management).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Including an SDK does not guarantee security.
- B: SDK components can and should be scanned.
- C: Testing is still required when using SDKs.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Software Development Kit (SDK)*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0052 — Application Security Testing / SAST  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Static Application Security Testing (SAST) analyzes:

- **A.** A running application by sending it inputs
- **B.** Only the network perimeter
- **C.** Only physical access logs
- **D.** Source code or binaries WITHOUT executing the application

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** SAST (white-box) examines source code/binaries without running the application, finding issues early in development.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Analyzing a running app via inputs is DAST.
- B: Network-perimeter testing is not SAST.
- C: Physical access logs are unrelated to SAST.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Testing Approaches*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0053 — Application Security Testing / DAST  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Dynamic Application Security Testing (DAST) is BEST described as:

- **A.** Reading source code statically without running it
- **B.** A disaster recovery drill
- **C.** A formal code inspection meeting
- **D.** Testing a running application from the outside (black-box) by sending inputs and observing behavior

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** DAST (black-box) tests a running application by sending inputs and observing responses, finding runtime/exploitable issues.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Static source reading is SAST.
- C: A formal inspection is a manual code review (e.g., Fagan).
- B: A DR drill is a continuity exercise.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Testing Approaches*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0054 — Application Security Testing / IAST  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Interactive Application Security Testing (IAST) combines aspects of SAST and DAST by:

- **A.** Only scanning the network perimeter
- **B.** Instrumenting the running application to observe code behavior during execution/testing
- **C.** Replacing the need for all other testing
- **D.** Only reading static source code

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** IAST instruments the application (agents inside the running app) to observe code execution during testing, combining static insight with dynamic context.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Static-source-only analysis is SAST.
- A: Perimeter scanning is not IAST.
- C: IAST complements, not replaces, other testing.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Types of Fuzz Testing*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0055 — Application Security Testing / White box vs black box  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A tester is given full access to the application's source code and internal design to perform the assessment. This testing approach is:

- **A.** White box testing
- **B.** A tabletop exercise
- **C.** A reciprocal agreement
- **D.** Black box testing

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** White box testing gives the tester full knowledge of internals (source, design); black box provides no internal knowledge.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Black box testing provides no internal knowledge.
- C: A reciprocal agreement is a DR site arrangement.
- B: A tabletop exercise is a DR test.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Testing Approaches*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0056 — Application Security Testing / Fuzz testing  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Fuzz testing assesses software by:

- **A.** Encrypting the database
- **B.** Reviewing the marketing plan
- **C.** Only validating documented happy-path inputs
- **D.** Sending invalid, unexpected, or random inputs to find flaws or crashes

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Fuzz testing injects invalid/unexpected/random inputs to discover vulnerabilities, crashes, or unexpected behavior.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Only happy-path validation misses edge cases fuzzing targets.
- B: Marketing plans are unrelated.
- A: Database encryption is a separate control.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Additional Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0057 — Application Security Testing / Mutational fuzzing  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Mutational fuzzing generates test inputs by:

- **A.** Building inputs from a formal model of the expected format
- **B.** Encrypting the inputs
- **C.** Mutating existing valid data samples into malformed variants
- **D.** Reading the source code only

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Mutational fuzzing alters existing valid inputs to create malformed test cases (vs. generational/smart fuzzing, which builds inputs from a model).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Building from a model describes generational (smart) fuzzing.
- D: Reading source only is static analysis (SAST).
- B: Fuzzing malforms inputs; it does not encrypt them.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Types of Fuzz Testing*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0058 — Application Security Testing / Testing-type mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each application security testing technique (1-4) to its description (A-D).

1. SAST
2. DAST
3. IAST
4. Fuzz testing

- **A.** Tests a running application from the outside (black-box)
- **B.** Sends invalid/random inputs to find flaws
- **C.** Instruments the running app to observe code during execution
- **D.** Analyzes code without executing it (white-box)

**Correct answer:** 1-D,2-A,3-C,4-B

**Why correct:** SAST → static code analysis (B); DAST → running-app black-box testing (A); IAST → instrumented runtime (D); Fuzz testing → invalid/random inputs (C).

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Testing Approaches*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0059 — Software Assurance / ASVS Level 3  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A critical application must defend against advanced persistent threats (APTs) with the most extensive controls. Which OWASP ASVS level applies?

- **A.** Level 2 (Moderate)
- **B.** Level 3 (High)
- **C.** There is no such standard
- **D.** Level 1 (Low)

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** ASVS Level 3 (High) is for applications critical to operations, protecting against advanced threats including APTs, with the most extensive controls.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Level 1 (Low) is the most basic assurance level.
- A: Level 2 (Moderate) defends against most risks but is below the APT-focused Level 3.
- C: OWASP ASVS is a recognized standard.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > ASVS Assurance Levels*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0060 — Software Assurance / ASVS Level 2  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

OWASP ASVS Level 2 (Moderate) is intended to:

- **A.** Eliminate the need for any testing
- **B.** Defend only against advanced persistent threats
- **C.** Defend against most application security risks, including skilled/motivated attackers
- **D.** Provide only the most basic assurance

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** ASVS Level 2 is sufficient for most applications, defending against most risks and skilled, motivated attackers (e.g., standard pen-test resistance).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Basic assurance is Level 1.
- B: APT-grade defense is Level 3.
- A: ASVS guides testing requirements; it does not remove testing.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > ASVS Assurance Levels*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0061 — Software Assurance / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Software assurance aims to ensure that software:

- **A.** Functions as intended while protecting against threats and vulnerabilities
- **B.** Is the cheapest possible to produce
- **C.** Never needs to be tested
- **D.** Has the most marketing features

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Software assurance provides confidence that software functions as intended and is protected against threats/vulnerabilities.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Lowest cost is not the goal of assurance.
- D: Marketing features are not software assurance.
- C: Assurance relies on testing, not its absence.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Software Assurance*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0062 — Software Assurance / Determining ASVS level  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

The appropriate ASVS assurance level for an application is determined primarily by:

- **A.** The color of the application's logo
- **B.** The brand of the developers' laptops
- **C.** The application's criticality and risk (informed by the SDLC)
- **D.** The number of developers on the team

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** The required assurance level is chosen based on the application's criticality and risk profile, informed by the SDLC—higher risk warrants higher assurance.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Logo color is irrelevant to assurance level.
- D: Team size does not determine the assurance level.
- B: Laptop brand is irrelevant.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Determining Assurance Levels*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0063 — Application Security Testing / Unit vs integration  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Which test verifies that two or more components work correctly TOGETHER?

- **A.** Unit test
- **B.** Smoke test
- **C.** Sanity test
- **D.** Integration test

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Integration testing verifies that combined components/modules interact correctly; unit testing checks a single component in isolation.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Unit testing checks one component in isolation.
- B: Smoke testing is a quick post-build basic-functionality check.
- C: A sanity test quickly validates a specific feature's viability.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Additional Types of Software Tests*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0064 — Programming Languages / Runtime environment  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A runtime environment provides:

- **A.** The platform/services in which a program executes (e.g., interpreters, libraries, memory management)
- **B.** A backup generator
- **C.** Physical security for the data center
- **D.** A wireless access point

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** A runtime environment supplies the execution platform and services (interpreters/VMs, libraries, memory management) a program relies on at run time.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Physical security is a facility control.
- B: A generator is power equipment.
- D: A WAP is a wireless network device.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Runtime Environment*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0065 — Application Security Testing / Testing approaches  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are recognized application security testing techniques? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** War driving
- **B.** Degaussing
- **C.** DAST (dynamic)
- **D.** SAST (static)

**Correct answer:** C, D

**Why correct:** SAST and DAST (along with IAST and fuzzing) are application security testing techniques.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Degaussing is a media-destruction method.
- A: War driving is a wireless reconnaissance technique.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Testing Approaches*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0066 — Programming Languages / IDE  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

An Integrated Development Environment (IDE) primarily provides developers with:

- **A.** A backup power supply
- **B.** A physical door lock
- **C.** A fire suppression system
- **D.** A unified application combining editor, build tools, and debugger

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** An IDE bundles a code editor, build/compile tools, and a debugger into one application to streamline development.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A backup power supply is a UPS/generator.
- B: A door lock is a physical control.
- C: Fire suppression is a physical safety control.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Software Development Kit (SDK)*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0067 — Software Assurance / ASVS Level 1  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

OWASP ASVS Level 1 represents:

- **A.** The lowest/basic security assurance level
- **B.** A moderate assurance level
- **C.** The highest assurance level for APT defense
- **D.** A networking protocol

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** ASVS Level 1 is the lowest, basic assurance level; Level 2 is moderate and Level 3 is high (APT-grade).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: APT-grade high assurance is Level 3.
- B: Moderate assurance is Level 2.
- D: ASVS is an assurance standard, not a networking protocol.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > ASVS Assurance Levels*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0068 — Application Security Testing / Approach selection  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A team wants to catch insecure coding patterns as early as possible during development, before the app even runs. Which testing technique fits BEST?

- **A.** War driving
- **B.** A full interruption DR test
- **C.** SAST (static analysis of source code)
- **D.** DAST (testing the running application)

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SAST examines source code without execution, enabling early detection of insecure patterns during development (shift-left).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: DAST requires a running application, so it is later than SAST.
- B: A full interruption test is a DR exercise.
- A: War driving is wireless reconnaissance, unrelated to code testing.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Application Security Testing*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0069 — Application Attacks / SQL injection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

SQL injection occurs when:

- **A.** A backup tape is degaussed
- **B.** A user changes the wireless encryption key
- **C.** Untrusted input is included in a database query, allowing an attacker to run unauthorized SQL
- **D.** A server loses power

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SQL injection sends crafted untrusted input that a web application incorporates into a database query, letting an attacker execute unauthorized SQL commands.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Changing a wireless key is unrelated to SQL injection.
- A: Degaussing destroys magnetic media.
- D: Power loss is unrelated to injection attacks.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Injection Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0070 — Application Attacks / Injection mitigation  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A web app builds SQL queries by concatenating user input. Which control MOST directly prevents SQL injection?

- **A.** Disabling logging on the database
- **B.** Using a faster database server
- **C.** Input validation plus parameterized queries/approved stored procedures
- **D.** Increasing the office Wi-Fi range

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Validating input and using parameterized queries/whitelisted stored procedures (so input is treated as data, not executable SQL) directly prevent SQL injection.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Disabling logging hides activity and does not prevent injection.
- B: A faster server does not address the injection vulnerability.
- D: Wi-Fi range is unrelated to injection defense.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Mitigations for Injection Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0071 — Application Attacks / LDAP/XML injection  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

LDAP injection differs from SQL injection in that LDAP injection targets:

- **A.** A backup generator
- **B.** A wireless access point
- **C.** A directory server (e.g., LDAP/Active Directory) rather than a SQL database
- **D.** A fire suppression system

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** LDAP injection submits unauthorized queries to a directory server (LDAP/Active Directory); SQL injection targets relational databases; both exploit unvalidated input.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A WAP is a wireless device, not a directory server.
- A: A generator is power equipment.
- D: Fire suppression is a physical control.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Injection Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0072 — Application Attacks / Cross-site scripting  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) primarily allows an attacker to:

- **A.** Physically enter a secure facility
- **B.** Drain a UPS battery
- **C.** Reformat a hard drive remotely with no access
- **D.** Inject malicious scripts that run in other users' browsers via a vulnerable web app

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** XSS injects attacker-controlled scripts that execute in victims' browsers (e.g., to steal sessions/data); output encoding and input validation mitigate it.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: XSS is a web attack, not physical entry.
- B: XSS does not drain batteries.
- C: XSS does not reformat drives without access.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > OWASP Top 10 Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0073 — Application Attacks / Session hijacking  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Session hijacking takes over an active session by:

- **A.** Stealing/replaying session tokens, cookies, or credentials
- **B.** Physically removing the server
- **C.** Disabling the air conditioning
- **D.** Degaussing a backup tape

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Session hijacking uses stolen session tokens/cookies/credentials (sometimes via replay) to assume a legitimate user's active session.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Physically removing a server is theft, not session hijacking.
- D: Degaussing destroys media.
- C: Disabling AC is an environmental disruption, unrelated to sessions.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Hijacking Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0074 — Application Attacks / TOCTOU  _(hard, scenario_based)_

An attacker alters a file AFTER an application checks its permissions but BEFORE the application uses it, exploiting the timing gap. This is a:

- **A.** War driving
- **B.** SQL injection
- **C.** Cross-site scripting
- **D.** Time-of-Check to Time-of-Use (TOCTOU) race-condition attack

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** A TOCTOU attack exploits a race condition by changing a resource between the moment it is checked and the moment it is used, altering the outcome.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: SQL injection manipulates database queries, not check/use timing.
- C: XSS injects scripts into browsers.
- A: War driving is wireless reconnaissance.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Rootkit Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0075 — Application Attacks / Privilege escalation  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Vertical privilege escalation occurs when an attacker:

- **A.** Encrypts files for ransom
- **B.** Moves laterally to another account at the same privilege level
- **C.** Sends spam over instant messaging
- **D.** Gains higher privileges (e.g., from a normal user to administrator)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Vertical privilege escalation raises the attacker's privilege level (e.g., user to admin); horizontal escalation moves laterally at the same level.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Same-level lateral movement is horizontal privilege escalation.
- A: Encrypting files for ransom is ransomware.
- C: Spam over IM is spim, unrelated to escalation.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Rootkit Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0076 — Application Attacks / Rootkit detection  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Rootkits, which provide stealthy root/admin-level access, are BEST detected by:

- **A.** Host-based intrusion detection systems (HIDS)
- **B.** A wireless access point
- **C.** A backup generator
- **D.** A taller perimeter fence

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Because rootkits embed deep in the host (OS, boot sector, firmware), host-based IDS (HIDS) is best positioned to detect their presence/changes.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A fence is a physical control, irrelevant to rootkit detection.
- C: A generator provides power.
- B: A WAP is a wireless network device.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Rootkit Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0077 — Application Attacks / Backdoor  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A backdoor in software is:

- **A.** A type of fire suppression
- **B.** A backup power source
- **C.** A means of bypassing normal authentication, sometimes left by developers for maintenance
- **D.** A standard secure login mechanism

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A backdoor bypasses normal authentication to grant access; developers may leave them for maintenance, but they are a serious security risk if discovered/abused.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: A backdoor circumvents, rather than provides, secure authentication.
- A: Fire suppression is a physical control.
- B: A backdoor is not a power source.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Hijacking Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0078 — Application Attacks / Attack-to-description mapping  _(hard, matching)_

Match each application attack (1-4) to its description (A-D).

1. SQL injection
2. Cross-site scripting (XSS)
3. TOCTOU
4. Session hijacking

- **A.** Runs unauthorized SQL via untrusted input
- **B.** Exploits a race condition between check and use
- **C.** Runs malicious scripts in other users' browsers
- **D.** Takes over an active session using stolen tokens

**Correct answer:** 1-A,2-C,3-B,4-D

**Why correct:** SQL injection → unauthorized SQL via input (B); XSS → scripts in users' browsers (A); TOCTOU → check/use race condition (D); Session hijacking → stolen-token session takeover (C).

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Application Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0079 — OWASP Top 10 / Purpose  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

The OWASP Top 10 is BEST described as:

- **A.** A physical access control model
- **B.** A wireless encryption standard
- **C.** A backup rotation schedule
- **D.** A list of the most critical web application security risks

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** The OWASP Top 10 ranks the most critical web application security risks (e.g., injection, broken access control), guiding secure development.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Wireless encryption standards are WPA2/WPA3.
- C: Backup rotation is an operations schedule.
- A: Physical access models are unrelated to OWASP Top 10.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > OWASP Top 10 Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0080 — OWASP Top 10 / Broken access control  _(medium, scenario_based)_

A user changes the account ID in a URL and gains access to another user's data. Which OWASP category does this represent?

- **A.** A wireless jamming attack
- **B.** A backup failure
- **C.** A power surge
- **D.** Broken access control

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Accessing another user's data by manipulating a parameter is broken access control (e.g., insecure direct object reference)—a top OWASP risk.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: A backup failure is an availability/recovery issue.
- C: A power surge is an electrical anomaly.
- A: Wireless jamming is a network DoS.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > OWASP Top 10 Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0081 — OWASP Top 10 / SSRF  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF), added in the OWASP 2021 list, allows an attacker to:

- **A.** Physically unlock a door
- **B.** Encrypt all backups
- **C.** Make the server send crafted requests to unintended internal/external resources
- **D.** Drain a UPS battery

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** SSRF abuses a server's ability to make requests, tricking it into accessing unintended internal/external resources (e.g., internal metadata services).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: SSRF is a web attack, not a physical door control.
- B: SSRF does not encrypt backups.
- D: SSRF does not drain batteries.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > OWASP Top 10 Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0082 — OWASP Top 10 / 2021 additions  _(hard, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO risks were notably added/elevated in the OWASP Top 10 2021 list? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** UPS battery aging
- **B.** Insecure design
- **C.** Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF)
- **D.** Wet-pipe sprinkler failure

**Correct answer:** B, C

**Why correct:** The 2021 OWASP Top 10 added insecure design and SSRF (among reorganizations); the others are physical/facility issues.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Sprinkler failure is a physical fire-safety issue.
- A: UPS battery aging is a power-equipment concern.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > OWASP Top 10 Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0083 — Secure Coding / Input validation  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Which is a fundamental secure coding practice that defends against many injection attacks?

- **A.** Hardcoding admin passwords in the source
- **B.** Trusting all user input by default
- **C.** Validating and sanitizing all untrusted input
- **D.** Disabling error handling entirely

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Input validation/sanitization (treating untrusted input as untrusted) is a core secure-coding practice that mitigates injection and related attacks.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Trusting all input invites injection and other attacks.
- D: Proper error handling is itself a secure-coding practice.
- A: Hardcoding credentials is a serious vulnerability.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Secure Coding Practices*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0084 — Secure Coding / Error handling  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Proper secure error handling means an application should:

- **A.** Fail gracefully without revealing sensitive details (e.g., stack traces) to users
- **B.** Crash without logging anything
- **C.** Email passwords on every error
- **D.** Display full database errors and stack traces to all users

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Secure error handling avoids leaking sensitive internal details (stack traces, queries) to users while logging enough internally for diagnosis.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Exposing detailed errors aids attackers (information disclosure).
- B: Crashing without logging removes diagnostic/audit capability.
- C: Emailing passwords is a severe security flaw.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Secure Coding Practices*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0085 — API Security / Risks  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A common API security risk highlighted in the guide is:

- **A.** Insufficient authentication and excessive data exposure
- **B.** Overuse of backup generators
- **C.** Excessive fire drills
- **D.** Too much physical security

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** APIs commonly suffer from weak authentication and excessive data exposure; strong authentication, encryption, and input validation mitigate these.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Physical security is unrelated to API risk.
- B: Generator usage is unrelated to API security.
- C: Fire drills are a safety activity, not an API risk.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Software API Security*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0086 — API Security / Controls  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO controls help secure an API? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Returning all internal data by default
- **B.** Encryption of API traffic with TLS
- **C.** Open access with no authentication
- **D.** Strong authentication of API callers

**Correct answer:** B, D

**Why correct:** APIs should enforce strong authentication and encrypt traffic (TLS), along with input validation, to limit exposure and abuse.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Open, unauthenticated access invites abuse.
- A: Returning all internal data is excessive data exposure.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Software API Security*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0087 — Acquired Software / COTS  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Commercial Off-The-Shelf (COTS) software is:

- **A.** Government-licensed software
- **B.** A custom in-house build
- **C.** Free software maintained by a community
- **D.** Commercial software purchased and managed by the organization

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** COTS is commercially available software the organization purchases and manages (e.g., a licensed productivity suite).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- C: Community-maintained free software is OSS/FOSS.
- A: Government-licensed software is GOTS.
- B: A custom in-house build is bespoke development, not COTS.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Types of Acquired Software*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0088 — Acquired Software / OSS/FOSS  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Open-Source/Free Software (OSS/FOSS) is characterized by:

- **A.** Always being purchased from a single commercial vendor
- **B.** Being community-maintained and often hosted on platforms like GitHub
- **C.** Being government-only software
- **D.** Having no source code available

**Correct answer:** B

**Why correct:** OSS/FOSS is typically free, community-maintained software with available source, often hosted on platforms like GitHub.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: Commercial single-vendor purchase describes COTS.
- C: Government-only software is GOTS.
- D: OSS makes source code available by definition.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Types of Acquired Software*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0089 — Acquired Software / NDI supply chain  _(hard, scenario_based)_

A COTS product bundles several third-party libraries (NDIs). What is the PRIMARY security concern, and the BEST response?

- **A.** Bundled libraries are always more secure than custom code
- **B.** There is no risk because the vendor is commercial
- **C.** Supply chain risk from the bundled components; vet them and apply supply chain risk management
- **D.** Third-party components never need updates

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Non-Developmental Items (NDIs)—third-party libraries bundled with products—introduce supply chain risk; they must be vetted, tracked, and managed (SCRM).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Commercial vendors' bundled components can still contain vulnerabilities.
- A: Bundled libraries are not inherently more secure.
- D: Third-party components require updates as vulnerabilities emerge.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Types of Acquired Software*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0090 — Acquired Software / Software-type mapping  _(easy, single_best_answer)_

Software acquired from the government, usually under a government license, is categorized as:

- **A.** COTS
- **B.** OSS/FOSS
- **C.** NDI
- **D.** GOTS

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** GOTS (Government Off-The-Shelf) is software acquired from the government, typically used under a government license.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: COTS is commercial purchased software.
- B: OSS/FOSS is community-maintained free software.
- C: NDIs are third-party components bundled into other products.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Types of Acquired Software*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0091 — Acquired Software / Acquisition policy  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

A security-focused software acquisition policy should ensure the organization:

- **A.** Trusts all vendors without evaluation
- **B.** Buys whichever software is cheapest with no security review
- **C.** Communicates security/compliance requirements to vendors and applies supply chain risk management
- **D.** Never tests acquired software before deployment

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** A sound acquisition policy communicates security/compliance requirements (e.g., GDPR, HIPAA) to vendors, uses trusted vendors, and applies SCRM—especially for bundled NDIs.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Cheapest-with-no-review ignores security risk.
- D: Acquired software should be tested before production.
- A: Vendors should be evaluated, not blindly trusted.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Security Considerations for Acquired Software*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0092 — Acquired Software / NIST acquisition steps  _(hard, scenario_based)_

Per NIST acquisition guidance, an organization confirms a provider meets its security needs BEFORE finalizing the purchase. Which step is this?

- **A.** Acceptance
- **B.** Selection
- **C.** Monitoring
- **D.** Preparation

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** In the NIST acquisition approach (Preparation, Selection, Agreement Management, Monitoring, Acceptance), Acceptance confirms compliance with security needs before purchasing/licensing.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Preparation defines security needs and includes them in requests.
- B: Selection evaluates vendors for trustworthiness.
- C: Monitoring assesses ongoing performance/SLA adherence after acquisition.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > NIST Acquisition Controls*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0093 — Acquired Software / Cloud lease models  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

In the SaaS model for acquired software, the customer is primarily responsible for:

- **A.** Its own data and access controls (the provider manages the OS and supporting applications)
- **B.** Maintaining the provider's physical data center
- **C.** Writing the SaaS application's source code
- **D.** Patching the provider's operating system

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** In SaaS, the provider manages the OS and supporting applications, while the customer manages its data and access controls.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: OS patching is the provider's responsibility in SaaS.
- B: Physical data-center maintenance is the provider's responsibility.
- C: The provider develops/maintains the SaaS application.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Cloud-Based Lease Software Models*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0094 — Acquired Software / Acquired software testing  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Before deploying acquired software to production, a best practice is to:

- **A.** Assume it is secure because it was purchased
- **B.** Deploy it immediately with no evaluation
- **C.** Disable all monitoring of it
- **D.** Conduct security testing (e.g., vulnerability and privacy impact assessments)

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** Acquired software should undergo security testing (vulnerability assessments, privacy impact assessments) before production and be monitored continuously afterward.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Immediate deployment without evaluation risks importing vulnerabilities.
- A: Purchased software is not automatically secure.
- C: Monitoring is needed to catch emerging issues.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Best Practices for Acquired Software*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0095 — Acquired Software / Acquired software types  _(medium, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are categories of acquired software described in the guide? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** OSS/FOSS (open-source/free)
- **B.** COTS (commercial off-the-shelf)
- **C.** XSS
- **D.** TOCTOU

**Correct answer:** A, B

**Why correct:** COTS and OSS/FOSS are acquired-software categories (along with GOTS and NDIs); TOCTOU and XSS are attack types.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: TOCTOU is a race-condition attack.
- C: XSS is a web application attack.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Types of Acquired Software*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0096 — OWASP Top 10 / Top 10 risks  _(easy, multiple_correct_answers)_

Which TWO are risks included in the OWASP Top 10? (Select TWO.)

- **A.** Broken access control
- **B.** Backup tape aging
- **C.** Air-conditioning failure
- **D.** Injection

**Correct answer:** A, D

**Why correct:** Injection and broken access control are OWASP Top 10 web application security risks; the others are physical/facility issues.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Backup tape aging is a media/operations concern, not an OWASP risk.
- C: Air-conditioning failure is an environmental issue, not an OWASP risk.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > OWASP Top 10 Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0097 — Application Attacks / Domain hijacking  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Domain hijacking involves:

- **A.** Draining a UPS battery
- **B.** Stealing a single session cookie
- **C.** Unauthorized changes to a domain's registration/DNS records to redirect traffic
- **D.** Injecting SQL into a database

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Domain hijacking makes unauthorized changes to registered domain records (e.g., DNS) to redirect traffic to attacker-controlled destinations.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Stealing a session cookie is session hijacking.
- D: SQL injection targets databases, not domain records.
- A: Draining a UPS is unrelated to domain hijacking.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Hijacking Attacks*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0098 — Secure Coding / Defense in depth in code  _(hard, scenario_based)_

To mitigate the OWASP Top 10 across an application, the MOST effective approach is to:

- **A.** Add a single firewall rule and consider the app secure
- **B.** Hardcode credentials to simplify access
- **C.** Apply secure coding practices (input validation, output encoding, parameterization, error handling) throughout development
- **D.** Rely solely on a post-release penetration test

**Correct answer:** C

**Why correct:** Building secure coding practices into development (validation, encoding, parameterized queries, proper error handling) addresses the root causes behind the OWASP Top 10.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- A: A single firewall rule does not address application-layer code flaws.
- D: A post-release pen test alone is too late and not comprehensive.
- B: Hardcoding credentials introduces a serious vulnerability.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Secure Coding Practices*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0099 — OWASP Top 10 / Broken authentication  _(medium, single_best_answer)_

Broken authentication (an OWASP Top 10 risk) typically results from:

- **A.** Poorly managed credentials, sessions, or authentication logic
- **B.** Overly strict input validation
- **C.** Too many fire drills
- **D.** Excessive backup power

**Correct answer:** A

**Why correct:** Broken authentication stems from weaknesses in credential, session, or authentication management (e.g., weak passwords, exposed session IDs), enabling account compromise.

**Why the others are wrong:**
- D: Backup power is unrelated to authentication.
- C: Fire drills are a safety activity.
- B: Strict input validation strengthens, not breaks, security.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > OWASP Top 10 Vulnerabilities*

---

### CISSP-D08-Q0100 — Acquired Software / PaaS responsibility  _(hard, single_best_answer)_

In a PaaS model, the customer typically:

- **A.** Has no responsibility for the application
- **B.** Manages the provider's physical facilities
- **C.** Patches the underlying operating system
- **D.** Owns the application (with some limited control over its security) while the provider manages the OS

**Correct answer:** D

**Why correct:** In PaaS, the provider manages the OS/platform while the customer owns the application (with limited control over underlying security).

**Why the others are wrong:**
- B: Physical facilities are the provider's responsibility.
- A: The customer owns/operates the application in PaaS.
- C: OS patching is the provider's responsibility in PaaS.

*Source: Domain 8 > Application Security > Cloud-Based Lease Software Models*
